# International Relations, 1919-39 - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches the twenty years in which the states of the world tried, for the first time in history, to run international relations through a permanent institution rather than through the balance of power alone - and it teaches why that attempt held for roughly a decade and then came apart in eight years. It begins where the previous owner stops: with a settlement already signed, a set of frontiers already drawn, a reparations bill about to be fixed, and a League of Nations that came formally into existence on 10 January 1920, the same day the Versailles Treaty came into operation. It teaches the League as machinery rather than as a slogan - its two stated aims, its five organs, the unanimity rule that governed both the Assembly and the Council, the four permanent Council members, the Permanent Court at the Hague, the Secretariat, and the commissions that handled mandates, minorities, labour, health and disarmament. It teaches the League's real record on both sides of the ledger: the International Labour Organization under Albert Thomas, the refugee work under Fridtjof Nansen, the campaign against typhus, the administration of the Saar and its plebiscite of 1935, and the settlements over the Aaland Islands, Upper Silesia, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier and Mosul - together with the recorded qualification that none of those disputes seriously threatened world peace and none of those decisions went against a major state. It teaches the structural limits that made collective security fragile before any aggressor tested it: the link to the Versailles order, the absence of the United States, the late entry of Germany in 1926 and of the USSR in 1934, the Conference of Ambassadors that overrode the League at Vilna in 1920 and over Corfu in 1923, the absence of a League army, and the resolution of 1923 under which each member would decide for itself whether or not to fight. It teaches the attempt to strengthen the Covenant through the Geneva Protocol of 1924 and its rejection by the next British government. It teaches the reparations question as a security question - the figure of £6600 million fixed in 1921, the failed Genoa Conference of 1922, the Franco-Belgian occupation of the Ruhr in January 1923, the passive resistance, the collapse of the mark, and the divergence between a France that wanted Germany weak and a Britain that wanted Germany solvent. It teaches the reconstruction of the 1920s as a sequence of instruments with dates and contents: the Washington Conferences of 1921-22, the Dawes Plan of 1924 with its loan of 800 million gold marks, the Locarno Treaties of 1925, Germany's entry into the League in 1926, the Kellogg-Briand Pact of 1928 signed by sixty-five states without a sanctions clause, and the Young Plan of 1929 that cut reparations to £2000 million over fifty-nine years. It teaches Locarno exactly - what was guaranteed, by whom, and the eastern frontiers that were not guaranteed - because that single omission is the most examinable fact in the topic. It teaches the disarmament failure, the World Disarmament Conference of 1932-33, the German demand for equality of armaments and the German withdrawal from the conference and then from the League in October 1933. It teaches the Great Depression as a diplomatic variable whose economics belong to another owner, and it teaches the three aggressions that tested collective security and found it hollow: Manchuria from 1931, with the Lytton report and Japan's withdrawal in March 1933; Abyssinia from October 1935, with sanctions that excluded oil, coal and steel; and the Spanish Civil War from 1936, with German and Italian intervention and Anglo-French non-intervention. It teaches German revisionism step by step from 1933 to 1938 - the Polish pact of January 1934, the Saar plebiscite of January 1935, conscription in March 1935, the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935 that broke the Stresa Front, the Rhineland in March 1936, the Rome-Berlin Axis and the Anti-Comintern Pact. It teaches appeasement as a definable policy with two phases and six contemporary justifications, and it teaches the French counter-current that is usually left out - Poincaré, Barthou, Laval - because appeasement was mainly a British policy with which the French did not always agree. It teaches the Anschluss of March 1938, the Sudeten campaign, the Munich Conference of 29 September 1938 from which the Czechs and the Russians were both excluded, and the occupation of Prague on 15 March 1939 that destroyed appeasement's own justification. It teaches Soviet diplomacy across the whole period - Rapallo, the Treaty of Berlin, entry into the League, the pacts of 1935 and their missing military clauses, exclusion from Munich, and the Nazi-Soviet Pact of 23 August 1939. It teaches United States policy as something more complicated than isolation, and British and French constraints as real rather than as excuses. It teaches the failure of collective security as a weighted causal problem rather than a verdict, and it teaches the blame debate with the scholars named and their positions stated as they are recorded. And it teaches counterfactuals honestly - what would have had to be different, and what the evidence does and does not license. Responsibility here is stated about governments, cabinets, foreign ministries and named individuals, never about nations or peoples. The military course of the war that begins on 1 September 1939 belongs to the next owner; the fascist and militarist regimes whose domestic systems generated these foreign policies belong to their own owner; this session ends with the declarations of 3 September 1939.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of a narrative textbook, and the difference matters. A narrative account lists the League, then lists the treaties of the 1920s, then lists the crises of the 1930s, then concludes that appeasement failed - and that structure produces the standard failing script, which is a chronicle that has described a collapse without ever explaining a mechanism. Here the inherited problem is described before the institution, because the League was built to manage a settlement it did not make. The institution's machinery is taught before its record, because the record cannot be assessed without knowing what the machinery could and could not do. The record is taught before the failure analysis, because a verdict reached before the evidence is a prejudice. The reconstruction of the 1920s is taught as a sequence of instruments with their contents and their omissions, because the omissions are what the 1930s used. The Depression is taught as a variable, not as a subject. The three aggressions are taught one at a time and each is scored twice - for what it achieved and for what it taught every watching government. Appeasement is taught as a policy with reasons before it is taught as a failure. Only then does the session turn to weighting, blame and counterfactuals.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the subject's master chronology, the ages-and-eras revision chart, the official syllabus mapping, the folder README, the one core world-history book read for this session, a verified official question ledger, or a verified official statutory or award record | Assert it. Dates, names, instruments, recorded sequences, statutory contents and recorded scholarly positions can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

One further habit belongs with those labels, and it is a teaching discipline rather than a caveat. Where a standard connecting name, date or instrument is needed to hold a sequence together, this session supplies it instead of retreating into vague formulations such as "an agreement was signed", "sanctions were imposed" or "the powers protested". Every such item is verified, and its provenance is recorded in the final ledger rather than repeated in the teaching. Five points in this topic are unsettled or inconsistent inside the evidence base itself - the date on which the secret protocol partitioning Poland was agreed, the year in which Italy's League membership ended, the description of the Marseilles assassination of October 1934, the inclusion of the March 1935 conscription announcement among the results of an invasion that began in October 1935, and the Sudeten German population figure given as both 3 million and 3.5 million - and in every case the disagreement is displayed rather than resolved by preference. One further statement in a held source is corrected rather than displayed, because it is demonstrably wrong on its own terms: the claim that the three Locarno ministers were jointly awarded a single Nobel Peace Prize.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic, and each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent but wrong answer is unusually easy to write.

**The anti-hindsight discipline.** Every decision in this topic is taught from the information available to the person taking it. ✅ The source states the point about appeasement directly: at the time it was being followed there seemed to be many very good things in its favour, and the appeasers were convinced their policy was right. ⚠️ A script that treats 1930s statesmen as either fools or cowards has not explained anything, because it has not explained why intelligent people with access to intelligence reports reached those conclusions. The correct operation is to reconstruct the reasoning, then show where the reasoning was wrong, then say what the error was. The error in appeasement, on the evidence assembled here, was diagnostic rather than moral.

**The anti-inevitability discipline.** The words "inevitable" and "bound to happen" are used in this session only where a named scholar's position is being reported, and they are always accompanied by the record of the position that rejects them. ✅ The evidence base holds both: it records the view that Hitler intended a major war from the beginning and that war was therefore inevitable sooner or later, and it records A. J. P. Taylor's view that the war of 1939, far from being premeditated, was a mistake resulting on both sides of diplomatic blunders. ⚠️ Those are rival readings of the same record, and an answer that states either as settled fact has misreported the state of knowledge.

**The attribution discipline.** Responsibility is stated about governments, cabinets, foreign ministries, general staffs, parties, named organisations and named individuals, never about nations or peoples. This is not politeness; it is accuracy. ⚠️ Collective formulations - "the Germans wanted war", "the British were cowards", "the Italians were opportunists" - are treated in this session as errors of fact, and the remediation set includes them. Where a held source uses a collective formulation, the formulation is reported as that source's wording and is not adopted as this session's own.

**The evidentiary discipline for numbers.** This evidence base holds a specific and limited set of quantities, and this session uses only those it can source: the reparations figures of £6600 million and £2000 million, the Dawes loan of 800 million gold marks, the world arms-expenditure rise from $3.5 billion to around $5 billion between 1925 and 1933, the American wartime loans of almost twelve billion dollars, the naval ratio of three-fifths at Washington and 35 per cent in the Anglo-German agreement, the German military figures announced or reached in 1935 and by the end of 1938, the Manchurian population of thirty million, the 3.5 million Sudeten Germans, the Czechoslovak national composition, the Saar plebiscite result, Henlein's 44 seats in 1935, the Czechoslovak losses after Munich, the German unemployment figure of over six million by 1932, the Yugoslav export share taken by Germany by 1938, and the two-thirds and one-quarter figures on German war-economy investment and employment. No other number appears anywhere. Where the standard literature has series this base does not hold, the session says so and supplies nothing.

**The instrument discipline.** Every agreement in this topic is taught by what it actually obliged its signatories to do, not by its reputation. Locarno is taught by which frontiers it guaranteed and which it did not; Kellogg-Briand by the absence of a sanctions clause; the Covenant by the unanimity rule and by the 1923 resolution; the Franco-Soviet and Czechoslovak-Soviet pacts of 1935 by the military clauses they lacked. ⚠️ A script that names instruments without stating their contents is describing a filing cabinet, not an international order.

**The two-level discipline for responsibility.** ✅ The advanced owner records the rule explicitly for this topic: a blame question must be answered on at least two levels - the immediate decision-makers and the wider structural setting. This session teaches both levels separately so that they can be combined under time pressure, and it teaches the recorded disagreement between scholars who place the weight in different places rather than presenting one of them as settled.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. The First World War's course and the peace settlements of 1919-23 - the terms of Versailles, the mandate mechanism and the redrawal of boundaries - belong to the First World War and aftermath owner; this session uses only the recorded consequences that the diplomats of the 1920s had to manage. The domestic rise and internal systems of the fascist, Nazi and Japanese-militarist regimes belong to the rise-of-fascism owner; this session uses their foreign policies and says so. The Russian Revolution and the internal history of the USSR belong to their own owner; this session uses Soviet diplomacy only. The causes, transmission channels and policy instruments of the Great Depression belong to the world-economy owner; this session uses the Depression as a causal variable and does not re-derive it. The military course of the Second World War from September 1939, and the United Nations, belong to their owners; this session stops at the declarations of 3 September 1939 and hands over.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What is actually being explained - a failed institution, a failed policy, or a failed order? | Foundation |
| 2 | What did the diplomats of 1920 inherit, and which of those inheritances were unmanageable? | Foundation |
| 3 | How was the League built, and what could its machinery actually compel anyone to do? | Foundation |
| 4 | What did the League actually achieve, and why does the list of achievements prove less than it looks? | Core |
| 5 | Was the League's failure a design fault or a political choice? | Core |
| 6 | Reparations and security: why did the same problem look different in Paris and in London? | Core |
| 7 | Dawes and the dollar: how did European stability come to depend on American credit? | Core |
| 8 | Locarno: what exactly was guaranteed, and what was deliberately left open? | Core |
| 9 | Kellogg-Briand and Young: was the Locarno spirit real, or was it prosperity wearing a diplomatic hat? | Core |
| 10 | Why did disarmament fail, and what did its failure hand to Germany? | Core |
| 11 | How did an economic collapse become a diplomatic collapse? | Core |
| 12 | Manchuria, 1931-33: the first defiance, and what every watching government learned from it | Core |
| 13 | Abyssinia, 1935-36: the crisis that destroyed the League's credibility - and the Stresa Front with it | Core |
| 14 | German revisionism, 1933-38: what was taken, in what order, and against what resistance | Core |
| 15 | Spain, 1936-39: what non-intervention was, and what it demonstrated | Core |
| 16 | Appeasement: what was it, why did it look right, and when did it stop being defensible? | Advanced |
| 17 | Britain and France: were the constraints real, and were the two capitals following the same policy? | Advanced |
| 18 | Anschluss to Prague: the year in which appeasement was tested to destruction | Advanced |
| 19 | Soviet diplomacy and the road to Poland: exclusion, the Pact, and 3 September 1939 | Advanced |
| 20 | Why did collective security fail? Weighting the causes without collapsing them | Advanced |
| 21 | Blame, counterfactuals, and how all of this is converted into marks | Synthesis |

*Read the diagram downward once for sequence and then across once for argument. The horizontal pairings are where the marks are. The left-hand column alone produces an institutional essay in which the League fails because it was badly designed; the middle column alone produces a chronicle of crises; the right-hand column alone produces a moral verdict on Neville Chamberlain. The topic is the relation between them, and the examiner's question is almost always a question about that relation: how much of the collapse belongs to the design, how much to the economy, how much to the policy, and how much to the intentions of the governments that broke the order.*

**Scope of this session.** The terms of the Treaty of Versailles and the associated treaties, the mandate mechanism and the redrawal of national boundaries belong to the First World War and aftermath owner; this session uses only those provisions that the interwar diplomats were still arguing about. The domestic rise of Mussolini, Hitler and the Japanese military, and the internal character of their regimes, belong to the rise-of-fascism owner; their foreign policies are owned here. The Russian Revolution and Stalin's internal transformation of the USSR belong to their own owner; Soviet diplomacy is owned here. The causes, transmission and policy instruments of the Great Depression belong to the world-economy owner; the Depression enters this session only as a variable with a diplomatic effect. The military course of the Second World War, and the founding and design of the United Nations, belong to their owners. India's interwar politics belong to the Modern Indian History owners and appear here in one bounded sentence in Lesson 20 and nowhere else.

### Visual: one title, three questions, three different answers

   | THIS IS THE RESPONSIBILITY QUESTION                           |

   AN ANSWER THAT USES THE EVIDENCE FOR QUESTION 1 TO ANSWER
   QUESTION 2 IS THE COMMONEST FAILURE IN THIS TOPIC. THE LEAGUE'S

*The diagram is the reason "the League failed because it had no army" is not a compressed answer but an incomplete one. It is a partial answer to Question 1 and no answer at all to Question 2. ✅ The source draws the line itself in one sentence: the real explanation for the failure of the League was that when aggressive states defied it, the League members, especially France and Britain, were not prepared to support it either by decisive economic measures or by military action.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Put yourself in Geneva in 1930. Fifty-five states are members of an organisation that did not exist eleven years earlier. Germany has been inside it for four years with a permanent Council seat. Sixty-five states have signed a pact renouncing war. Reparations have just been cut and rescheduled. A South African statesman has called the League one of the great miracles of history, and supporters of the League feel optimistic about its future. ✅ All of that is recorded.

Nine years later, the organisation has not met since December 1939 and will be dissolved in 1946. Between those two points, three of its leading members have left, three wars of conquest have been fought by member or former-member states, and the powers that founded it are at war.

The historian's problem, and the examiner's, is the gap between those two paragraphs. Something in the system was not what the optimism of 1930 assumed it was. If you say the design was weak, you have to explain the decade in which the design worked. If you say the members were cowards, you have to explain why the same governments went to war in 1939 over Poland, which was harder to defend than Czechoslovakia. If you say it was the Depression, you have to show the mechanism by which an economic contraction becomes a diplomatic collapse. Each of those is a real historical position, and each is contested.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the single most useful move at the start of any answer on this topic is to say which of the three questions you are answering, and to say that the other two have different answers. It costs one sentence and it organises everything after it.

### 2. Institution, doctrine and order: three words that are not synonyms

This distinction is the working equipment of the whole session, so it is defined once and then used everywhere.

| Term | What it means | In this topic | The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Institution** | A standing body with organs, rules, staff and a budget | ✅ The League of Nations: Assembly, Council, Permanent Court, Secretariat, commissions | Treating a building in Geneva as if it were a policy |
| **Doctrine** | A principle that states agree to act on | ✅ Collective security: if one state attacks another, the members act together to restrain the aggressor by economic or military sanctions | Confusing the failure of a body with the failure of an idea |
| **Order** | The whole set of arrangements - institutional, financial, territorial and diplomatic - that keeps a state system stable | ⚠️ The Versailles-Washington-Locarno complex, plus American credit | Explaining a systemic collapse by naming one of its parts |
| **Instrument** | A specific agreement with named parties and stated obligations | ✅ Locarno 1925; Kellogg-Briand 1928; the Anglo-German Naval Agreement 1935 | Naming treaties without saying what they obliged anyone to do |
| **Policy** | A course of action chosen by a government, for reasons, in conditions | ✅ Appeasement, as defined by the source and pursued by identifiable ministers | Turning a policy into a personal failing |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these five are not five names for the same thing, and an answer that treats them as interchangeable cannot grade anything. The relationship between them is the answer: an institution was built to serve a doctrine, a set of instruments was assembled around it, an order rested on all three plus a flow of credit, and a policy was adopted when the order began to fail. Each layer failed for a different reason and at a different time.

### 3. Where the period divides, and why the division is not arbitrary

✅ **Fact:** the core account states that international relations between the two world wars fall into two distinct phases, with the division at January 1933, the month in which Adolf Hitler came to power in Germany. ✅ Before that, it records, there seemed reasonable hope that world peace could be maintained, in spite of the League's failure to curb Japanese aggression in Manchuria. ✅ After it, there was little chance of preventing a war of some sort, either limited or full-scale, depending on one's interpretation of Hitler's intentions.

✅ **Fact:** the first phase is itself divided into three - 1919-23, the years of dispute over the settlement; 1924-29, the years of improvement; and 1930-33, the years in which economic difficulty contributed to a deterioration in international relations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what the source does *not* say. It does not say that January 1933 made war certain; it says the interpretation of what followed depends on a contested reading of intentions. That qualification is carried through this entire session. The division at 1933 is a division in the *probability distribution*, not a switch from peace to war.

| Phase | Dates | Governing problem | What the period produced |
|---|---|---|---|
| Settlement disputes | ✅ 1919-23 | Reparations, enforcement, revisionism, new states | ✅ Genoa's failure, the Ruhr occupation, the collapse of the mark |
| Reconstruction | ✅ 1924-29 | Reconciling France and Germany without weakening either | ✅ Dawes, Locarno, Germany in the League, Kellogg-Briand, Young |
| Deterioration | ✅ 1930-33 | Economic contraction and its political effects | ✅ Manchuria, the disarmament failure, the Nazi accession |
| Breakdown | ✅ 1933-39 | Three revisionist powers and no enforcement | ✅ Abyssinia, the Rhineland, Spain, Munich, Prague, Poland |

### 4. What the syllabus actually owns here

✅ **Fact:** the subject's syllabus mapping quotes the official General Studies Paper I clause verbatim: "History of the world will include events from 18th century such as industrial revolution, world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.- their forms and effect on the society." ✅ It also quotes a second clause, for Paper II: "Important International institutions, agencies and fora-their structure, mandate."

✅ **Fact:** the mapping records that this folder's direct official ownership is Mains Paper I only, that it supports the international-institutions clause in a background capacity, and that no separate World History clause appears in the supplied verbatim Prelims syllabus.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow. First, this topic sits between two named sub-clauses rather than squarely inside one: it is the connective tissue between "world wars" and the institutional clause, which is exactly why it is more often examined as a "why did X fail" demand than as a "describe X" demand. Second, because the topic is owned by an essay-type paper and not by an objective paper, the preparation target is an argument producible in 150 or 250 words, not a stock of retrievable dates - the dates exist to support the argument. Third, the League's institutional description has a legitimate second use in a Paper II institutions answer, where the comparison with the United Nations is the point; that comparison is owned elsewhere and is used here only as a boundary marker.

### 5. Where this topic starts and stops

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner states the boundary in its own words: the First World War and aftermath owner holds the war's course and the 1919-23 peace settlement; the rise-of-fascism owner holds the fascist regimes and their domestic power systems; the Second World War owner holds that war's military course; and this topic owns the interwar international-relations mechanism connecting those topics.

| Material | Owner | What this session may use |
|---|---|---|
| The terms of Versailles and the associated treaties; the mandate system; the redrawal of national boundaries | The First World War and aftermath owner | ✅ Only the provisions still being argued about after 1920 - reparations, the army limit, the demilitarised Rhineland, the Saar arrangement, the ban on Austro-German union, and the frontiers of the successor states |
| Mussolini's rise and the Italian fascist state; Hitler's rise and the Nazi state; Japanese militarism as a domestic system | The rise-of-fascism owner | ✅ Only their recorded foreign policies and the dates of their accession to power |
| The Russian Revolution; the Soviet economy, collectivisation and the purges | The Russian Revolution and USSR owner | ✅ Only Soviet diplomacy - Comintern's foreign effect, Rapallo, Berlin, League entry, the 1935 pacts, Munich exclusion, the 1939 pact |
| The causes, transmission channels and policy instruments of the Great Depression | The world-economy owner | ✅ Only its recorded diplomatic effects, and the credit-withdrawal mechanism the owner supplies |
| The military course of the Second World War from September 1939 | The Second World War owner | Nothing; this session stops at the declarations of 3 September 1939 |
| The United Nations: founding, structure, mandate, record | The United Nations owner | ✅ Only the recorded statement that the League's organs supplied the UN's institutional blueprint |
| India's interwar politics; the Government of India Act 1935; the Congress ministries | Modern Indian History owners | One bounded sentence in Lesson 20 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary is not bureaucratic. A script that opens a "why did the League fail" answer by narrating the Treaty of Versailles is not showing range; it is answering the previous owner's question and losing the marks allotted to this one. The correct move is a single clause - the League was too closely linked with the Versailles settlement - and then forward into the League's own record.

### 6. The six formulations that lose marks before the first paragraph ends

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each of these is common, each sounds confident, and each is refuted by evidence recorded in this session's own sources.

| Formulation | Why it fails | What to write instead |
|---|---|---|
| "The League failed because it had no army" | True but insufficient: it explains why enforcement was hard, not why members declined the economic measures that were available. ✅ Over Manchuria the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised | "The League lacked coercive instruments and, more decisively, lacked members willing to use the instruments it did have" |
| "The League was a complete failure" | ✅ Contradicted by the recorded ILO, refugee, health, Saar and arbitration record, and by Henig's explicitly recorded corrective | "It failed as a coercive security system and succeeded as the first working experiment in permanent international administration" |
| "Appeasement was cowardice" | Hindsight judgement that explains nothing and matches no scholar's position. ✅ The source records six contemporary justifications and names the ministers who held them | "Appeasement was a rational response to a misdiagnosed problem" |
| "Versailles caused the Second World War" | ✅ Contradicted by the source's own observation that by the end of 1938 most of Germany's grievances had been removed and Germany was a great power again | "The settlement supplied the grievance; it does not explain the demands made after the grievance had been satisfied" |
| "Hitler had a blueprint and followed it" | Reports one contested position as settled. ✅ The source records the blueprint reading, Taylor's denial of it, Broszat's qualification and Mazower's further doubt | "A substantial body of scholarship reads Hitler's aims as programmatic; that reading is contested, and the contest is part of the answer" |
| "The Depression caused fascism, and fascism caused the war" | Two compressions in one sentence, and it skips the transmission mechanism entirely | "Credit withdrawal after 1929 narrowed the space for international co-operation and strengthened governments prepared to risk aggression - a mechanism the world-economy owner supplies and this topic applies" |

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is the three-question separation not an artificial refinement? Examiners set questions about the interwar period as a whole, and a candidate who spends the opening insisting that there are three questions may look evasive rather than precise.*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede that the separation must be done in one sentence and never announced as a method: an answer that says "there are three questions here" and then lists them has spent marks on procedure. Second, note that the evidence base itself refuses to merge them - it grades the League's institutional record and its security record separately, it records that none of the background causes was sufficient and that something more was needed, and it presents the blame question as a live disagreement rather than as a corollary of the institutional failure. ⚠️ A candidate who merges what the sources keep apart is not being concise; they are reporting the evidence inaccurately. Third, observe what the separation buys under time pressure: it tells you immediately which evidence bank to open and which verdict scaffold to close with, which is worth more in a twelve-minute answer than any amount of additional recall.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner records that this topic has **zero direct topic-only verified question routes** in the 2018-2025 span held in this repository, and that the 2021 General Studies Paper I demand on the challenge to the democratic state system between the two World Wars remains owned by the rise-of-fascism topic. ✅ The routing ledgers confirm this: **five** items across 2018-2025 carry the ledger's **World History** subject tag - three in 2018-2023 and two in 2024-2025 - and they are routed to the American Revolution, French Revolution, Industrial Revolution, rise-of-fascism and outbreak-of-WWI owners, none to this one. ✅ A sixth item, the 2024 General Studies Paper I Question 13 on the Industrial Revolution and Indian handicrafts, is tagged **Modern History** in the ledger and routed to a Modern Indian History owner; it is audited here as an adjacent item and is **not** part of the World History allocation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and the difference is not that it matters less. The basic owner's directive map lists six demand families that this topic must independently support, and the folder's answer-worthiness audit treats those families as live. ⚠️ The correct preparation target is therefore *structures*, not *answers*: the ability to produce the right architecture from a cold stem in ninety seconds, for any of the six families. Lesson 21 supplies that procedure and the verified-question section records the audit in full.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the League of Nations fail to preserve peace?
> ⚠️ "Collective security failed because it was never attempted." Examine.
> ⚠️ Account for the breakdown of the interwar international order.

**Answer-use guidance.** In any of these, spend the first two sentences establishing which of the three questions you are answering and stating the institution-doctrine distinction. ⚠️ Examiners reward that opening because it demonstrates that the candidate has grasped the analytical shape of the topic rather than memorised its chronology.

### Traps

- Writing that the League "collapsed in 1939". ✅ The recorded formulation is that after December 1939 it did not meet again and it was dissolved in 1946.
- Treating "the League failed" and "collective security failed" as the same proposition. They take different evidence and have different verdicts.
- Opening a 1930s question with the Treaty of Versailles. One clause of linkage is the maximum.
- Using "inevitable" in your own voice anywhere in this topic.
- Carrying the military course of the Second World War, or the founding of the United Nations, into an interwar answer.
- Saying "the League had no successes" or "the League had many successes" without the recorded qualification that none of its successful cases threatened world peace or went against a major state.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three questions inside one title: did the institution fail, did the doctrine fail, and who caused the war?
2. ✅ The recorded bottom line: the League was only as strong as the determination of its leading members to stand up to aggression, and that determination was lacking in the 1930s.
3. ✅ The period divides at January 1933, with the first phase subdivided 1919-23, 1924-29 and 1930-33.
4. ⚠️ Five layers, not one list: institution, doctrine, order, instrument, policy - each failing for a different reason at a different time.
5. ✅ Syllabus ownership: General Studies Paper I; support only for the Paper II institutions clause; no separate Prelims clause.
6. ✅ Boundary: the settlement belongs to the previous owner, the regimes to the fascism owner, the Depression's mechanism to the world-economy owner, the war's course to the next owner.
7. ✅ This session starts on 10 January 1920 and stops on 3 September 1939.
8. ⚠️ Six prohibited formulations, each refuted by the session's own sources.
9. ✅ Zero verified questions are routed to this topic in the 2018-2025 span; the preparation target is architecture, not a rehearsed answer.
10. ⚠️ Opening architecture: name the question, state the institution-doctrine distinction, promise a graded verdict. Three sentences.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script opens: "The League of Nations was a complete failure because it had no army, and this failure caused the Second World War." Identify the three separate errors and rewrite the sentence as an opening that would earn marks.

**Model answer:** The first error is the word "complete". The evidence base records a substantial non-coercive record - the International Labour Organization under Albert Thomas, the refugee work under Fridtjof Nansen that returned about half a million former prisoners of war from Russia, the campaign against typhus, the administration of the Saar and its 1935 plebiscite, and arbitrated settlements over the Aaland Islands, Upper Silesia, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier and Mosul - together with Ruth Henig's explicitly recorded argument that the League should be seen as a bold step towards international co-operation that failed in some of its aims and succeeded comprehensively in others, and that its organs supplied the blueprint for the United Nations. The second error is the causal weight put on the missing army. The absence of a League force is recorded, as is the 1923 resolution under which each member would decide for itself whether or not to fight; but over Manchuria the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, and economic sanctions required no army. The decisive variable was the willingness of Britain and France, not the contents of the Covenant. The third error is the leap from institutional failure to the outbreak of war, which skips the entire causal chain - the Depression, the three aggressions, the policy of appeasement and the intentions of the governments that broke the order - and cannot be defended against the source's own observation that by the end of 1938 most of Germany's grievances had been removed and Germany was a great power again. A marks-earning opening would read: the League failed as a coercive security system while succeeding as the first working experiment in permanent international administration; its coercive failure was political rather than architectural, because its principal members would not accept risk in advance; and the war of 1939 requires a separate explanation that weighs the unenforced settlement, the economic collapse, the policy of appeasement and the aims of the revisionist governments against one another.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the institution-doctrine distinction is a refinement to be added at the end if there is time. It is the organising principle of the answer. A script that never makes it cannot answer a "how far" or "critically examine" question at all, because every such question asks the candidate to grade one kind of failure against another.

*The next lesson supplies what this one assumed. It has been said that the League was too closely linked with the Versailles order, that reparations were unfixed, that the successor states had problems, and that a French security guarantee lapsed. None of that has yet been described. Before any institution can be assessed, the problem it was built to manage has to be put on the table - and that problem was an inheritance, not a creation.*

## Lesson 2 - The inheritance: what the diplomats of 1920 were handed, and what they could not fix

Progress: 2/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the four inherited problems - reparations unfixed, a lapsed security guarantee, revisionist dissatisfaction and a belt of new states; the recorded terms France obtained and the guarantee that vanished; Italy and Turkey as dissatisfied victors; Soviet Russia outside the system; the successor states named and characterised; the Washington Conferences of 1921-22 as the non-European leg of the same order; the boundary with the settlement owner

Book context: queried - the core account's survey of 1919-23 disturbances arising from the peace settlement; the French security provisions and the lapse of the Anglo-American guarantee; the successor states and their recorded weaknesses; and the Washington Conferences with their five recorded terms and the reality behind them
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 8
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: four inheritances, and which of them the 1920s actually settled

```text
   WHAT THE SETTLEMENT HANDED OVER (owned by the previous topic)
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
        |                |                |                  |
   [1] REPARATIONS   [2] SECURITY    [3] REVISIONISM   [4] NEW STATES
   sum not fixed     German army     Germany resents   Yugoslavia,
   until 1921        capped at       the settlement;   Czechoslovakia,
   at £6600m;        100 000 men;    Italy "cheated";  Austria, Hungary,
   66 annual         Rhineland       Turkey prepared   Poland - every
   instalments       demilitarised   to defy it        frontier contains
                     50 km east;                       a minority
                     Saar to France
                     for 15 years;
                     Anglo-American
                     guarantee ...
                     ... WITHDRAWN
        |                |                |                  |
        v                v                v                  v
   SETTLED BY        NEVER SETTLED   PARTLY SETTLED    NEVER SETTLED
   1924-29:          France tried    Germany brought   Depression hits
   Dawes 1924,       three methods   back into the     agrarian and
   Young 1929,       - all three     club 1925-26;     export economies;
   Lausanne 1932     failed          but the EAST      Austria and
   -> reparations                    left open         Hungary stay
   largely                                             revisionist;
   cancelled                                           only one democracy
                                                       survives to 1938
        |                |                |                  |
        +----------------+----------------+------------------+
                                |
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   NOTE WHAT THIS PATTERN MEANS FOR AN ANSWER: the interwar order
   SOLVED THE FINANCIAL QUESTION AND FAILED ON THE SECURITY QUESTION.
   That is the opposite of the popular account, which says reparations
   ruined everything. Reparations were negotiated down twice and then
   largely cancelled. The frontier and enforcement questions never were.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram is the whole argument of this lesson in one picture. ✅ The source records the cancellation directly: at the Lausanne Conference of 1932 Britain and France released Germany from most of the remaining reparations payments; and it records in its blame chapter that by the end of 1938 reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses had been ignored, the Rhineland was remilitarised, Austria and Germany were united, and 3.5 million Germans had been brought into the Reich from Czechoslovakia. ⚠️ An order that conceded all of that and then went to war cannot have gone to war over the concessions.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A peace settlement is not a peace. It is a list of instructions that somebody has to carry out over the following decades, and the people who carry them out are usually not the people who wrote them. The diplomats of the 1920s inherited a document, a set of frontiers and a bill, and they inherited them in a Europe where the state that had been defeated was still, in population and industry, the strongest on the continent, and where the state that had insisted most loudly on the terms - France - had just lost the guarantee that had persuaded it to accept them.

That is the situation to hold in mind. It is not a situation of good and bad faith. It is a situation in which one government needs enforcement and another needs revision, in which both have domestic publics, and in which the machinery for reconciling the two has just been built and has never been used.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why the topic rewards the word "manage" rather than "solve". The interwar diplomats were managers of an inheritance, and the question the examiner is really asking is which parts of that inheritance were manageable and which were not.

### 2. The security inheritance, stated exactly - and the guarantee that vanished

✅ **Fact:** the source records what France obtained at the Paris conference on Clemenceau's insistence: the German army was to number no more than 100 000 men, with severe limitations on armaments; the German Rhineland was to be demilitarised to a distance of 50 kilometres east of the river; and France was to have the use of the area known as the Saar for fifteen years. ✅ It records that Britain and the United States promised to help France if Germany attacked again, and that although many in France were disappointed - Foch wanted France to be given the whole of the German Rhineland west of the river, whereas the French were only allowed to occupy it for fifteen years - it looked at first as though security was guaranteed.

✅ **Fact:** the guarantee did not survive the year. The source records that the Americans, afraid that membership of the League might involve them in another war and preferring a policy of isolation, rejected the entire peace settlement in March 1920 and abandoned their guarantees of assistance; that the British used this as an excuse to cancel their promises; and that the French understandably felt betrayed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most under-used fact in the whole topic, and it is worth a full sentence in any answer on French policy, on the League, or on the origins of the Second World War. France accepted a settlement that was less than it wanted in exchange for a guarantee, and within fifteen months it had the settlement and no guarantee. Everything French governments did between 1920 and 1935 - the Ruhr, the Little Entente, the demand for a strong League, the Geneva Protocol, the alliance with the USSR - is an attempt to replace a promise that was withdrawn. ⚠️ A script that presents French policy as vindictiveness has not read the sequence.

### 3. The reparations inheritance

✅ **Fact:** the figure was fixed in 1921 at £6600 million, to be paid in annual instalments over sixty-six years. ✅ The source records the reasoning: it was thought that the strain of paying this huge amount would keep Germany economically weak for that period and that another German attack on France would consequently be less likely. ✅ It also records the French justification in its own terms - the French claimed to need the cash from reparations to balance their budget and to pay their own debts to the United States.

✅ **Fact:** the Anglo-French divergence is recorded explicitly and is not a matter of interpretation: France wanted a weak Germany; Britain wanted an economically strong Germany which would be able to buy British exports.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single sentence contains the structural fault of the western alliance for twenty years. It is not a disagreement about Germany's guilt; it is a disagreement about what a safe Europe looks like. A France that measures safety by German weakness and a Britain that measures safety by German prosperity cannot produce a joint policy under pressure - and in 1923, in 1935 and in 1938 they did not.

### 4. The revisionist inheritance: who was dissatisfied, and how dissatisfied

✅ **Fact:** the source records that in the aftermath of the war, relations were disturbed by problems arising from the peace settlement while the newborn League struggled to sort things out, and it names the dissatisfied parties precisely:

| Party | Recorded grievance | Recorded action in 1919-23 |
|---|---|---|
| **Turkey** | ✅ Dissatisfied with its treatment | ✅ Prepared to defy the settlement |
| **Italy** | ✅ Dissatisfied with its treatment; ✅ later recorded as having been "cheated at Versailles" in the appeasers' own view | ✅ Seizure of Fiume, which had been awarded to Yugoslavia; ✅ the Corfu Incident of 1923 |
| **Germany** | ✅ Reparations and the question whether she could afford to pay | ✅ Falling behind with payments; ✅ threatening at Genoa to stop paying |
| **Soviet Russia** | ✅ Not invited to Versailles; ✅ intervened against by several western countries and Japan in the civil war of 1918-20 | ✅ Viewed with suspicion by the western countries |

✅ **Fact:** Fiume is recorded with its own mechanism: by an agreement signed at Rapallo in 1920 it was to be a "free city", used jointly by Italy and Yugoslavia; after Italian troops moved in, Yugoslavia agreed that it should belong to Italy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Fiume episode deserves attention out of proportion to its size, because it is the template for everything that followed. A frontier arrangement was made; a state broke it with force; no third party acted; and the injured party legalised the outcome. That is the whole mechanism of the 1930s, performed in 1923 by a power that was not yet regarded as an aggressor.

### 5. The successor states: what they were and why they were fragile

✅ **Fact:** the source records that one important result of the war in eastern Europe was the break-up of the Austro-Hungarian or Habsburg Empire and the loss of extensive territory by Germany and Russia, and that a number of new national states were formed, of which the most important were Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Austria, Hungary and Poland. ✅ Two guiding principles behind their formation were self-determination and democracy, and it was hoped they would act as a stabilising influence in central and eastern Europe and as a buffer against potential attacks from communist Russia.

✅ **Fact:** the source then records four weaknesses common to all of them:

1. ✅ There were so many different nationalities in the region that it was impossible for them all to have their own state; only the larger national groups had a homeland, and smaller nationalities found themselves once again under what they considered foreign governments - Croats in Yugoslavia, Slovaks and Germans in Czechoslovakia, and Germans, White Russians and Ukrainians in Poland.
2. ✅ Although each state began with a democratic constitution, Czechoslovakia was the only one in which democracy survived for a significant length of time - until the Germans moved in in March 1939.
3. ✅ They all suffered economic difficulties, especially after the onset of the Great Depression in the early 1930s.
4. ✅ They were divided by rivalries and disputes over territory: Austria and Hungary had been on the losing side and greatly resented the settlement, wanting a complete revision of the terms, while Czechoslovakia, Poland and Serbia - which became Yugoslavia - were represented at the peace conference and were on the whole satisfied with the outcome.

✅ **Fact:** Czechoslovakia is recorded in detail because it becomes the crisis of 1938. It was a multinational state of some 6.5 million Czechs, 2.5 million Slovaks, 3 million Germans, 700 000 Hungarians, 500 000 Ruthenians, 100 000 Poles and smaller numbers of Romanians and Jews. ✅ It worked well, being based on a solid partnership between Czechs and Slovaks, with an elected parliament of two houses and an elected president who could choose and dismiss ministers; Tomas Masaryk was president from 1918 until his retirement in 1935. ✅ It was the only example in eastern Europe of a successful western-style liberal democracy. ✅ It contained about three-quarters of the industries of the old Habsburg Empire, with textile and glass factories, valuable mineral resources and rich agricultural lands, and the 1920s were a period of great prosperity in which it became a major exporting country.

✅ **Fact:** the German-speaking population of Bohemia, Moravia and the frontier areas - the Sudetenland - had previously been citizens of the Habsburg Empire and complained at being forced to live in a "Slav" state where they were discriminated against, or so they claimed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "or so they claimed" is the source's own, and it is the correct register for this topic. The Sudeten grievance is recorded as a claim whose factual basis is separately assessed later - and when the source does assess it, it finds that unemployment was indeed more serious among the Germans, but because a large proportion of them worked in industry, where unemployment was most severe because of the depression. ⚠️ That is the difference between a real hardship and a national persecution, and getting it right is worth marks in a Munich question.

### 6. The Washington Conferences, 1921-22: the same order, in a different ocean

✅ **Fact:** the purpose of these meetings was to try to improve relations between the United States and Japan. ✅ The United States was increasingly suspicious of growing Japanese power in the Far East and of Japanese influence in China, especially bearing in mind that during the First World War Japan had seized Kiaochow and all the German islands in the Pacific. ✅ They were called by President Harding.

| Recorded term | Content |
|---|---|
| Naval ratio | ✅ To prevent a naval building race, the Japanese navy was to be limited to three-fifths the size of the American and British navies |
| Shantung | ✅ Japan agreed to withdraw from Kiaochow and the Shantung province of China, which she had occupied since 1914 |
| Mandates | ✅ In return she was allowed to keep the former German Pacific islands as mandates |
| Bases | ✅ The western powers promised not to build any more naval bases within striking distance of Japan |
| Guarantee | ✅ The USA, Japan, Britain and France agreed to guarantee the neutrality of China and to respect each other's possessions in the Far East |

✅ **Fact:** the source's verdict is two-sided and both sides are examinable. At the time the agreements were regarded as a great success and relations between the powers involved improved. ✅ In reality, however, Japan was left supreme in the Far East, possessor of the world's third largest navy, which she could concentrate in the Pacific, while the navies of Britain and the United States, though larger, were spread more widely - which was to have unfortunate consequences for China in the 1930s when the United States refused to become involved in checking Japanese aggression.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Washington is the clearest single illustration of the defect that runs through the whole interwar order. A ratio agreement measures capability globally and is applied regionally. Three-fifths of a globally distributed fleet is more than three-fifths of a locally concentrated one, and the guarantee of Chinese neutrality carried no enforcement clause at all. ⚠️ The instrument was real, the arithmetic was sound, and the strategic effect was the reverse of the intention. This is the same fault that Locarno had in Europe, and stating the parallel in one sentence is a high-value move in a "why did collective security fail" answer.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the settlement was manageable and reparations were negotiated away, why is the settlement blamed at all? Is the "Versailles caused the war" argument simply wrong?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the strongest part of the case: the source itself records that the Versailles Treaties have been blamed for filling the Germans with bitterness and the desire for revenge, and that the League was too closely linked with the Versailles Treaties, which made it seem like an organisation created especially for the benefit of the victorious powers and required it to defend a peace settlement which was far from perfect. ✅ It specifies which provisions were bound to cause trouble - the disappointing territorial gains of the Italians and the inclusion of Germans in Czechoslovakia and Poland. Second, state the limit: grievance explains the demand for revision; it does not explain the demands made after the revision was granted, and the source's own audit of the position at the end of 1938 records that almost all of the original grievances had by then been removed. Third, state the residue: what the settlement left behind that was never removed was not a grievance but a *structural* problem - a belt of small multinational states between two great powers, one of which was excluded from the system and the other of which was a revisionist. ⚠️ That structure, not the resentment, is the settlement's genuine contribution to 1939.

### 8. UPSC application

The material in this lesson serves three of the six demand families identified by the basic owner, and it is used differently in each.

| Demand family | Use of this lesson | Length |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ *Why did the League fail?* | One clause only: too closely linked with the Versailles order, which it had to defend | One sentence |
| ⚠️ *Who caused the Second World War?* | The concession paragraph: grievance was real, grievance was removed, the structure remained | Two to three sentences |
| ⚠️ *Collective security* | Washington as the non-European proof that a well-drafted instrument without enforcement produces a regional imbalance | Two sentences |

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far did the peace settlement of 1919 determine the course of international relations in the 1920s?
> ⚠️ Examine the problems inherited by the international order of the 1920s.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Washington Conferences in interwar diplomacy.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Resist the temptation to narrate the settlement. The mark-earning move is to name the four inheritances, say which two were settled and which two were not, and proceed. Everything the examiner wants from this lesson fits into four sentences.

### Traps

- Narrating the terms of the Treaty of Versailles in an interwar answer. The previous owner holds them; you hold their consequences.
- Forgetting the lapsed Anglo-American guarantee. It is the single most economical explanation of French behaviour for fifteen years.
- Saying reparations "crippled Germany until 1939". ✅ They were rescheduled in 1924, cut in 1929 and largely cancelled at Lausanne in 1932.
- Treating the successor states as uniformly unstable. ✅ Czechoslovakia is recorded as a working liberal democracy with three-quarters of the old Habsburg industry.
- Calling Washington a failure. ✅ It was recorded as a success at the time and produced a real naval limitation; its defect was strategic distribution, not drafting.
- Describing Italy as an aggressor in 1919-23 without noting that it is also recorded among the dissatisfied victors whose grievance the appeasers later regarded as genuine.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four inheritances: reparations unfixed, a lapsed guarantee, revisionist dissatisfaction, a belt of new multinational states.
2. ✅ French security terms: army capped at 100 000; Rhineland demilitarised 50 km east; Saar to France for fifteen years; Anglo-American guarantee promised.
3. ✅ March 1920: the US Senate rejects the settlement and the League; the guarantee lapses; Britain cancels its promise.
4. ✅ Reparations fixed 1921 at £6600 million over sixty-six years; cut to £2000 million in 1929; largely cancelled at Lausanne in 1932.
5. ✅ The structural divergence: France wanted a weak Germany, Britain wanted a solvent one.
6. ✅ Dissatisfied parties: Turkey, Italy (Fiume, Corfu), Germany (reparations), Soviet Russia (excluded and intervened against).
7. ✅ Successor states: Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Austria, Hungary, Poland; four recorded weaknesses; only Czechoslovakia's democracy survives to March 1939.
8. ✅ Czechoslovak composition: 6.5m Czechs, 2.5m Slovaks, 3m Germans, 700 000 Hungarians, 500 000 Ruthenians, 100 000 Poles; about three-quarters of old Habsburg industry.
9. ✅ Washington 1921-22: three-fifths naval ratio; Shantung returned; Pacific mandates retained; no new bases near Japan; four-power guarantee of Chinese neutrality.
10. ⚠️ The Washington lesson, transferable: a correctly drafted global ratio can produce a regional imbalance, and a guarantee without enforcement is a statement of intent.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The harshness of Versailles made German revisionism unstoppable and therefore made the Second World War inevitable." Using only what this lesson establishes, construct the two-part reply that concedes what is true and states precisely where the argument fails.

**Model answer:** The concession is substantial and must be made first, because an answer that refuses it looks evasive. The settlement did generate bitterness and a desire for revenge; the League was too closely tied to it and had to defend a peace that was far from perfect, which made the organisation look like a body created for the benefit of the victors; and specific provisions were always going to cause trouble, notably the disappointing territorial gains of the Italians and the inclusion of German populations inside Czechoslovakia and Poland. The failure of the argument is chronological and is settled by the source's own audit. By the end of 1938 reparations had been rescheduled by the Dawes Plan of 1924, cut from £6600 million to £2000 million by the Young Plan of 1929 and largely cancelled at Lausanne in 1932; the disarmament clauses had been ignored since 1935; the Rhineland had been remilitarised in March 1936; Austria and Germany had been united in March 1938; and 3.5 million Germans had been brought into the Reich from Czechoslovakia in September 1938. Germany was a great power again, and the grievances that revisionism claimed to be correcting had been corrected. An argument that explains 1939 by the resentment of 1919 therefore has to explain why the war came after the resentment had been satisfied rather than while it was live, and it cannot. What the settlement did leave permanently was structural rather than emotional: a belt of small multinational successor states, every one of which contained a minority with a claim, positioned between a revisionist Germany and a Soviet Russia excluded from the system, with a France whose promised Anglo-American guarantee had lapsed in March 1920. That structure is the settlement's real contribution to the later crisis, and it is a different and much stronger argument than harshness.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "the settlement was resented" and "the settlement caused the war" as the same claim. The first is recorded and true. The second requires the resentment to be still operative at the moment of decision, and the evidence base records the opposite. The disciplined move is to convert a moral claim about harshness into a structural claim about geography and enforcement, which survives the objection.

*The inheritance is now on the table. The next lesson turns to the instrument built to manage it. The League has been referred to six times already - as an organisation tied to Versailles, as a body without an army, as a forum that struggled - and it has not yet been described. Before its record can be judged, its machinery has to be known precisely, because almost every question in this topic turns on what that machinery could and could not compel a government to do.*

## Lesson 3 - The League of Nations: origins, aims, organs and the rules that governed them

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the origins as a collective authorship rather than a single brainchild; Wilson's actual contribution; the two stated aims; the five organs with their composition and powers; the unanimity rule in both the Assembly and the Council; the Permanent Court and its record; the commissions and committees; the intended peacekeeping mechanism and the wording of Article 16's expectation; membership numbers and the great-power table

### Visual: the machinery, with the veto point marked at every level

 GENERAL    COUNCIL    PERMANENT  SECRETARIAT  COMMISSIONS

 annual;    smaller;    JUSTICE      paperwork,  mandates,

 members;   least 3x    15 judges    resolutions affairs,

 policy;    Britain,    political)   over 30     for labour,
 finances;  France,     disputes;    countries   health,
 may        Italy,      functioning              economic and
 propose    Japan;      from 1922;               financial

 of peace   (9 by 1926) dealt with               child welfare,

   |          |
