```text
        |
   INTENDED MECHANISM:
   all disputes threatening war -> submitted to the League
   any member resorting to war, thus breaking the Covenant
       -> faces COLLECTIVE ACTION by the rest
   the Council would RECOMMEND "what effective military, naval
   or air force the members should contribute to the armed forces"
        |
   NOTE THE VERB. The Council RECOMMENDS. It does not command,
   it does not possess, and it does not compel. Everything after
   1931 follows from that verb.
```

*The diagram's last line is the lesson. ⚠️ The League was not a government and was never designed to be one; it was a standing conference with a permanent staff and a recommending power. The interesting historical question is therefore not why a recommending body failed to compel - it could not - but why its members, who could have acted, chose not to.*

### 1. Origins: a collective authorship, not a single brainchild

✅ **Fact:** the source corrects the popular attribution directly. The League is often spoken of as being the brainchild of the American President Woodrow Wilson; although Wilson was certainly a great supporter of the idea of an international organisation for peace, the League was the result of a coming together of similar suggestions made during the First World War by a number of world statesmen.

✅ **Fact:** it names them. Lord Robert Cecil of Britain, Jan Smuts of South Africa and Leon Bourgeois of France put forward detailed schemes showing how such an organisation might be set up. Lloyd George referred to it as one of Britain's war aims, and Wilson included it as the last of his Fourteen Points.

✅ **Fact:** Wilson's own contribution is recorded precisely, and it is procedural rather than conceptual. His great contribution was to insist that the League Covenant - the list of rules by which the League was to operate, drawn up by an international committee including Cecil, Smuts, Bourgeois and Paul Hymans of Belgium as well as Wilson himself - should be included in each of the separate peace treaties. ✅ This ensured that the League actually came into existence instead of merely remaining a topic for discussion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Wilson's insistence is the origin of the League's most quoted weakness. Embedding the Covenant in the peace treaties guaranteed that the organisation would exist; it also guaranteed that it would be inseparable from a settlement that a substantial part of Europe rejected. ⚠️ That is a genuine trade-off, not a blunder, and saying so - that the decision which created the League also compromised it - is a more sophisticated observation than either praising or condemning Wilson.

### 2. The two aims, and why their order matters

✅ **Fact:** the source states two main aims. First, to maintain peace through collective security: if one state attacked another, the member states of the League would act together, collectively, to restrain the aggressor, either by economic or by military sanctions. Second, to encourage international co-operation, in order to solve economic and social problems.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two aims are institutionally separable, and the whole verdict on the League depends on noticing that. The second aim required a secretariat, expertise and money, all of which the League had. The first required members to accept in advance an obligation to take costly action against a state that had not attacked them - which is a political commitment, not an institutional capacity. ⚠️ The League therefore succeeded at the aim its machinery could deliver and failed at the aim only its members could deliver. Stating the verdict in that form converts a list into an argument.

### 3. The organs, with composition and powers

| Organ | Composition and frequency | Function | Decision rule |
|---|---|---|---|
| **General Assembly** | ✅ Met annually; representatives of all member states, each with one vote | ✅ Decide general policy; could propose a revision of peace treaties; handled the League's finances | ✅ Decisions had to be unanimous |
| **Council** | ✅ Much smaller; met at least three times a year; four permanent members - Britain, France, Italy and Japan; four other members elected by the Assembly for three-year terms, increased to nine non-permanent members by 1926 | ✅ Deal with specific political disputes as they arose | ✅ Decisions had to be unanimous |
| **Permanent Court of International Justice** | ✅ Based at the Hague in Holland; fifteen judges of different nationalities | ✅ Legal disputes between states, as opposed to political ones | ✅ Started to function in 1922; by 1939 had dealt successfully with 66 cases |
| **Secretariat** | ✅ Members from over thirty different countries | ✅ All the paperwork - preparing agendas, writing resolutions and reports so that decisions could be carried out; a sort of international civil service | ✅ Won respect for the high quality of its organisation and administration |
| **Commissions and committees** | ✅ Formed to deal with specific problems, some arising from the war | ✅ Main commissions: mandates, military affairs, minority groups, disarmament. Committees: international labour, health, economic and financial organization, child welfare, drug problems, women's rights | — |

✅ **Fact:** the United States was to have been a permanent member of the Council but decided not to join.

✅ **Fact:** the source records one advantage of the Assembly that is usually omitted from summaries: it gave small and medium-sized states a chance to raise issues that concerned them and to have their say on world developments.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that clause is worth carrying, because it supplies the evidence for one of the strongest defences of the League. An organisation in which a small state could place an item on the agenda of the great powers was a genuine innovation in international practice, and it is the direct ancestor of the General Assembly of the United Nations. ⚠️ It also explains why small states' loss of faith in the League after 1936 is recorded as a specific consequence and not merely as atmosphere: they had more to lose from its collapse than the great powers did.

### 4. The intended peacekeeping mechanism, quoted where the wording matters

✅ **Fact:** the main function of the League was meant to be peacekeeping, and the intended operation is recorded in sequence: all disputes threatening war would be submitted to the League; any member which resorted to war, thus breaking the Covenant, would face collective action by the rest; and the Council would recommend "what effective military, naval or air force the members should contribute to the armed forces".

✅ **Fact:** the source records separately, in its analysis of failure, that Article 16 expected member states to supply troops if necessary, and that the League had no military force of its own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the two records together and the design becomes clear. The Covenant created an *expectation* backed by a *recommendation*. It did not create an obligation enforceable against a member, and it did not create a force. ⚠️ The distinction between expectation and obligation is the single most transferable idea in this lesson, and it is exactly the distinction a "why did collective security fail" question is testing. Collective security requires states to accept a definite risk in advance in exchange for an indefinite benefit later. The Covenant asked them to; it could not make them.

### 5. Membership: who was in, who was out, and when

✅ **Fact:** there were 42 member states at the beginning and 55 by 1926, when Germany was admitted.

✅ **Fact:** the great-power membership record is set out in the source's own figure:

| Power | Joined | Membership status recorded |
|---|---|---|
| Britain | ✅ 1919 | ✅ Still a member in 1939 |
| France | ✅ 1919 | ✅ Still a member in 1939 |
| Japan | ✅ 1919 | ✅ Left; the narrative dates the withdrawal to March 1933 |
| Italy | ✅ 1919 | ⚠️ The figure's departure cell is ambiguous in the copy held here; the narrative records only that Mussolini threatened withdrawal in 1923 and that Italy was hostile to the League by the mid-1930s. **No departure year is asserted in this session** |
| Germany | ✅ 1926 | ✅ Left in 1933 |
| USSR | ✅ 1934 | ✅ Still a member in 1939 |
| USA | ✅ Never joined | ✅ The Senate rejected both the Versailles settlement and the League in March 1920 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** lay the joining and leaving dates against each other and a striking fact appears: there is no single year in which the League contained all of the world's great powers at once. Before 1926 it lacked Germany and the USSR; from 1933 it lacked Germany and Japan; and it never at any point contained the United States. ⚠️ An organisation designed to deter aggression by the combined weight of its membership was therefore never in possession of that weight. That is a stronger and more specific point than "the great powers were absent", and it can be made in one sentence.

### 6. Comparison: what the machinery could and could not do

| Task | Could the League do it? | Recorded evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Arbitrate a legal dispute between willing parties | ✅ Yes | ✅ 66 cases successfully dealt with by the Permanent Court by 1939 |
| Administer a territory | ✅ Yes | ✅ The Saar, administered efficiently, concluding with the 1935 plebiscite |
| Run a technical programme across frontiers | ✅ Yes | ✅ ILO standards, refugee return, the typhus campaign |
| Settle a frontier dispute between small states | ✅ Yes, when they accepted the verdict | ✅ Aaland 1920; Upper Silesia 1921; Greek-Bulgarian; Mosul |
| Override a great power | ❌ No | ✅ Overruled twice by the Conference of Ambassadors, at Vilna in 1920 and over Corfu in 1923 |
| Compel a member to contribute forces | ❌ No | ✅ Article 16 expected; the 1923 resolution left each member to decide for itself |
| Change its own rules to become stronger | ❌ No | ✅ Several attempts failed because a unanimous vote was needed to change the Covenant, and this was never achieved |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the cruellest and the most examinable. The unanimity rule that paralysed the League in a crisis also prevented the League from removing the unanimity rule. ⚠️ An institution that cannot reform itself except by the procedure that is the problem has a structural, not a political, defect - and that is the strongest form of the "design failure" case. It is answered, but not destroyed, in Lesson 5.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is this level of institutional detail worth memorising for a Mains paper that will never ask "name the organs of the League"?*

⚠️ **The reply.** The organs are not the point; the decision rules are. Every high-value sentence about the League's failure depends on one of four facts from this lesson - unanimity in both political organs, the absence of a force, the recommending rather than commanding power of the Council, and the impossibility of amending the Covenant without unanimity. ⚠️ A candidate who knows those four can write a precise diagnostic paragraph; a candidate who has memorised the five organs without the rules can only produce an organisation chart. The names of the committees, by contrast, are worth one illustrative clause and no more.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Discuss the structure and mandate of the League of Nations and assess its institutional design.
> ⚠️ "The League of Nations was designed to fail." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Compare the institutional design of the League of Nations with that of its successor. *(Boundary: the successor's side of this comparison belongs to the United Nations owner; this session supplies only the League side and the recorded statement that the League's organs supplied the blueprint.)*

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ In a design question, lead with the decision rules and treat the organs as illustration. In a failure question, take one sentence from this lesson - unanimity, no force, recommending power - and spend the rest of the answer on the political question of will, which is where the marks are.

### Traps

- Calling the League "Wilson's creation". ✅ The source records a collective authorship and specifies Wilson's contribution as the insistence that the Covenant be written into each peace treaty.
- Saying the Council had "five permanent members". ✅ It had four - Britain, France, Italy and Japan - with the United States intended as a fifth that never took its seat.
- Saying decisions were taken by majority. ✅ Both the Assembly and the Council required unanimity.
- Dating the League from the signing of the Treaty of Versailles. ✅ It formally came into existence on 10 January 1920, the day the treaty came into operation.
- Asserting a year for Italy's departure from the League. The copy of the membership figure held here is ambiguous on that cell, and this session does not fill the gap.
- Treating the Permanent Court as a criminal tribunal. ✅ It dealt with legal disputes between states, as opposed to political ones.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Formally in existence 10 January 1920, the same day Versailles came into operation; headquarters at Geneva.
2. ✅ Collective authorship: Cecil, Smuts, Bourgeois, Hymans, Wilson; Lloyd George named it a British war aim; Wilson's Fourteen Points included it last.
3. ✅ Wilson's decisive contribution: the Covenant written into each separate peace treaty.
4. ✅ Two aims: peace through collective security by economic or military sanctions; international co-operation on economic and social problems.
5. ✅ Assembly - annual, all members, one vote each, unanimous, could propose treaty revision, handled finances, gave small states a platform.
6. ✅ Council - at least three meetings a year; four permanent members (Britain, France, Italy, Japan); four elected members rising to nine by 1926; unanimous.
7. ✅ Permanent Court - the Hague, fifteen judges, functioning from 1922, 66 cases successfully dealt with by 1939.
8. ✅ Secretariat - staff from over thirty countries; respected for administrative quality. Commissions - mandates, military affairs, minorities, disarmament; committees for labour, health, finance, child welfare, drugs and women's rights.
9. ✅ Intended mechanism: submission of all war-threatening disputes; collective action against a Covenant-breaker; the Council *recommends* force contributions. Article 16 expected troops; there was no League army.
10. ✅ Membership: 42 at the start, 55 by 1926. Germany in 1926, out 1933; Japan out March 1933; USSR in 1934; USA never. ⚠️ No year contained all the great powers at once.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the unanimity rule is a more damaging institutional fact than the absence of a League army, and identify the one recorded consequence that makes the point unanswerable.

**Model answer:** The absence of an army is a limitation on one instrument; the unanimity rule is a limitation on every instrument, including those the League did possess. Economic sanctions required no army, and the Covenant provided for them explicitly as one of the two forms of collective restraint; but a resolution to impose them had to pass the Council, where decisions had to be unanimous, and where the aggressor itself might be a permanent member - Japan and Italy both were. Diplomatic condemnation, arbitration, reference to the Permanent Court and the recommendation of force contributions were all subject to the same rule. The absence of a force therefore constrained the final step in a sequence that unanimity could block at the first. The consequence that makes the point unanswerable is recorded directly: several attempts were made to strengthen the Covenant, and these failed because a unanimous vote was needed to change it, and this was never achieved. An organisation whose defect can only be cured by the procedure that constitutes the defect has no internal route to reform, which is why every subsequent attempt to strengthen collective security in this period - the Geneva Protocol of 1924 most notably - had to be attempted from outside the Covenant and depended on the voluntary consent of the same governments that had declined to act inside it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that identifying design faults answers the examiner's question. It does not, and Lesson 5 will show why: the source's own verdict places the decisive weight on the unwillingness of Britain and France rather than on the Covenant's drafting. The disciplined use of this lesson is to establish precisely what the machinery could do, so that the later argument - that the members declined to use what they had - cannot be deflected by the claim that they had nothing.

*The machinery is now known. The next lesson puts it to work. Between 1920 and 1930 the League did a great deal, some of it genuinely novel, and it is recorded in detail - which is why the standard dismissal of the organisation is wrong. It is also why the standard defence of the organisation is incomplete, because the record contains a qualification that changes what the successes prove.*

## Lesson 4 - What the League actually did, and the qualification that changes what it proves

### Visual: the record, and the test it was never given

```text
    none of the decisions went against a major state that might

   ---------------------------------   ------------------------------

   . VILNA 1920 - League supported Lithuania's claim; the Conference
     of Ambassadors awarded Vilna to Poland; the League allowed it
   . CORFU 1923 - Mussolini refused to recognise the League's
     competence; the Ambassadors ordered Greece to pay in full
   => THE LEAGUE MADE NO RESPONSE TO THESE ACTS OF DEFIANCE
```

*The two columns are the whole lesson. ⚠️ Column A is real and should be conceded generously in any answer, because a candidate who concedes it is visibly arguing rather than reciting a verdict. The qualification underneath is what converts the concession into an argument: a body that succeeds in every case where no great power's interest is engaged has not yet been tested at all.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

There is a familiar shape to arguments about international organisations, and it has not changed since 1923. One side lists the technical achievements - the standards set, the refugees returned, the epidemics contained - and concludes that criticism is unfair. The other side lists the wars not prevented and concludes that the achievements are decoration. Both are describing the same record.

The way out of that argument is not to split the difference. It is to ask what class of problem each success belonged to. ✅ The source does exactly this, and in a single sentence: it is significant that none of these disputes seriously threatened world peace, and none of the decisions went against a major state that might have challenged the League's verdict.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that sentence reclassifies the entire success list. The successes are not false; they are *unrepresentative*. They are drawn from a population of cases that excludes, by construction, the cases the League existed to handle. An answer that reproduces this observation is doing something an examiner can recognise as historical reasoning rather than as balance.

### 2. The technical and social record, with the names attached

✅ **Fact:** the source opens the section with an explicit instruction: it would be unfair to dismiss the League as a total failure. Many of the committees and commissions achieved valuable results and much was done to foster international co-operation.

✅ **Fact:** the International Labour Organization, under its French socialist director Albert Thomas, was one of the most successful. Its purpose was to improve conditions of labour all over the world by persuading governments to fix a maximum working day and week, specify adequate minimum wages, introduce sickness and unemployment benefit, and introduce old-age pensions. ✅ It collected and published a vast amount of information, and many governments were prevailed upon to take action.

✅ **Fact:** the Refugee Organization, led by Fridtjof Nansen, a Norwegian explorer, solved the problem of thousands of former prisoners of war marooned in Russia at the end of the war; about half a million were returned home. ✅ After 1933, valuable help was given to thousands of people fleeing from the Nazi persecution in Germany.

✅ **Fact:** the Health Organization did good work investigating the causes of epidemics and was especially successful in combating a typhus epidemic in Russia which at one time seemed likely to spread across Europe.

✅ **Fact:** the Mandates Commission supervised the government of the territories taken from Germany and Turkey, while another commission administered the Saar - which it did very efficiently, concluding by organising the 1935 plebiscite in which a large majority voted for the Saar to be returned to Germany.

✅ **Fact:** not all were successful. The Disarmament Commission made no progress in the near-impossible task of persuading member states to reduce armaments, even though they had all promised to do so when they agreed to the Covenant.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three observations earn marks here and none of them is the list itself. First, the Russian cases - refugees and typhus - were performed for a state that was not a member and was regarded with suspicion by the League's principal members, which is the strongest available evidence that the technical work was genuinely international rather than an extension of Anglo-French policy. Second, the Saar is the only case in the whole period in which the League administered a disputed territory and transferred it by vote, and it therefore functions as the counter-example to the claim that the League achieved nothing on the ground. Third, the Disarmament Commission's failure is recorded *inside* the successes section, which tells you the source is not arranging evidence to support a verdict - and a candidate who notices that a source qualifies its own heading is reading properly.

### 3. The political record, case by case

✅ **Fact:** several political disputes were referred to the League in the early 1920s, and in all but two cases the League's decisions were accepted.

| Dispute | Parties | Recorded outcome |
|---|---|---|
| **Aaland Islands, 1920** | ✅ Finland and Sweden | ✅ The verdict went in favour of Finland |
| **Upper Silesia, 1921** | ✅ Germany and Poland, over an important industrial area | ✅ The League decided it should be partitioned between the two |
| **Greek-Bulgarian frontier** | ✅ Greece and Bulgaria, after shooting incidents on the frontier | ✅ The League intervened swiftly; Greek troops were withdrawn and damages were paid to Bulgaria |
| **Mosul** | ✅ Turkey's claim to a province of the British mandated territory of Iraq | ✅ The League decided in favour of Iraq |
| **South America** | ✅ Peru and Colombia; Bolivia and Paraguay | ✅ Squabbles settled |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the parties column rather than the outcome column and the pattern is immediate. Finland, Sweden, Poland, Greece, Bulgaria, Turkey, Peru, Colombia, Bolivia, Paraguay. Of the parties named, none is a great power, and the one great-power interest in the list - Britain's, over Mosul - was upheld. ⚠️ That is the evidence for the qualification, and reproducing it by naming the parties rather than asserting the generalisation is what a high-scoring script does.

### 4. The two cases that were not accepted, and what they revealed

✅ **Fact:** during the same period the League found itself twice overruled by the Conference of Ambassadors, based in Paris, which had been set up to deal with problems arising out of the Versailles Treaties. ✅ This gathering of leading ambassadors was only intended to function until the League machinery was up and running, but it lingered on, and on several occasions it took precedence over the League.

✅ **Fact:** in 1920 the League supported Lithuania in her claim to Vilna, which had just been seized from her by the Poles; but when the Conference of Ambassadors insisted on awarding Vilna to Poland, the League allowed it to go ahead.

✅ **Fact:** the Corfu Incident of 1923 arose from a boundary dispute between Greece and Albania in which three Italian officials working on the boundary commission were killed. Mussolini blamed the Greeks, demanded huge compensation, and bombarded and occupied the Greek island of Corfu. Greece appealed to the League, but Mussolini refused to recognise its competence to deal with the problem. He threatened to withdraw Italy from the League, whereupon the Ambassadors ordered Greece to pay the full amount demanded.

✅ **Fact:** the League made no response to these acts of defiance, and the source records that this was not a promising sign. ✅ It also records the contemporary reaction: at this early stage, supporters of the League dismissed these incidents as teething troubles.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Corfu is the most under-used case in this topic and it is worth building an argument around. Every element of 1935 is present in 1923 - a great power using force against a small one, a refusal to recognise the League's jurisdiction, a threat of withdrawal used as leverage, a settlement imposed by the great powers outside the League, and a payment made by the victim. ⚠️ The difference is only that in 1923 the powers involved were still willing to call it a teething trouble. A script that says "the pattern of 1935 was rehearsed in 1923 and was described at the time as a teething trouble" has demonstrated command of the period rather than knowledge of a crisis.

### 5. Balfour's question, and why 1923 is the hinge of the whole topic

✅ **Fact:** the source places a contemporary question at exactly this point. At the time of the Corfu Incident in 1923, many people wondered what would happen if a powerful state were to challenge the League on a matter of major importance, for example by invading an innocent country - and how effective the League would be then. ✅ The former British prime minister, Lord Balfour, remarked: "The danger I see in the future is that some powerful nation will pursue a realpolitik ... as in the past. I do not believe we have yet found, or can find, a perfect guarantee against such a calamity."

✅ **Fact:** the source's own comment follows: unfortunately several such challenges occurred during the 1930s, and on every occasion the League was found wanting.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Balfour's remark is the single most useful quotation available in this topic and it should be used sparingly and precisely. Its value is not rhetorical. It establishes that the League's central weakness was identified, in public, by a former prime minister of one of its two principal members, eight years before Manchuria and twelve before Abyssinia. ⚠️ That weakens the defence that the statesmen of the 1930s were dealing with an unforeseeable problem, and it weakens equally the claim that hindsight is required to see the flaw. The flaw was seen. What was absent was the willingness to act on having seen it - which is Lesson 5's subject.

### 6. Comparison: three verdicts on the League's record, and what each licenses

| Verdict | Its strongest evidence | What it licenses you to write | Its weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Total failure** | ✅ Manchuria, Abyssinia, disarmament, the silence over the Rhineland; ✅ the source's own summary that it was a complete failure as far as preventing war was concerned | A direct answer to a peace-enforcement question | ✅ Contradicted by the technical record and by Henig's explicitly recorded corrective |
| ⚠️ **Qualified success** | ✅ ILO, Nansen, typhus, the Saar, the Permanent Court's 66 cases, the arbitrated disputes | A balanced answer that earns the "both sides" marks | ⚠️ Risks evading the question if the qualification about case selection is omitted |
| ⚠️ **Successful as an experiment, failed as a guarantor** | ✅ Henig: a bold step towards international co-operation which failed in some of its aims but succeeded comprehensively in others; ✅ the recorded institutional transmission to the United Nations | A graded verdict, which is what "how far" and "critically examine" demand | ⚠️ Requires the candidate to state *which* aims, or it collapses into the second verdict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third verdict is the one to write, and the reason is structural rather than diplomatic. The League had two stated aims. It achieved the second and failed at the first. A verdict that maps success and failure onto the organisation's own stated objectives is not a compromise between two opinions; it is a finding.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the successes were all in cases that did not matter, why mention them at all? Is the balanced paragraph not simply padding?*

⚠️ **The reply, in two moves.** First, they establish capability, and capability is what the failure argument needs. If the League had been incapable of administering anything, the 1930s would prove only that a weak body was weak. Because the League demonstrably could run a plebiscite in the Saar, return about half a million people from Russia and settle a partition in Upper Silesia, the failure over Manchuria cannot be attributed to incompetence, and the explanation is forced back onto the political choices of its members - which is the conclusion the source itself reaches. Second, the successes are the *historical* reason the organisation survived long enough to be tested: the optimism of 1930, recorded in Smuts's remark about witnessing one of the great miracles of history, is unintelligible without them. ⚠️ A paragraph that establishes capability is therefore not balance; it is the premise of the argument.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The League of Nations was not a complete failure." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the achievements of the League of Nations in the 1920s.
> ⚠️ Why was the League of Nations hailed as a success in the 1920s and regarded as a failure by the later 1930s?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ In a 10-mark answer, use three named successes and the qualification, and spend the remaining space on the decisive crisis. In a 15-mark answer, add the Vilna and Corfu overrulings, because they show that the failure pattern predates the Depression. ⚠️ Never list more than four successes; the marks are in the qualification, not in the length of the list.

### Traps

- Listing successes without the recorded qualification that none threatened world peace and none went against a major state. The list without the qualification is a school answer.
- Forgetting that the Disarmament Commission's failure is recorded within the successes section.
- Attributing the Corfu settlement to the League. ✅ It was the Conference of Ambassadors that ordered Greece to pay.
- Dating Corfu to the 1930s or treating it as a minor curiosity. ✅ It is 1923 and it is the rehearsal.
- Saying the League "did nothing" about Vilna. ✅ It supported Lithuania's claim and then allowed the Ambassadors' award to stand - which is worse and more interesting.
- Using Balfour's remark as decoration. Its analytical value is the date.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The instruction in the source's own words: it would be unfair to dismiss the League as a total failure.
2. ✅ ILO under Albert Thomas: maximum hours, minimum wages, sickness and unemployment benefit, old-age pensions; vast published information.
3. ✅ Refugee Organization under Fridtjof Nansen: about half a million former prisoners of war returned from Russia; help after 1933 to those fleeing Nazi persecution.
4. ✅ Health Organization: epidemics; the typhus campaign in Russia.
5. ✅ Mandates Commission; the Saar administered efficiently, ending in the 1935 plebiscite with a large majority for return to Germany.
6. ✅ The Disarmament Commission made no progress, although all members had promised to disarm on accepting the Covenant.
7. ✅ Political settlements: Aaland 1920 (Finland); Upper Silesia 1921 (partition); Greek-Bulgarian frontier (withdrawal and damages); Mosul (Iraq); Peru-Colombia and Bolivia-Paraguay.
8. ✅ The qualification: none seriously threatened world peace; none went against a major state.
9. ✅ Twice overruled by the Conference of Ambassadors: Vilna 1920 and Corfu 1923; the League made no response; supporters called them teething troubles.
10. ✅ Balfour, 1923: the danger that a powerful nation will pursue a realpolitik, and no perfect guarantee against it - stated eight years before Manchuria.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate lists six League successes and concludes that the organisation "was more successful than is usually admitted". Show how to convert that list into an argument, and state the one piece of evidence that prevents the list from being conclusive.

**Model answer:** The list becomes an argument when each item is given a function rather than a mention. The International Labour Organization under Albert Thomas, the refugee work under Fridtjof Nansen that repatriated about half a million former prisoners of war from Russia, and the Health Organization's campaign against typhus establish that a permanent international secretariat could deliver programmes across frontiers, including for a state that was not a member. The Saar administration, concluded by the plebiscite of 1935, establishes that the League could govern a disputed territory and transfer it by vote. The Permanent Court's sixty-six successfully concluded cases by 1939 and the settlements over the Aaland Islands in 1920, Upper Silesia in 1921, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier and Mosul establish that arbitration worked where the parties accepted it. Together these establish capability, and capability is exactly what a failure argument requires, because it forecloses the defence that the League failed in the 1930s through simple incompetence. The evidence that prevents the list from being conclusive is the source's own qualification: none of these disputes seriously threatened world peace, and none of the decisions went against a major state that might have challenged the verdict. Every party named in the political cases is a small or medium state, and the one great-power interest involved, Britain's over Mosul, was upheld. The successes are therefore drawn from a class of cases that excludes, by construction, the class the League was founded to handle - and the two cases in the same period that did involve great-power defiance, Vilna in 1920 and Corfu in 1923, were both removed from the League's hands by the Conference of Ambassadors without any League response.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the "not a complete failure" paragraph is an act of fairness to be inserted for balance marks. It is a premise. Its purpose is to rule out incompetence as an explanation, so that the analysis is forced onto the political will of the members - which is where the source's own verdict lies and where the marks are.

*The capability is established and the qualification is in place. The next lesson asks the question both of them set up: when the League did fail, was the failure written into the Covenant or chosen by its members? The evidence base answers with unusual directness, and the answer is not the one most scripts give.*

## Lesson 5 - Design fault or political choice? The structural limits of collective security

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the seven recorded weaknesses in order; Article 16 and the 1923 opt-out resolution; the Geneva Protocol of 1924 and its rejection by the succeeding British government; "lacking teeth"; the recorded reasons for British reluctance and the "leave it to the others" logic; the League as a French-British affair; Locarno signed outside the League; the recorded bottom line and its correct weighting

*The box at the bottom is the most important sentence in this lesson and the one most candidates never reach. ⚠️ A source that has just listed six structural defects then states that none of them necessarily doomed the League is telling you that the structural account, by itself, is not the answer. That is a gift: it licenses a graded verdict in which design and will are both present and one of them is decisive.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

There are two ways to explain a failed institution and they lead to different conclusions. The architectural explanation says the thing was built wrong: remove the unanimity rule, give it an army, and it works. The political explanation says the thing was built to do what its members wanted it to do, and they did not want it to constrain them: give it an army and they would not have contributed troops.

The distinction matters practically, not just philosophically. If the League failed architecturally, then the United Nations - which fixed some of the architecture - should have been correspondingly better, and any future organisation can be designed out of the problem. If the League failed politically, then no design fixes it, because the problem is the willingness of states to accept a cost today against a risk tomorrow.

✅ The evidence base contains both explanations and it weights them. ⚠️ Candidates who present only the architectural list are giving half the source's own answer.

### 2. The seven recorded weaknesses, in the source's order

✅ **Fact (a):** it was too closely linked with the Versailles Treaties. This initial disadvantage made the League seem like an organisation created especially for the benefit of the victorious powers; in addition it had to defend a peace settlement which was far from perfect, and it was inevitable that some of its provisions would cause trouble - for example, the disappointing territorial gains of the Italians and the inclusion of Germans in Czechoslovakia and Poland.

✅ **Fact (b):** it was rejected by the United States. The League was dealt a serious blow in March 1920 when the US Senate rejected both the Versailles settlement and the League. The absence of the United States meant that the League was deprived of a powerful member whose presence would have been of great psychological and financial benefit.

✅ **Fact (c):** other important powers were not involved. Germany was not allowed to join until 1926 and the USSR only became a member in 1934, when Germany left. For the first few years of its existence the League was deprived of three of the world's most important powers.

✅ **Fact (d):** the Conference of Ambassadors in Paris was an embarrassment, lingering on past its intended life and taking precedence over the League at Vilna in 1920 and over Corfu in 1923.

✅ **Fact (e):** there were serious weaknesses in the Covenant. It was difficult to get unanimous decisions; the League had no military force of its own; and though Article 16 expected member states to supply troops if necessary, a resolution was passed in 1923 that each member would decide for itself whether or not to fight in a crisis. ✅ The source's comment on that resolution is unusually blunt: this clearly made nonsense of the idea of collective security.

✅ **Fact (f):** it was very much a French/British affair. The continued absence of the United States and the USSR, plus the hostility of Italy, made the League very much a French/British affair; and the British Conservatives were never very enthusiastic about it, preferring to sign the Locarno Treaties of 1925 outside the League instead of conducting negotiations within it.

✅ **Fact (g):** the world economic crisis began in 1929. The situation really began to drift out of control with the onset of the economic crisis; it brought unemployment and falling living standards to most countries, and caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany, which together with Mussolini refused to keep to the rules.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** sort those seven and the shape of the answer appears. Items (a) and (e) are architectural. Items (b), (c), (d) and (f) are political decisions taken by governments that could have decided otherwise - the Senate could have ratified, the Allies could have admitted Germany earlier, the Ambassadors could have deferred, the British Conservatives could have negotiated inside the League. Item (g) is exogenous. ⚠️ Four of seven are choices. That is the arithmetic behind the source's verdict.

### 3. The Geneva Protocol, 1924: the attempt, the reversal and what it proves

✅ **Fact:** several attempts were made to strengthen the Covenant, but these failed because a unanimous vote was needed to change it, and this was never achieved. ✅ The most notable attempt was made in 1924 by the British Labour prime minister, Ramsay MacDonald, a great supporter of the League. He introduced a resolution known as the Geneva Protocol, which pledged members to accept arbitration and help any victim of unprovoked aggression.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the outcome with its own comment attached. With supreme irony, the Conservative government which followed MacDonald informed the League that they could not agree to the Protocol; they were reluctant to commit Britain and the Empire to the defence of all the 1919 frontiers. ✅ A resolution proposed by one British government was thus rejected by the next British government, and the League was left, as its critics remarked, still "lacking teeth".

✅ **Fact:** the recorded reasons for the British attitude are specific and are not dismissed as weakness. British public opinion was strongly pacifist, and there was a feeling that Britain was now so militarily weak that armed interventions of any sort should be avoided.

✅ **Fact:** the source then generalises the position and names the logic. Many other League members felt the same as Britain; and so, perversely, they were all basing their security on a system whose success relied on their support and commitment, but which they were not prepared to uphold. The attitude seemed to be: leave it to the others.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "leave it to the others" is the most economical statement of the collective-action problem in this entire topic, and it should be used as an analytical term rather than as a phrase. Collective security has the structure of a public good: every member benefits from deterrence, every member bears a cost only if it acts, and the rational individual choice is therefore to hope somebody else acts. ⚠️ That is not cowardice and it is not peculiar to the 1930s; it is a predictable property of any security arrangement that does not convert the obligation into an automatic commitment. The Geneva Protocol was precisely an attempt to make the obligation automatic, and it is the strongest evidence that contemporaries understood the problem exactly.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the British reason for rejection also deserves precision, because it is usually caricatured. The objection was not to helping victims in principle; it was to committing Britain *and the Empire* to the defence of *all* the 1919 frontiers - that is, to an open-ended and geographically unlimited guarantee covering eastern European borders that Britain did not regard as defensible. ⚠️ The same reasoning reappears exactly at Locarno, where Britain guaranteed the western frontiers and not the eastern ones, and again in Austen Chamberlain's remark about the Polish Corridor. It is a single continuous British position held for fifteen years, not a series of failures of nerve, and recognising the continuity is worth a mark.

### 4. The bottom line, and how to weight it

✅ **Fact:** the source states the crucial qualification after listing six of the seven weaknesses: none of these weaknesses necessarily doomed the League to failure, provided all the members were prepared to refrain from aggression and accept League decisions; between 1925 and 1930 events ran fairly smoothly.

✅ **Fact:** and it states the final verdict after the three crises: the real explanation for the failure of the League was simple - when aggressive states such as Japan, Italy and Germany defied it, the League members, especially France and Britain, were not prepared to support it, either by decisive economic measures or by military action. The League was only as strong as the determination of its leading members to stand up to aggression; unfortunately, determination of that sort was sadly lacking during the 1930s.

| Reading | What it claims | Status in the evidence base |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Pure design failure | The Covenant made failure certain | ✅ Explicitly denied: none of these weaknesses necessarily doomed the League |
| ⚠️ Pure political failure | The design was irrelevant; only will mattered | ⚠️ Overstated: the unanimity rule and the amendment rule are recorded as real obstacles, and the 1923 resolution is recorded as making nonsense of the idea |
| ⚠️ **Design as permission, will as cause** | The architecture lowered the cost of inaction; the members chose inaction | ✅ Consistent with both recorded statements, and it is the reading this session teaches |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third reading is what a "critically examine" demand is asking for. Phrase it as a mechanism rather than as a compromise: the Covenant's design did not compel the failure, but it removed every device that would have made inaction embarrassing, and a system in which inaction is costless will produce inaction whenever action is costly.

### 5. The proof that the members knew what they were doing

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strongest single argument against the pure-design reading is that at every point where the members *could* have acted without amending the Covenant, they did not, and the source records each occasion.

| Occasion | Action available without amending the Covenant | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Vilna, 1920 | Insist on the League's own supported verdict | ✅ The League allowed the Ambassadors' award to go ahead |
| ✅ Corfu, 1923 | Assert the League's competence against a member state | ✅ The League made no response |
| ✅ Geneva Protocol, 1924-25 | Accept an arbitration-and-assistance pledge | ✅ Rejected by the succeeding British government |
| ✅ Manchuria, 1931-33 | Impose economic sanctions | ✅ The question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised |
| ✅ Abyssinia, 1935-36 | Extend sanctions to oil, coal and steel | ✅ Sanctions were introduced but not applied to those exports, and were abandoned a few weeks after the conquest |
| ✅ Conscription 1935 and the Rhineland 1936 | Raise the matter at the Council | ✅ Neither matter was raised at the League Council |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is decisive and is almost never used. Two open breaches of the Versailles Treaty, one of them also a breach of Locarno, were never even placed on the Council's agenda - and the recorded reason is that France and Britain were afraid Hitler would reject any decision that went against Germany, and were reluctant to be forced into military action. ⚠️ An institution whose members decline to table an item because they fear winning the vote has not been defeated by its rules. That single observation, correctly stated, converts a descriptive answer into an analytical one.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is it fair to say the members "chose" inaction, when the source itself records that Britain and France had serious economic problems, were ill-equipped to win a war and could expect no American help?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the constraint fully: the source records it in exactly those terms over Manchuria, and it records over Abyssinia that the Anglo-French motive was the desire not to antagonise Mussolini too much so as to keep him as an ally against the real danger, Germany. Those are strategic calculations, not failures of nerve, and treating them as cowardice is a category error. Second, distinguish constraint from choice *at the margin*: a government that cannot fight a war can still table a resolution, extend an embargo to oil, or decline to sign a separate naval agreement with the state it is trying to restrain. Each of those was available and each was declined. Third, note the strategic self-defeat: the Abyssinian calculation - conciliate Mussolini to keep him against Germany - produced the opposite of its intention, because the source records that Mussolini was annoyed by the sanctions anyway and began to draw closer to Hitler. ⚠️ The correct verdict is therefore that the constraints were real and the policy chosen within them was worse than the alternatives available within the same constraints. That formulation concedes everything the defence deserves and still reaches a judgement.

### 7. UPSC application

This lesson is the analytical core of the most likely question in the whole topic, and the basic owner's directive map states the demand precisely: a question asking why the League failed is really testing structural design **and** member-state will, plus its real successes - and the answer that must not be written is a list of failures.

| Marks | Structure for "Why did the League fail?" |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **10** | Thesis (enforcement failed, administration did not) → two structural weaknesses **and** one genuine success → the decisive crisis → verdict |
| ⚠️ **15** | Thesis → aims and design → the 1920s partial recovery → the Depression as the turning point → the 1930s crisis sequence → verdict |
| ⚠️ **20** | All of the above → plus appeasement evaluated on its own terms, the non-European dimension of Manchuria and Abyssinia, and the origins-of-war question → verdict |

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the League of Nations fail to preserve peace?
> ⚠️ "The League of Nations failed because collective security was never seriously attempted." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the reasons why there were no major international conflicts during the 1920s.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The qualified thesis supplied by the basic owner is the one to rehearse until it is automatic: the League failed not because its design was worthless but because collective security requires states to accept risk in advance, and its principal members would not - a political failure that its institutional weaknesses merely made easier.

### Traps

- Listing the seven weaknesses and stopping. ✅ The source itself says none of them necessarily doomed the League.
- Saying the Covenant "had no provision for sanctions". ✅ It provided for economic or military sanctions; the defect was the unanimity rule, the absence of a force and the 1923 opt-out.
- Attributing the Geneva Protocol's failure to foreign opposition. ✅ It was rejected by the next British government after being proposed by the previous one.
- Describing British reluctance as cowardice. ✅ The recorded reason is an unwillingness to guarantee all the 1919 frontiers, plus pacifist opinion and a sense of military weakness.
- Forgetting that Locarno was deliberately negotiated outside the League. It is direct evidence that its principal member preferred bilateral guarantees to collective ones.
- Omitting the fact that conscription in 1935 and the Rhineland in 1936 were never raised at the Council at all.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Seven recorded weaknesses: Versailles linkage; US rejection; Germany and the USSR excluded; the Conference of Ambassadors; Covenant defects; a French/British affair; the economic crisis from 1929.
2. ✅ Covenant defects in detail: unanimity; no force; Article 16 expected troops; the 1923 resolution let each member decide for itself - which made nonsense of collective security.
3. ✅ Amendment required unanimity, so every strengthening attempt failed.
4. ✅ Geneva Protocol 1924: MacDonald's resolution for arbitration and assistance to victims of unprovoked aggression; rejected by the succeeding Conservative government, unwilling to commit Britain and the Empire to all the 1919 frontiers; the League left "lacking teeth".
5. ✅ Recorded British reasons: strongly pacifist opinion; a sense of military weakness; and the general attitude "leave it to the others".
6. ✅ The League became a French/British affair because of US and Soviet absence and Italian hostility; the British Conservatives preferred Locarno, signed outside the League.
7. ✅ The qualification: none of the weaknesses necessarily doomed the League; between 1925 and 1930 events ran fairly smoothly.
8. ✅ The verdict: the League was only as strong as the determination of its leading members, and that determination was lacking in the 1930s.
9. ⚠️ The teaching reading: design as permission, will as cause.
10. ✅ Six recorded occasions on which action was available without amending the Covenant and was not taken - Vilna, Corfu, the Protocol, Manchurian sanctions, oil sanctions over Abyssinia, and the un-tabled breaches of 1935 and 1936.

### Concept check

**Question:** Construct the argument that the League's failure was political rather than architectural, and then state the strongest counter-argument that a careful examiner would expect you to answer.

**Model answer:** The political argument rests on four moves. First, the source explicitly denies that the design was fatal: none of the recorded weaknesses necessarily doomed the League provided members were prepared to refrain from aggression and accept its decisions, and between 1925 and 1930 events ran fairly smoothly. Second, the decisive institutional weakness was itself created by a political decision rather than by the drafters: Article 16 expected members to supply troops, and it was a resolution of 1923 - taken by the members, not written into the Covenant - that left each member free to decide for itself whether to fight, which the source says made nonsense of the idea of collective security. Third, at every point where action was available without amending the Covenant, it was declined: the Vilna award was allowed to stand, Corfu produced no response, the Geneva Protocol was rejected by the next British government after being proposed by the previous one, economic sanctions over Manchuria were never even raised, sanctions over Abyssinia excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned within weeks of the conquest, and the reintroduction of German conscription in 1935 and the remilitarisation of the Rhineland in 1936 were never brought before the Council at all. Fourth, the source's own closing verdict places the weight there: the League was only as strong as the determination of its leading members to stand up to aggression. The strongest counter-argument is the amendment trap. Because changing the Covenant required unanimity, the members could not have built a stronger instrument even had they wished to, and every attempt to do so failed for that reason; on this reading the political failure is itself a product of an architecture that foreclosed reform. The honest reply is not to dismiss this but to grade it: the architecture made inaction costless and reform impossible, which is a real and serious defect, but it did not make inaction compulsory, and the specific decisions taken between 1931 and 1936 were available under the existing rules.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "political will" as a mood rather than as a structure of incentives. The recorded phrase "leave it to the others" names a collective-action problem, not a character flaw, and the Geneva Protocol shows that contemporaries identified the problem and attempted to legislate against it. An answer that explains the failure by the timidity of individual statesmen has substituted personalities for a mechanism and will not survive the observation that the same governments declared war in 1939.

*Five lessons have dealt with the institution. The next three deal with the substance the institution was supposed to manage: money, security and frontiers. The story begins in the one place where the interwar order came closest to open war between two of its own architects - the Ruhr valley in January 1923.*

## Lesson 6 - Reparations and security: the Ruhr, and why the same problem looked different in Paris and in London

*The diagram explains something that otherwise looks like French vindictiveness. ✅ France tried coercion, containment and conciliation in that order, and the source records that all three failed. ⚠️ A script that presents the Ruhr as an act of spite has missed the sequence; a script that presents French policy as consistently harsh has missed Briand, who was Foreign Minister in eleven successive governments between 1925 and 1932.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Two governments look at the same neighbour and reach opposite conclusions about what makes them safe. For Paris, safety means that the neighbour cannot do it again, so the neighbour must be weak. For London, safety means that the neighbour has something to lose and someone to trade with, so the neighbour must be prosperous. Both positions are rational. Neither can be half-adopted. And the two governments are formally allies who will have to agree on a common line in every crisis for the next twenty years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the structural fault of the western alliance, and it is worth stating once, early, in almost any interwar answer. It explains the Ruhr, the rejection of the Geneva Protocol, the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of 1935, the divergence over the Rhineland in 1936 and the pressure on Prague in 1938. ⚠️ The two capitals were not failing to co-operate; they were co-operating while holding incompatible theories of security.

### 2. The reparations figure and what it was for

✅ **Fact:** the figure to be paid for reparations was fixed in 1921 at £6600 million. ✅ It was thought that the strain of paying this huge amount would keep Germany economically weak for the next sixty-six years - the period over which reparations were to be paid in annual instalments - and that consequently another German attack on France would be less likely.

✅ **Fact:** financial troubles in Germany soon caused the government to fall behind with its payments, and the French, who claimed to need the cash from reparations to balance their budget and to pay their own debts to the United States, became desperate.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the rationale recorded here settles a common confusion. Reparations were not primarily a punishment or even primarily a revenue measure; the sixty-six-year schedule was designed as a *security instrument* - a mechanism for keeping German capacity suppressed for two generations. ⚠️ Once that is understood, the whole 1924-29 sequence reads differently: every reduction in reparations was simultaneously a reduction in French security, which is why each one had to be traded for something else, and why Locarno follows Dawes rather than preceding it.

### 3. Genoa, 1922: the conference that produced the opposite of its purpose

✅ **Fact:** the Genoa Conference of 1922 was the brainchild of the British prime minister Lloyd George, who hoped it would solve three pressing problems: Franco-German hostility, with the Germans threatening to stop paying reparations; European war debts to the United States; and the need to resume proper diplomatic relations with Soviet Russia.

✅ **Fact:** it failed on all three. The French refused all compromise and insisted on full reparations payments; the Americans refused even to attend; and the Russians and Germans withdrew, moved to Rapallo - a resort about twenty miles from Genoa - and signed a mutual agreement there.

✅ **Fact:** the Rapallo Treaty, signed on Easter Sunday 1922, restored full diplomatic relations and cancelled reparations claims between the two states. ✅ Both could look forward to advantages: they could co-operate to keep Poland weak, which was in both their interests; the USSR had Germany as a buffer against any future attack from the west; the Germans were allowed to build factories in Russia for the manufacture of aeroplanes and ammunition, enabling them to get round the Versailles disarmament terms, and German officers trained in Russia in the use of the new forbidden weapons; and in return the Russians would supply Germany with grain.

✅ **Fact:** Britain and France were alarmed and could see no good coming from what Lloyd George called "this fierce friendship" between the two outcast nations of Europe.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Rapallo is the most instructive single episode of the early 1920s and it is routinely left out of scripts. It establishes three things at once. First, that the two states excluded from the postwar order found each other immediately, which is a structural property of exclusion rather than an accident of personality. Second, that the disarmament clauses of the settlement were being evaded from 1922 by a formal treaty arrangement, which means that German rearmament did not begin in 1935. Third, that the states standing between them - Poland above all - were the object of a shared interest from the beginning. ⚠️ Anyone who wants to understand August 1939 should start at Rapallo in April 1922, and a single sentence saying so is worth a mark in an origins-of-war answer.

### 4. The Ruhr, January 1923: coercion attempted and costed

✅ **Fact:** Poincaré, the next prime minister and recorded as anti-German, decided that drastic methods were needed to force the Germans to pay and to weaken their powers of revival. ✅ In January 1923 French and Belgian troops occupied the Ruhr, the important German industrial area which includes the cities of Essen and Düsseldorf.

✅ **Fact:** the Germans replied with passive resistance, strikes and sabotage, and a number of nasty incidents between troops and civilians resulted in the deaths of over a hundred people.

✅ **Fact:** although the French managed to seize goods worth about £40 million, the whole episode caused galloping inflation and the collapse of the German mark, which by November 1923 was completely valueless. ✅ The summary section records the same judgement more briefly: the occupation succeeded only in bringing about the collapse of the German currency.

✅ **Fact:** it also revealed the basic difference between the French and British attitudes towards Germany. While France adopted a hard line and wanted Germany completely crippled, Britain now saw moderation and reconciliation as the best security and believed that an economically healthy Germany would be good for the stability of Europe, as well as for British exports. ✅ Consequently Britain strongly disapproved of the Ruhr occupation and sympathised with Germany.

| Intended effect | Actual recorded effect |
|---|---|
| ✅ Force payment in goods where cash was refused | ✅ Goods worth about £40 million seized |
| ✅ Weaken Germany's powers of revival | ✅ Galloping inflation and a currency completely valueless by November 1923 |
| ⚠️ Demonstrate French resolve to Britain | ✅ Britain strongly disapproved and sympathised with Germany |
| ⚠️ Break German resistance to the settlement | ✅ Passive resistance, strikes and sabotage; over a hundred deaths |
| ⚠️ Secure French finances | ✅ By summer 1924 the failure of Poincaré's occupation was obvious and the new premier Herriot accepted a compromise |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Ruhr is the interwar order's controlled experiment in unilateral enforcement, and its result is unambiguous. Enforcement by one creditor against a debtor state, without allied support, destroyed the debtor's capacity to pay, alienated the ally whose backing the creditor most needed, and was abandoned within eighteen months. ⚠️ Every later French government read that result, and it is a large part of the explanation of French passivity in March 1936 - the one previous occasion on which France had acted alone against Germany had ended in a currency collapse and a diplomatic breach with London.

### 5. The Little Entente: containment on paper

✅ **Fact:** at the same time the French tried to increase their security by building up a network of alliances - first with Poland in 1921 and later with Czechoslovakia in 1924, Romania in 1926 and Yugoslavia in 1927. ✅ This network, known as the "Little Entente", though impressive on paper, did not amount to much because the states involved were comparatively weak.

✅ **Fact:** the source states what France actually needed: a renewal of the old alliance with Russia, which had served her well during the First World War - but this seemed out of the question now that Russia had become communist.

✅ **Fact:** the eastern network had its own architect on the other side. Edvard Benes, the Czech Foreign Minister, was the instigator of a Little Entente with Yugoslavia and Romania in 1920-21 and signed treaties with Italy and France in 1924; he was involved in the Locarno agreements of 1925, in which France promised to guarantee Czechoslovakia's frontiers and Germany promised that any frontier disputes would be settled by arbitration.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "impressive on paper" is the source's, and it identifies the recurring defect of French policy. France assembled commitments whose aggregate military weight was small and whose geography was indefensible without a great-power partner in the east. ⚠️ The only such partner available was the USSR, and ideology ruled it out for most of the period. That constraint - a security architecture that requires a partner the architects cannot politically accept - runs directly from 1921 to the failed Anglo-French-Soviet negotiations of summer 1939, and naming the continuity is one of the most effective single sentences available in an origins-of-war answer.

### 6. Comparison: three ways a creditor can respond to a defaulting debtor

| Approach | Adopted when | Recorded outcome |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Coercion** | ✅ January 1923, the Ruhr | ✅ Currency collapse; ally alienated; abandoned by 1924 |
| ⚠️ **Rescheduling with third-party credit** | ✅ 1924, the Dawes Plan | ✅ German economy began to recover; tensions relaxed; French troops withdrawn |
| ⚠️ **Reduction and then cancellation** | ✅ 1929 Young Plan; ✅ 1932 Lausanne | ✅ Reparations cut to £2000 million and then largely cancelled |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence runs in one direction only - from coercion to concession - and it never reverses. That is worth stating, because it disposes of the claim that the interwar powers were inflexible about reparations. They were inflexible for about three years and then conceded continuously for nine. ⚠️ The unsolved problem was never the money.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the British reading was correct and the French reading was wrong, why did the British reading not produce security?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the British case at full strength: the Dawes Plan worked, the German economy began to recover on the basis of American loans, international tensions gradually relaxed, and the whole 1924-29 reconstruction followed. On its own terms the economic reading was vindicated. Second, state its limit: prosperity is a condition of stability, not a guarantee of it, and the British reading contained no answer to the question of what happens if a prosperous Germany still wants the eastern frontiers changed - which is exactly the question Locarno declined to answer. Third, note the dependency that neither capital examined: the recovery rested on American credit, so a theory of security that depended on German prosperity was, without anyone deciding it, a theory that depended on Wall Street. ⚠️ The correct verdict is that the British reading solved the problem of the 1920s and created the vulnerability of the 1930s, and the French reading identified the right problem and chose an instrument that made it worse.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far did the reparations question shape international relations in the 1920s?
> ⚠️ Examine French policy towards Germany between 1919 and 1933.
> ⚠️ "Anglo-French disagreement, not German revisionism, was the fundamental weakness of the interwar order." Critically examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ In any of these, the Ruhr is the case study and the Anglo-French divergence is the thesis. Give the occupation four facts - the date and the two occupying states, the passive resistance, the £40 million and the valueless mark - and then spend the space on what the episode revealed rather than on what it did.

### Traps

- Saying France occupied the Ruhr alone. ✅ French **and Belgian** troops.
- Dating the occupation to 1922 or 1924. ✅ January 1923.
- Saying the occupation "caused German hyperinflation" without the source's own framing: it caused galloping inflation and the collapse of the mark, which by November 1923 was completely valueless.
- Treating French policy as uniformly hard. ✅ Three methods were tried; Herriot accepted compromise in 1924 and Briand pursued reconciliation from 1925 to 1932.
- Forgetting Rapallo. It is where German rearmament and the shared Russo-German interest in a weak Poland both begin.
- Presenting the Little Entente as a serious counterweight. ✅ The source's own words are "impressive on paper".

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Reparations fixed 1921 at £6600 million over sixty-six annual instalments, designed to keep Germany weak for that period.
2. ✅ France tried three methods - keeping Germany weak, alliances plus a strong League, and reconciliation. All three failed.
3. ✅ Genoa 1922: Lloyd George's conference on Franco-German hostility, war debts and relations with Soviet Russia; France refused compromise, the USA refused to attend, and Russia and Germany withdrew.
4. ✅ Rapallo, Easter Sunday 1922: diplomatic relations restored, mutual reparations claims cancelled, a shared interest in keeping Poland weak, German aircraft and ammunition factories and officer training in Russia, Russian grain in return; renewed by the Treaty of Berlin in 1926.
5. ✅ January 1923: French and Belgian troops occupy the Ruhr, including Essen and Düsseldorf.
6. ✅ German passive resistance, strikes and sabotage; over a hundred deaths; goods worth about £40 million seized; galloping inflation; the mark valueless by November 1923.
7. ✅ The revealed divergence: France wanted Germany crippled; Britain wanted Germany economically healthy and strongly disapproved of the occupation.
8. ✅ The Little Entente: Poland 1921, Czechoslovakia 1924, Romania 1926, Yugoslavia 1927 - impressive on paper, comparatively weak in fact.
9. ✅ What France actually needed was the old Russian alliance, ruled out because Russia had become communist.
10. ⚠️ The transferable finding: unilateral enforcement destroyed the debtor's capacity and the creditor's alliance, and was never repeated.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Ruhr occupation of 1923 is more useful in an interwar answer as evidence about the Anglo-French alliance than as evidence about reparations.

**Model answer:** As evidence about reparations the episode is quickly exhausted: France and Belgium occupied the Ruhr in January 1923 to seize in goods what Germany was refusing to pay in cash, seized goods worth about £40 million, and produced galloping inflation and a mark that was completely valueless by November 1923, so that the coercive route was abandoned and replaced within eighteen months by the Dawes Plan. That is a complete account of a failed collection policy, and it is worth three sentences. As evidence about the alliance it is far richer, because it exposed a divergence that the two governments never resolved and that shaped every subsequent crisis. The occupation revealed the basic difference between French and British attitudes: France adopted a hard line and wanted Germany completely crippled, while Britain saw moderation and reconciliation as the better security and believed that an economically healthy Germany would be good for European stability and for British exports; Britain consequently disapproved strongly of the occupation and sympathised with Germany. That is not a tactical disagreement about a debt. It is a disagreement about what makes a country safe, and it recurs in identifiable form at every later decision point - in the British rejection of the Geneva Protocol on the ground that Britain would not guarantee all the 1919 frontiers, in the eastern omission at Locarno, in the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935 signed without consulting France, and in the joint Anglo-French pressure on Prague in September 1938. The Ruhr is therefore best deployed as the moment the fault line became visible, with the £40 million and the valueless mark as the evidence that the French method had failed on its own terms.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading French policy as vengeance. The recorded motive is security after two invasions in less than fifty years, and the recorded sequence is coercion, then containment, then conciliation - with Briand as Foreign Minister in eleven successive governments between 1925 and 1932. A script that cannot explain why the same country that occupied the Ruhr in 1923 signed Locarno in 1925 has not understood the period.

*Coercion has failed and the mark is worthless. The next lesson is the recovery - and it begins not in Paris or Berlin but in a London conference room in 1924, with an American general in the chair and a loan of 800 million gold marks on the table. That loan is the hinge of the entire decade, and its consequences are not all good.*

## Lesson 7 - Dawes and the dollar: how European stability came to rest on American credit

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the three newcomers and the change of political leadership; the Dawes Plan's four recorded terms; American isolation as a political doctrine with an economic exception; war debts and the recorded figure; the circular flow of payments and the tariff problem; the Neutrality Acts of 1935-37 and the cash-and-carry revision of November 1939; the recorded consequence that the USA returned to strict isolation over Manchuria; the dependency that nobody decided to create
