### Visual: the circular flow, and the single point at which it can be cut

```text
                      THE UNITED STATES
                 (politically isolated; economically
                  the indispensable participant)
                              |
           war loans to Britain and her allies during
           the war: ALMOST 12 BILLION DOLLARS
           Harding and Coolidge INSIST on repayment
           ("They hired the money, didn't they?" is the
            popular version; the recorded position is
            simply that repayments be made, with much
            lower interest rates by agreement)
                              |
                              v
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
   |                  THE CIRCULAR FLOW                        |
   |                                                           |
   |   AMERICA lends to GERMANY  --- Dawes 1924: 800 million   |
   |        |                         gold marks, mostly from  |
   |        |                         the USA                  |
   |        v                                                  |
   |   GERMANY pays REPARATIONS to FRANCE and BRITAIN          |
   |        |                                                  |
   |        v                                                  |
   |   FRANCE and BRITAIN repay WAR DEBTS to AMERICA           |
   |        |                                                  |
   |        +----------------> back to the top                 |
   |                                                           |
   |   AND AT THE SAME TIME: American insistence on keeping    |
   |   HIGH TARIFFS, which made it harder for Europeans to     |
   |   earn dollars by selling into the American market        |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   THE STRUCTURAL PROPERTY:  the loop has ONE source of new
   money and it is at the top. Cut the American loan and every
   arrow below it stops - not gradually, but at once.
                              |
                              v
   1929: the Crash. The loans stop and short-term loans already
   made are called in. The loop is cut at the only place that
   matters. -> Lesson 11.
```

*The diagram is not an economics lesson; the economics belongs to the world-economy owner. ⚠️ It is a diagram about dependency in diplomacy. What the powers built between 1924 and 1929 was a political settlement financed by a private capital market in a country that had refused to join the political settlement. ⚠️ Nobody chose that arrangement; it emerged. And because nobody chose it, nobody was responsible for maintaining it.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

By the end of 1923 everybody involved had lost. France had occupied the Ruhr and got £40 million of goods and a diplomatic breach with London. Germany had defended its territory with passive resistance and destroyed its own currency doing so. Britain had watched both. The only remaining move was a solution that nobody present could fund - and the only country that could fund it had voted three years earlier to have nothing to do with European political commitments.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the way this was resolved is the most quietly important fact of the decade. The Americans did not join the political order; they financed it privately while remaining outside it. That is the arrangement that produced the recovery, and it is also the arrangement that made the recovery reversible by a decision taken in New York for reasons that had nothing to do with European security.

### 2. The change of leadership, which the source treats as causal

✅ **Fact:** the source attributes the general improvement in the international atmosphere from 1924 partly to changes in political leadership. In France, Edouard Herriot and Aristide Briand; in Germany, Gustav Stresemann; and in Britain, James Ramsay MacDonald came to power, and all were keen to improve relations.

✅ **Fact:** the three newcomers to international politics - MacDonald, Herriot and Stresemann, the last of whom was German Foreign Minister from 1924 to 1929 - were eager for reconciliation. ✅ Stresemann was in charge of German foreign policy from November 1923 until 1929.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an account that treats the 1924-29 recovery as purely structural - credit arrived, therefore relations improved - is incomplete, because the source explicitly assigns weight to personnel. ⚠️ The strongest formulation is a conditional one: the credit created the possibility of reconciliation and a particular set of ministers converted the possibility into instruments. That formulation matters later, because when Stresemann died in October 1929 the personnel half of the explanation disappeared in the same month as the financial half.

### 3. The Dawes Plan, 1924: the four terms

✅ **Fact:** the plan was worked out at a conference in London in 1924, as an attempt to break the general deadlock. ✅ The Americans were persuaded to take part, and the conference was chaired for part of the time by the American representative, General Dawes.

| Term | Content |
|---|---|
| The total | ✅ **No reduction** was made in the total amount the Germans were expected to pay |
| The schedule | ✅ It was agreed that they should pay annually only what they could reasonably afford, until they became more prosperous |
| The credit | ✅ A foreign loan of **800 million gold marks**, mostly from the USA, was to be made to Germany |
| The quid pro quo | ✅ The French, now assured of at least some reparations, agreed to **withdraw their troops from the Ruhr** |

✅ **Fact:** the plan was successful. The German economy began to recover on the basis of the American loans, and international tensions gradually relaxed, preparing the way for the next agreements.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the design. Dawes did not settle the quarrel; it *suspended* it by converting a political dispute about capacity into an annual technical assessment, and it bought French consent with a credible flow of payments rather than with a security guarantee. ⚠️ That is why Locarno had to follow: the security question had been deferred, not answered, and France required an answer before it would accept Germany's return to the diplomatic club. Dawes and Locarno are therefore two halves of a single bargain, and treating them as separate items on a list loses the logic.

### 4. United States policy: isolation with an economic exception

✅ **Fact:** the United States had been deeply involved in the First World War, and to Wilson's bitter disappointment the Senate voted to reject both the Versailles peace settlement and the League.

✅ **Fact:** what followed was not total withdrawal. The source records that the Americans avoided political disputes with other states and the signing of treaties, but that during the prosperous years of the 1920s they tried to increase trade and profits by investment abroad - in Europe, Canada, and Central and South America.

✅ **Fact:** the Washington Conferences of 1921-22 were called by President Harding, which is itself evidence that the isolation was political rather than absolute.

✅ **Fact:** on war debts the record is specific. The American government had organised loans to Britain and her allies amounting to almost twelve billion dollars. ✅ Some argued that the United States should cancel the debts, since it had done well out of the war by taking over former European markets, but both Harding and Coolidge insisted that repayments be made; ✅ the United States allowed much lower interest rates depending on the agreement reached with each state.

✅ **Fact:** faced with the German financial crisis of 1923, the Americans had to change their approach and take part in the Dawes and Young Plans of 1924 and 1929. ✅ The source states the resulting loop directly: America lent money to Germany so that she could pay reparations to France and Britain, who used the money to repay America. ✅ It also records the aggravating factor: American insistence on keeping high tariffs was a contributory problem.

✅ **Fact:** the Kellogg-Briand Pact of 1928 is recorded as another notable, though useless, American initiative.

✅ **Fact:** relations with Britain were uneasy, not only because of war debts but because British Conservatives resented the limitations on British naval expansion.

✅ **Fact:** and the decisive record for the 1930s: the United States returned to a policy of strict isolation when the Japanese invaded Manchuria, refusing to join in economic sanctions or to make any move which might lead to war.

**The statutory form the isolation took, and the point at which it was relaxed.** ⚠️ The held sources record the *posture* and not the *legislation*, which leaves the standard phrase "the USA was still in favour of isolation" - used in the appeasement chapter and repeated in every account of 1937-39 - without the instruments that gave it force. Those instruments are supplied here, labelled as material-fact-pass items and recorded in the final ledger.

✅ **Fact:** on 31 August 1935 Congress passed the first Neutrality Act, prohibiting the export of "arms, ammunition, and implements of war" from the United States to foreign nations at war and requiring American arms manufacturers to apply for an export licence; American citizens travelling in war zones were advised that they did so at their own risk. President Roosevelt originally opposed the legislation and relented in the face of strong Congressional and public opinion.

✅ **Fact:** on 29 February 1936 Congress renewed the Act until May 1937 and prohibited Americans from extending any loans to belligerent nations.

✅ **Fact:** the Neutrality Act of 1937, whose extension was supported by the outbreak of the Spanish Civil War and the rising tide of fascism in Europe, forbade American citizens to travel on belligerent ships, prevented American merchant ships from transporting arms to belligerents even if the arms were produced outside the United States, gave the President authority to bar all belligerent ships from American waters and to extend the export embargo to any additional "articles or materials", and brought civil wars within its terms. ✅ It contained one concession: belligerents might, at the President's discretion, acquire any items **except arms** so long as they paid immediately and carried the goods in non-American ships - the "cash-and-carry" provision, set to expire after two years, while the rest of the Act was permanent.

✅ **Fact:** after the German occupation of Czechoslovakia in March 1939 an attempt to renew and widen cash-and-carry was defeated in Congress; in November 1939, after war had begun in Europe, a final Neutrality Act lifted the arms embargo and placed all trade with belligerent nations under cash-and-carry, while the ban on loans remained and American ships were barred from carrying goods to belligerent ports.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** with the legislation in view, "strict isolation" can be answered precisely rather than repeated. Three findings follow. First, the isolation of the later 1930s was **statutory rather than merely attitudinal**: after August 1935 an American administration that wished to supply a victim of aggression was prohibited from doing so by its own law, which is why the recorded refusal to join sanctions over Manchuria hardened into a general incapacity rather than a series of separate refusals. Second, the legislation was **neutral in form and unequal in effect**: an arms embargo applied to both sides in a conflict withholds supplies from the state that cannot manufacture its own, and the cash-and-carry concession of 1937 was usable only by a belligerent with hard currency and ships - a structure that in practice favoured Britain and France. Third, the November 1939 revision dates the **end of the strict phase** to two months after this topic closes, which is the correct answer to any question asking how long American isolation lasted: the posture was continuous from 1920, the statutory form ran from August 1935, and the relaxation began in November 1939 - after, and not before, the European war had started. ⚠️ Nothing in this legislation was available to Britain and France when they took the decisions of 1935-38, and that is its significance for this topic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "isolationism" is therefore the wrong word for the whole period and the right word for the decisive moments. The United States shaped the interwar economy continuously and withdrew from the interwar security order at every point where a commitment might have followed, and from 1935 it bound itself by statute against the kind of commitment that collective security required. ⚠️ The most precise formulation, and one that will serve in any answer, is that the United States accepted economic leadership and declined political responsibility - and that the gap between the two is one of the four or five load-bearing causes of the collapse of collective security.

### 5. The Washington precedent, read alongside Dawes

| Instrument | American role | Its structural defect |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Washington Conferences, 1921-22 | ✅ Convened by Harding to improve relations with Japan | ✅ Left Japan supreme in the Far East with a concentrated third-largest navy while British and American fleets were spread more widely; the guarantee of Chinese neutrality carried no enforcement |
| ✅ Dawes Plan, 1924 | ✅ Chaired in part by the American representative; supplied the 800 million gold marks | ⚠️ Made European political stability contingent on a private American capital flow that no government had promised to maintain |
| ✅ Young Plan, 1929 | ✅ Committee chaired by the American banker Owen Young | ⚠️ Arrived in the same year as the Crash, so the relief it offered was overtaken by the contraction it could not anticipate |
| ✅ Kellogg-Briand Pact, 1928 | ✅ Kellogg proposed widening Briand's bilateral idea to the whole world | ✅ No sanctions clause; recorded as completely useless in consequence |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** all four instruments share one property. In each case the United States supplied the convening power, the chairmanship or the money, and in none of them did it accept an obligation that could later be invoked against it. ⚠️ That is a consistent policy, not a series of accidents, and stating it as a policy is the difference between describing American behaviour and explaining it.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is it not unfair to blame the United States for the failure of a European security system it never joined and never promised to defend?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the point of principle entirely: no obligation was undertaken, the Senate's rejection in March 1920 was a lawful exercise of a constitutional power, and the source records the American reasoning - fear that membership might involve the country in another war. Second, distinguish blame from causation. The historical question is not whether the United States was culpable but whether its absence is part of the explanation, and the source records two specific effects: the League was deprived of a powerful member whose presence would have been of great psychological and financial benefit, and over Manchuria Britain and France were reluctant to apply a trade boycott of Japan especially without American help. Third, note the asymmetry that does create a genuine historical criticism: the country that declined political responsibility did not decline economic leadership, and it insisted on debt repayment while maintaining high tariffs that made repayment harder. ⚠️ The fair verdict is that American absence was a cause and not a fault, with one exception - the combination of debt insistence and tariff protection, which the source itself identifies as a contributory problem.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far can it be said that the United States followed a policy of strict isolation in foreign affairs between the wars?
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of American economic policy for interwar international relations.
> ⚠️ "The stability of the 1920s was borrowed, not built." Examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The first of those framings has a precise answer and it is worth rehearsing as a template: politically yes at every decision point and statutorily so from August 1935, economically no at any point, and the gap between the two is the historically significant fact. ⚠️ Supply three items of evidence on each side, date the statutory phase from the first Neutrality Act to the cash-and-carry revision of November 1939, and give a verdict; do not narrate the Senate debate.

### Traps

- Saying the Dawes Plan reduced reparations. ✅ It made **no reduction in the total** and changed the annual schedule.
- Forgetting the French quid pro quo. ✅ The Ruhr withdrawal is part of the plan's political content.
- Calling American policy "isolationism" without qualification. ✅ Harding convened Washington; American capital financed the recovery; Americans chaired both reparations committees.
- Treating the circular flow as an inference. ✅ It is recorded explicitly, as is the tariff problem.
- Omitting the recorded war-debt total of almost twelve billion dollars, which is the reason the loop existed at all.
- Missing the Manchuria clause: the recorded return to strict isolation, with a refusal to join economic sanctions.
- Describing American isolation after 1935 as a mood. It was statutory, and the statutes have dates.
- Dating cash-and-carry to 1939 alone. The 1937 Act contained a two-year cash-and-carry concession for non-arms goods; the November 1939 Act extended it to arms and lifted the embargo.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Leadership change from 1924: Herriot and Briand in France, Stresemann in Germany, MacDonald in Britain - all keen to improve relations.
2. ✅ Stresemann was German Foreign Minister from November 1923 until 1929.
3. ✅ Dawes Plan 1924: London conference, chaired in part by General Dawes; no reduction in the total; annual payments according to capacity; a loan of 800 million gold marks mostly from the USA; French withdrawal from the Ruhr.
4. ✅ The plan worked: German recovery on the basis of American loans, and a gradual relaxation of tension.
5. ✅ March 1920: the Senate rejected both the settlement and the League, out of fear of involvement in another war.
6. ✅ American war loans to Britain and her allies: almost twelve billion dollars; Harding and Coolidge insisted on repayment with reduced interest rates.
7. ✅ The circular flow, recorded: America lends to Germany; Germany pays reparations to France and Britain; they repay America - alongside American high tariffs, a contributory problem.
8. ✅ Kellogg-Briand 1928 recorded as a notable but useless American initiative.
9. ✅ Over Manchuria the United States returned to strict isolation, refusing economic sanctions or any move that might lead to war.
10. ✅ The statutory form: Neutrality Act of 31 August 1935 - arms embargo on belligerents, export licences, travel at own risk; February 1936 renewal to May 1937 with a ban on loans; the 1937 Act - no travel on belligerent ships, no American carriage of arms, presidential power to bar belligerent ships and widen the embargo, civil wars included, and a two-year cash-and-carry concession for non-arms goods; November 1939 - embargo lifted, all trade placed under cash-and-carry, loan ban retained, American ships barred from belligerent ports.
11. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: economic leadership accepted, political responsibility declined and after 1935 legally foreclosed - and the gap between them is a load-bearing cause.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain precisely why the Dawes Plan should be described as a political instrument rather than as a financial one, and state the vulnerability it created.

**Model answer:** It was financial in form and political in function. Its financial content was modest and is quickly stated: no reduction was made in the total sum Germany was expected to pay, the annual instalments were to be limited to what Germany could reasonably afford until she became more prosperous, and a foreign loan of 800 million gold marks, mostly from the United States, was to be provided. Its political content is what made it decisive. It removed the reparations question from the arena of unilateral coercion, where the Ruhr occupation had just demonstrated that coercion destroyed the debtor's capacity to pay and split the Anglo-French alliance; it gave France a credible flow of receipts in exchange for withdrawing its troops from the Ruhr, which was a security concession purchased with money rather than with a guarantee; it brought the United States back into European affairs in a technical capacity that involved no treaty obligation, with the conference chaired for part of the time by the American representative; and it created the conditions in which reconciliation ministers in three capitals could negotiate Locarno the following year. The vulnerability it created was structural and nobody decided upon it. European political stability now depended on a continuing flow of private American credit that no government had undertaken to maintain, inside a circular arrangement in which America lent to Germany, Germany paid reparations to France and Britain, and they repaid America - while American tariffs remained high, making it harder for Europeans to earn dollars by any other route. The loop had a single source of new money at its top, so a contraction of American lending would stop every subsequent transfer simultaneously rather than gradually, and the political settlement resting on those transfers would lose its financial foundation at the same moment.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the 1920s recovery as evidence that the interwar order was fundamentally sound and was merely interrupted by the Depression. The recovery was genuine, but its foundation was a capital flow from a state that had refused to join the security order, and its security content was deferred rather than settled. An answer that grasps this can explain why the collapse after 1929 was so rapid; an answer that treats the 1920s as a stable order interrupted by bad luck cannot.

*The money is in place, the French have left the Ruhr and three reconciliation ministers are in office. The next lesson is the high point of the interwar order - and the exact place where it built its own weakness in, deliberately, with everyone present understanding what was being left out.*

## Lesson 8 - Locarno, 1925: what was guaranteed, by whom, and what was deliberately left open

Progress: 8/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the parties and the core guarantee; the guarantor role of Britain and Italy; the arbitration treaties with Poland and Czechoslovakia and the frontiers Germany would not guarantee; the French promise to Poland and Czechoslovakia; the "Locarno honeymoon" and the Nobel award; Germany's entry into the League in 1926 with a permanent Council seat; the Rhineland withdrawal agreement of September 1926; the recorded historians' verdict on the omission; Taylor on sincerity and Stresemann's recorded private aims; the single current linkage of this session

Book context: queried - the core account's Locarno section with the parties, the mutual guarantee, the British and Italian guarantor role, the arbitration agreements, the explicit refusal to guarantee the eastern frontiers, the reaction, Germany's League entry with a Permanent Council seat, the Nobel award, the September 1926 Rhineland agreement, and the later historians' criticism; also its record of Taylor's judgement on Briand and Stresemann and of Stresemann's private determination
CA search: executed - one bounded search on official commemoration of the Locarno centenary, for the single current linkage permitted in this session

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the guarantee map - solid lines west, dotted lines east

```text
   LOCARNO, 1925 - parties: GERMANY, FRANCE, BRITAIN, ITALY,
   BELGIUM, POLAND, CZECHOSLOVAKIA
   ==================================================================

   THE WESTERN SETTLEMENT (solid: a mutual guarantee with guarantors)

        GERMANY =============== FRANCE =============== BELGIUM
            \\                    ||                    //
             \\   "promise to respect their JOINT       //
              \\   FRONTIERS"                          //
               \\                ||                   //
                \\               ||                  //
            +----------------------------------------------+
            |   IF ONE OF THE THREE BREAKS THE AGREEMENT,  |
            |   BRITAIN AND ITALY WILL ASSIST THE STATE    |
            |   WHICH IS BEING ATTACKED                    |
            +----------------------------------------------+
                      guarantors: BRITAIN, ITALY

   ------------------------------------------------------------------

   THE EASTERN SETTLEMENT (dotted: arbitration only, no guarantee)

        GERMANY - - - - - - - - POLAND
        GERMANY - - - - - - - - CZECHOSLOVAKIA
             arbitration agreements for possible disputes
             BUT: "Germany would NOT guarantee her frontiers
                   with Poland and Czechoslovakia"
             AND: no guarantee by BRITAIN either

        FRANCE ---------------- POLAND
        FRANCE ---------------- CZECHOSLOVAKIA
             France would help them if Germany attacked
             (a French obligation, not a collective one -
              and France's route to them runs through the
              German frontier it has just promised to respect)

   ==================================================================
   THE RECORDED VERDICT OF LATER HISTORIANS:
   "there was one glaring omission from the agreements - no
    guarantees were given by Germany or Britain about Germany's
    eastern frontiers with Poland and Czechoslovakia, THE VERY AREAS
    WHERE TROUBLE WAS MOST LIKELY TO ARISE. By ignoring this problem,
    the British gave the impression that they might not act if
    Germany attacked Poland or Czechoslovakia."
   ==================================================================
   AND THE CONTEMPORARY BRITISH GLOSS, FROM THE SAME MOMENT:
   Austen Chamberlain remarked at the time of Locarno that no British
   government would ever risk the bones of a single British grenadier
   in defence of the Polish Corridor.
   => "it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on
       eastern Europe."
```

*This diagram is the most valuable single page in the topic and it should be reproducible from memory. ⚠️ Locarno did not fail to consider the eastern frontiers; it considered them and chose a weaker instrument for them. The asymmetry was designed, was publicly explained by a British foreign minister, and was read correctly in Berlin at the time.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A guarantee is a promise to fight. No government gives one lightly, and no government gives one where it cannot fight effectively or where its public will not support the fight. Britain in 1925 was willing to promise to fight on the Rhine, where it had fought in living memory, where its own security was engaged through Belgium and the Channel, and where its public would accept the case. It was not willing to promise to fight on the Vistula.

That is a coherent position and it was stated openly. The difficulty is not that it was dishonest; the difficulty is that it was *published*. Everybody in Europe now knew where the line of British commitment ran, including the governments whose plans depended on knowing it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical key to Locarno is therefore not hypocrisy but information. A security system that publicly grades its commitments has told potential revisionists exactly where revision will be cheapest. ⚠️ That is a general proposition about deterrence, it can be stated in one sentence, and it is worth more than a paragraph of narrative about the Locarno honeymoon.

### 2. What was actually agreed

✅ **Fact:** the Locarno Treaties were a number of different agreements involving Germany, France, Britain, Italy, Belgium, Poland and Czechoslovakia.

✅ **Fact:** the most important was that Germany, France and Belgium promised to respect their joint frontiers; and if one of the three broke the agreement, Britain and Italy would assist the state which was being attacked.

✅ **Fact:** Germany signed agreements with Poland and Czechoslovakia providing for arbitration over possible disputes, but Germany would not guarantee her frontiers with Poland and Czechoslovakia.

✅ **Fact:** it was also agreed that France would help Poland and Czechoslovakia if Germany attacked them.

| Relationship | Instrument | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| Germany-France-Belgium frontiers | ✅ Mutual promise to respect, with British and Italian guarantee of assistance | **Guarantee** |
| Germany-Poland; Germany-Czechoslovakia | ✅ Arbitration agreements for possible disputes | **Procedure only** |
| Germany's eastern frontiers | ✅ Expressly **not** guaranteed by Germany; ✅ and not guaranteed by Britain | **No commitment** |
| France-Poland; France-Czechoslovakia | ✅ French promise of help if Germany attacked | **Bilateral obligation, no guarantor** |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read down the strength column and the whole later crisis is visible in 1925. The western frontier had a guarantee and two guarantors. The eastern frontier had a procedure and no guarantor. France had an obligation it could discharge only by attacking across a frontier it had just promised to respect - which is the precise trap that immobilised France in March 1936 and again in September 1938. ⚠️ Stating that contradiction explicitly is one of the highest-value single observations available in this topic.

### 3. The reception, and the instruments that followed

✅ **Fact:** the agreements were greeted with wild enthusiasm all over Europe, and the reconciliation between France and Germany was referred to as the "Locarno honeymoon". ✅ It was regarded as Stresemann's greatest success to date.

✅ **Fact:** for the time being, as the world enjoyed a period of great economic prosperity, uneasy thoughts about the eastern omission were pushed into the background, and Germany was allowed to enter the League in 1926 with a seat on the Permanent Council.

✅ **Fact:** Stresemann and Briand - French Foreign Minister from 1925 to 1932 - met regularly and had friendly discussions, often joined by Austen Chamberlain, British Foreign Minister from 1924 to 1929. ✅ All three were awarded the Nobel Peace Prize for their part in Locarno. ⚠️ **A correction of the core account on this point.** It records that "the three of them were jointly awarded" the prize, which compresses two separate awards: ✅ **Fact:** the 1925 Peace Prize was shared by Austen Chamberlain, cited for his role in bringing about the Locarno Treaty, with Charles G. Dawes, cited for the Dawes Plan; the 1926 Peace Prize was shared by Briand and Stresemann, both cited for Locarno. ⚠️ The three Locarno ministers were therefore honoured in two successive years and never in a single joint award, and Dawes rather than Briand or Stresemann was Chamberlain's co-laureate.

✅ **Fact:** in September 1926 Stresemann and Briand reached agreement on the withdrawal of French troops from the Rhineland.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence Dawes → Locarno → League entry → Rhineland withdrawal is a single bargain paid in instalments, and it is worth presenting that way. Germany received credit, then equality of status, then a permanent Council seat, then the removal of foreign troops from its territory - and paid with a permanent renunciation of the western frontier claim and nothing at all in the east. ⚠️ Whether that was a good bargain for France is the real question a Locarno essay is asking, and the answer has to be graded rather than asserted.

### 4. The two recorded qualifications

✅ **Fact, the historians' verdict:** later historians were not so enthusiastic about Locarno. There was one glaring omission from the agreements - no guarantees were given by Germany or Britain about Germany's eastern frontiers with Poland and Czechoslovakia, the very areas where trouble was most likely to arise. ✅ By ignoring this problem, the British gave the impression that they might not act if Germany attacked Poland or Czechoslovakia.

✅ **Fact, the sincerity question:** there is some debate among historians about how genuine the apparent reconciliation between France and Germany really was. A. J. P. Taylor suggested that though Briand and Stresemann were sincere, "they did not carry their peoples with them"; nationalist feeling in the two countries was so strong that both men were limited in the concessions they could offer.

✅ **Fact, Stresemann's private aims:** the fact that Stresemann was secretly determined to get the frontier with Poland redrawn to Germany's advantage would have caused friction later, since Poland was France's ally. ✅ He was equally determined to work for union with Austria and a revision of the Versailles terms.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most delicate judgement in the lesson and it must be made carefully. Stresemann's private aims do not make Locarno a fraud. They make it an accurate instrument: he signed away the western frontier because he did not want it, and refused to sign away the eastern frontier because he did. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that Locarno recorded, rather than concealed, the actual distribution of German revisionist intent - and that the powers who signed it therefore had, from 1925, documentary evidence of where German revisionism would be directed. That is a considerably stronger claim than "Locarno was an illusion", and it is better supported.

### 5. Comparison: three verdicts on the Locarno phase

| Verdict | Its strongest evidence | Its weakness |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Genuine reconciliation** | ✅ Wild enthusiasm across Europe; the Nobel award; regular Stresemann-Briand-Chamberlain meetings; the Rhineland withdrawal agreement of September 1926; Germany's League entry with a permanent Council seat | ⚠️ Confuses the improvement of atmosphere with the settlement of a dispute |
| ⚠️ **An illusion dependent on prosperity** | ✅ The recorded judgement that the Locarno spirit proved an illusion because so much depended on economic prosperity, and that when this evaporated all the old hostilities and suspicions surfaced again | ⚠️ True but incomplete: it explains the timing of the collapse, not the direction it took |
| ⚠️ **Genuine but geographically incomplete** | ✅ The guarantee structure itself; ✅ the recorded glaring omission; ✅ Austen Chamberlain's grenadier remark; ✅ Stresemann's recorded private determination on the Polish frontier | ⚠️ Requires the candidate to know the instrument's contents, not just its reputation |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third is the verdict to write, and the basic owner supplies it in a rehearsable form: the Locarno settlement was genuine but conditional, resting on economic recovery and on leaving the eastern frontiers unguaranteed, and both conditions failed together after 1929. ⚠️ Note the word "together". The two failures were simultaneous but independent, and saying so prevents the lazy claim that the Depression caused the eastern crisis - the eastern gap was there from 1925 and would have been there in any economic weather.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Given that Britain would not in practice have fought for Poland in 1925, was the eastern omission not simply honest? Would a guarantee Britain did not mean have been better?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the realism: an unbacked guarantee is worse than no guarantee, because it invites the protected state to run risks it cannot cover, and the British position in 1925 - unwilling to commit Britain and the Empire to the defence of all the 1919 frontiers, as the Geneva Protocol rejection had already shown - was consistent and publicly reasoned. Second, identify what was actually lost: not the promise but the *ambiguity*. Before Locarno a revisionist government had to guess whether Britain would act in the east; after Locarno it did not have to guess. ⚠️ Deterrence is produced by uncertainty about response, and Locarno removed the uncertainty in precisely the theatre where revision was most likely. Third, note the confirming evidence: the source records that it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe - that is, the signal was received, and received in the right capital. ⚠️ The disciplined verdict is that the British position was defensible as policy and damaging as signalling, and that the damage was done by publication rather than by content.

### 7. The single current linkage carried in this session

📰 **Current linkage, recorded once and used once.** The United Kingdom's Foreign Secretary delivered a Locarno Centenary Speech, published on gov.uk on 9 December 2025, at the Foreign Office in the rooms that take their name from the Treaty-signing of 1925, co-hosted with the Swiss Mission in London and attended by the Mayor of Locarno and by representatives of other states that signed the Treaties in that room. The speech commemorates the hundredth anniversary of the signing and reflects on what it calls the spirit of Locarno for contemporary security policy.

📰 Two elements of that text, and only two, are used here. First, the official reflection names the contested flaws of the Treaties in its own words - that one could spend hours debating how far the flaws in the Treaties led to their demise: the weakness of the guarantees of Polish and Czech sovereignty, the limited institutional underpinnings, and the lack of resilience within the signatory nations. Second, it records the distinction between the *letter* of the Treaties, which it describes as still contested, and the *spirit* of the common endeavour, and it quotes the Nobel Peace Prize award speech of the following year to the effect that the accomplishment cannot be appreciated without recognising the violent nationalistic opposition in their own countries which several of the statesmen had to overcome.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the linkage carries **no historical fact** that the held sources do not already supply, and it is used for one purpose only. It shows that the verdict taught in this lesson from the historical sources - that the eastern guarantee gap is the decisive flaw, and that the signatories were constrained by nationalist opinion at home - is also the verdict stated in an official commemorative setting a century later, by the state whose foreign minister made the grenadier remark in 1925. ⚠️ The speech's contemporary policy content concerns present-day security questions and is outside the scope of this session; none of it is used, and no teaching claim anywhere in this session depends on it. ⚠️ Note also the exact correspondence with Taylor's recorded judgement: the official text's phrase about violent nationalistic opposition at home is the same finding as Taylor's "they did not carry their peoples with them", reached independently and stated in a different register.

### 8. UPSC application

The basic owner's directive map gives the demand precisely: a question asking you to assess the Locarno phase is really testing whether reconciliation was genuine or conditional on prosperity - and the answer that must not be written is "the Locarno spirit was good".

| Step | Content | Words at 250 |
|---|---|---|
| Thesis | ⚠️ Genuine but conditional: prosperity-dependent and geographically incomplete | 25 |
| The instrument | ✅ Western frontiers mutually guaranteed with Britain and Italy as guarantors; arbitration only in the east; Germany's refusal to guarantee the Polish and Czechoslovak frontiers; France's unsupported bilateral promise | 60 |
| The achievement | ✅ The honeymoon; the Nobel award; League entry in 1926 with a permanent Council seat; the Rhineland withdrawal agreement of September 1926 | 45 |
| The two conditions | ✅ Prosperity - the spirit proved an illusion because so much depended on it; ✅ geography - the glaring omission in the very areas where trouble was most likely | 60 |
| The evidence of intent | ✅ Austen Chamberlain's grenadier remark; ✅ Stresemann's private determination on the Polish frontier and union with Austria | 35 |
| Verdict | ⚠️ It reduced visible tension in western Europe without resolving the deeper revisionist question in eastern Europe | 25 |

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Locarno phase in interwar diplomacy.
> ⚠️ "Locarno was a peace for western Europe only." Examine.
> ⚠️ How far had international relations genuinely improved by 1929?

### Traps

- Saying Locarno guaranteed all German frontiers. ✅ Western frontiers only; the eastern frontiers were expressly not guaranteed.
- Saying Germany guaranteed nothing in the east. ✅ It signed arbitration agreements; it declined to guarantee the frontiers themselves. The distinction is the whole point.
- Forgetting the guarantors. ✅ Britain and Italy undertook to assist the state attacked - which is why Italy's later realignment mattered institutionally and not merely diplomatically.
- Omitting France's bilateral promise to Poland and Czechoslovakia, and the contradiction it created with the western guarantee.
- Dating Germany's League entry to 1925. ✅ 1926, with a seat on the Permanent Council.
- Presenting Stresemann as either a sincere European or a concealed revisionist. ✅ The record supports both simultaneously and the instrument reflects it.
- Using the centenary speech as historical evidence. It corroborates a verdict; it establishes nothing.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Parties: Germany, France, Britain, Italy, Belgium, Poland, Czechoslovakia.
2. ✅ Core term: Germany, France and Belgium promise to respect their joint frontiers; Britain and Italy will assist whichever of the three is attacked.
3. ✅ East: arbitration agreements with Poland and Czechoslovakia; Germany would **not** guarantee those frontiers; Britain gave no guarantee either.
4. ✅ France promised to help Poland and Czechoslovakia if Germany attacked them.
5. ✅ Reception: wild enthusiasm; the "Locarno honeymoon"; Stresemann's greatest success to date.
6. ✅ 1926: Germany enters the League with a seat on the Permanent Council. ✅ Nobel Peace Prizes for Locarno in two successive years - 1925 to Austen Chamberlain, shared with Charles G. Dawes for the Dawes Plan, and 1926 to Briand and Stresemann. ⚠️ The core account's "jointly awarded" formulation compresses the two awards and is corrected here.
7. ✅ September 1926: Stresemann and Briand agree on the withdrawal of French troops from the Rhineland.
8. ✅ The recorded glaring omission: no eastern guarantees, in the very areas where trouble was most likely; the British thereby gave the impression they might not act.
9. ✅ Austen Chamberlain at the time of Locarno: no British government would ever risk the bones of a single British grenadier in defence of the Polish Corridor; it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe.
10. ✅ Taylor: Briand and Stresemann were sincere but did not carry their peoples with them. ✅ Stresemann was privately determined on redrawing the Polish frontier, union with Austria and revision of Versailles.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the eastern omission at Locarno is better analysed as a signalling failure than as a moral failure, and show how that analysis changes the answer to a "was Locarno genuine?" question.

**Model answer:** A moral analysis asks whether Britain and Germany should have guaranteed the Polish and Czechoslovak frontiers and concludes that they should, which leaves nothing further to say and cannot be tested against evidence. A signalling analysis asks what information the instrument transmitted and to whom, and it can be tested. The instrument guaranteed the Franco-German and Belgo-German frontiers with Britain and Italy as guarantors, provided only arbitration procedures in the east, recorded that Germany would not guarantee her frontiers with Poland and Czechoslovakia, and left France with a bilateral promise to those two states that it could discharge only by crossing the frontier it had just guaranteed. The recorded consequence is precisely a transmission effect: by ignoring the eastern problem the British gave the impression that they might not act if Germany attacked Poland or Czechoslovakia, and it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe - a reading reinforced at the time by Austen Chamberlain's remark that no British government would risk the bones of a single British grenadier in defence of the Polish Corridor. Deterrence depends on a potential revisionist being uncertain about the response; Locarno replaced that uncertainty with a published map of where the guarantee ended. The change to the "was it genuine?" question is substantial. On a moral analysis the answer collapses into an accusation of hypocrisy, which fits neither Briand nor Stresemann as they are recorded. On a signalling analysis the answer is that Locarno was entirely genuine and precisely accurate: Germany surrendered the claim it did not intend to press and retained the claim it did, Stresemann being privately determined to have the Polish frontier redrawn and to work for union with Austria; and Britain promised what it meant to perform and no more. Locarno was therefore a truthful document whose truth was the problem.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from Stresemann's private aims that the Locarno phase was a deception and the 1920s recovery unreal. The recovery was real - Dawes credit, the honeymoon, League entry, the Rhineland withdrawal agreement - and the source's own caution is different and more precise: the spirit proved an illusion because so much depended on economic prosperity. Two separate conditions failed after 1929, one financial and one geographical, and collapsing them into a single accusation of bad faith loses both.

*Locarno is signed and the honeymoon is under way. The next lesson takes the phase to its end: a pact renouncing war that sixty-five states signed and nobody had to obey, a final reparations settlement that arrived too late, and the month - October 1929 - in which the two supports of the whole arrangement were removed within weeks of each other.*

## Lesson 9 - Kellogg-Briand and Young: the end of the Locarno phase

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the origin of the Kellogg-Briand Pact and the clause it did not contain; the Japanese counter-example recorded by the source itself; the Young Plan's figures, schedule and Keynesian antecedent; the Nazi campaign against acceptance; Stresemann's death and the Crash in the same month; the Lausanne Conference of 1932; the recorded verdict on the whole reconstruction; how to answer "was the 1920s recovery real?"

*The visual makes a point that a chronological list cannot. ⚠️ The high point of interwar conciliation and the removal of its two supports are not separated by a decade of decline; they are the same twelve months. That timing is the strongest single argument for the "conditional" verdict on the Locarno phase, and it is the reason the 1930s feel so abrupt.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In 1928 sixty-five states signed a document renouncing war. Three years later one of them invaded a province of another. Nothing followed. The document did not fail because it was ignored; it had nothing in it that could be enforced.

This is the clearest available illustration of a distinction that runs through the whole topic and through international relations generally: the difference between a *norm* and a *commitment*. A norm declares what states should do. A commitment specifies what will happen to a state that does otherwise, and who will do it. The interwar order produced a great many norms and very few commitments, and the difference is where the marks are.

### 2. The Kellogg-Briand Pact, 1928

✅ **Fact:** the Pact was originally Briand's idea. He proposed that France and the United States should sign a pact renouncing war. ✅ Frank B. Kellogg, the American Secretary of State, proposed that the whole world should be involved, and eventually sixty-five states signed, agreeing to renounce war as an instrument of national policy.

✅ **Fact:** the source's assessment is direct: this sounded impressive but was completely useless, because no mention was made of sanctions against any state which broke its pledge. ✅ It supplies its own counter-example: Japan signed the Pact, but this did not prevent her from waging war against China only three years later.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Briand's original conception deserves a moment, because it explains the defect. He proposed a *bilateral* Franco-American pact - which, from the French point of view, would have re-established the American connection that had lapsed in 1920. Kellogg's counter-proposal to universalise it transformed a security instrument into a declaration. ⚠️ Universalising a commitment dilutes it: a promise between two states is a relationship, while a promise among sixty-five is a statement of principle. That observation is transferable to almost any question about international institutions and it costs one sentence.

### 3. The Young Plan, 1929

✅ **Fact:** the aim of this new initiative was to settle the remaining problem of reparations, because the Dawes Plan had left the total amount payable uncertain. ✅ In the improved atmosphere, the French were willing to compromise, and a committee chaired by an American banker, Owen Young, decided to reduce reparations from £6600 million to £2000 million, to be paid on a graded scale over the next fifty-nine years.

✅ **Fact:** this was the figure that Keynes had urged at Versailles, and its acceptance ten years later was an admission of error by the Allies.

✅ **Fact:** the plan was welcomed by many in Germany, but the Nazi party campaigned against accepting it, because they thought it offered Germany far too little; they wanted a much quicker and a much more radical revision of the peace settlement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Nazi campaign against the Young Plan is a fact of first-class analytical importance and it is almost always omitted. It establishes, in 1929, that the German revisionist right was not seeking relief from reparations - relief was on offer and it opposed the offer. What it sought was revision of the settlement as such, and at a pace that negotiation could not deliver. ⚠️ Anyone writing on appeasement must reckon with this, because it is the earliest clear evidence that the assumption underlying appeasement - that German grievances were finite and could be satisfied by concession - was already contradicted by the behaviour of the movement that would be in power four years later.

### 4. The two removals, and what they were removals of

✅ **Fact:** even before there was time to put the Young Plan into operation, a series of events following in rapid succession destroyed the fragile harmony of Locarno.

✅ **Fact, first:** the death of Stresemann in October 1929, reportedly from overwork at the age of only fifty-one. ✅ The source records what this removed: one of the outstanding "men of Locarno", a German leader who aimed at peaceful change in Europe and hoped that his country's economic recovery would be successful enough to prevent the extremists of both right and left from gaining power in Germany.

✅ **Fact, second:** the Wall Street Crash on the American stock exchange in the same month, which soon developed into a worldwide economic crisis; by 1932 there were over six million people unemployed in Germany.

✅ **Fact:** hope was kept alive by the Lausanne Conference of 1932, at which Britain and France released Germany from most of the remaining reparations payments. However, in January 1933 Hitler became German Chancellor, and after that international tension mounted.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what the Lausanne release proves and what it does not. It proves that the reparations question was, in the end, conceded in full - which destroys the claim that the war of 1939 was caused by reparations. It does not prove generosity, because by 1932 the payments were not being made in any case. ⚠️ The precise formulation is that Lausanne converted a fact into a policy: it recognised a default and called it a release. That is a more accurate sentence than either "the Allies finally showed generosity" or "the Allies bled Germany until 1932", and it can be defended from the record.

### 5. The recorded retrospective verdict on the whole 1920s reconstruction

✅ **Fact:** the source closes its survey of the attempts to improve international relations with an explicit retrospective judgement: in retrospect it can be seen that the statesmen of the world had only limited success in improving international relations. ✅ Even the "Locarno spirit" proved an illusion, because so much depended on economic prosperity; when this evaporated, all the old hostilities and suspicions surfaced again, and authoritarian regimes came to power which were prepared to risk aggression.

| Instrument | ✅ Year and recorded content | ⚠️ Built-in weakness |
|---|---|---|
| Washington Conferences | ✅ 1921-22: naval limits and a temporary Far Eastern easing | ⚠️ Did not restrain land forces or Japanese continental ambition |
| Dawes Plan | ✅ 1924: American loan of 800 million gold marks; payment by capacity; French withdrawal from the Ruhr | ⚠️ Made European stability dependent on American credit |
| Locarno Treaties | ✅ 1925: western frontiers guaranteed, with Britain and Italy as guarantors | ✅ Eastern frontiers **not** guaranteed |
| Germany enters the League | ✅ 1926, with a seat on the Permanent Council | ✅ Reversed in 1933 |
| Kellogg-Briand Pact | ✅ 1928: sixty-five states renounce war as an instrument of national policy | ✅ No sanctions clause; no enforcement mechanism |
| Young Plan | ✅ 1929: reparations cut to £2000 million over fifty-nine years | ⚠️ Arrived as the Depression began; opposed by the Nazi party as too little |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the right-hand column downward and a single fault repeats in six different forms. Every instrument of the 1920s addressed the *manifestation* of insecurity - a naval building race, a reparations schedule, a western frontier dispute, a legal status, a moral principle, a payment total - and none addressed the *source*, which was that a dissatisfied great power intended to revise an eastern settlement that nobody had guaranteed. ⚠️ Saying that in one sentence is the single most efficient way to answer "how far had international relations genuinely improved by 1929?"

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is the dismissal of Kellogg-Briand as "completely useless" too severe? Did the Pact not establish a norm against aggressive war that mattered later?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, hold the source's finding where it applies: as an instrument of restraint in the period 1928-39 it accomplished nothing, and the source's own example - Japan signing and then waging war against China three years later - is decisive on that point. Second, concede the norm argument at its strongest and mark it as inference rather than as recorded fact: ⚠️ a universal renunciation of war as an instrument of national policy is a change in the vocabulary of legitimacy, and every subsequent aggressor in this period felt obliged to supply a justification - self-determination, the protection of a minority, the prevention of communism, a frontier incident - rather than simply announcing a conquest. ⚠️ That is a real effect, and it is not nothing. Third, state the limit precisely: a norm that changes the *rhetoric* of aggression without changing its *cost* alters the language and not the behaviour, and the evidence base records the behaviour. ⚠️ The disciplined verdict is that Kellogg-Briand was consequential for international law and inconsequential for international security, and that a candidate should say which of the two the question is about.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The achievements of the 1920s were real but conditional." Examine with reference to interwar diplomacy.
> ⚠️ Assess the reasons why there were no major international conflicts during the 1920s.
> ⚠️ How far did the instruments of the 1920s address the real causes of insecurity in Europe?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The six-row instrument table is the highest-yield single item in this lesson: two columns, six rows, and a verdict. Reproduced accurately it will carry the evidence requirement of any 15-mark question on the 1920s, leaving the rest of the answer free for argument. ⚠️ Do not narrate the Crash; the world-economy owner holds it, and one clause is the correct allocation.

### Traps

- Saying Kellogg-Briand "outlawed war" without the recorded qualification that it contained no sanctions clause.
- Giving the number of signatories as anything other than sixty-five.
- Saying the Young Plan cancelled reparations. ✅ It cut them from £6600 million to £2000 million over fifty-nine years; ✅ the cancellation of most of the remainder came at Lausanne in 1932.
- Omitting the Nazi campaign against the Young Plan, which is the best single piece of evidence against the finite-grievance assumption.
- Separating Stresemann's death from the Crash. ✅ They are the same month, October 1929.
- Treating the Locarno spirit as ended by Hitler. ✅ The recorded sequence is death, Crash, Depression, then Hitler in January 1933.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Kellogg-Briand 1928: Briand's original bilateral Franco-American idea; Kellogg proposed universalising it; sixty-five states signed, renouncing war as an instrument of national policy.
2. ✅ The defect and the proof: no mention of sanctions; Japan signed and waged war against China three years later; the source calls it completely useless.
3. ✅ Young Plan 1929: committee chaired by the American banker Owen Young; reparations reduced from £6600 million to £2000 million on a graded scale over fifty-nine years.
4. ✅ That was the figure Keynes had urged at Versailles; acceptance ten years later was an admission of error by the Allies.
5. ✅ The Nazi party campaigned against accepting the Young Plan as offering Germany far too little.
6. ✅ October 1929: Stresemann dies, reportedly from overwork, aged only fifty-one; the Wall Street Crash occurs in the same month.
7. ✅ By 1932 there were over six million unemployed in Germany.
8. ✅ Lausanne 1932: Britain and France release Germany from most of the remaining reparations payments.
9. ✅ January 1933: Hitler becomes German Chancellor; after that international tension mounted.
10. ✅ The recorded retrospective verdict: the statesmen had only limited success; the Locarno spirit proved an illusion because so much depended on economic prosperity.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the Kellogg-Briand Pact and the Young Plan, explain the difference between a norm and a commitment, and show why the distinction determines the verdict on the 1920s.

**Model answer:** A norm declares what states ought to do; a commitment specifies what will be done to a state that does otherwise, and by whom. Kellogg-Briand is the purest available example of a norm. It began as Briand's proposal for a bilateral Franco-American renunciation of war, which would have had the character of a commitment because it would have re-created a relationship between two named parties; Kellogg's proposal to involve the whole world converted it into a universal declaration, eventually signed by sixty-five states, renouncing war as an instrument of national policy and containing no mention of sanctions against any state that broke its pledge. The source's own test case follows: Japan signed the Pact and waged war against China three years later, and nothing in the instrument provided for a response. The Young Plan illustrates the same distinction from the other direction. It was a genuine commitment, because it specified sums, a schedule and parties - £2000 million on a graded scale over fifty-nine years, replacing the £6600 million fixed in 1921 - and it was honoured to the extent that the parties were able, being superseded by the Lausanne release of 1932. The distinction determines the verdict on the decade because the achievements of the 1920s divide unevenly between the two categories. The commitments were financial - Dawes, Young, Lausanne - and they were delivered. The commitments that were security commitments existed only in western Europe, where Locarno created a mutual guarantee with Britain and Italy as guarantors. Everywhere else - the eastern frontiers, the Far East after Washington, and the whole field covered by Kellogg-Briand - the order consisted of norms. A decade that settled every financial question by commitment and left every security question east of the Rhine to norms had not resolved its central problem; it had reclassified it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the instruments of the 1920s were therefore worthless or cynical. They were neither. Dawes restored a wrecked economy, Locarno removed a live frontier dispute in the west, and League entry reintegrated a great power. The precise criticism is narrower and stronger: the order built commitments where agreement was easy and norms where agreement was hard, and it was in the second category that it was tested.

*The instruments are now all on the table and their weaknesses are identified. Before the tests begin, one more failure has to be examined, because it is the only one that was already complete before the Depression had done its work. Every member had promised to disarm when it accepted the Covenant. In 1932 they met to do it.*

## Lesson 10 - Disarmament: the failure that preceded the crisis

Progress: 10/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Covenant promise and the Disarmament Commission's record; Stresemann's recorded point about German compliance; the arms expenditure figures for 1925 and 1933; the World Disarmament Conference of 1932-33; the German demand for equality and the French eight-year condition; the British imperial argument; Hitler's withdrawal in October 1933 and Germany's exit from the League a week later; why disarmament failure is a structural argument and not an episode
