### Visual: the disarmament trap, drawn as a two-sided demand that cannot be met

```text
   THE COVENANT PROMISE: all member states had UNDERTAKEN TO REDUCE
   ARMAMENTS when they accepted the Covenant.
   THE RECORD BY 1932: the Disarmament Commission made NO PROGRESS.
   Only GERMANY had made any moves towards disarmament, as
   STRESEMANN REGULARLY POINTED OUT. In fact the rest seem to have
   INCREASED their arms expenditure:
        world expenditure on arms, 1925  ->  $3.5 BILLION
        world expenditure on arms, 1933  ->  around $5 BILLION
   ==================================================================

              THE WORLD DISARMAMENT CONFERENCE, GENEVA, 1932-3

   GERMANY SAYS:                         FRANCE SAYS:
   "equality of armaments with           "postponed for at least
    France"                               EIGHT YEARS"
        |                                      |
   two ways to equality:                  reason, recorded: alarmed by
   (a) France disarms down to             the rapid increase in support
       the German level, OR               for the Nazis in Germany;
   (b) Germany rearms up to               refused EITHER to disarm OR to
       the French level                   allow Germany equality
        |                                      |
        +------------------+-------------------+
                           |
   BRITAIN SAYS: "we need MORE armaments to protect the empire"
                           |
   ==================================================================
   THE TRAP, STATED AS A PROPOSITION:
   France will not disarm until it feels secure.
   France cannot feel secure while Germany is capable of rearming.
   Germany will not accept permanent inferiority.
   Therefore the conference has no solution available to it at all,
   and its failure is not a failure of will or of drafting.
   ==================================================================
                           |
   OCTOBER 1933: Hitler, knowing that Britain and Italy sympathised
   with Germany, WITHDREW FROM THE CONFERENCE - "which was doomed
   from that moment". A WEEK LATER Germany also withdrew from the
   LEAGUE.
                           |
   THE EXCUSE AND THE TECHNIQUE: he used the French attitude as the
   excuse; at the same time he insisted Germany was willing to disarm
   if other states would do the same, and that he wanted only peace.
   "This was one of his favourite techniques: to act boldly while at
   the same time soothing his opponents with the sort of conciliatory
   speeches he knew they wanted to hear."
```

*The trap in the middle box is the reason this lesson exists. ⚠️ Most scripts treat disarmament as an episode in which the powers failed to agree. It is better treated as a demonstration that in a system without a security guarantee, disarmament is unobtainable by negotiation, because the state that must disarm first is the state with the most to lose. ⚠️ The Geneva conference did not fail because the delegates were unreasonable. It failed because the problem, as posed, had no solution.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Two neighbours each keep a weapon because the other has one. Both would be better off if neither did. Neither will put it down first, because the moment between one disarming and the other doing so is the moment of maximum danger. This is the oldest problem in international politics, and the standard solution is a third party who guarantees the first mover.

In Europe after 1919 that third party was supposed to be the League. The League had no force, its principal member had declined in 1925 to guarantee all the 1919 frontiers, and in 1924-25 the one proposal to convert the Covenant's expectation into an obligation had been withdrawn by the government that succeeded its author. ⚠️ By 1932 there was therefore no guarantor, and a disarmament conference without a guarantor is a negotiation in which the first concession is the most dangerous act available.

### 2. The record before the conference

✅ **Fact:** all member states of the League had undertaken to reduce armaments when they accepted the Covenant. ✅ The Disarmament Commission made no progress in the near-impossible task of persuading them to do so.

✅ **Fact:** only Germany had made any moves towards disarmament, as Stresemann regularly pointed out. ✅ In fact the rest seem to have increased their arms expenditure: between 1925 and 1933 world expenditure on arms rose from $3.5 billion to around $5 billion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the German complaint was therefore accurate on its face and was made by the most conciliatory German statesman of the period rather than by a revisionist. ⚠️ That matters for an appeasement answer, because one of the recorded justifications for appeasement is that many felt Germany had been treated too harshly and that the most hated clauses of Versailles should be revised. The disarmament record supplies the strongest factual basis that justification ever had: Germany had disarmed under treaty compulsion, the other members had promised to follow and had instead spent more, and the promise was in the Covenant that every one of them had signed. ⚠️ An answer that presents German grievance as wholly manufactured cannot survive this fact.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, and a necessary caution:** the same record must be read alongside Rapallo. From 1922 German aircraft and ammunition factories operated in Russia and German officers trained there in the use of forbidden weapons. ⚠️ Formal compliance and covert evasion were simultaneous, and a disciplined answer states both rather than choosing the convenient one.

### 3. The conference, position by position

| Party | Recorded position | Recorded reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Germany** | ✅ Asked for equality of armaments with France | ⚠️ Equality was the minimum consistent with great-power status, and the Covenant promise gave the demand a legal basis |
| **France** | ✅ Demanded that equality be postponed for at least eight years; ✅ refused either to disarm or to allow Germany equality with them | ✅ Alarmed by the rapid increase in support for the Nazis in Germany |
| **Britain** | ✅ Said they needed more armaments to protect their empire | ⚠️ The same imperial reasoning that had produced the rejection of the Geneva Protocol in 1924-25 |
| **Germany after January 1933** | ✅ Hitler, knowing that Britain and Italy sympathised with Germany, withdrew from the conference in October 1933 | ✅ He used the French attitude as the excuse |

✅ **Fact:** the conference was doomed from the moment of the German withdrawal, and a week later Germany also withdrew from the League.

✅ **Fact:** the League chapter records the same event as a grave disappointment, and the same causal chain: the Germans asked for equality of armaments with France, and when the French demanded that this should be postponed for at least eight years, Hitler was able to use the French attitude as an excuse to withdraw Germany from the conference and later from the League.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "was able to use ... as an excuse" is the source's and it is exact. The French position supplied a pretext, not a cause. ⚠️ An answer that says French intransigence drove Germany out of the League has confused the two, and an answer that ignores the French position entirely has removed the reason the pretext was available. The correct formulation is that the French demand gave a government that wanted to leave a respectable reason for leaving.

### 4. The technique on display, and why it belongs in this lesson

✅ **Fact:** the source records the method alongside the event. Given that Germany was still militarily weak in 1933, Hitler had to move cautiously at first. He withdrew Germany from the World Disarmament Conference and from the League on the grounds that France would not agree to Germany having equality of armaments. ✅ At the same time he insisted that Germany was willing to disarm if other states would do the same, and that he wanted only peace. ✅ The source's comment: this was one of his favourite techniques - to act boldly while at the same time soothing his opponents with the sort of conciliatory speeches he knew they wanted to hear.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the first appearance of a pattern that recurs at every later stage - the Polish pact of 1934, the naval agreement of 1935, the twenty-five-year peace offer of March 1936, the Munich "scrap of paper" - and naming it once, here, saves a great deal of space later. ⚠️ The pattern is that each unilateral action was accompanied by a reassurance calibrated to what the other side wished to hear, and the reassurance was not an afterthought but part of the operation. ⚠️ A candidate who identifies this as a technique rather than as a series of broken promises is explaining rather than complaining.

### 5. Why the disarmament failure is structurally important

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences follow, and they are worth more than the episode itself.

1. **It removed the last device for limiting German rearmament by agreement.** After October 1933 the only remaining instruments were enforcement, which nobody would undertake, and bilateral bargains, which is what the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935 turned out to be.
2. **It transferred initiative to the revisionist.** From 1933 the question in every European capital was what Germany would do next, not what the League would decide.
3. **It gave the 1935 breaches a legitimating narrative.** ✅ When conscription was announced in March 1935, the recorded excuse was that Britain had just announced air force increases and France had extended conscription from twelve to eighteen months - a claim available only because disarmament had visibly failed.
4. **It separated Britain from France on rearmament policy.** ✅ Britain's stated need for more armaments to protect the empire and France's refusal to concede equality were not the same position, and the difference became an opening.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Was the French position not simply correct? Given what happened after 1935, was refusing German equality of armaments in 1932-33 not prudent rather than obstructive?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the prudence: the recorded French reason is alarm at the rapid increase in support for the Nazis, which was a correct reading of German politics and was vindicated within a year. Second, identify what the position could not deliver: refusing equality prevents equality only if the refusal can be enforced, and by 1932 France had no means of enforcement - the Ruhr experiment had failed in 1923-24, the Geneva Protocol had been rejected, the League had no force, and the eastern allies were, in the source's words, comparatively weak. ⚠️ A veto that cannot be backed converts into a grievance without preventing the outcome it vetoes, and that is exactly what happened between October 1933 and March 1935. Third, state the counterfactual honestly: ⚠️ it is not demonstrable that conceding equality in 1932 would have restrained German rearmament, and the source supplies no evidence either way. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is therefore narrow: the French position was well-founded and unenforceable, and its cost was that it handed a pretext to a government that wanted one - not that it caused the rearmament it failed to stop.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did disarmament fail in the interwar period?
> ⚠️ "Collective security and disarmament were incompatible in the absence of a guarantor." Examine.
> ⚠️ Account for the collapse of the Versailles disarmament regime by 1935.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The disarmament failure is more often a paragraph inside a larger answer than a question of its own, and the paragraph has a fixed shape: the Covenant promise, the record that only Germany complied while world arms spending rose from $3.5 billion to around $5 billion, the three positions at Geneva, the October 1933 withdrawal and the League exit a week later, and the structural point that disarmament without a guarantor asks the most exposed state to move first. ⚠️ Six sentences, and it will serve in a League question, an appeasement question and an origins-of-war question alike.

### Traps

- Saying nobody disarmed. ✅ Germany had, under treaty compulsion, as Stresemann regularly pointed out - while covertly evading the terms in Russia from 1922.
- Misquoting the expenditure figures or attaching them to the wrong years. ✅ From $3.5 billion in 1925 to around $5 billion in 1933, world expenditure on arms.
- Dating the conference to 1933 alone. ✅ 1932-33, meeting at Geneva.
- Saying Germany left the League and the conference simultaneously. ✅ The conference first, in October 1933; the League a week later.
- Treating the French eight-year demand as the cause of the German withdrawal. ✅ The source's word is "excuse".
- Forgetting that Britain's position at Geneva was a demand for *more* armaments, to protect the empire.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ All League members had undertaken to reduce armaments on accepting the Covenant; the Disarmament Commission made no progress.
2. ✅ Only Germany had made any moves towards disarmament, as Stresemann regularly pointed out.
3. ✅ World expenditure on arms rose from $3.5 billion in 1925 to around $5 billion in 1933.
4. ⚠️ Simultaneous evasion: from Rapallo in 1922, German aircraft and ammunition factories and officer training in Russia.
5. ✅ The World Disarmament Conference met at Geneva in 1932-33.
6. ✅ Germany asked for equality of armaments with France; France demanded postponement for at least eight years and refused both to disarm and to concede equality, being alarmed at the rise of the Nazis.
7. ✅ Britain said it needed more armaments to protect its empire.
8. ✅ October 1933: Hitler withdrew from the conference, which was doomed from that moment; a week later Germany withdrew from the League.
9. ✅ The technique: bold action combined with conciliatory speeches of the kind his opponents wished to hear.
10. ⚠️ The structural point: disarmament by negotiation requires a guarantor for the first mover, and after the Geneva Protocol's rejection there was none.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the World Disarmament Conference had no solution available to it, and identify the earlier decision that had closed the only route by which such a conference could have succeeded.

**Model answer:** The conference had no solution because the three positions in the room were jointly unsatisfiable and each was rational on its own terms. Germany asked for equality of armaments with France, which could be achieved only by French reduction or German rearmament. France refused both, demanding that equality be postponed for at least eight years, and its recorded reason was alarm at the rapid increase in support for the Nazis - a fear that was vindicated within a year. Britain, the third principal, was not seeking reduction at all: it said it needed more armaments to protect its empire, and world expenditure on arms had in fact risen from $3.5 billion in 1925 to around $5 billion in 1933 even though every member had undertaken to reduce armaments on accepting the Covenant and only Germany, as Stresemann regularly pointed out, had made any moves towards doing so. The underlying structure is the standard first-mover problem: disarmament requires the most exposed state to reduce its forces before it knows whether the other will follow, and it will do so only if a third party guarantees it during the interval. The earlier decision that closed that route was the rejection of the Geneva Protocol. Ramsay MacDonald's resolution of 1924 would have pledged members to accept arbitration and to help any victim of unprovoked aggression, which is precisely the guarantee a disarming state requires; the Conservative government that followed him informed the League that it could not agree, being unwilling to commit Britain and the Empire to the defence of all the 1919 frontiers, and the League was left, in its critics' phrase, lacking teeth. Without a guarantor, the Geneva conference of 1932-33 was asking France to accept a risk that nobody would insure, and its failure was therefore determined before it met. Hitler's withdrawal in October 1933, followed a week later by Germany's departure from the League, used the French attitude as an excuse rather than as a cause.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the disarmament failure as a missed opportunity that better diplomacy could have taken. The evidence points the other way: the conference was attempting to solve a problem whose solution had been foreclosed by a decision taken eight years earlier in London. The transferable lesson - that disarmament and collective security are not two separate policies but two halves of one, and that neither is obtainable alone - is worth more than any narrative of the conference's proceedings.

*The instruments are built, their weaknesses are identified and the last mechanism for limiting rearmament by agreement has gone. What follows is the collapse. The next lesson supplies the variable that turned a set of manageable weaknesses into a sequence of crises - and it does so without straying into the economics, which belong to another owner.*

## Lesson 11 - The Depression as a diplomatic variable: the transmission, not the economics

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the boundary with the world-economy owner, stated and observed; the recorded diplomatic effects in the source's own words; the credit-withdrawal mechanism supplied by the owner; the Austro-German customs union of 1931 and the Hague ruling; the Kreditanstalt episode; "every country for itself"; the trade reorientation of south-eastern Europe; why the Depression explains timing rather than direction

### Visual: the transmission chain, with the boundary marked

```text
   domestic conditions in which the revisionists governed.
   IT EXPLAINS TIMING AND INTENSITY. IT DOES NOT EXPLAIN DIRECTION.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The top box is not decoration. ⚠️ The most common failure in this part of the syllabus is a script that answers an international-relations question with an economics essay. The boundary is stated by the owners themselves: the Depression is a mechanism in this topic and a subject in the world-economy topic, and a Depression question must not be answered from here.*

### 1. The boundary, stated once and observed throughout

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner records the rule explicitly. This topic uses the Depression as a *causal variable*; its causes, its transmission channels and the policy instruments used against it are owned by the world-economy topic, whose relevant section supplies the credit-withdrawal evidence that this chain depends on - that after the Crash the United States stopped any further loans and began to call in many of the short-term loans already made to Germany, producing a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks. ✅ The instruction attached is equally explicit: use that mechanism here, do not re-derive it, and do not answer a Depression question from this file alone.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the practical effect on an answer is simple and should be followed literally. One sentence establishes the mechanism; everything else in the paragraph must be about diplomacy. ⚠️ A candidate who writes three sentences about bank failures in an international-relations answer has spent a third of a paragraph in another topic's territory.

### 2. What the source records as the diplomatic effects

✅ **Fact:** the situation really began to drift out of control with the onset of the economic crisis, or the Great Depression as it was sometimes known. It brought unemployment and falling living standards to most countries, and caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany; together with Mussolini, they refused to keep to the rules and took a series of actions which revealed the League's weaknesses.

✅ **Fact:** towards the end of 1929 the world began to run into economic difficulties, which contributed towards a deterioration in international relations. ✅ It was partly for economic reasons that Japanese troops invaded Manchuria in 1931, and mass unemployment in Germany was important in enabling Hitler to come to power.

✅ **Fact:** the chapter covering 1933-39 opens with the formulation directly: economic problems caused the Locarno spirit to fade away, and the new rule seemed to be - every country for itself.

✅ **Fact:** the effect on enforcement capacity is recorded at the point where it mattered. Over Manchuria, the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, because Britain and France had serious economic problems; they were reluctant to apply a trade boycott of Japan in case it led to war, which they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last record is the single most useful Depression sentence in the whole topic, because it connects the economic variable to a specific diplomatic decision through a stated reasoning. ⚠️ The chain is: contraction → fiscal and industrial weakness → unwillingness to risk a trade war → unwillingness to risk a shooting war → sanctions not raised. Every link is recorded. A candidate who can reproduce that chain has converted "the Depression weakened the League" from an assertion into an explanation.

### 3. The Austro-German customs union of 1931: an economic proposal killed by security politics

✅ **Fact:** in 1931 the Germans proposed an Austro-German customs union to ease the economic crisis. ✅ The French insisted that the matter be referred to the International Court of Justice at the Hague, on the grounds that it was a violation of the Versailles Treaty. ✅ Though a customs union made economic sense, the court ruled against it, and the plan was dropped.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the French motive in context: the death of Stresemann, the world economic crisis and the growth of support in Germany for the Nazis had alarmed the French and made them adopt a tougher attitude towards Germany.

✅ **Fact:** the Austrian side of the same episode is recorded from the Austrian direction: Austria was scarcely a viable state economically or politically; in 1931 it was announced that Austria was preparing to enter a customs union with Germany in the hope of easing the economic situation; and France withdrew all its funds from the leading Austrian bank, the Kreditanstalt.

✅ **Fact:** the source also records the prior constraint. Most Austrians felt that the natural solution to their problems was union - Anschluss - with Germany; the Austrian parliament had voted to join Germany, but the Treaty of St Germain of September 1919 required the Austrians to promise not to unite with Germany for at least twenty years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this episode is worth a full paragraph in an answer on the Depression's diplomatic effects, because it contains the whole logic of the period in one case. An economic remedy was proposed, it was defensible on economic grounds, and it was blocked on security grounds by a legal instrument and a financial weapon. ⚠️ The consequence was that the economic problem remained, the political grievance grew, and the case for the *forcible* union of 1938 was strengthened by the demonstration that the peaceful version would be prevented. ⚠️ An answer that can state that sequence - remedy blocked, grievance compounded, forcible version later accepted without resistance - is doing causal history rather than chronology.

### 4. The trade reorientation of south-eastern Europe

✅ **Fact:** Yugoslavia is the recorded case. Disappointed with the economic help they had received from France, and nervous of Mussolini's intentions, Prince Paul, the regent, began to look towards Nazi Germany for trade and protection. ✅ In 1936 a trade treaty was signed with Germany; this led to a significant increase in trade, so that by 1938 Germany was taking over 40 per cent of Yugoslavia's exports. ✅ Friendship with Germany reduced the threat from Mussolini, who had signed the Rome-Berlin Axis agreement with Hitler in 1936; in 1937 Italy therefore signed a treaty with Yugoslavia; and as the international situation deteriorated during 1939, Yugoslavia found itself uncomfortably aligned with the Axis powers.

✅ **Fact:** the parallel effect on Czechoslovakia is recorded from the other direction. The surrounding states of central and eastern Europe reacted to the depression by increasing import duties and reducing imports; demand for Czech manufactures fell; and there was severe unemployment, especially in the industrial areas where the Sudeten Germans lived. ✅ Now they really had something to complain about, and both they and the Slovaks blamed the Czechs for their problems.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two distinct mechanisms are visible here and they should not be merged. The Yugoslav case is *external* realignment: economic dependence on Germany preceded and enabled diplomatic alignment with Germany, without any German political action inside Yugoslavia. ⚠️ The Czechoslovak case is *internal* destabilisation: a trade contraction produced regional unemployment that fell disproportionately on a national minority, converting a claimed grievance into a real one. ⚠️ Together they explain why the Depression mattered more in eastern and south-eastern Europe than anywhere else, and why the crises of 1938-39 occurred there.

### 5. Comparison: what the Depression does and does not explain

| Phenomenon | Does the Depression explain it? | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Japanese interest in Manchuria | ❌ No - it predates the Depression | ✅ Japan had been involved in the province since the 1890s and was given Port Arthur and a privileged position |
| The **timing** of the Manchurian invasion | ✅ Partly | ✅ It was partly for economic reasons that Japanese troops invaded in 1931 |
| German revisionism | ❌ No | ✅ Stresemann was privately determined on redrawing the Polish frontier and union with Austria by 1925; the Nazi party campaigned against the Young Plan in 1929 |
| Hitler's accession | ✅ Substantially, as a condition | ✅ Mass unemployment in Germany was important in enabling Hitler to come to power - the mechanism itself being owned by the fascism topic |
| Italian aggression in Abyssinia | ✅ Partly, as a motive | ✅ Italy was suffering from the depression, and a victorious war would divert attention from internal troubles and provide a new market |
| Anglo-French passivity over Manchuria | ✅ Directly | ✅ Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to apply a trade boycott |
| The eastern guarantee gap | ❌ No | ✅ It was written into Locarno in 1925 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the column of noes is what makes this a usable analytical position rather than a slogan. The Depression is a *permissive and accelerating* cause: it removed resistance and supplied motives, and it created the domestic conditions for governments that were already revisionist in intent. ⚠️ It did not create the revisionist programmes, and it did not create the structural gap in the security order. An answer that says "the Depression caused the Second World War" has attributed to an economic contraction a direction of travel that was set by 1925.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the Depression is only a permissive cause, why does the evidence base give it so much weight - including in the list of reasons for the League's failure?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the weight: the source lists the world economic crisis as the seventh and climactic reason for the League's failure and says the situation really began to drift out of control with its onset, and it is recorded as a proposed cause of the war in its own right. Second, distinguish weight from type. A permissive cause can carry enormous weight without supplying direction: remove the Depression and the revisionist programmes remain but the capacity to resist them is greater, the domestic base of the revisionist governments is narrower, and the enforcement decisions of 1931 and 1935 are taken in different fiscal conditions. Third, note the recorded qualification the source attaches to all three background causes - Versailles, the League, and the economic crisis - namely that while these factors no doubt helped to create the sort of atmosphere and tensions which might well lead to a war, something more was needed. ⚠️ That sentence is the source's own statement of the permissive-cause position, and quoting its substance is the safest way to give the Depression its weight without giving it the direction.

### 7. UPSC application

The basic owner's directive map is explicit about this demand family: a question on the Depression and international relations is testing the economic-to-diplomatic *transmission mechanism*, and the answer that must not be written is economics without diplomacy.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How did the Great Depression affect international relations in the 1930s?
> ⚠️ "The Depression destroyed the diplomacy of the 1920s." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the relative weight of economic and political factors in the collapse of collective security.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the mechanism in one sentence, then give three transmission channels with named cases - enforcement capacity via the Manchurian sanctions decision, regime change via Japan and Germany, and realignment via the Yugoslav trade figure or the Sudeten unemployment effect - and close with the timing-not-direction verdict. ⚠️ That structure fits 150 words and scales cleanly to 250.

### Traps

- Explaining the Crash. It belongs to the world-economy owner; one clause is the allocation.
- Saying the Depression "caused fascism". ✅ The recorded formulation is that mass unemployment was important in enabling Hitler to come to power, and the mechanism belongs to the fascism owner.
- Forgetting the Manchurian sanctions sentence, which is the best-evidenced transmission in the whole topic.
- Treating the Austro-German customs union as an economic footnote. It is the clearest case of an economic remedy blocked for security reasons, with a compounding political cost.
- Omitting the small-state realignment channel. ✅ Over 40 per cent of Yugoslavia's exports were going to Germany by 1938.
- Claiming the Depression created the eastern guarantee gap. ✅ That was written into Locarno in 1925.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Boundary: the Depression's causes, transmission and policy instruments are owned by the world-economy topic; this topic uses the mechanism and does not re-derive it.
2. ✅ The mechanism in one sentence: after the Crash the United States stopped further loans and called in short-term loans already made to Germany, producing a currency crisis and a run on the banks.
3. ✅ Recorded effects: unemployment and falling living standards in most countries; extreme right-wing governments in Japan and Germany; over six million unemployed in Germany by 1932.
4. ✅ "Every country for itself" is the recorded formulation for the new rule of the 1930s.
5. ✅ Enforcement capacity: over Manchuria, sanctions were never raised because Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to risk a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help.
6. ✅ 1931 Austro-German customs union: proposed to ease the crisis; France referred it to the Hague as a Versailles violation; the court ruled against it and the plan was dropped.
7. ✅ France withdrew all its funds from the Kreditanstalt, the leading Austrian bank, which collapsed.
8. ✅ St Germain, September 1919, had required Austria to promise not to unite with Germany for at least twenty years, although most Austrians favoured union.
9. ✅ Yugoslav realignment: trade treaty with Germany in 1936; by 1938 Germany was taking over 40 per cent of Yugoslavia's exports.
10. ✅ Czechoslovak destabilisation: neighbours raised import duties; demand for Czech manufactures fell; severe unemployment fell especially on the industrial areas where the Sudeten Germans lived.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the Depression is converted from an assertion into an explanation in an international-relations answer, using exactly three transmission channels and naming the case for each.

**Model answer:** The assertion is that the Depression destroyed the diplomacy of the 1920s. It becomes an explanation when each step from economic contraction to diplomatic decision is named. The mechanism is stated once and taken from the owner: after the Crash the United States stopped further lending and called in short-term loans already made to Germany, producing a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks. The first channel is enforcement capacity, and its case is Manchuria: over the Japanese invasion of 1931 the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, because Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to apply a trade boycott in case it led to a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help. The second channel is regime composition, and its cases are Japan and Germany: the crisis brought unemployment and falling living standards to most countries and caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in both, so that with Mussolini they refused to keep to the rules and took the series of actions that revealed the League's weaknesses; in Germany mass unemployment, over six million by 1932, was important in enabling Hitler to come to power. The third channel is realignment and internal destabilisation among the small states, and its cases are Yugoslavia and Czechoslovakia: Prince Paul, disappointed with French economic help and nervous of Mussolini, signed a trade treaty with Germany in 1936, and by 1938 Germany was taking over 40 per cent of Yugoslav exports, leaving the country uncomfortably aligned with the Axis by 1939; meanwhile central and eastern European states raised import duties, demand for Czech manufactures fell, and severe unemployment struck the industrial areas where the Sudeten Germans lived, converting a claimed grievance into a real one. Each channel connects an economic fact to a named diplomatic outcome, which is what an explanation requires.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that giving the Depression great weight means giving it explanatory direction. The source's own qualification governs here: the settlement, the League and the economic crisis helped to create the atmosphere and tensions in which a war might arise, but something more was needed. The Depression removed resistance and supplied motive and opportunity; the programmes it enabled were already written, and the gap in the security order it exploited had been designed into Locarno in 1925.

*The variable is in place. The next three lessons are the tests. Each is taught the same way - what happened, what was available and not done, and what every watching government learned - because it is the learning, rather than the territory, that makes these three crises the causal engine of the decade.*

## Lesson 12 - Manchuria, 1931-33: the first defiance and the lesson it taught

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Japanese interests before 1931 and the recorded motives; the invasion and the Chinese appeal; the Lytton Commission and its finding; Japan's rejection and withdrawal in March 1933; the recorded reason no sanctions were raised; the advance after 1933 and the full invasion of 1937; the League's second condemnation and its powerlessness; American isolation at the decisive moment; the demonstration effect on Rome and Berlin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's League chapter treatment of the Manchurian invasion, the Lytton Commission and the reasons no sanctions were raised; its chapter-5 section on relations between Japan and China with the recorded pre-1931 interests, the population figure, Manchukuo, the 1933 advance, the 1937 invasion and the position on the eve of war; and its record that Hitler and Mussolini both took note of the League's failure
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage was carried in Lesson 8
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one crisis, four audiences, four conclusions

```text
   THE EVENT: 1931, Japanese troops invade the Chinese territory of
   MANCHURIA - a valuable province with a population of 30 MILLION.
   Japan had been involved there since the 1890s and was given PORT
   ARTHUR and a privileged position. The recorded fear: China seemed
   to be growing stronger under CHIANG KAI-SHEK.
   Japan announces it has turned Manchuria into the "independent"
   state of MANCHUKUO.
   ==================================================================

   CHINA APPEALS TO THE LEAGUE
        |
   THE LEAGUE CONDEMNS JAPAN and orders her troops to be withdrawn
        |
   JAPAN REFUSES
        |
   THE LEAGUE APPOINTS A COMMISSION UNDER LORD LYTTON
        |
   LYTTON REPORTS (1932): "faults on both sides"; suggests Manchuria
   should be governed by the LEAGUE
        |
   JAPAN REJECTS THIS AND WITHDRAWS FROM THE LEAGUE, MARCH 1933
        |
   THE QUESTION OF ECONOMIC SANCTIONS, LET ALONE MILITARY ONES,
   WAS NEVER EVEN RAISED
   recorded reason: Britain and France had serious economic problems;
   reluctant to apply a trade boycott in case it led to war, which
   they were ill-equipped to win, ESPECIALLY WITHOUT AMERICAN HELP
   - and the USA had returned to a policy of STRICT ISOLATION,
   refusing to join in economic sanctions or any move that might
   lead to war
   ==================================================================

   FOUR AUDIENCES, FOUR CONCLUSIONS DRAWN:

   TOKYO      ->  defiance is survivable; the province is kept;
                  advance continues from 1933 into north-eastern
                  China; full invasion July 1937
   ROME       ->  "Both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the
                  failure of the League of Nations to curb
                  Japanese aggression"
   BERLIN     ->  the same lesson, drawn by a government that came
                  to power in the same month Japan left the League
   GENEVA     ->  "Japan had successfully defied the League, whose
                  prestige was damaged, THOUGH NOT YET FATALLY"
   ==================================================================
   THE PHRASE TO CARRY: "not yet fatally". The source grades the
   damage. Manchuria wounded the League; Abyssinia killed it. An
   answer that treats the two as equivalent has lost a distinction
   the evidence base makes explicitly.
```

*The four-audience structure is the reason this lesson exists. ⚠️ A crisis in international relations has two kinds of consequence: what it does to the parties, and what it teaches everybody else. The second is usually larger, and in this case the source says so directly - the two European revisionist leaders took note.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A member of the League's Council invades the territory of another member. The victim appeals to the machinery exactly as the machinery intends. The machinery condemns, investigates, reports and proposes an internationalised solution. The aggressor declines all of it and leaves. And at no point does anybody propose the economic measure that the Covenant explicitly provides for.

That last sentence is the crisis. The failure was not that sanctions were tried and failed; it is that they were never put on the table. ⚠️ The distinction is worth marks because it changes the diagnosis from institutional weakness to political choice - which is the argument of Lesson 5 arriving, for the first time, in a real case.

### 2. What Japan wanted, and why the province

✅ **Fact:** the Japanese feared that China, which seemed to be growing stronger under the rule of Chiang Kai-shek, might become strong enough to threaten Japanese interests.

✅ **Fact:** Japan had been involved in the province since the 1890s and was given Port Arthur and a privileged position there. ✅ Manchuria was a valuable province with a population of thirty million.

✅ **Fact:** it was partly for economic reasons that Japanese troops invaded in 1931.

✅ **Fact:** the Japanese announced that they had turned Manchuria into the independent state of Manchukuo.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pre-1931 involvement matters for an answer because it disposes of the idea that Manchuria was a sudden departure. Japanese presence in the province was four decades old and had a treaty basis; what changed in 1931 was the conversion of a privileged position into direct control, under a nominal independence that nobody was obliged to believe. ⚠️ The device of a client state with a declared independence is itself worth noting, because it is the first appearance in this period of a technique - the manufactured legal form - that recurs in Slovakia in March 1939.

### 3. The League's response, step by step

✅ **Fact:** China appealed to the League, which condemned Japan and ordered her troops to be withdrawn. ✅ When Japan refused, the League appointed a commission under Lord Lytton, which decided in 1932 that there were faults on both sides and suggested that Manchuria should be governed by the League. ✅ Japan rejected this and withdrew from the League in March 1933.

✅ **Fact:** the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, because Britain and France had serious economic problems. ✅ They were reluctant to apply a trade boycott of Japan in case it led to war, which they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help.

✅ **Fact:** the verdict recorded: Japan had successfully defied the League, whose prestige was damaged, though not yet fatally.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Lytton finding of "faults on both sides" is the most instructive detail and it is almost always omitted. A commission appointed to investigate an invasion produced a verdict of shared responsibility and a proposal to remove the territory from both parties. ⚠️ That is not a neutral procedural outcome; it is the characteristic product of an institution that is trying to find a formula its most powerful members will accept rather than a finding the evidence supports. ⚠️ And the practical consequence is severe: a finding of faults on both sides makes sanctions rhetorically impossible even before they become politically impossible, because sanctions require an identified aggressor. A candidate who notices that the report disarmed the remedy is reading the case properly.

### 4. What followed, and how far it went

✅ **Fact:** in 1933 the Japanese began to advance from Manchuria into the rest of north-eastern China, and by 1935 large areas north of Beijing had fallen under Japanese political and commercial control. ✅ This was made easier by the civil war between the Kuomintang government and the communists led by Mao Zedong.

✅ **Fact:** after signing the Anti-Comintern Pact with Germany in 1936, the Japanese army seized the excuse provided by an incident between Chinese and Japanese troops in Peking to begin an invasion of other parts of China in July 1937. ✅ Although the prime minister, Prince Konoye, was against such massive intervention, he had to give way to the wishes of General Sugiyama, the war minister.

✅ **Fact:** by the autumn of 1938 the Japanese had captured Shanghai, Nanking - Chiang Kai-shek's capital - and Hankow, committing terrible atrocities against Chinese civilians. ✅ Complete victory eluded them: Chiang had reached an understanding with his communist enemies that they would both co-operate against the invaders, a new capital was established well inland at Chungking, and spirited Chinese resistance was mounted with help from the Russians. ✅ Japanese troops landed in the south and quickly captured Canton, but Chiang still refused to surrender or accept Japanese terms.

✅ **Fact:** meanwhile the League had again condemned Japanese aggression but was powerless to act, since Japan was no longer a member and refused to attend a conference to discuss the situation in China. ✅ Britain and France were too busy coping with Hitler to take much notice of China; the Russians did not want full-scale war with Japan; and the United States, the only power capable of effectively resisting Japan, was still bent on isolation. ✅ Thus on the eve of the Second World War the Japanese controlled most of eastern China, though outside the cities their hold was shaky, while Chiang held out in the centre and west.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1937 escalation belongs in an interwar answer for one reason above all others, and it is a boundary reason. ✅ The resulting Sino-Japanese War eventually became part of the Second World War. ⚠️ That means the interwar collapse of collective security is not a European story with a Far Eastern appendix; it is a global story with two theatres, and one of the theatres was already at war two years before the European one began. ⚠️ A script that mentions Manchuria only as a League failure and never returns to Asia has misrepresented the period's geography.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, on attribution:** the atrocities are recorded and must not be softened, and they must also not be attributed collectively. The recorded actors are the Japanese army and its commanders; the prime minister is recorded as having opposed the escalation and having been overruled by the war minister. ⚠️ Naming the institutions and the individuals is both more accurate and more informative than a national formulation, and it also demonstrates the civil-military relationship that the fascism owner holds in detail.

### 5. Comparison: three ways of describing what Manchuria proved

| Description | What it claims | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ "The League was exposed as powerless" | The institution lacked capacity | ⚠️ Partly true and incomplete: the capacity that existed - economic sanctions - was never invoked |
| ⚠️ "Britain and France were unwilling" | The members made a political choice | ✅ Directly supported: the recorded reason is their economic problems and their reluctance to risk war without American help |
| ⚠️ "The absence of the United States was decisive" | The missing member determined the outcome | ✅ Supported but requires care: the recorded phrase is "especially without American help", and the United States is separately recorded as returning to strict isolation at exactly this moment |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strongest single formulation combines the second and third: the decision not to raise sanctions was taken by Britain and France, and it was taken in the knowledge that the one power capable of making a Far Eastern boycott effective had already announced it would not participate. ⚠️ That is both a choice and a constraint, and presenting it as both is what distinguishes an analytical answer from a partisan one. ⚠️ Note also that in 1931-33 the American refusal was still a matter of policy: the statutory neutrality regime set out in Lesson 7 begins in August 1935, so over Manchuria the United States could have joined sanctions and declined to, whereas after 1935 its own law would have forbidden much of what collective security required.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Was a trade boycott of Japan in 1931-32, at the trough of the Depression and with the United States refusing to join, ever a realistic option? Is the criticism of Britain and France not simply unfair?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the difficulty in full: the recorded reasoning is economic weakness plus the risk of a war they were ill-equipped to win without American help, and every element of that is supported. Second, distinguish the decision from the action. The criticism that survives is not that sanctions were not imposed but that they were *never even raised* - a phrase the source uses deliberately. ⚠️ Raising a measure and declining it after debate produces a record, a division and a cost to the aggressor's reputation; never raising it produces none of those, and it signals to every other government that the Covenant's central provision is inoperative. Third, note the recorded confirmation of the signal: both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the failure of the League to curb Japanese aggression. ⚠️ The disciplined verdict is that the constraint on *action* was genuine, the constraint on *procedure* was not, and the cost was paid in the information transmitted to Rome and Berlin.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Manchurian crisis for the League of Nations.
> ⚠️ "Collective security failed in Asia before it failed in Europe." Examine.
> ⚠️ Why did the League fail to restrain the aggressor states of the 1930s?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Manchuria's place in a League answer is as the *first* failure, and the word that must appear is "first". ⚠️ Give the sequence in four clauses - invasion, appeal, Lytton, withdrawal - then the never-raised sanctions with their recorded reason, then the demonstration effect on Rome and Berlin, then the graded verdict that the prestige was damaged but not yet fatally. ⚠️ Six sentences, and it leaves room for Abyssinia, which is where the fatal damage is recorded.

### Traps

- Saying the League imposed sanctions on Japan. ✅ The question was never even raised.
- Saying the Lytton Commission condemned Japan. ✅ It decided there were faults on both sides and proposed League government of Manchuria.
- Dating Japan's withdrawal from the League to 1931 or 1932. ✅ March 1933, after rejecting the Lytton proposal.
- Treating Manchuria as fatal to the League. ✅ The recorded grading is "damaged, though not yet fatally"; Abyssinia is recorded as the most serious blow.
- Forgetting the pre-1931 Japanese position - involvement since the 1890s, Port Arthur and a privileged position - which makes the 1931 action an escalation rather than an irruption.
- Attributing the 1937 atrocities collectively. ✅ The recorded actors are the army and named ministers, with the prime minister recorded as opposed and overruled.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1931: Japanese troops invade Manchuria, a valuable province with a population of thirty million; Japan had been involved since the 1890s and held Port Arthur and a privileged position.
2. ✅ Recorded fear: a China growing stronger under Chiang Kai-shek; and partly economic reasons.
3. ✅ Japan announced the "independent" state of Manchukuo.
4. ✅ China appealed; the League condemned Japan and ordered withdrawal; Japan refused.
5. ✅ The Lytton Commission reported in 1932: faults on both sides; Manchuria to be governed by the League.
6. ✅ Japan rejected this and withdrew from the League in March 1933.
7. ✅ Economic sanctions, let alone military ones, were never even raised: Britain and France had serious economic problems and feared a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help; the United States had returned to strict isolation.
8. ✅ Verdict: Japan had successfully defied the League, whose prestige was damaged, though not yet fatally.
9. ✅ 1933 onward: advance into north-eastern China; by 1935 large areas north of Beijing under Japanese control; July 1937 full invasion after the Peking incident; Shanghai, Nanking and Hankow taken by autumn 1938; Chungking the new capital; the League condemned again and was powerless.
10. ✅ Both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the League's failure to curb Japanese aggression; the resulting Sino-Japanese War eventually became part of the Second World War.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the phrase "never even raised" is the analytically decisive detail of the Manchurian crisis, and state what it establishes that the Abyssinian crisis cannot.

**Model answer:** It is decisive because it separates incapacity from choice at the one point where the two are easiest to confuse. The League had no army, and Article 16's expectation that members would supply troops had been neutralised by the resolution of 1923 leaving each member to decide for itself; so military enforcement in the Far East in 1931 was, on any realistic view, unavailable. Economic sanctions were different. They were one of the two instruments the Covenant expressly named, they required no force, and they had been used in no great-power case up to that point. The record is that the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, and the recorded reason is that Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to apply a trade boycott in case it led to a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help - the United States having returned to a policy of strict isolation and refused to join in economic sanctions. A measure that is proposed, debated and rejected produces a record and imposes a reputational cost on the aggressor; a measure that is never tabled produces neither, and it communicates that the Covenant's central provision is inoperative. What this establishes, and what Abyssinia cannot, is the condition of the system before it had been publicly tested. In 1935-36 sanctions were introduced, so the Abyssinian evidence shows that sanctions applied without oil, coal and steel are ineffective - a finding about instruments. In 1931-33 the evidence shows something prior: that the members would not initiate the procedure at all. That is a finding about will, and it is why the source records that both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the League's failure over Manchuria, and why it grades the prestige damage as serious but not yet fatal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Manchuria and Abyssinia as two examples of the same thing. The evidence base distinguishes them explicitly: Manchuria damaged the League's prestige though not yet fatally, while the Italian invasion of Abyssinia was the most serious blow to its prestige and credibility. The two crises also test different things - the first tests whether the members will begin, the second tests whether the instrument works when they do. An answer that uses both, and says what each proves, is worth substantially more than one that uses them as a pair of illustrations.

*Manchuria has taught the lesson and Rome has learned it. The next lesson is the crisis the evidence base itself calls the most serious blow to League prestige - and it is also the crisis in which the anti-German front that might have restrained Hitler was assembled and destroyed within ten weeks.*

### Visual: ten weeks in 1935 - a front assembled and dismantled

```text
                     |

                     |

   3. Hitler was encouraged to break Versailles - conscription

   It cannot have encouraged CONSCRIPTION, announced in MARCH 1935,
```
