*The ten-week arithmetic is the argument. ⚠️ A candidate who narrates Abyssinia as a colonial war and a failed sanctions regime has described the event. A candidate who shows that the same ten weeks assembled and destroyed the only available anti-German combination has explained the year. ⚠️ And the rider to the third result - two Versailles breaches never brought before the Council at all - is the moment the institutional story ends.*

### 1. Mussolini before 1934: the restraint that is usually forgotten

✅ **Fact:** in the early days of his regime Italian foreign policy seemed rather confused. Mussolini knew what he wanted - to make Italy great, respected and feared - but he was not sure how to achieve this, apart from agitating for a revision of the 1919 peace settlement in Italy's favour. ✅ At first an adventurous foreign policy seemed his best line of action, hence the Corfu Incident and the occupation of Fiume in 1923; after these early successes he became more cautious, perhaps alarmed by Italy's isolation at the time of Corfu.

✅ **Fact:** between 1923 and 1934 his policy was determined by rivalry with the French in the Mediterranean and the Balkans, and by the Italian fear that the weak state of Austria might fall too much under the influence of Germany - Mussolini being worried about a possible German threat via the Brenner Pass. ✅ He attended the Locarno Conference in 1925 but was disappointed that the agreements did not guarantee the Italian frontier with Austria. ✅ He cultivated Greece, Hungary and especially Albania, which was virtually controlled by Italy; he supported Britain over Mosul and received a small part of Somaliland in return; and Italy became the first state after Britain to recognise the USSR, signing a non-aggression pact with it in September 1933.

✅ **Fact, and this is the key one:** he tried to bolster up Austria against the threat from Nazi Germany by supporting the anti-Nazi government of Chancellor Dollfuss and by signing trade agreements with Austria and Hungary. ✅ When Dollfuss was murdered by the Austrian Nazis in July 1934, Mussolini sent three Italian divisions to the frontier in case the Germans invaded Austria; the Nazis immediately called off their attempt to seize power in Austria. ✅ This decisive anti-German stand improved relations between Italy and France.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** July 1934 is the fact that makes the whole Abyssinian argument work, and it is regularly omitted. In that month Italy was the state that physically stopped a German move on Austria, and it did so alone and successfully. ⚠️ Four years later the same move succeeded without resistance, and the reason is recorded: Mussolini had by then dropped his objections to a German takeover of Austria. ⚠️ Abyssinia is therefore not only a League crisis; it is the transaction by which the one power willing to garrison the Brenner changed sides. A script that states that is answering a causation question rather than describing a colonial war.

### 2. The Stresa Front, and the silence inside it

✅ **Fact:** when Hitler announced the reintroduction of conscription in March 1935, Mussolini joined the British and French in condemning the German action and guaranteeing Austria - the Stresa Front, April 1935, at Stresa on Lake Maggiore in northern Italy. ✅ This breach of Versailles had caused Britain, France and Italy to draw together briefly in suspicion of Germany, and soon afterwards, in May, the French signed a treaty of mutual assistance with the USSR.

✅ **Fact:** both British and French carefully avoided mentioning the Abyssinian crisis, which was already brewing. ✅ Mussolini took this to mean that they would turn a blind eye to an Italian attack on Abyssinia, regarding it as a bit of old-fashioned colonial expansion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a case of a diplomatic signal transmitted by omission, and it should be named as such. ⚠️ Nothing was promised and nothing was conceded; a subject was not raised, and the inference drawn from the silence was that the subject would not be pursued. ⚠️ Whether the inference was correct is beside the point analytically: it was drawn, and it shaped a decision. The same mechanism - a commitment gap read as permission - has already appeared at Locarno and will appear again at Munich, and identifying it as a recurring mechanism rather than as three separate misunderstandings is what a strong answer does.

### 3. The Anglo-German Naval Agreement, June 1935

✅ **Fact:** shrewdly realising how frail the Stresa Front was, Hitler detached Britain by offering to limit the German navy to 35 per cent of the strength of the British navy. ✅ Britain eagerly accepted, signing the Anglo-German Naval Agreement in June 1935. ✅ British thinking seems to have been that since the Germans were already breaking Versailles by building a fleet, it would be as well to have it limited. ✅ The agreement allowed the Germans to build submarines - another breach of Versailles.

✅ **Fact:** without consulting her two allies, Britain had condoned German rearmament, which went ahead with gathering momentum. ✅ This astonishing move by Britain disgusted France and Italy and destroyed any trust which had existed between the three of them. ✅ It broke the Stresa Front, gravely shook French confidence in Britain, and encouraged Laval to look for understandings with Mussolini and Hitler. ✅ It convinced Mussolini of British cynicism and self-interest.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the British reasoning deserves to be stated fairly before it is criticised, because it is not absurd: an unlimited illegal fleet is worse than a limited illegal fleet, and a ratio agreed is better than a race. ⚠️ The objection is not to the logic but to the unit of analysis. Britain optimised a bilateral naval relationship and in doing so destroyed a trilateral political one, ten weeks after helping to create it and two months after its partners had joined it at British and French invitation. ⚠️ That is the single clearest instance in the topic of a government treating a security problem as a technical problem, and it is the best available evidence for the claim that the failure of the 1930s was a failure of co-ordination rather than of resolve alone.

### 4. Abyssinia: motives, course and sanctions

✅ **Fact:** the Italian invasion of Abyssinia, or Ethiopia, in October 1935 was the great turning point in Mussolini's career. ✅ Italian involvement in the country - the only remaining independent state left in Africa - went back to 1896, when an Italian attempt to colonise it had ended in defeat at Adowa.

✅ **Fact:** the recorded motives are three:

1. ✅ Italy's existing colonies in East Africa, Eritrea and Somaliland, were not very rewarding, and attempts through a treaty of "friendship" signed in 1928 to reduce Abyssinia to a position equivalent to that of Albania had failed - the Emperor of Abyssinia, Haile Selassie, having done all he could to avoid falling under Italian economic domination.
2. ✅ Italy was suffering from the depression, and a victorious war would divert attention from internal troubles and provide a new market for Italian exports.
3. ✅ It would please the nationalists and colonialists, avenge the defeat of 1896 and boost Mussolini's sagging popularity.

✅ **Fact:** the Italian victory over the ill-equipped and unprepared Ethiopians was a foregone conclusion, though they made heavy weather of it.

✅ **Fact:** this was the most serious blow to the League's prestige and credibility. ✅ The League condemned Italy and introduced economic sanctions; however, these were not applied to exports of oil, coal and steel to Italy. ✅ So half-hearted were the sanctions that Italy was able to complete the conquest of Abyssinia without too much inconvenience by May 1936. ✅ A few weeks later sanctions were abandoned, and Mussolini had successfully flouted the League.

✅ **Fact:** Britain and France must share the blame for the League's failure. ✅ Their motive was the desire not to antagonise Mussolini too much, so as to keep him as an ally against the real danger - Germany.

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The page of the core account that sets out the detailed consequences of the invasion, including the Anglo-French proposal associated with the names of Hoare and Laval, is badly damaged in the full-text extraction held for this session. What survives legibly and is used here is limited to the following: that a plan was contemplated to hand over a large sector of Abyssinia to Italy, amounting to more than the Italians had captured at that point; that public opinion in Britain was so outraged that the idea was dropped; and that the reasons for Anglo-French caution were that Britain and France were militarily and economically unprepared for war and were anxious to avoid military action. ✅ The existence and failure of the Hoare-Laval Pact are independently confirmed elsewhere in the same account, where it is recorded that Laval pinned his main hopes on friendship with Mussolini and that these were dashed by the failure of the Hoare-Laval Pact. ⚠️ **No further detail of that proposal is supplied in this session, and no figure, date or territorial description beyond the above is given, because the page that would supply them is not legible in the copy held here.**

### 5. The three recorded results, the chronology error inside the third, and the sentence that ends the institutional story

✅ **Fact:** the recorded results were disastrous, and the source lists **three**.

| # | Recorded result | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Mussolini was annoyed by the sanctions anyway and began to draw closer to Hitler | ⚠️ The Anglo-French policy achieved the exact reverse of its stated purpose: sanctions were limited in order to keep Mussolini as an ally against Germany, and the limited sanctions pushed him towards Germany |
| 2 | ✅ Small states lost all faith in the League | ⚠️ These were the members for whom collective security was the only available security, and their defection is why the League could not recover |
| 3 | ✅ Hitler was encouraged to break the Versailles Treaty by introducing conscription in March 1935 and sending troops into the demilitarised Rhineland in March 1936 - ✅ **and neither matter was raised at the League Council**, mainly because France and Britain were afraid Hitler would reject any decision that went against Germany, and were reluctant to be forced into military action | ⚠️ The demonstration effect, plus the rider that two open treaty breaches were not placed on the agenda because the members feared the consequences of winning the vote |

⚠️ **A chronology error inside the third result, disclosed rather than repeated.** The Italian invasion began in **October 1935**; the reintroduction of German conscription was announced in **March 1935**. An event of October cannot have encouraged a decision taken seven months earlier, so the source's inclusion of conscription in a list of Abyssinian consequences is a chronological slip, and this session does not reproduce it as causation. ⚠️ **What the evidence does support**, and supports strongly, is the forward half of the same claim: ✅ the source records separately that Hitler took advantage of the general preoccupation with Abyssinia to send troops into the Rhineland in March 1936, and ✅ that Mussolini, in return for the Anglo-French handling of the affair, dropped his objections to a German takeover of Austria. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore that the Abyssinian crisis and the Anglo-French response to it encouraged the **later** breaches - the Rhineland above all - while the March 1935 conscription announcement belongs to the *preceding* sequence that produced the Stresa Front in April 1935 and was then undercut by the Anglo-German Naval Agreement in June.

✅ **Fact:** after 1935 the League was never taken seriously again.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the rider to the third result deserves to be stated as the formal end of the League as a security institution, and it can be dated. After March 1936 the Covenant's procedures were not invoked in any European case; the Rhineland was handled by protest, Spain by a non-intervention arrangement outside the League, Austria by protest, and Czechoslovakia by a four-power conference from which the League was absent and to which two interested states were not invited. ⚠️ Saying that the institution ceased to be used, rather than that it failed, is both more accurate and more striking.

### 5A. The trajectory completed: Albania, April 1939, and the Pact of Steel, May 1939

⚠️ Abyssinia begins Italy's realignment and two acts of 1939 complete it. They belong here because they are the end point of the foreign policy this lesson has traced, and because they date the moment at which Italy's commitment stopped being a matter of understanding and became a treaty obligation.

✅ **Fact:** Mussolini's popularity revived temporarily with his part in the Munich agreement of September 1938, which seemed to have secured peace; but he failed to draw the right conclusion from his people's relief - that most of them did not want another war - and committed a further act of aggression.

✅ **Fact:** in April 1939 Italian troops suddenly occupied Albania, meeting very little resistance. ✅ The source's own assessment is that this was a pointless operation, since Albania was already under Italian economic control, and that Mussolini wanted a triumph to imitate Hitler's recent occupation of Czechoslovakia.

✅ **Fact:** carried away by his successes, Mussolini then signed a full alliance with Germany, the Pact of Steel, in May 1939, in which Italy promised full military support if war came. ✅ The recorded judgement is that he was committing Italy to deeper and deeper involvement with Germany, which in the end would ruin him.

⚠️ **Qualification, and it matters.** "Pointless" is the source's characterisation of Albania and is offered as a judgement about Italian interest, not as a statement that the operation had no effect: Albania had already been recorded as virtually controlled by Italy since the 1920s, giving Italy a strong position around the Adriatic, so the April 1939 occupation converted an existing dependency into direct possession rather than creating a new one. ⚠️ Its significance for this topic is therefore demonstrative rather than strategic - it is the clearest evidence that Italian policy had become imitative of German policy, timed within weeks of the occupation of Prague. ⚠️ The Pact of Steel is the opposite case: its significance is precisely strategic and legal, because a promise of full military support if war came removed the ambiguity that had allowed Italy to act as a restraining power in July 1934. ⚠️ Neither event is a cause of the September crisis, and neither should be presented as one; they are the terminal points of a realignment whose hinge was the Abyssinian sanctions of 1935-36.

### 6. Comparison: Manchuria and Abyssinia, scored against each other

| Dimension | Manchuria, 1931-33 | Abyssinia, 1935-36 |
|---|---|---|
| Aggressor's status | ✅ Permanent Council member | ✅ Permanent Council member and a Locarno guarantor |
| League procedure used | ✅ Condemnation, commission, report, proposal | ✅ Condemnation and economic sanctions |
| Sanctions | ✅ Never even raised | ✅ Introduced, but excluding oil, coal and steel; abandoned a few weeks after the conquest |
| Outcome for the aggressor | ✅ Province retained; withdrawal from the League, March 1933 | ✅ Conquest completed by May 1936 without too much inconvenience |
| Recorded damage to the League | ✅ Prestige damaged, though not yet fatally | ✅ The most serious blow to its prestige and credibility; never taken seriously again after 1935 |
| Effect on the anti-German coalition | ⚠️ Indirect: demonstrated impunity | ✅ Direct and severe: the Stresa Front destroyed, Italy realigned, Austria's guarantor withdrawn |
| What it proves | ⚠️ That the members would not initiate | ⚠️ That the instrument, when used at partial strength, is worse than useless - it antagonises without restraining |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the transferable finding and it generalises beyond this topic. Partial sanctions imposed against a determined aggressor combine the costs of confrontation with the benefits of neither confrontation nor conciliation. ⚠️ The recorded outcome - Mussolini annoyed by the sanctions anyway and drawing closer to Hitler, while completing the conquest without too much inconvenience - is the empirical demonstration, and it is one of the few places in this topic where a general proposition can be stated with a specific case attached.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Britain and France faced a genuine dilemma: alienate Mussolini and lose him against Germany, or restrain him and keep him. They chose to keep him. Was that not the correct strategic judgement, badly executed?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the dilemma as recorded: the motive was the desire not to antagonise Mussolini too much, so as to keep him as an ally against the real danger, Germany - which is a serious strategic argument, and one supported by the evidence of July 1934, when Italian divisions at the Austrian frontier had stopped a German move. Second, identify the execution failure precisely: the policy chosen was neither of the two coherent options. Full sanctions would have restrained or at least punished; no sanctions would have preserved the relationship; partial sanctions annoyed him without stopping him, and the recorded outcome is that he was annoyed anyway and drew closer to Hitler. Third, note that the same governments had already destroyed the relationship they were trying to preserve. ⚠️ The Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935 was signed without consulting Italy, disgusted her, and convinced Mussolini of British cynicism - four months before the invasion. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is that the strategic judgement was sound, the instrument chosen was the worst available, and the relationship being protected had already been damaged by the protector.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Abyssinia, not Manchuria, destroyed the League of Nations." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the consequences of the Abyssinian crisis for European international relations.
> ⚠️ Why did Britain and France fail to restrain the revisionist powers between 1935 and 1938?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The highest-value structure for any of these is the ten-week arithmetic: conscription in March, Stresa in April with Abyssinia unmentioned, the naval agreement in June, the invasion in October. ⚠️ Then the sanctions with their three excluded commodities, then the three recorded results, then the verdict that the League was never taken seriously again. ⚠️ Do not narrate the campaign; the source itself gives it one sentence. ⚠️ Never write that Abyssinia encouraged the March 1935 conscription announcement; the invasion is seven months later than the announcement.

### Traps

- Saying the League imposed no sanctions on Italy. ✅ It did; they excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned a few weeks after the conquest.
- Dating the completion of the conquest to 1935. ✅ May 1936.
- Omitting the Stresa Front, or dating it to 1934. ✅ April 1935, after the March conscription announcement.
- Treating the Anglo-German Naval Agreement as a separate episode. ✅ It is recorded as the thing that broke the Front, ten weeks after the Front was formed.
- Forgetting July 1934, when Mussolini sent three divisions to the Austrian frontier and the Austrian Nazi attempt collapsed.
- Supplying detail about the Hoare-Laval proposal beyond what is legible in the copy held here. The page is damaged and the gap is declared.
- Saying Hitler remilitarised the Rhineland "because of" Abyssinia without the recorded formulation: he took advantage of the general preoccupation with Abyssinia.
- Repeating the source's inclusion of March 1935 conscription among the Abyssinian results. The invasion is October 1935; use the third result forwards, to the Rhineland, never backwards.
- Calling the occupation of Albania in April 1939 a strategic gain. ✅ Albania was already under Italian economic control; the recorded motive is imitation of Hitler's occupation of Czechoslovakia.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Before 1934 Mussolini restrained Germany over Austria: support for Dollfuss, and three Italian divisions at the frontier in July 1934, after which the Austrian Nazi attempt was called off.
2. ✅ March 1935: German conscription announced; peacetime army to be 36 divisions, about 600 000 men.
3. ✅ April 1935: the Stresa Front - Britain, France and Italy condemn the breach and guarantee Austria; May 1935, France signs a mutual assistance treaty with the USSR.
4. ✅ Both British and French avoided mentioning the brewing Abyssinian crisis; Mussolini inferred a blind eye.
5. ✅ June 1935: the Anglo-German Naval Agreement - 35 per cent of British naval strength, including submarines; signed without consulting France and Italy; destroyed trust between the three.
6. ✅ October 1935: Italy invades Abyssinia, the only remaining independent state in Africa; Italian involvement dated to 1896 and the defeat at Adowa.
7. ✅ Motives: unrewarding colonies and the failure of the 1928 "friendship" treaty against Haile Selassie's resistance; the depression and the search for diversion and markets; nationalist satisfaction, revenge for 1896 and sagging popularity.
8. ✅ Sanctions introduced but not applied to oil, coal and steel; conquest completed May 1936 without too much inconvenience; sanctions abandoned a few weeks later.
9. ✅ **Three** recorded results: Mussolini drew closer to Hitler; small states lost all faith; Hitler was encouraged to breach Versailles, with the rider that neither the conscription announcement nor the Rhineland was raised at the Council. ⚠️ Conscription (March 1935) predates the invasion (October 1935) and is a chronology error in the source; carry result 3 forwards to the Rhineland only.
10. ✅ After 1935 the League was never taken seriously again; Mussolini dropped his objections to a German takeover of Austria; Hitler used the preoccupation with Abyssinia to move into the Rhineland.
11. ✅ April 1939: Italian troops occupy Albania, meeting very little resistance - recorded as pointless, since Albania was already under Italian economic control, and motivated by a wish to imitate Hitler's occupation of Czechoslovakia.
12. ✅ May 1939: the Pact of Steel, a full alliance with Germany in which Italy promised full military support if war came - committing Italy to deeper and deeper involvement with Germany.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935 is more damaging to the interwar order than the Abyssinian invasion of October 1935, and state the counter-argument.

**Model answer:** The Abyssinian invasion destroyed the League's credibility; the Naval Agreement destroyed the alternative to the League. In April 1935 Britain, France and Italy met at Stresa, condemned the German reintroduction of conscription and guaranteed Austria, producing the only three-power combination in the period that was both willing and positioned to restrain Germany - and Italy's willingness was not theoretical, since in July 1934 Mussolini had moved three divisions to the Austrian frontier after the murder of Dollfuss and the Austrian Nazi attempt had immediately collapsed. Ten weeks after Stresa, Britain signed a bilateral agreement permitting Germany to build to 35 per cent of British naval strength, including submarines, without consulting France or Italy. The recorded effects are that this astonishing move disgusted France and Italy and destroyed any trust existing between the three, broke the Stresa Front, gravely shook French confidence in Britain, encouraged Laval to look for understandings with Mussolini and Hitler, and convinced Mussolini of British cynicism and self-interest. It also condoned German rearmament, which went ahead with gathering momentum. The Abyssinian crisis, by contrast, destroyed an institution whose central provision had already been shown to be inoperative over Manchuria, when sanctions were never even raised. The counter-argument is that the Naval Agreement's damage was contingent on Abyssinia and not independent of it: without the invasion, French and Italian irritation at a naval ratio need not have produced a realignment, whereas the sanctions - limited so as not to antagonise Mussolini, yet annoying him anyway - are what actually moved him towards Hitler and led him to drop his objections to a German takeover of Austria. The defensible verdict is that the Naval Agreement made the Stresa Front unreliable and Abyssinia made it dead, and that a candidate should say which effect the question is about.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Italy's realignment as inevitable because Mussolini was a fascist. The evidence base records the opposite trajectory before 1934 - disappointment at Locarno's failure to guarantee the Austrian frontier, worry about a German threat via the Brenner Pass, support for Dollfuss, a non-aggression pact with the USSR in September 1933, and the decisive anti-German stand of July 1934 that improved relations with France. Ideological affinity did not determine alignment; a sequence of specific decisions taken in London, Paris and Rome between April 1935 and May 1936 did.

*The League is finished as a security institution and Italy has changed sides. The next lesson steps back from the crises to the state that was exploiting them, and sets out what was taken, in what order, with what excuse and against what resistance - because the order of the steps is itself an argument about intention.*

## Lesson 14 - German revisionism, 1933-38: the sequence, the technique and the resistance that was not offered

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded aims and the disagreement about what lay beyond them; the nine steps from the League exit to the Anschluss; the Polish pact of January 1934 and its effect on the French alliance system; the Saar plebiscite; conscription and the recorded figures; the Rhineland as a double breach and the recorded withdrawal order; the Axis and the Anti-Comintern Pact; the rearmament totals by the end of 1938; why the sequence is evidence in the intention debate

*Read the resistance column downward. ⚠️ It contains exactly one entry - Italy, July 1934 - and the recorded consequence of that entry is that the operation was abandoned and Hitler accepted that Germany was not yet strong enough to force the issue. ⚠️ That single row is the entire evidentiary basis of the argument that earlier firmness would have worked, and it is also the single strongest thing an appeasement answer can cite. It should never be omitted.*

### 1. The recorded aims, and the disagreement about what lay beyond them

✅ **Fact:** Hitler aimed to make Germany into a great power again, and hoped to achieve this by destroying the hated Versailles settlement; building up the army; recovering lost territory such as the Saar and the Polish Corridor; and bringing all German-speaking peoples inside the Reich - which would involve annexing Austria and taking territory from Czechoslovakia and Poland, both of which had large German minorities as a result of the peace settlement.

✅ **Fact:** there is some disagreement about what, if anything, he intended beyond these aims. ✅ Some historians believe that annexing Austria and parts of Czechoslovakia and Poland was only a beginning, and that he planned to follow it up by seizing the rest of Czechoslovakia and Poland and then conquering and occupying Russia as far east as the Ural Mountains, to provide Lebensraum - living space - on the claim that Germany's population was much too large for the area into which it was constrained. ✅ He had made clear his hatred of what he called "Jewish Bolshevism", which suggests that war with the USSR was unavoidable at some point in order to destroy communism; the next stage would be colonies in Africa and naval bases in and around the Atlantic.

✅ **Fact:** other historians disagree about these further aims. ✅ A. J. P. Taylor claimed in 1961 that Hitler never had any detailed plans worked out for acquiring Lebensraum and never intended a major war; at most he was prepared only for a limited war against Poland, and "he got as far as he did because others did not know what to do with him". ✅ Martin Broszat, writing in 1983, also believed that Hitler's writings and statements about Lebensraum did not amount to an actual programme followed step by step, and that they were more likely a propaganda exercise designed to attract support and unite the Nazi party. ✅ More recently Mark Mazower, in *Hitler's Empire: Nazi Rule in Occupied Europe* (2008), suggests that there is very little evidence that Hitler had given much serious thought to the problems of creating and organising a Nazi empire in Europe.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of this debate should be held precisely, because it recurs in Lesson 21. The *first* set of aims is not in dispute - it is recorded without qualification and the steps that achieved it are documented. What is disputed is whether there was a *second* set and whether it was programmatic. ⚠️ That means a candidate can write with complete confidence about everything up to the end of 1938 and must write with explicit qualification about everything attributed to intentions beyond it. Separating the two is the single most important discipline in this part of the topic.

### 2. The sequence, with the details that carry marks

✅ **Fact, step 1:** given that Germany was still militarily weak in 1933, Hitler had to move cautiously at first. He withdrew Germany from the World Disarmament Conference and from the League in October 1933, on the grounds that France would not agree to Germany having equality of armaments, while insisting that Germany was willing to disarm if other states would do the same and that he wanted only peace.

✅ **Fact, step 2:** he signed a ten-year non-aggression pact with the Poles in January 1934, who were showing alarm in case the Germans tried to take back the Polish Corridor. ✅ This was something of a triumph: Britain took it as further evidence of his peaceful intentions; it ruined France's Little Entente, which depended very much on Poland; and it guaranteed Polish neutrality whenever Germany decided to move against Austria and Czechoslovakia. ✅ On the other hand it improved relations between France and Russia, who were both worried by the apparent threat from Nazi Germany.

✅ **Fact, step 3:** in July 1934 Hitler suffered a setback to his ambitions of an Anschluss. The Austrian Nazis, encouraged by Hitler, staged a revolt and murdered Chancellor Engelbert Dollfuss, who had been supported by Mussolini. ✅ When Mussolini moved Italian troops to the Austrian frontier and warned the Germans off, the revolt collapsed. ✅ Hitler, taken aback, had to accept that Germany was not yet strong enough to force the issue, and he denied responsibility for the actions of the Austrian Nazis. ✅ The source's separate account adds that what is certain is that the local Nazis took the initiative, and that although Hitler probably knew something about their plans, he was not himself prepared to help them.

✅ **Fact, step 4:** the Saar was returned to Germany in January 1935 after a plebiscite resulting in a 90 per cent vote in favour. ✅ Though the vote had been provided for in the peace settlement, Nazi propaganda made the most of the success, and Hitler announced that now all causes of grievance between France and Germany had been removed.

✅ **Fact, step 5:** the first successful breach of Versailles came in March 1935 with the reintroduction of conscription. ✅ The excuse was that Britain had just announced air force increases and France had extended conscription from twelve to eighteen months, their justification being German rearmament. ✅ Hitler told his startled generals and the rest of the world that he would build up his peacetime army to 36 divisions - about 600 000 men - six times more than was allowed by the peace treaty. ✅ Although the Stresa Front condemned the violation, no action was taken; the League was helpless, and the Front collapsed anyway as a result of his next success.

✅ **Fact, step 6:** the Anglo-German Naval Agreement of June 1935, treated in full in Lesson 13.

✅ **Fact, step 7:** encouraged by his successes, Hitler took the calculated risk of sending troops into the demilitarised zone of the Rhineland in March 1936, a breach of both Versailles and Locarno. ✅ Though the troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition, no resistance was offered except the usual protests. ✅ At the same time, well aware of the mood of pacifism among his opponents, he soothed them by offering a peace treaty to last for twenty-five years.

✅ **Fact, step 8:** later in 1936 he consolidated Germany's position by reaching an understanding with Mussolini - the Rome-Berlin Axis - and by signing the Anti-Comintern Pact with Japan, which Italy also joined in 1937. ✅ Mussolini said the Axis was a line drawn between Rome and Berlin, around which all European states that desire peace can revolve. ✅ The three signatories of the Anti-Comintern Pact pledged themselves to stand side by side against Bolshevism, the Comintern having been set up in 1919 by Lenin with the aim of helping communist parties in other countries to work for revolution. ✅ Germans and Italians gained military experience helping Franco to victory in the Spanish Civil War, one of the most notorious exploits being the bombing of the defenceless Basque market town of Guernica by the German Condor Legion.

✅ **Fact, step 9:** the Anschluss with Austria in March 1938 was Hitler's greatest success to date. ✅ Matters came to a head when the Austrian Nazis staged huge demonstrations in Vienna, Graz and Linz which Chancellor Schuschnigg's government could not control. ✅ Realising that this could be the prelude to a German invasion, Schuschnigg announced a referendum about whether or not Austria should remain independent; Hitler decided to act before it was held, in case the vote went against union; German troops moved in and Austria became part of the Third Reich. ✅ It was a triumph: it revealed the weakness of Britain and France, who again only protested; it showed the value of the new German understanding with Italy; and it dealt a severe blow to Czechoslovakia, which could now be attacked from the south as well as from the west and north.

### 3. The rearmament totals, and what may and may not be said about them

✅ **Fact:** by the end of 1938 the army stood at 51 divisions - about 800 000 men - plus reserves; there were 21 large naval vessels, being battleships, cruisers and destroyers, with many more under construction, and 47 U-boats; and a large air force of over 5000 aircraft had been built up.

✅ **Fact:** by the end of 1938 almost every one of the first set of aims had been achieved, without war and with the approval of Britain. ✅ Only the Germans in Poland remained to be brought within the Reich, and it was when Hitler failed to achieve this by peaceful means that he took the decision to invade Poland.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** compare the March 1935 announcement with the end-1938 position and the rate becomes visible: from a treaty limit of 100 000 men, to an announced target of about 600 000 in March 1935, to about 800 000 plus reserves by the end of 1938, with a navy and an air force that the treaty prohibited entirely. ⚠️ Two conclusions follow and both are examinable. First, the rearmament was not concealed - it was announced, and the naval element was formalised by agreement with Britain. Second, and this is the point that matters for appeasement, the argument that delay bought time for British rearmament has to be weighed against the fact that the same delay was being used at a faster rate by the other side. ⚠️ The evidence base does not settle that comparison, and a candidate should say so rather than assert a winner.

### 4. The Rhineland, and the counterfactual the evidence actually supports

✅ **Fact:** the troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition.

✅ **Fact:** the French, though disturbed at the reoccupation, did not mobilise their troops; they were deeply divided and ultra-cautious, and they received no backing from the British, who were impressed by Hitler's offer of a twenty-five-year peace. ✅ Lord Londonderry, a Conservative and Secretary of State for Air from 1931 to 1935, was reported to have sent Hitler a telegram congratulating him on his success, and Lord Lothian remarked that German troops had merely entered their own "back garden".

✅ **Fact:** the blame chapter records the counterfactual as a historical argument: some historians have suggested that Britain and France should have taken a firm line before Germany became too strong, and that an Anglo-French attack on western Germany in 1936 at the time of the Rhineland occupation would have taught Hitler a lesson and might have toppled him from power.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most disciplined counterfactual available in the topic and it should be handled with the care it deserves. ⚠️ The evidence for it is not speculation: it consists of the withdrawal order, which shows the operation was designed to be reversible, and the Austrian precedent of July 1934, which shows that a credible military signal had already caused a German operation to be abandoned once. ⚠️ The evidence against it is equally real: the French were deeply divided and ultra-cautious, they had no British backing, and the claim that toppling Hitler would have followed is stated in the source as a possibility - "might have" - and not as a finding. ⚠️ The correct formulation is therefore that the Rhineland is the best-supported counterfactual in the period and still falls short of demonstration, and that a candidate who says exactly that has done something better than choosing a side.

### 5. Comparison: three readings of the sequence

| Reading | What the sequence shows | Its best evidence | Its weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Programme** | Each step was a planned stage towards a defined end | ✅ The recorded first set of aims, achieved in order and almost completely by the end of 1938 | ✅ Taylor, Broszat and Mazower each deny that the aims beyond that set were programmatic |
| ⚠️ **Opportunism** | Each step exploited a specific weakness that happened to present itself | ✅ The Abyssinian preoccupation used for the Rhineland; the Stresa Front's frailty exploited by the naval offer; Schuschnigg's referendum pre-empted | ⚠️ Cannot explain why the opportunities were taken in an order that matched the stated aims |
| ⚠️ **Programme executed opportunistically** | The objectives were settled; the timing and method were improvised against available weakness | ✅ The consistency of the aims with the outcomes, plus the recorded technique of bold action with conciliatory cover | ⚠️ Risks becoming unfalsifiable if stated loosely; must be tied to the recorded aims rather than to a general disposition |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third is the reading this session teaches, with one qualification that keeps it honest: it applies confidently only to the first set of aims, which is what the evidence base records without dispute. ⚠️ For anything beyond it - Lebensraum to the Urals, colonies, Atlantic bases - the debate in Lesson 21 governs, and the correct register is explicit uncertainty.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If each of the nine steps is individually defensible - self-determination in the Saar, equality in armaments, sovereignty in the Rhineland, union with Austria desired by most Austrians - on what basis were Britain and France supposed to resist any of them?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the individual defensibility, because it is genuine and it is recorded: the Saar vote was provided for in the peace settlement and returned a 90 per cent majority; most Austrians felt that union with Germany was the natural solution to their problems, and many in Britain saw the Anschluss as the natural union of one German group with another; the demand for equality of armaments had a basis in the Covenant promise that only Germany had honoured. Second, identify what the individual defensibility conceals: each step altered the *capacity* to resist the next. ✅ The Polish pact of January 1934 ruined France's Little Entente and guaranteed Polish neutrality for a move against Austria and Czechoslovakia; ✅ the Rhineland reoccupation removed the demilitarised zone through which France would have had to attack to honour its guarantees to Prague and Warsaw; ✅ the Anschluss meant Czechoslovakia could now be attacked from the south as well as from the west and north. ⚠️ A sequence in which every step is separately reasonable and cumulatively disarming cannot be assessed step by step, which is exactly the error appeasement made. Third, note the one recorded case in which resistance was offered and worked - Italy in July 1934 - and the recorded consequence: the revolt collapsed and Hitler accepted that Germany was not yet strong enough.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Hitler's foreign-policy successes between 1935 and 1938 resulted from his own tactical skill and his ability to exploit his opponents' weaknesses." How far do you agree?
> ⚠️ Examine the evidence for and against the view that Hitler had no clear long-term plans for war.
> ⚠️ Trace the stages by which the Versailles settlement was dismantled between 1933 and 1938.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The nine-step table is the evidence bank for all three framings, but it must never be reproduced as a list. ⚠️ Use it in three columns - step, excuse, resistance - and let the resistance column carry the argument, because a column with one entry in nine rows makes a point that no amount of narrative can.

### Traps

- Dating the German withdrawal from the League to 1934 or 1935. ✅ October 1933, a week after leaving the Disarmament Conference.
- Forgetting that the Polish pact was a ten-year non-aggression pact signed in January 1934, and that it ruined the Little Entente.
- Saying Hitler ordered the July 1934 Austrian coup. ✅ The recorded position is that the local Nazis took the initiative, that he probably knew something of their plans, that he was not prepared to help them, and that he denied responsibility.
- Giving the Saar plebiscite as anything other than a 90 per cent vote in favour, or forgetting that the vote was provided for in the peace settlement.
- Omitting the Rhineland withdrawal order, which is the evidential basis of the strongest counterfactual in the topic.
- Saying the Rhineland breached Versailles only. ✅ It breached both Versailles and Locarno.
- Dating Italy's accession to the Anti-Comintern Pact to 1936. ✅ Germany and Japan in 1936; Italy joined in 1937.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The recorded first set of aims: destroy Versailles; build the army; recover lost territory such as the Saar and the Polish Corridor; bring all German-speakers into the Reich.
2. ✅ The dispute concerns what lay beyond: Lebensraum to the Urals, then African colonies and Atlantic bases - asserted by some, denied by Taylor (1961), qualified by Broszat (1983) and doubted by Mazower (2008).
3. ✅ October 1933: out of the Disarmament Conference, and out of the League a week later.
4. ✅ January 1934: ten-year non-aggression pact with Poland; ruined the Little Entente; guaranteed Polish neutrality; improved Franco-Russian relations.
5. ✅ July 1934: Austrian Nazi revolt and the murder of Dollfuss; Mussolini's troops at the frontier; revolt collapsed; Hitler accepted Germany was not yet strong enough.
6. ✅ January 1935: Saar returned after a 90 per cent plebiscite vote; Hitler announced all Franco-German grievances removed.
7. ✅ March 1935: conscription reintroduced - 36 divisions, about 600 000 men, six times the treaty allowance; Stresa condemned, no action taken.
8. ✅ March 1936: Rhineland reoccupied, breaching Versailles and Locarno; troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition; no resistance except protests; a twenty-five-year peace treaty offered.
9. ✅ October-November 1936: Rome-Berlin Axis; Anti-Comintern Pact with Japan, joined by Italy in 1937; Guernica bombed by the Condor Legion.
10. ✅ March 1938: Anschluss, the greatest success to date; by the end of 1938 - 51 divisions, about 800 000 men plus reserves, 21 large naval vessels, 47 U-boats, over 5000 aircraft - almost every first-set aim achieved without war and with the approval of Britain.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the sequence of German revisionist steps cannot properly be assessed step by step, and identify the single row of the sequence that carries the most analytical weight.

**Model answer:** It cannot be assessed step by step because each step was individually defensible and cumulatively disarming, so that a method which evaluates each in isolation will approve them all and arrive at a position from which resistance is no longer possible. The individual defensibility is real and recorded: the Saar returned to Germany in January 1935 by a plebiscite provided for in the peace settlement and carried by 90 per cent; the demand for equality of armaments rested on a Covenant promise that only Germany had honoured; the Rhineland lay within German sovereign territory; and most Austrians regarded union with Germany as the natural solution to their problems, with many in Britain viewing the Anschluss as the natural union of one German group with another. The cumulative effect is of a different order. The ten-year non-aggression pact with Poland in January 1934 ruined France's Little Entente, which depended very much on Poland, and guaranteed Polish neutrality for any later move against Austria and Czechoslovakia; the reoccupation of the Rhineland in March 1936 removed the demilitarised zone through which France would have had to advance to honour its guarantees to Prague and Warsaw; and the Anschluss of March 1938 meant Czechoslovakia could be attacked from the south as well as from the west and north. Each step therefore reduced the capacity to resist the next, so the correct unit of assessment is the sequence and not the step. The row carrying the most analytical weight is July 1934, the failed Austrian Nazi revolt in which Dollfuss was murdered: Mussolini moved Italian troops to the frontier and warned the Germans off, the revolt collapsed, and Hitler had to accept that Germany was not yet strong enough to force the issue and denied responsibility for the Austrian Nazis. It is the only occasion in the whole sequence on which resistance was offered, and it is the only direct evidence that resistance worked.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the disputed second set of aims as though it were as well established as the first. The first set - destroying Versailles, rebuilding the army, recovering the Saar and the Corridor, and gathering German-speakers into the Reich - is recorded without qualification and was almost wholly achieved by the end of 1938 without war and with British approval. Everything beyond it is contested by named historians, and a script that writes about Lebensraum to the Urals in the same register as the Saar plebiscite has misreported the state of the evidence.

*Eight of the nine steps met no resistance at all. Before the last two - the Sudetenland and Prague - one further theatre has to be dealt with, because it is where the two sides of the European question fought each other directly for three years while the governments that claimed to be neutral watched. It is also where the fear that made appeasement popular was manufactured.*

## Lesson 15 - Spain, 1936-39: intervention, non-intervention and the manufacture of a fear

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the boundary with the Spanish-politics owner; what the interwar-IR topic owns in this case; German and Italian intervention and Soviet counter-intervention; Anglo-French non-intervention; Mussolini's recorded motives; the Condor Legion and Guernica; the recorded effect of the horrors of the Spanish war on British opinion; the Japanese use of European preoccupation in 1937; what Spain demonstrated and what it did not

### Visual: one civil war, four external policies, five consequences

```text

   |  and the character of the Franco regime. DO NOT NARRATE.      |
   |  WHAT THIS TOPIC OWNS: the war as an arena in which the       |
   |  European powers acted, and the effects of their action       |
   |  on the wider international order.                            |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+

   SUMMER 1936: right-wing groups (Nationalists) try to overthrow
   the left-wing Republican government. The conflict "quickly
   developed an international significance".

   FOUR EXTERNAL POLICIES:

   ITALY                    GERMANY                USSR
   extensive help to        help to Franco;        help to the
   FRANCO. Motives          military experience    Republicans
   recorded:                gained; the CONDOR
   - a THIRD FASCIST        LEGION bombs the
     STATE in Europe        defenceless Basque
   - NAVAL BASES in         market town of
     Spain from which       GUERNICA
     to threaten France
   - stated justification:
     to prevent the spread
     of communism
                                  |
   BRITAIN AND FRANCE: "Predictably, Britain and France refused
   to intervene." NEITHER intervened, "though Germany and Italy
   sent decisive help to Franco". By 1939 FRANCO WAS VICTORIOUS.
                                  |
   ==================================================================
   FIVE CONSEQUENCES FOR THE INTERNATIONAL ORDER:

   1. DEMONSTRATION - two revisionist powers intervened decisively
      and two status-quo powers did not; the outcome followed
      from the difference
   2. COMBAT EXPERIENCE - "Germans and Italians gained military
      experience by helping Franco to victory"
   3. CONVERGENCE - Italy and Germany acting together in a third
      country, in the same months as the Rome-Berlin Axis and the
      Anti-Comintern Pact
   4. THE FEAR - "It was thought essential to avoid war, which was
      likely to be even more devastating than ever before, AS THE
      HORRORS OF THE SPANISH CIVIL WAR DEMONSTRATED. THE GREAT
      FEAR WAS THE BOMBING OF DEFENCELESS CITIES." -> this is one
      of the six recorded justifications of APPEASEMENT
   5. DISTRACTION - "In 1937 the Japanese took full advantage of
      Europe's preoccupation with events in Spain to embark on a
      full-scale invasion of northern China."
   ==================================================================
   THE PARADOX TO STATE EXPLICITLY: the same intervention that
   supplied Germany with combat experience also supplied Britain

   Spain armed one side and disarmed the other.
```

*The paradox in the closing box is the reason this lesson is worth a full place in the sequence. ⚠️ Most treatments give Spain two sentences as a rehearsal for the Second World War. Its larger interwar significance is that it produced, in the same months, the military experience of one coalition and the political paralysis of the other - and the evidence base records both effects explicitly.*

### 1. The boundary, and what this topic owns

✅ **Fact:** the internal history of the Spanish conflict is routed elsewhere in the evidence base; the interwar international-relations account carries it as a crisis of the international order. ✅ The basic owner's crisis table records the case in one line: Germany and Italy helped Franco; Britain and France stayed out.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the allocation is practical rather than pedantic. A script that explains the Spanish political background has spent its words on another owner's material and will still have to explain the international consequences in the space remaining. ⚠️ The correct allocation for this topic is one clause on the conflict and the rest on the four external policies and their effects.

### 2. What the external powers did

✅ **Fact:** during the summer of 1936 the Spanish Civil War broke out when right-wing groups, the Nationalists, tried to overthrow the left-wing Republican government. ✅ The conflict quickly developed an international significance when both Hitler and Mussolini, flexing their military muscles, sent help to Franco, the Nationalist leader, while the Republicans received Soviet help. ✅ Predictably, Britain and France refused to intervene, and by 1939 Franco was victorious.

✅ **Fact:** Mussolini sent extensive help to Franco, hoping to establish a third fascist state in Europe and to get naval bases in Spain from which he could threaten France. ✅ His justification was that he wanted to prevent the spread of communism.

✅ **Fact:** Germans and Italians gained military experience by helping Franco to victory. ✅ One of the most notorious exploits in this war was the bombing of the defenceless Basque market town of Guernica by the German Condor Legion.

✅ **Fact:** among the recorded examples of appeasement at work is the entry that neither Britain nor France intervened in the Spanish Civil War, though Germany and Italy sent decisive help to Franco.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the asymmetry is the analytical content. Non-intervention was presented as neutrality between two sides in a civil conflict, but it operated in a situation where two external powers were intervening decisively and a third was intervening partially. ⚠️ A policy of abstention in a contest where others are not abstaining is not neutral in effect, whatever its intention - and the recorded outcome, that Franco was victorious by 1939 with decisive German and Italian help, is the demonstration. ⚠️ That formulation is transferable and it is the sentence to write.

### 3. The fear, and where it appears in the appeasement case

✅ **Fact:** the first of the six recorded justifications for appeasement is stated in these terms: it was thought essential to avoid war, which was likely to be even more devastating than ever before, as the horrors of the Spanish Civil War demonstrated; the great fear was the bombing of defenceless cities. ✅ Memories of the horrors of the First World War still haunted many people; Britain, still in the throes of the economic crisis, could not afford vast rearmament and the crippling expenses of a major war; and British governments seemed to be supported by a strongly pacifist public opinion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Guernica and the appeasement justification belong in the same paragraph, and they are very rarely placed there. The German air force demonstrated, in Spain, the capability that made British governments unwilling to confront the German air force. ⚠️ That is not a coincidence of timing; it is a mechanism, and it can be stated in one sentence: the demonstration of a capability is itself an instrument of deterrence, and the Condor Legion's operations deterred the country that was not being attacked.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, with a necessary caution:** the evidence base does not record a deliberate German intention to deter Britain by bombing a Spanish town, and no such intention is asserted here. ⚠️ What is recorded is the capability, the event and the fear, and the causal link between the event and the fear is stated by the source itself in the appeasement justification. That is enough for the argument and no more should be claimed.

### 4. The distraction effect, and the global character of the crisis

✅ **Fact:** in 1937 the Japanese took full advantage of Europe's preoccupation with events in Spain to embark on a full-scale invasion of northern China. ✅ The resulting Sino-Japanese War eventually became part of the Second World War.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the clearest recorded instance of the interaction between the two theatres, and it deserves a sentence in any "why did collective security fail" answer. ⚠️ The three revisionist powers were not co-ordinating a global strategy - the evidence base supplies no such claim - but they were each exploiting the attention and capacity that the others consumed. ⚠️ A system with three simultaneous challengers and two functioning defenders is not three times as hard to defend as a system with one; it is harder than that, because each challenge reduces the resources available against the others. Naming that as a structural property is worth more than a list of crises.

### 5. What Spain demonstrated, and what it did not

| Claim | Status | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Two revisionist powers were now acting jointly outside their own frontiers | ✅ Supported | ✅ Both sent help to Franco; the Axis and the Anti-Comintern Pact were concluded in the same months |
| ⚠️ The status-quo powers would not intervene even where an ally's strategic position was at stake | ✅ Supported | ✅ Non-intervention recorded as an example of appeasement at work; ✅ Mussolini's recorded aim of naval bases from which to threaten France |
| ⚠️ German and Italian forces acquired operational experience | ✅ Supported | ✅ Recorded directly |
| ⚠️ The bombing of cities became the dominant fear in British policy | ✅ Supported | ✅ Recorded as the first justification of appeasement |
| ⚠️ The Spanish war made the European war inevitable | ❌ Not supported | The evidence base makes no such claim, and the anti-inevitability discipline applies |
| ⚠️ Britain and France were pro-Franco | ❌ Not supported here | The recorded position is non-intervention; no statement of sympathy is recorded in this evidence base, and none is asserted |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last two rows matter as much as the first four. This is a topic on which strong positions are easy to assert and hard to source, and the discipline of saying what the evidence does not establish is part of the answer. ⚠️ A candidate who writes "Britain and France secretly favoured Franco" has made a claim this evidence base does not support, and an examiner who knows the historiography will notice.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Was non-intervention not the responsible policy? Intervening in a civil war risks converting it into a general European war, which is precisely what Britain and France were trying to avoid.*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the logic and its context: the fear of general war was genuine and is recorded as the first reason for appeasement, and a great-power intervention on the Republican side against German and Italian forces already in the field carried an obvious escalation risk. Second, distinguish the policy from its effect, which is the analytical move this lesson exists to teach: abstention in a contest where two other powers are not abstaining transfers the advantage to them, and the recorded outcome was a Nationalist victory achieved with decisive German and Italian help. Third, note the strategic cost that the policy did not weigh. ✅ Mussolini's recorded motive included obtaining naval bases in Spain from which he could threaten France - so the non-intervening powers accepted, without contest, a change in the Mediterranean and Atlantic position of one of them. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is that non-intervention was a coherent policy for avoiding war in 1936 and an incoherent one for preserving the position from which a war might later have to be fought.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the international significance of the Spanish Civil War.
> ⚠️ "Non-intervention was intervention by another name." Examine with reference to the 1930s.
> ⚠️ How far did the crises of 1936 mark the end of the postwar international order?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Spain is usually a paragraph rather than an answer, and the paragraph has a fixed shape: one clause on the conflict, the four external policies with Mussolini's three recorded motives, Guernica and the Condor Legion, the recorded appeasement justification that names the Spanish horrors, and the 1937 Japanese exploitation. ⚠️ Close with the paradox - the war armed one coalition and disarmed the other - which is the sentence an examiner will remember.

### Traps

- Narrating Spanish internal politics. Another owner holds it; one clause is the allocation here.
- Saying the League handled Spain. It did not; the arrangement was made outside the League, as with Locarno in 1925.
- Forgetting Soviet help to the Republicans, which is recorded and which matters for the Soviet-exclusion argument of Lesson 19.
- Omitting Mussolini's naval-base motive, which is the strategic content of the case.
- Treating Guernica as an atrocity anecdote. Its analytical function is the link to the recorded appeasement justification about the bombing of defenceless cities.
- Asserting British or French sympathy for either side. The recorded position is non-intervention and nothing further is supported here.
- Missing the 1937 Japanese exploitation of European preoccupation, which is the recorded link between the two theatres.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Summer 1936: war breaks out when right-wing Nationalist groups try to overthrow the left-wing Republican government; it quickly acquires international significance.
2. ✅ Germany and Italy send help to Franco; the Republicans receive Soviet help; Britain and France refuse to intervene; Franco is victorious by 1939.
3. ✅ Mussolini's three recorded motives: a third fascist state in Europe; naval bases in Spain from which to threaten France; and the stated justification of preventing the spread of communism.
4. ✅ Germans and Italians gained military experience helping Franco to victory.
5. ✅ The Condor Legion bombed the defenceless Basque market town of Guernica.
6. ✅ Non-intervention is listed among the recorded examples of appeasement at work, with the note that Germany and Italy sent decisive help.
7. ✅ The first recorded justification of appeasement names the horrors of the Spanish Civil War and the great fear of the bombing of defenceless cities.
8. ✅ In 1937 Japan took full advantage of Europe's preoccupation with Spain to begin a full-scale invasion of northern China.
9. ✅ Spain coincided with the Rome-Berlin Axis of October 1936 and the Anti-Comintern Pact of November 1936, which Italy joined in 1937.
10. ⚠️ The paradox: the same three years supplied combat experience to one coalition and the decisive political argument against fighting to the other.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the sense in which Anglo-French non-intervention in Spain was not a neutral policy, and identify the recorded consequence that makes the point without relying on inference.

**Model answer:** Non-intervention would be neutral in a contest in which no external power was intervening. The Spanish case was not such a contest. Both Hitler and Mussolini sent help to Franco - described in the record as decisive - while the Republicans received Soviet help, and Britain and France refused to intervene. A policy of abstention adopted while two external powers are supplying one side and a third is partially supplying the other does not hold the balance; it removes from the calculation the two powers whose weight might have offset the intervention already occurring, and the recorded outcome is that by 1939 Franco was victorious. The consequence that makes the point without relying on inference is the strategic one attached to Mussolini's motives: he sent extensive help hoping to establish a third fascist state in Europe and to obtain naval bases in Spain from which he could threaten France. The non-intervening powers therefore permitted, without contest, a change in the strategic position of one of them - a naval threat to France on a second front - which is not something a neutral policy can be said to have left unaffected. Two further recorded effects reinforce it: Germans and Italians gained military experience by helping Franco to victory, while the horrors of the Spanish war became the first of the six recorded justifications for British appeasement, with the great fear being the bombing of defenceless cities that the German Condor Legion had demonstrated at Guernica. The policy therefore strengthened one coalition militarily and weakened the other politically, which is the opposite of neutrality in effect however neutral it was in intention.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Spanish Civil War as a rehearsal for the Second World War and stopping there. The rehearsal point is true but thin, and it invites narrative. The interwar significance is causal and can be stated in one sentence: in the same three years the war supplied one coalition with combat experience and convergence and supplied the other with the fear that made confrontation politically impossible, while drawing European attention away from the Far East at the moment Japan escalated.

*The crises are now complete up to the spring of 1938. The next two lessons turn from events to the policy that governed the response to all of them. Appeasement has been named eleven times in this session and defined once. It now has to be examined on its own terms - which means reconstructing why it was believed, before showing where the belief was wrong.*

## Lesson 16 - Appeasement: the definition, the two phases and the six reasons it looked right
