*Reconstruct the policy before judging it. ⚠️ Read the six boxes as a single argument and it is coherent: a country that cannot fight three enemies at once, whose public will not support rearmament, whose ally is divided, whose principal potential partner is distrusted on ideological grounds and whose opponent has demands that seem partly legitimate, should buy time and test whether the demands are finite. ⚠️ Every premise in that sentence is recorded. The conclusion is wrong, and showing why it is wrong while preserving the premises is what a high-scoring appeasement answer does.*

### 1. The definition, and the qualifier that carries the argument

✅ **Fact:** appeasement was the policy followed by the British, and later by the French, of avoiding war with aggressive powers such as Japan, Italy and Germany, by giving way to their demands, provided they were not too unreasonable.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the final clause is the whole policy, and it converts appeasement from a disposition into a decision procedure. The procedure requires two judgements: whether a given demand is reasonable, and whether the demands are finite. ⚠️ On the first, appeasement was frequently right - the Saar plebiscite was provided for in the settlement, German equality of armaments had a basis in the Covenant, and the Danzig claim is recorded as "in fact, not unreasonable, since Danzig was mainly German-speaking". ⚠️ On the second it was wrong, and the whole outcome follows from the second error rather than from the first. ⚠️ A script that states this - that the policy's judgements about individual demands were often sound and its judgement about the *series* was not - has produced the analysis the demand map asks for.

### 2. The two phases, and why the distinction matters

✅ **Fact:** there were two distinct phases of appeasement. From the mid-1920s until 1937 there was a vague feeling that war must be avoided at all cost, and Britain and sometimes France drifted along, accepting the various acts of aggression and breaches of Versailles - Manchuria, Abyssinia, German rearmament, the Rhineland reoccupation. ✅ When Neville Chamberlain became British prime minister in May 1937 he gave appeasement new drive; he believed in taking the initiative - he would find out what Hitler wanted and show him that reasonable claims could be met by negotiation rather than by force.

✅ **Fact:** the beginnings can be seen in British policy during the 1920s with the Dawes and Young Plans, which tried to conciliate the Germans, and with the Locarno Treaties and their vital omission - Britain did not agree to guarantee Germany's eastern frontiers, which even Stresemann, the "good German", said must be revised. ✅ When Austen Chamberlain remarked at the time of Locarno that no British government would ever risk the bones of a single British grenadier in defence of the Polish Corridor, it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe.

✅ **Fact:** appeasement reached its climax at Munich, where Britain and France were so determined to avoid war with Germany that they made Hitler a present of the Sudetenland, and so set in motion the destruction of Czechoslovakia. ✅ Even with such big concessions, appeasement failed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phase distinction is the most useful single organising device for an appeasement answer, and it is worth an explicit sentence. The first phase is a *failure to act*; the second is an *initiative*. They require different criticisms: the first can be defended by weakness, since a state that cannot act cannot be blamed for not acting; the second cannot, because it involved seeking out the aggressor and offering terms. ⚠️ And the second phase is the one Chamberlain personally owns, which is why the historiographical debate of Lesson 21 is about 1937-39 and not about 1931-36.

### 3. The six justifications, and how to handle each

| # | Justification, as recorded | ⚠️ Its strength | ⚠️ Where it fails |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Avoiding a war more devastating than ever, as Spain demonstrated; the fear of the bombing of defenceless cities; memories of 1914-18; inability to afford vast rearmament in the economic crisis; strongly pacifist opinion, evidenced by the Oxford Union vote of February 1933 and Baldwin's election victory of November 1935 after pledging no great armaments | Very strong: the cost of war was correctly estimated and the domestic constraint was real | ⚠️ It is a reason to delay a war, not a reason to believe one can be avoided; and it confuses the two |
| 2 | ✅ Many felt Germany and Italy had genuine grievances - Italy cheated at Versailles, Germany treated too harshly - so Britain should try to revise the most hated clauses, removing the need for German aggression and leading to Anglo-German friendship | Strong: the disarmament record and the Italian territorial disappointment are both recorded | ⚠️ Assumes grievances are finite and satisfiable; the Nazi campaign against the Young Plan in 1929 had already contradicted this |
| 3 | ✅ Since the League seemed helpless, Chamberlain believed the only way to settle disputes was by personal contact between leaders, by which he thought he could control and civilize Hitler, and Mussolini into the bargain, and bring them to respect international law | Strong on its premise: the League *was* helpless after 1935 | ⚠️ The conclusion does not follow: personal diplomacy without leverage is negotiation from weakness, and it removed the multilateral cover that a small state needed |
| 4 | ✅ Economic co-operation between Britain and Germany would be good for both; if Britain helped the German economy recover, Germany's internal violence would die down | Consistent with British policy since 1919 and with the Dawes experience | ⚠️ Applies a theory validated in the 1920s to a government whose economic policy from 1936 was directed to rearmament |
| 5 | ✅ Fear of communist Russia, especially among British Conservatives, many of whom believed the communist threat greater than the danger from Hitler; some were willing to ignore the unpleasant features of Nazism in the hope that Germany would be a buffer against communist expansion westwards; many admired Hitler's drive and his achievements | Explains behaviour that is otherwise puzzling, including the slowness of the 1939 negotiations | ⚠️ It selected the opponent by ideology rather than by capability, and it produced the exclusion that made the Nazi-Soviet Pact possible |
| 6 | ✅ Britain was not strong enough to fight more than one country at a time; the navy, strongest in the world apart from the American, would have struggled to defend the Empire and protect merchant shipping against Germany, Japan and Italy simultaneously; the air force was woefully short of long-range bombers and fighters; the USA was still isolationist and France weak and divided; Chamberlain speeded up rearmament so that nobody should treat Britain with anything but respect | The strongest of the six, and the basis of the Chamberlain defence in Lesson 21 | ⚠️ The time bought was used by both sides, and the evidence base does not establish which side used it better |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** justification 6 is the one to take most seriously and justification 5 is the one that did most damage. ⚠️ The unreadiness argument is a genuine strategic constraint with a named source - the military chiefs' advice to Chamberlain - and it produces a defensible policy of delay. ⚠️ The anti-communist argument is different in kind: it is a judgement about which of two opponents is more dangerous, it was made on ideological rather than capability grounds, and its consequences ran directly into 1939, because the state excluded from the anti-German front by that judgement was the state whose neutrality Germany then purchased.

### 4. Appeasement in action: the recorded examples

✅ **Fact:** the source lists six examples of appeasement at work.

1. ✅ No action was taken to check the obvious German rearmament. Lord Lothian, a Liberal, had a revealing comment after visiting Hitler in January 1935: "I am convinced that Hitler does not want war ... what the Germans are after is a strong army which will enable them to deal with Russia."
2. ✅ The Anglo-German Naval Agreement condoning German naval rearmament was signed without any consultation with France and Italy. This broke the Stresa Front, gravely shook French confidence in Britain, and encouraged Laval to look for understandings with Mussolini and Hitler.
3. ✅ There was only half-hearted British action against the Italian invasion of Abyssinia.
4. ✅ The French, though disturbed at the German reoccupation of the Rhineland in March 1936, did not mobilise their troops; they were deeply divided and ultra-cautious and received no backing from the British, who were impressed by Hitler's offer of a twenty-five-year peace. Lord Londonderry was reported to have sent Hitler a telegram congratulating him; Lord Lothian remarked that German troops had merely entered their own "back garden".
5. ✅ Neither Britain nor France intervened in the Spanish Civil War, though Germany and Italy sent decisive help to Franco. Britain tried to entice Mussolini to remove his troops by officially recognising Italian possession of Abyssinia in April 1938; however, Mussolini failed to keep his side of the bargain.
6. ✅ Though both Britain and France protested strongly at the Anschluss of March 1938, many in Britain saw it as the natural union of one German group with another. But Britain's lack of action encouraged Hitler to make demands on Czechoslovakia, which produced Chamberlain's supreme act of appeasement and Hitler's greatest triumph to date - Munich.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lothian's remark of January 1935 is the most revealing item in the list and it should be used rather than the Londonderry telegram, which is merely embarrassing. ⚠️ Lothian states the appeasement diagnosis in a single sentence - Hitler does not want war, and German rearmament is directed eastward against Russia - and that diagnosis, if correct, makes every element of British policy rational. ⚠️ The failure of appeasement is therefore best presented as the failure of a *hypothesis about German intentions*, and Lothian is the evidence that the hypothesis was held explicitly by people who had met Hitler and had reasons for holding it.

### 5. Comparison: three ways of characterising appeasement's failure

| Characterisation | What it says | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Moral failure** | The appeasers were weak, and betrayed small states | ⚠️ Rejected here: it explains nothing and is contradicted by the recorded reasoning and by the named appeasers' conviction that their policy was right |
| ⚠️ **Strategic failure** | The policy misjudged the balance of forces and the time available | ⚠️ Partly right: it captures justification 6's weakness, but the source records the military advice as accurate |
| ⚠️ **Diagnostic failure** | The policy correctly identified individual demands as reasonable and incorrectly assumed the series was finite | ⚠️ The reading taught here, and the one the basic owner supplies: appeasement treated Hitler as a revisionist with finite grievances rather than as an expansionist with unlimited ones |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the diagnostic reading has a practical advantage as well as an evidential one: it generates a testable claim. If appeasement's error was diagnostic, then the moment the diagnosis was falsified should be identifiable - and it is. ✅ In March 1939, when non-German territory was seized, even the appeasers were unable to justify what Hitler had done, and Chamberlain's attitude hardened. ⚠️ A policy that changes within days of its central hypothesis being disproved was a hypothesis-driven policy, not a character flaw.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Does the diagnostic reading not excuse the appeasers too easily? Churchill and Duff Cooper reached the right conclusion at the time, on the same information.*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the point about contemporaries: the source records that not everybody was enthusiastic - Churchill called Munich a total and unmitigated defeat, and Duff Cooper, First Lord of the Admiralty, resigned from the cabinet saying that Hitler could not be trusted to keep the agreement - and it adds, flatly, that they were right. ⚠️ The existence of a correct contemporary view removes the defence that the error was unavoidable. Second, distinguish availability from demonstrability: the correct view was available and was not demonstrable from the information then held, which is why it was a minority view held by individuals rather than the assessment of the intelligence and service departments whose advice is separately recorded. Third, note that the diagnostic reading is not an excuse but a *sharper* criticism, because it identifies the exact assumption that was wrong and the exact evidence that should have overturned it earlier - the Nazi campaign against the Young Plan in 1929, the failure of the July 1934 Austrian operation when resistance appeared, and Lothian's own formulation, which located German ambition in the east and then required Britain to be indifferent to eastern Europe. ⚠️ A criticism that names the assumption is worth more than one that names a failing.

### 7. UPSC application

The basic owner's directive map states this demand family exactly: a question asking you to examine appeasement is testing why it looked rational in context **and** why it failed, and the answer that must not be written is hindsight condemnation.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Critically examine the policy of appeasement.
> ⚠️ How far can appeasement be justified?
> ⚠️ "Appeasement was rational in motive and disastrous in effect." Discuss.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the recorded definition including the qualifier. ⚠️ Give the two phases and date the second to May 1937. ⚠️ Select three of the six justifications - the fear of war, the belief in genuine grievances and military unreadiness are the strongest - and state each at full strength before qualifying it. ⚠️ Then the diagnostic verdict, and then the falsification date of March 1939. ⚠️ Never open with a condemnation; the marks are in the reconstruction.

### Traps

- Omitting the qualifier "provided they were not too unreasonable" from the definition. It is the whole policy.
- Treating appeasement as beginning in 1937. ✅ Two phases, with the first running from the mid-1920s.
- Calling it a purely British policy without qualification. ✅ The definition says British and later French, and Lesson 17 sets out the French counter-current.
- Presenting the appeasers as a single figure. ✅ MacDonald, Baldwin, Simon and Hoare are named alongside Neville Chamberlain.
- Missing the anti-communist justification, which is the one with the largest consequences in 1939.
- Attributing any quotation to Chamberlain that the evidence base does not hold. ✅ It records "peace for our time" at Munich; the basic owner's factual-risk control separately warns against attributing unsourced quotations, and nothing beyond the recorded phrases is used here.
- Writing hindsight condemnation. It is the single most common way to lose marks on this question.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Definition: the British, later French, policy of avoiding war with aggressive powers such as Japan, Italy and Germany by giving way to their demands, provided they were not too unreasonable.
2. ✅ Phase 1, mid-1920s to 1937: drift, accepting Manchuria, Abyssinia, German rearmament and the Rhineland. Phase 2, from May 1937: Chamberlain gives it new drive and takes the initiative.
3. ✅ Origins in the 1920s: Dawes and Young; the Locarno omission; Austen Chamberlain's grenadier remark; the impression that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe.
4. ✅ Justification 1: avoid a war more devastating than ever, as Spain showed; the fear of bombing defenceless cities; the Oxford Union vote of February 1933; Baldwin's November 1935 victory after pledging no great armaments.
5. ✅ Justification 2: Germany and Italy had genuine grievances; revising the most hated clauses would remove the need for aggression.
6. ✅ Justification 3: with the League helpless, only personal contact between leaders could settle disputes and bring Hitler and Mussolini to respect international law.
7. ✅ Justification 4: Anglo-German economic co-operation would reduce Germany's internal violence.
8. ✅ Justification 5: fear of communism, especially among Conservatives; Germany as a buffer; admiration for Hitler's drive and achievements.
9. ✅ Justification 6: the military chiefs' advice that Britain could not fight more than one country at a time; naval overstretch; a shortage of long-range bombers and fighters; American isolation and French weakness; rearmament accelerated so that nobody should treat Britain with anything but respect.
10. ✅ The named appeasers: MacDonald, Baldwin, Simon, Hoare and Neville Chamberlain, all convinced their policy was right. ⚠️ The verdict taught: a diagnostic failure, falsified in March 1939.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconstruct the appeasement argument as a contemporary would have stated it in 1937, using at least four recorded justifications, and then identify the single assumption on which the whole argument depends.

**Model answer:** Stated from inside, the argument runs as follows. Another general war would be more devastating than anything previously experienced, as the horrors of the Spanish Civil War have demonstrated, and the great fear is the bombing of defenceless cities; the memory of 1914-18 is still fresh, the country is in the throes of an economic crisis and cannot afford vast rearmament, and public opinion is strongly pacifist, as the Oxford Union's vote in February 1933 that it would not fight for King and Country and Baldwin's election victory of November 1935 after pledging that there would be no great armaments both indicate. Second, the demands now being made are not all illegitimate: many hold that Germany was treated too harshly and Italy cheated at Versailles, so revising the most hated clauses should remove the need for German aggression and produce Anglo-German friendship. Third, the League has shown itself helpless over Manchuria and Abyssinia, so the only remaining method is personal contact between leaders, by which Hitler and Mussolini may be brought to respect international law. Fourth, and decisively, the military chiefs advise that Britain is not strong enough to fight more than one country at a time: the navy, the strongest in the world apart from the American, could not simultaneously defend the Empire and protect merchant shipping against Germany, Japan and Italy; the air force is woefully short of long-range bombers and fighters; the United States remains isolationist and France is weak and divided. Rearmament is therefore being accelerated, and the longer appeasement lasts the stronger Britain becomes. Every premise in that argument is recorded, and together they make a coherent case for concession combined with delay. The single assumption on which the whole structure depends is that the demands are finite - that there exists a set of concessions after which the aggressive power will be satisfied. Without it, concession does not reduce the probability of war but merely postpones it on worse terms, and the time bought is bought for both sides.

**Misconception to avoid:** Confusing the claim that appeasement was rational with the claim that it was right. The reconstruction above establishes rationality given a premise; the premise was false, and the falsification is datable to March 1939, when non-German territory was seized and even the appeasers were unable to justify what Hitler had done. Holding rationality and error together is precisely what a "critically examine" demand requires.

*The policy is now reconstructed. The next lesson tests two things the standard account leaves out: whether the constraints the appeasers cited were real, and whether Britain and France were in fact pursuing the same policy at all. The answer to the second is no, and the French counter-current has three names and a date attached to each.*

## Lesson 17 - Britain and France: the constraints, the divergence and the French counter-current

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: appeasement as mainly a British policy; the recorded French exceptions - Poincaré, Briand on the customs union, Barthou; Barthou's programme and his assassination in October 1934; Laval, the Franco-Soviet pact of May 1935 and its missing military clause; the Czechoslovak-Soviet pact and the French proviso; the collapse of French policy into the British line; the recorded British constraints tested; the asymmetry that produced Munich

### Visual: two capitals, two policies, and the moment they converge

```text
   THE STANDARD ACCOUNT: "Britain and France appeased Hitler."
   THE RECORD: "It is important to emphasize that appeasement was
   MAINLY A BRITISH POLICY, WITH WHICH THE FRENCH DID NOT ALWAYS
   AGREE."
   ==================================================================

   THE FRENCH COUNTER-CURRENT, NAME BY NAME

   POINCARE        "stood up to the Germans" - the Ruhr, Jan 1923
      |
   BRIAND          in favour of conciliation, but "even he DREW THE
   (1925-32)       LINE at the proposed Austro-German customs union
      |            in 1931"
      |
   LOUIS BARTHOU   Foreign Minister for A FEW MONTHS IN 1934.
   (1934)          Believed in FIRMNESS towards Hitler. Aimed to
      |            build a strong ANTI-GERMAN GROUP including ITALY
      |            AND THE USSR. Pressed for Russia's entry into
      |            the LEAGUE - which took place SEPTEMBER 1934.
      |            Told the British that France "REFUSED TO LEGALIZE
      |            GERMAN REARMAMENT", contrary to the Versailles
      |            Treaties.
      |

      |   ALEXANDER OF YUGOSLAVIA, who was on a state visit; both
      |   shot by Croat terrorists shortly after the king arrived

      |
   PIERRE LAVAL    Signed an ALLIANCE WITH RUSSIA, MAY 1935 -
   (from 1934)     "though it was a weak affair - THERE WAS NO

      |            Laval DISTRUSTED THE COMMUNISTS". Pinned his main
      |            hopes on friendship with MUSSOLINI - "these were

      |
      v
   "After this the French were so DEEPLY SPLIT BETWEEN LEFT AND
    RIGHT that NO DECISIVE FOREIGN POLICY SEEMED POSSIBLE; since the
    right admired Hitler, THE FRENCH FELL IN BEHIND THE BRITISH."
   ==================================================================
   THE CONVERGENCE POINT IS THEREFORE DATABLE: after the failure of
   the Hoare-Laval proposal, i.e. from 1936. BEFORE that date,
   France had an independent policy - firmness, an anti-German
   group, and Russia inside the system. AFTER it, France had
   Britain's policy.

   AND NOTE THE INSTRUMENT THAT WAS LEFT BEHIND:
   FRANCE ---- alliance with the USSR, May 1935, NO MILITARY CLAUSE
   USSR ------ agreement with CZECHOSLOVAKIA, 1935: each will help
               the other if attacked - BUT WITH ONE VITAL PROVISO:
               "HELP WOULD BE GIVEN ONLY IF FRANCE ASSISTED THE
                COUNTRY UNDER ATTACK"
   => the entire eastern security system of 1935 has a French
      trigger and no French military machinery. In September 1938
      the trigger was not pulled.
```

*The last box is the most economical explanation of Munich available anywhere in this topic. ⚠️ Czechoslovakia's Soviet guarantee was conditional on French action; France's Soviet alliance had no military provision; and France's obligation to Czechoslovakia dated from Locarno, where France had simultaneously guaranteed the German frontier it would have to cross. ⚠️ A candidate who reproduces that three-link chain has explained the September 1938 outcome structurally rather than morally.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

It is convenient to treat "Britain and France" as a single actor because they ended on the same side. They were not a single actor, and the difference is not a footnote. One of them had a land frontier with Germany, had been invaded twice in fifty years, had alliances in eastern Europe and had a foreign minister in 1934 who was building an anti-German coalition that included both Italy and the USSR. The other had an empire, a navy, a pacifist electorate and a settled view that a prosperous Germany was a safe Germany.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** so the historical question is not only why the two governments appeased, but why the one with the stronger incentive to resist stopped resisting. ⚠️ The evidence base answers that question with a sequence of specific events between 1934 and 1936, and reproducing the sequence is worth considerably more than the generalisation it replaces.

### 2. The recorded French exceptions, one by one

✅ **Fact:** appeasement was mainly a British policy, with which the French did not always agree.

✅ **Fact:** Poincaré stood up to the Germans, and although Briand was in favour of conciliation, even he drew the line at the proposed Austro-German customs union in 1931.

✅ **Fact:** Louis Barthou, foreign minister for a few months in 1934, believed in firmness towards Hitler and aimed to build up a strong anti-German group which would include Italy and the USSR. ✅ This is why he pressed for Russia's entry into the League of Nations, which took place in September 1934. ✅ He told the British that France refused to legalise German rearmament, contrary to the Versailles Treaties.

✅ **Fact:** Barthou was assassinated in October 1934, along with King Alexander of Yugoslavia, who was on a state visit to France; the core account's appeasement chapter records that they were both shot by Croat terrorists shortly after the king had arrived in Marseilles. ⚠️ **A fuller description is recorded elsewhere in the same account**, which states that the king's murderer was a Macedonian connected with a group of Croat revolutionaries living in Hungary. ⚠️ Both records are held here; consistent with the attribution discipline of this session, the individual and the organisation are named where the sources name them and no collective description is adopted.

✅ **Fact:** Barthou's successor, Pierre Laval, signed an alliance with Russia in May 1935, though it was a weak affair - there was no provision in it for military co-operation, since Laval distrusted the communists. ✅ He pinned his main hopes on friendship with Mussolini, but these were dashed by the failure of the Hoare-Laval Pact.

✅ **Fact:** after this the French were so deeply split between left and right that no decisive foreign policy seemed possible; since the right admired Hitler, the French fell in behind the British.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Barthou's programme is the most important road not taken in the whole period, and it deserves to be stated as a programme rather than as a biographical detail. It had three components - firmness, Italy, and the USSR - and all three were live in 1934: Italy had just stopped a German move on Austria in July, and the USSR entered the League in September at French urging. ⚠️ Within eighteen months all three were gone: Barthou was dead, Italy was estranged by the naval agreement and the Abyssinian sanctions, and the Soviet alliance had been signed without the military clause that would have made it operative. ⚠️ Stating that as a single sequence - the programme, its three components and their sequential removal - is one of the most effective paragraphs available in an origins-of-war answer.

### 3. The eastern security system of 1935, and the flaw built into it

✅ **Fact:** the French treaty of mutual assistance with the USSR was signed in May 1935, soon after the Stresa meeting, by governments that were both worried by the apparent threat from Nazi Germany. ✅ It contained no provision for military co-operation.

✅ **Fact:** Benes, alarmed by the growing success of Henlein and his party, looked for further protection and an agreement was signed with the USSR in 1935, under which the two states promised to help each other if attacked. ✅ But there was one vital proviso: help would be given only if France assisted the country under attack. ✅ Tragically, neither France nor Britain was prepared to give military support when the crisis came in 1938.

✅ **Fact:** the French obligation to Czechoslovakia dated from Locarno, where France promised to guarantee Czechoslovakia's frontiers while Germany promised only that any frontier disputes would be settled by arbitration.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three instruments, one dependency. ⚠️ The Soviet obligation to Czechoslovakia was conditional on France acting; the French obligation to act had no Soviet military machinery behind it; and France's own guarantee dated from an agreement in which it had simultaneously undertaken to respect the German frontier. ⚠️ The system therefore had exactly one point of failure, and when that point failed in September 1938 the entire structure produced no assistance from anybody. ⚠️ A candidate who shows that Munich was made possible by the architecture of 1935 rather than only by the decisions of 1938 is writing at a distinctly higher level.

### 4. Testing the British constraints

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the six appeasement justifications include factual claims that can be examined against the evidence base, and doing so is more useful than either accepting or dismissing them.

| Claim | Status against the evidence base |
|---|---|
| ✅ Britain could not fight more than one country at a time | ✅ Recorded as the military chiefs' advice to Chamberlain, with the naval overstretch and the shortage of long-range bombers and fighters specified. ⚠️ No contrary evidence is held. **Accept.** |
| ✅ The USA was still in favour of isolation | ✅ Confirmed: the Senate rejection of 1920; the return to strict isolation over Manchuria with refusal to join sanctions; the record that the USA, the only power capable of effectively resisting Japan, was still bent on isolation. ✅ **Fact:** and it was statutory from August 1935 - an arms embargo on belligerents, a loan ban from 1936, and in 1937 a permanent embargo extended to civil wars with only a two-year non-arms cash-and-carry concession. **Accept.** |
| ✅ France was weak and divided | ✅ Confirmed: deeply split between left and right after the Hoare-Laval failure, so that no decisive foreign policy seemed possible; deeply divided and ultra-cautious over the Rhineland. **Accept.** |
| ✅ Public opinion was strongly pacifist | ✅ Supported by the Oxford Union vote of February 1933 and by Baldwin's election victory of November 1935 after pledging no great armaments. **Accept, with the caution that both items are indicative rather than measurements.** |
| ⚠️ Delay would leave Britain relatively stronger | ⚠️ **Not established.** German forces grew from an announced 36 divisions in March 1935 to 51 divisions and about 800 000 men plus reserves by the end of 1938, with 21 large naval vessels, 47 U-boats and over 5000 aircraft. The evidence base records that Chamberlain speeded up British rearmament and that Munich arguably gained a crucial year, and it records no comparative series. **State as contested.** |
| ⚠️ A Grand Alliance was unavailable | ⚠️ **Contested inside the evidence base itself:** Charmley argues Chamberlain had little option and that his policies were more realistic than alternatives such as a Grand Alliance of Britain, France, Poland, Czechoslovakia, Romania and the USSR suggested at the time by Churchill, while Roberts argues that this was never a serious possibility because of the many points of disagreement. **Display, do not resolve.** |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this table is the disciplined form of the appeasement debate, and it converts a moral argument into an evidentiary one. ⚠️ Four of the six claims survive examination. The two that do not are precisely the two on which the defence of appeasement depends most heavily - that time favoured Britain, and that no alternative coalition existed. ⚠️ That is a sharper conclusion than either condemnation or exoneration, and it is fully sourced.

### 5. The asymmetry that produced Munich

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** by September 1938 the two capitals were in structurally different positions, and the difference determined the outcome.

| Dimension | Britain | France |
|---|---|---|
| Treaty obligation to Czechoslovakia | ✅ None | ✅ A guarantee of Czechoslovakia's frontiers dating from Locarno |
| Route to discharge it | Not applicable | ⚠️ Across a German frontier it had guaranteed at Locarno and which had been remilitarised in March 1936 |
| Eastern partner | ✅ None | ✅ The USSR, by a treaty of May 1935 with no military provision |
| Domestic condition | ✅ Pacifist opinion; a government with a large majority since November 1935 | ✅ Deeply split between left and right, with no decisive foreign policy possible |
| Military advice | ✅ Cannot fight more than one country at a time | ✅ Did not mobilise even over the Rhineland in 1936 |
| Position in the crisis | ✅ Chamberlain took the initiative, flying to Berchtesgaden and Godesberg | ✅ Daladier joined the pressure on the Czechs |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the asymmetry is that France had the obligation and Britain had the initiative. ⚠️ A state with an obligation it cannot discharge alone will follow a state with a policy, and that is what the record describes when it says the French fell in behind the British. ⚠️ The formulation to write is that by 1938 France was executing a British policy in an eastern crisis where only France had a treaty commitment - and that this, rather than any individual's weakness, is the structural reason Czechoslovakia received no help from its guarantor.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Does separating British and French policy not simply spread the blame more thinly? Both governments pressed the Czechs at Munich, and both had signed away the eastern frontier's protection at Locarno.*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the convergence at the end: the record is explicit that Chamberlain and Daladier both put tremendous pressure on the Czechs and that both were determined to go to almost any lengths to avoid war, so any attempt to exonerate France in September 1938 fails. Second, insist on the divergence before it, because it is where the explanatory content lies: the record states that appeasement was mainly a British policy with which the French did not always agree, names Poincaré, Briand and Barthou as exceptions, dates the French collapse into the British line to the period after the Hoare-Laval failure, and gives the reason as a left-right split so deep that no decisive foreign policy seemed possible. Third, note what the distinction buys analytically: it identifies a period in which an alternative policy existed and had an author, which is the necessary precondition for any serious counterfactual. ⚠️ Without Barthou, the claim that a firmer line was available in the mid-1930s is speculation; with him, it is a policy that was being pursued by a foreign minister of a great power and was interrupted by an assassination.

### 7. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Appeasement was a British policy, not an Anglo-French one." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess French policy towards Germany in the 1930s.
> ⚠️ To what extent were the constraints cited by the appeasers real?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The constraint-testing table is the highest-value item here and it converts any appeasement question into an evidence question. ⚠️ Use four accepted constraints and two contested ones, and say explicitly that the two contested ones are the two the defence depends on. ⚠️ For a French-policy question, the Barthou sequence - programme, three components, sequential removal - is the spine.

### Traps

- Writing "Britain and France appeased" without the recorded qualification that it was mainly a British policy.
- Omitting Barthou, or reducing him to a name. ✅ His programme was firmness, an anti-German group including Italy and the USSR, and Russia's entry into the League in September 1934.
- Dating Barthou's assassination wrongly or omitting that King Alexander of Yugoslavia was killed with him at Marseilles.
- Presenting the Franco-Soviet pact of May 1935 as a functioning alliance. ✅ It had no provision for military co-operation, because Laval distrusted the communists.
- Missing the proviso in the Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement of 1935 - help only if France assisted first. It is the structural key to September 1938.
- Saying French policy collapsed in 1938. ✅ The recorded convergence follows the Hoare-Laval failure, that is, from 1936.
- Accepting or rejecting all six appeasement constraints together. Four survive testing and two do not.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Appeasement was mainly a British policy, with which the French did not always agree.
2. ✅ Poincaré stood up to the Germans; Briand, though conciliatory, drew the line at the 1931 Austro-German customs union.
3. ✅ Barthou, foreign minister for a few months in 1934: firmness towards Hitler; an anti-German group including Italy and the USSR; Russia into the League in September 1934; France refused to legalise German rearmament.
4. ✅ October 1934: Barthou assassinated at Marseilles with King Alexander of Yugoslavia. ⚠️ The account's appeasement chapter attributes the shooting to Croat terrorists; its successor-states chapter records the king's murderer as a Macedonian connected with Croat revolutionaries living in Hungary. Both records are held; neither is adopted as a collective description.
5. ✅ Laval signed an alliance with Russia in May 1935 with no provision for military co-operation, distrusting the communists, and pinned his hopes on Mussolini until the Hoare-Laval failure.
6. ✅ After that the French were so split between left and right that no decisive foreign policy seemed possible; since the right admired Hitler, they fell in behind the British.
7. ✅ The Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement of 1935 promised mutual help, but only if France assisted the country under attack.
8. ✅ France's guarantee of Czechoslovakia's frontiers dated from Locarno; Germany there promised only arbitration.
9. ⚠️ Four British constraints survive testing - multi-front incapacity, American isolation, French weakness and pacifist opinion; two do not - that delay favoured Britain, and that no Grand Alliance was available.
10. ⚠️ The asymmetry of 1938: France had the obligation, Britain had the initiative, and the obligation followed the initiative.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the eastern security system as it stood in 1935 had only one point of failure, and name the three instruments that created that dependency.

**Model answer:** The three instruments were the Locarno arrangements of 1925, the Franco-Soviet treaty of May 1935 and the Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement of 1935, and together they routed every eastern guarantee through a single French decision. At Locarno France promised to guarantee Czechoslovakia's frontiers, while Germany gave only an undertaking that frontier disputes would be settled by arbitration and expressly refused to guarantee its eastern frontiers; France therefore held the obligation, and after March 1936, when the Rhineland was remilitarised in breach of both Versailles and Locarno, the only route by which France could discharge it ran across a German frontier that France had itself guaranteed and that was now garrisoned. The Franco-Soviet treaty of May 1935 appeared to add a great-power partner in the east, but it was a weak affair with no provision for military co-operation, because Laval distrusted the communists; it created a political alignment without a military mechanism. The Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement of 1935, concluded by Benes as Henlein's party grew, promised that the two states would help each other if attacked, but with one vital proviso: help would be given only if France assisted the country under attack. The consequence is that Soviet assistance to Prague was conditional on French action, French action had no Soviet military machinery behind it, and the French obligation itself had been created in the same instrument that constrained its execution. A single decision in Paris therefore governed whether any of the three instruments produced anything, and when neither France nor Britain was prepared to give military support in 1938, none of them did.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the exclusion of the USSR from Munich as the moment Soviet assistance became unavailable. The structure that made it unavailable was built in 1935, in the proviso that tied Soviet help to prior French action and in the absence of any military clause in the Franco-Soviet treaty. The exclusion from the conference confirmed and advertised a dependency that already existed, which is why it mattered politically for 1939 as much as militarily for 1938.

*The architecture is now visible and the trigger is in Paris. The next lesson is the year in which it was tested: from the annexation of Austria in March 1938 to the occupation of Prague in March 1939 - twelve months in which appeasement achieved its greatest apparent success and was then disproved.*

*The diagram is built around the last box. ⚠️ Munich is the more famous event and Prague is the more important one, because Munich was consistent with appeasement's own principle and Prague was not. ⚠️ Until 15 March 1939 every German acquisition could be described as the gathering of German-speakers; after it, none could. That is the date the hypothesis of Lesson 16 was falsified, and it is the date British policy changed.*

### 1. The Anschluss and its strategic consequence

✅ **Fact:** the Anschluss with Austria in March 1938 was Hitler's greatest success to date. ✅ Matters came to a head when the Austrian Nazis staged huge demonstrations in Vienna, Graz and Linz which Chancellor Schuschnigg's government could not control. ✅ Realising that this could be the prelude to a German invasion, Schuschnigg announced a referendum about whether or not Austria should remain independent; Hitler decided to act before it was held, in case the vote went against union; German troops moved in and Austria became part of the Third Reich.

✅ **Fact:** the background is recorded. Schuschnigg, the Chancellor after the murder of Dollfuss in July 1934, worked hard to preserve the alliance with Italy. ✅ But Mussolini, in return for the Anglo-French handling of the Abyssinian affair, dropped his objections to a German takeover of Austria, and the Anschluss is recorded as having shown the value of the new German understanding with Italy. ✅ Most Austrians had in any case felt that the natural solution to their problems was union with Germany, though the Treaty of St Germain of September 1919 had required them to promise not to unite with Germany for at least twenty years.

✅ **Fact:** though both Britain and France protested strongly, many in Britain saw it as the natural union of one German group with another. ✅ Britain's lack of action encouraged Hitler to make demands on Czechoslovakia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strategic consequence is the point that must not be omitted. ✅ Czechoslovakia could now be attacked from the south as well as from the west and north. ⚠️ An event that was defensible on the principle of national self-determination transformed the military geography of a neighbouring state without a shot being fired - which is the clearest single illustration of the argument in Lesson 14 that a sequence cannot be assessed step by step.

### 2. The Sudeten campaign, and the truth about the grievance

✅ **Fact:** it seems likely that Hitler had decided to destroy Czechoslovakia as part of his Lebensraum policy, because he detested the Czechs for their democracy and because they were Slavs, and because their state had been set up by the hated Versailles settlement; its situation was strategically important, and control of the area would bring great advantages for Germany's military and economic dominance of central Europe.

✅ **Fact:** the excuse for the opening propaganda campaign was that 3.5 million Sudeten Germans, under their leader Konrad Henlein, were being discriminated against by the Czech government. ✅ It is true that unemployment was more serious among the Germans, but this was because a large proportion of them worked in industry, where unemployment was most severe because of the depression.

⚠️ **A figure discrepancy inside the evidence base, displayed rather than resolved.** The same account gives **3 million** Germans in its table of Czechoslovakia's national composition and again in its summary sentence describing the Sudetenland as an area containing three million Germans, and **3.5 million** in its Sudeten-campaign section and again in its audit of what had been brought into the Reich by the end of 1938. ⚠️ Both figures are held here; neither is preferred, no average or reconciliation is invented, and the safe formulation for an answer is "about three to three and a half million Germans in the Sudetenland". ⚠️ Where this session uses 3.5 million it is reporting the campaign and audit passages; where it uses 3 million in Lesson 2 it is reporting the composition table.

✅ **Fact:** the party's rise is dated. After Hitler came to power in Germany, the Sudeten German party under Henlein became bolder, organising rallies and protest demonstrations; in the 1935 elections they won 44 seats, making them the second largest party in the lower house of parliament; the following year Henlein began to demand self-government for the German-speaking areas. ✅ But Hitler was determined on more: by 1938 he had decided that the Sudetenland must become part of Germany and that the state of Czechoslovakia itself must be destroyed.

✅ **Fact:** the Nazis organised huge protest demonstrations in the Sudetenland and clashes occurred between Czechs and Germans. ✅ The Czech president, Edvard Benes, feared that Hitler was stirring up the disturbances so that German troops could march in "to restore order". ✅ Chamberlain and Daladier, the French prime minister, were afraid that if this happened war would break out; they were determined to go to almost any lengths to avoid war, and they put tremendous pressure on the Czechs to make concessions to Hitler.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the unemployment explanation is the most valuable sentence in this lesson and it is almost never used. It converts the Sudeten question from a contest of national claims into a testable factual dispute with a recorded answer: the hardship was real, and its cause was the Depression's effect on industrial employment rather than governmental discrimination. ⚠️ A candidate who states that has done what the examiner most wants on a question of this kind - assessed a claimed grievance against evidence rather than reporting the claim or dismissing it.

### 3. Berchtesgaden, Godesberg and what the Czechs could have done

✅ **Fact:** eventually Benes agreed that the Sudeten Germans might be handed over to Germany. ✅ Chamberlain flew to Germany and had talks with Hitler at Berchtesgaden on 15 September, explaining the offer; Hitler seemed to accept.

✅ **Fact:** at a second meeting at Godesberg only a week later he stepped up his demands: he wanted more of Czechoslovakia and the immediate entry of German troops into the Sudetenland.

✅ **Fact:** Benes would not agree to this and immediately ordered the mobilisation of the Czech army. ✅ The Czechs had put great effort into fortifying their frontiers with Germany, Austria and Hungary, building bunkers and anti-tank defences; their army had been expanded; and they were hopeful that with help from their allies, particularly France and the USSR, any German attack could be repulsed. ✅ It would certainly not have been a walkover for the Germans.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Godesberg escalation is the strongest available evidence for the diagnostic reading of appeasement. A demand was made, conceded and then increased within a week. ⚠️ That is the behaviour of a party seeking a pretext rather than a settlement, and it was observed at the time by the man conducting the negotiation. ⚠️ The fact that the conference at Munich followed a week after Godesberg is therefore not evidence that Chamberlain was deceived; it is evidence that he preferred a conference to the alternative even after the deception had become visible.

### 4. Munich, and the four features that matter

✅ **Fact:** when it seemed that war was inevitable, Hitler invited Chamberlain and Daladier to a four-power conference which met in Munich on 29 September 1938. ✅ Here a plan produced by Mussolini, but actually written by the German Foreign Office, was accepted.

| Feature | Content | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| The terms | ✅ The Sudetenland handed over to Germany immediately; Poland given Teschen; Hungary given South Slovakia; Germany and the other three powers guaranteeing the rest of Czechoslovakia | ⚠️ The partition was multilateral, which converted two neighbours into beneficiaries of the German demand |
| The authorship | ✅ The plan was produced by Mussolini but actually written by the German Foreign Office | ⚠️ The mediation was formal rather than substantive |
| The exclusions | ✅ Neither the Czechs nor the Russians were invited | ⚠️ The state being partitioned and the state with a treaty obligation to defend it were both absent |
| The coercion | ✅ The Czechs were told that if they resisted the Munich decision they would receive no help from Britain or France, even though France had guaranteed the Czech frontiers at Locarno | ⚠️ A guarantor informing the guaranteed state that the guarantee will not be honoured |

✅ **Fact:** given this betrayal by France and the unsympathetic attitude of Britain, Czech military resistance seemed hopeless; they had no choice but to go along with the decision of the conference, and a few days later Benes resigned.

✅ **Fact:** the morning after the conference, Chamberlain had a private meeting with Hitler at which they both signed a statement, the "scrap of paper", prepared by Chamberlain, promising that Britain and Germany would renounce warlike intentions against each other and would use consultation to deal with any problems. ✅ On his return Chamberlain was given a rapturous welcome by a public that thought war had been averted, and remarked: "I believe it is peace for our time."

✅ **Fact:** not everybody was so enthusiastic. Churchill called Munich "a total and unmitigated defeat"; Duff Cooper, the First Lord of the Admiralty, resigned from the cabinet, saying that Hitler could not be trusted to keep the agreement. ✅ The source adds: they were right.

✅ **Fact:** as a result of the agreement Czechoslovakia was crippled by the loss of 70 per cent of her heavy industry, a third of her population, roughly a third of her territory and almost all her carefully prepared fortifications, mostly to Germany. ✅ Slovakia and Ruthenia were given self-government for internal affairs, though there was still a central government in Prague.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the advanced owner's formulation is the one to carry: Munich mattered not only because territory was conceded but because it destroyed the strongest central-European barrier to further German expansion. ⚠️ The four figures supply the proof, and they should be used as a group rather than singly, because together they establish that the state was not merely reduced but disarmed - the fortifications and the heavy industry being the two things that had made a German attack in September something other than a walkover.

### 5. Prague, March 1939: the sequence and the falsification

✅ **Fact:** early in 1939 Slovakia, encouraged by Germany, began to demand complete independence from Prague and it looked as if the country was about to fall apart. ✅ Hitler put pressure on the Slovak prime minister, Father Jozef Tiso, to declare independence and request German help, but Tiso was ultra-cautious.

✅ **Fact:** it was the new Czech president, Emil Hacha, who brought matters to a head. On 9 March 1939 the Prague government moved against the Slovaks to forestall the expected declaration of independence: their cabinet was deposed, Tiso was placed under house arrest, and the Slovak government buildings in Bratislava were occupied by police. ✅ This gave Hitler his chance: Tiso was brought to Berlin, where Hitler convinced him that the time was now ripe; back in Bratislava, Tiso and the Slovaks proclaimed independence on 14 March, and the next day they asked for German protection - although, as Ian Kershaw points out in *Hitler, 1936-1945: Nemesis*, this was only after German warships on the Danube had trained their sights on the Slovakian government offices.

✅ **Fact:** President Hacha was then invited to Berlin, where Hitler told him that in order to protect the German Reich a protectorate must be imposed over what was left of Czechoslovakia. German troops were poised to enter, and Hacha was to order the Czech army not to resist; Goering threatened that Prague would be bombed if he refused. ✅ Faced with such a browbeating, Hacha felt he had no alternative but to agree.

✅ **Fact:** consequently, on 15 March 1939 German troops occupied the rest of Czechoslovakia while the Czech army remained in barracks. ✅ Bohemia and Moravia were declared a protectorate within the German Reich, Slovakia was to be an independent state under the protection of the Reich, and Ruthenia was occupied by Hungarian troops. ✅ Britain and France protested but as usual took no action; Chamberlain said the guarantee of Czech frontiers given at Munich did not apply because technically the country had not been invaded - German troops had entered by invitation.

✅ **Fact:** however, the German action caused a great outburst of criticism: for the first time even the appeasers were unable to justify what Hitler had done - he had broken his promise and seized non-German territory. ✅ Even Chamberlain felt this was going too far, and his attitude hardened.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Kershaw's qualification about the warships is doing precise work and should be retained. The request for German protection was the legal form on which Chamberlain's "by invitation" argument depended; Kershaw establishes that the request followed a military threat. ⚠️ That is why the technique matters: the manufactured legal form - first seen in Manchukuo in 1932 - had been used to construct a defence that the appeasing power then used in public. ⚠️ Naming the technique across both cases is the kind of observation that distinguishes a strong answer.

### 6. Comparison: why Prague and not Munich is the turning point

| Criterion | Munich, September 1938 | Prague, March 1939 |
|---|---|---|
| Consistent with self-determination? | ✅ Yes in form: the Sudetenland contained 3.5 million Germans | ✅ No: non-German territory was seized |
| Consistent with appeasement's own test of reasonableness? | ⚠️ Arguably yes, which is why it was defended | ⚠️ No, which is why even the appeasers could not defend it |
| Did the policy change? | ✅ No: "peace for our time"; the scrap of paper | ✅ Yes: Chamberlain's attitude hardened; the guarantee to Poland followed |
| Was there a treaty basis for resistance? | ✅ Yes - France's Locarno guarantee of the Czech frontiers | ✅ Yes - the four-power guarantee of the rest of Czechoslovakia given at Munich |
| Was resistance militarily possible? | ✅ Recorded as not a walkover: fortifications, an expanded army, hopes of French and Soviet help | ✅ No: the fortifications had been transferred at Munich and the army remained in barracks |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the decisive one. A policy is identified by what changes it, and appeasement was changed by Prague and not by Munich. ⚠️ That is the strongest available evidence that appeasement was operating a test of reasonableness rather than a general disposition to concede - and it is also the strongest available criticism, because the test was applied to the *character* of each demand and never to the *pattern* of the series.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the Czech position was not a walkover, why did Britain and France not simply support resistance in September 1938? Was the pressure on Prague not the single clearest error of the whole period?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, state the case for resistance at full strength, because the evidence base supports it: the Czechs had fortified their frontiers with bunkers and anti-tank defences, had expanded their army, had hopes of French and Soviet help, and it would certainly not have been a walkover; France had guaranteed the Czech frontiers at Locarno; and the later criticism that Chamberlain chose the wrong issue is recorded - German claims for Danzig and the corridor were more reasonable than the demands for the Sudetenland, which contained almost a million non-Germans, and Poland was harder to defend and militarily much weaker than Czechoslovakia. Second, state the constraints as they were then understood: the military chiefs' advice that Britain could not fight more than one country at a time, the pacifist public, the fear of the bombing of cities after Spain, and the Soviet obligation's dependence on prior French action. Third, note what the argument requires and what the evidence gives: the case for resistance is *stronger* than the case against, and it is a case about probabilities rather than certainties, which is why Charmley, Roberts and Self can all be recorded as defending Chamberlain without the evidence base resolving the question. ⚠️ The disciplined verdict is that September 1938 was the best available opportunity for a stand and that the evidence does not establish that a stand would have succeeded.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Munich Agreement.
> ⚠️ "Munich destroyed Czechoslovakia; Prague destroyed appeasement." Examine.
> ⚠️ How far was the policy of appeasement responsible for the outbreak of the Second World War?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Do not narrate the diplomacy of September 1938 beyond four dated points - Berchtesgaden on 15 September, Godesberg a week later, the mobilisation, and the conference on 29 September. ⚠️ Spend the space instead on the four Munich features, the four loss figures and the Prague falsification. ⚠️ The sentence that earns the most is the advanced owner's: Munich mattered not only because territory was conceded but because it destroyed the strongest central-European barrier to further German expansion.

### Traps

- Saying Czechoslovakia could not have resisted. ✅ The recorded assessment is that it would certainly not have been a walkover.
- Omitting the exclusion of the Russians, which matters for 1939 as much as the exclusion of the Czechs mattered for 1938.
- Attributing the Munich plan to Mussolini without the recorded qualification that it was actually written by the German Foreign Office.
- Forgetting Teschen and South Slovakia. ✅ Poland and Hungary were beneficiaries of the partition.
- Giving the Czechoslovak losses imprecisely. ✅ 70 per cent of heavy industry, a third of the population, roughly a third of the territory and almost all the fortifications.
- Treating "peace for our time" as the only contemporary reaction. ✅ Churchill's "total and unmitigated defeat" and Duff Cooper's resignation are recorded, and the source says they were right.
- Missing Kershaw's qualification about the German warships on the Danube, which is what disposes of the "by invitation" defence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ March 1938: Anschluss after Austrian Nazi demonstrations in Vienna, Graz and Linz and Schuschnigg's pre-empted referendum; Czechoslovakia could now be attacked from the south as well as the west and north.
2. ✅ The Sudeten excuse: 3.5 million Germans under Konrad Henlein; his party won 44 seats in the 1935 elections, the second largest in the lower house; self-government demanded from 1936.
3. ✅ The recorded truth about the grievance: unemployment was worse among the Germans because a large proportion worked in industry, where the Depression hit hardest.
4. ✅ Chamberlain and Daladier put tremendous pressure on the Czechs; Benes agreed the Sudeten Germans might be handed over.
5. ✅ Berchtesgaden 15 September; Godesberg a week later with increased demands; Benes refused and mobilised; fortifications, bunkers and anti-tank defences meant it would not have been a walkover.
6. ✅ Munich, 29 September 1938: a four-power conference; a plan produced by Mussolini but written by the German Foreign Office; the Sudetenland immediately, Teschen to Poland, South Slovakia to Hungary, and a four-power guarantee of the rest.
7. ✅ Neither the Czechs nor the Russians were invited; the Czechs were told they would get no help even though France had guaranteed their frontiers at Locarno; Benes resigned.
8. ✅ The "scrap of paper"; "I believe it is peace for our time"; Churchill's "total and unmitigated defeat"; Duff Cooper's resignation; the source's verdict that they were right.
9. ✅ The cost: 70 per cent of heavy industry, a third of the population, roughly a third of the territory and almost all the fortifications, mostly to Germany.
10. ✅ March 1939: Tiso, Hacha, Goering's threat, the occupation of 15 March with the army in barracks, Bohemia and Moravia a protectorate, Slovakia protected, Ruthenia to Hungary; Chamberlain's "by invitation" defence; Kershaw on the warships; even the appeasers could not justify it and Chamberlain's attitude hardened.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why March 1939 rather than September 1938 is the date on which appeasement ended, and state what that dating establishes about the nature of the policy.

**Model answer:** It is the date on which the policy changed, and a policy is identified by what changes it. Munich was consistent with appeasement's own operating principle, which was to give way to demands provided they were not too unreasonable: the Sudetenland contained 3.5 million Germans under Konrad Henlein, the claimed grievance was at least plausible even though the recorded cause of Sudeten unemployment was the Depression's effect on industrial areas rather than Czech discrimination, and the transfer could be presented as an application of national self-determination. Accordingly Chamberlain returned describing it as peace for our time, signed the "scrap of paper" renouncing warlike intentions, and continued the policy. The occupation of 15 March 1939 could not be brought under the same principle. German troops occupied the rest of Czechoslovakia while the Czech army remained in barracks; Bohemia and Moravia were made a protectorate, Slovakia an independent state under the Reich's protection, and Ruthenia was occupied by Hungarian troops; and although Chamberlain argued that the Munich guarantee did not apply because technically the country had not been invaded, German troops having entered by invitation - a legal form that Kershaw shows was obtained only after German warships on the Danube had trained their sights on the Slovakian government offices - the action caused a great outburst of criticism because for the first time even the appeasers were unable to justify what had been done: a promise had been broken and non-German territory seized. Chamberlain felt this was going too far and his attitude hardened, and the guarantee to Poland followed. What the dating establishes is that appeasement was a hypothesis-driven policy rather than a disposition. It was operating a test of reasonableness applied to each demand in turn, and it terminated within days of the first demand that failed the test - which is also its sharpest criticism, because the test was never applied to the pattern of the series, only to the character of each item in it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Munich as the moment Czechoslovakia was lost and Prague as an epilogue. Munich is the moment Czechoslovakia was made indefensible - 70 per cent of heavy industry, a third of the population, roughly a third of the territory and almost all the fortifications - and Prague is the moment the policy that produced Munich was disproved. Each date answers a different question, and an answer that uses both and says what each establishes will outscore one that treats them as a single episode.

*The policy has ended and the guarantee to Poland has been given. The last of the narrative lessons deals with the six months between - the negotiation that was not pursued quickly enough, the pact that nobody in London expected, and the two declarations of 3 September that end this topic and begin the next.*

### Visual: twenty years of Soviet options, narrowing to one
