*Read the left-hand column downward. ⚠️ At every stage before 1939 the USSR had at least two options and chose between them. By August 1939 it had one European partner offering an immediate agreement and one offering slow negotiations, and it took the first. ⚠️ Whether that was a betrayal, a calculation or a necessity is the subject of the debate in Lesson 21; what this lesson establishes is the sequence by which the alternatives disappeared.*

### 1. The early period: revolution, intervention and the turn to coexistence

✅ **Fact:** for the first three years after the Bolsheviks came to power in November 1917, relations between the new government and the western countries deteriorated to the point of open war, mainly because the Bolsheviks tried to spread the revolution further, especially in Germany. ✅ As early as December 1917 they began to pour floods of propaganda into Germany. ✅ Lenin called representatives of communist parties from all over the world to a conference in Moscow in March 1919, known as the Third International or Comintern, whose aim was to bring the world's communists under Russian leadership and show them how to organise strikes and uprisings; Karl Radek went secretly to Berlin to plan the revolution while other agents did the same elsewhere; and Zinoviev, the chairman of the Comintern, confidently predicted that in a year the whole of Europe would be communist.

✅ **Fact:** this did not endear the communists to the governments of Britain, France, the United States, Czechoslovakia and Japan, which tried rather half-heartedly to destroy the Bolsheviks by intervening in the civil war on the side of the Whites; and the Russians were not invited to the Versailles Conference in 1919.

✅ **Fact:** by the middle of 1920 circumstances were gradually changing: the intervening countries had admitted failure and withdrawn, communist revolutions in Germany and Hungary had failed, and Russia was too exhausted by the civil war to think about stirring up more revolutions. ✅ At the Third Comintern Congress in June 1921 Lenin acknowledged that Russia needed peaceful coexistence and co-operation in the form of trade with, and investment from, the capitalist world.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two mutually reinforcing grievances are established by 1921 and both operate for the next eighteen years. The western governments had intervened militarily against the Soviet government and had excluded it from the peace conference; the Soviet government had attempted to subvert theirs and maintained a permanent organisation for doing so. ⚠️ Neither side's suspicion was irrational, and an answer that presents Soviet distrust in 1939 as paranoia, or western distrust as prejudice, has ignored a documented history on both sides.

### 2. The German option: Rapallo and Berlin

✅ **Fact:** the USSR's relations with Germany were more consistent and more friendly than with Britain, because the Germans saw advantages in exploiting friendship with the USSR and the Bolsheviks were anxious to have stable relations with at least one capitalist power.

✅ **Fact:** a trade treaty was signed in May 1921, followed by Russian trade and mineral concessions to some German industrialists. ✅ The Rapallo Treaty of Easter Sunday 1922, signed after both had withdrawn from the Genoa conference, restored full diplomatic relations and cancelled reparations claims between the two states; both could look forward to advantages - they could co-operate to keep Poland weak, which was in both their interests; the USSR had Germany as a buffer against any future attack from the west; the Germans were allowed to build factories in Russia for the manufacture of aeroplanes and ammunition, enabling them to get round the Versailles disarmament terms, and German officers trained in Russia in the use of the new forbidden weapons; and in return the Russians would supply Germany with grain.

✅ **Fact:** the Treaty of Berlin of 1926 renewed the Rapallo agreement for a further five years; it was understood that Germany would remain neutral if Russia were attacked by another power, and that neither would use economic sanctions against the other.

✅ **Fact:** about 1930 relations began to cool as some Russians expressed concern at the growing power of Germany; the German attempt to form a customs union with Austria in 1931 was taken as an ominous sign of increasing German nationalism; and Russian concern changed to alarm at the growth of the strongly anti-communist Nazi party. ✅ Though Stalin and Litvinov tried to continue the friendship with Germany, they also began approaches to Poland, France and Britain. ✅ In January 1934 Hitler abruptly ended Germany's special relationship with the Soviets by signing a non-aggression pact with Poland.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1939 pact is often presented as an astonishing reversal. It was a reversion. ⚠️ Germany and the USSR had a functioning special relationship from 1922 to 1933, with a formal renewal in 1926, a shared interest in a weak Poland written into it, and a military dimension that evaded the Versailles disarmament terms. ⚠️ It was Hitler who ended it, in January 1934, in favour of Poland. ⚠️ Stating that sequence disposes of the idea that August 1939 was ideologically impossible, and it also explains why the British, who knew of Rapallo, were nonetheless surprised: they had come to treat the Nazi-Soviet antagonism as structural, and the evidence of 1922-33 said otherwise.

### 3. The collective-security option and why it did not hold

✅ **Fact:** the USSR entered the League in September 1934, pressed for by Barthou as part of his design for a strong anti-German group including Italy and the USSR.

✅ **Fact:** Laval signed an alliance with Russia in May 1935, though it was a weak affair with no provision for military co-operation, since Laval distrusted the communists.

✅ **Fact:** the Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement of 1935 promised mutual assistance if either were attacked, with the vital proviso that help would be given only if France assisted the country under attack.

✅ **Fact:** the Republicans in the Spanish Civil War received Soviet help, while Britain and France refused to intervene.

✅ **Fact:** neither the Czechs nor the Russians were invited to the Munich conference.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the collective-security option was therefore offered to the USSR in a systematically weakened form: membership of an organisation whose central provision had been shown to be inoperative over Manchuria; an alliance with France that contained no military clause; an obligation to Czechoslovakia that could only be triggered by a French decision; and exclusion from the conference that decided Czechoslovakia's fate. ⚠️ A candidate is not required to sympathise with Soviet policy to observe that a state which has been offered four commitments of that quality will discount the fifth.

### 4. Poland: Memel, Danzig and the guarantee

✅ **Fact:** after taking over the Lithuanian port of Memel, which was admittedly peopled largely by Germans, Hitler turned his attention to Poland.

✅ **Fact:** the Germans resented the loss of Danzig and the Polish Corridor at Versailles, and now that Czechoslovakia was safely out of the way, Polish neutrality was no longer necessary. ✅ In April 1939 Hitler demanded the return of Danzig and a road and railway across the corridor linking East Prussia with the rest of Germany. ✅ This demand was, in fact, not unreasonable, since Danzig was mainly German-speaking; but coming so soon after the seizure of Czechoslovakia, the Poles were convinced that the German demands were only the preliminary to an invasion.

✅ **Fact:** already fortified by a British promise of help "in the event of any action which clearly threatened Polish independence", the Foreign Minister, Colonel Beck, rejected the German demands and refused to attend a conference; no doubt he was afraid of another Munich. ✅ British pressure on the Poles to surrender Danzig was to no avail. ✅ Hitler was probably surprised by Beck's stubbornness, and was still hoping to remain on good terms with the Poles, at least for the time being.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "in fact, not unreasonable" is the source's own and it must be handled carefully, because it is the foundation of the recorded criticism that Chamberlain chose the wrong issue. ⚠️ The observation is about the *ethnic merits* of the Danzig claim and nothing else; it carries no implication about the method by which the claim was pursued or about what followed. ⚠️ And the Polish refusal has a stated reason that is entirely rational: after March 1939 no eastern state could regard a German territorial demand as a self-limiting claim, because the one state that had conceded such a demand six months earlier no longer existed. ⚠️ Munich therefore produced the Polish refusal that made the war, which is a genuine and uncomfortable causal chain worth stating in an answer.

### 5. The Pact, and the declarations

✅ **Fact:** the only way the British promise of help to Poland could be made effective was through an alliance with Russia. ✅ But the British were so slow and hesitant in their negotiations for an alliance that Hitler got in first and signed a non-aggression pact with the USSR.

✅ **Fact:** they also reached a secret agreement to divide Poland up between Germany and the USSR. ⚠️ **A recorded discrepancy, displayed rather than resolved:** the Poland narrative dates that secret agreement to 24 August, while the blame chapter records the non-aggression pact as signed on 23 August 1939 and states that it also included a secret agreement for Poland to be partitioned between Germany and the USSR. ⚠️ Both records are held in the same account, neither is preferred here, and the safe formulation for an answer is "the Nazi-Soviet Pact of 23 August 1939, with a secret protocol partitioning Poland".

✅ **Fact:** Hitler was convinced that with Russia neutral, Britain and France would not risk intervention; when the British ratified their guarantee to Poland, he took it as a bluff. ✅ When the Poles still refused to negotiate, a full-scale German invasion began early on 1 September 1939.

✅ **Fact:** Chamberlain had still not completely thrown off appeasement and suggested that if German troops were withdrawn a conference could be held; there was no response from the Germans. ✅ Only when pressure mounted in parliament and in the country did Chamberlain send an ultimatum: if German troops were not withdrawn from Poland, Britain would declare war. ✅ Hitler did not even bother to reply; when the ultimatum expired at 11 a.m. on 3 September, Britain was at war with Germany. ✅ Soon afterwards, France also declared war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last sequence contains the single most important fact about the end of this topic and it is recorded by a named historian: ✅ Richard Overy points out that war in 1939 was declared by Britain and France on Germany, and not the other way round. ⚠️ That does not reverse responsibility for the invasion of Poland, and nothing here suggests it does. ⚠️ What it establishes is that the war of 1939 has two decisions in it, not one - the decision to invade and the decision to declare - and that a complete answer on origins has to explain both. Lesson 21 supplies Overy's account of the second.

### 6. Comparison: three readings of the Nazi-Soviet Pact

| Reading | Case | Recorded support |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Soviet betrayal** | Stalin made war possible; he ought to have allied with the west and with Poland, thus frightening Hitler into keeping the peace | ✅ Recorded as the charge: the USSR has been accused of making war inevitable by signing the pact and the secret partition agreement |
| ⚠️ **Soviet self-defence** | The west was unwilling and slow; the pact bought time to prepare defences | ✅ Recorded: the British were most reluctant to ally with the Russians, Chamberlain distrusted them because they were communists and so did the Poles, and he thought them militarily weak; Russian historians justify the pact on the grounds that it gave the USSR time to prepare its defences against a possible German attack |
| ⚠️ **Structural outcome of exclusion** | The alternatives had been systematically weakened since 1934, and the pact was the residue | ⚠️ Inference from the recorded sequence: the missing military clause in May 1935, the French-triggered proviso, the Munich exclusion and the slow 1939 negotiations |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three are not mutually exclusive and the strongest answer holds all three in order: the alternatives were degraded by western choices, the Soviet decision was a calculation rather than an accident, and the consequence was to remove the last obstacle to a German attack on Poland. ⚠️ That formulation assigns causal weight without assigning collective blame, which is the standard this session applies throughout.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *If the British were genuinely slow and hesitant, is the charge against the USSR not simply unfair? And if the USSR was genuinely excluded, why is the pact criticised at all?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, hold both records, because both are recorded: the charge is that Stalin ought to have allied with the west and Poland and thereby frightened Hitler into keeping the peace, and the defence is that the British were most reluctant, that Chamberlain and the Poles both distrusted the Russians, that he thought them militarily weak, and that Russian historians justify the pact as buying time for defence preparation. Second, note what the pact contained beyond neutrality: a secret agreement to partition Poland between Germany and the USSR. ⚠️ A neutrality agreement is a defensive measure; a partition protocol is a territorial acquisition, and the two require different justifications. ⚠️ The time-for-defence argument covers the first and does not cover the second. Third, state the causal finding without the moral one: Hitler was convinced that with Russia neutral, Britain and France would not risk intervention, and he took the British ratification of the guarantee as a bluff - so the pact's effect on the German decision is recorded, whatever view is taken of the Soviet motive. ⚠️ That is the disciplined position: a recorded effect, two recorded interpretations of the motive, and no verdict imported from outside the evidence.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Nazi-Soviet Pact made the Second World War possible." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess Soviet foreign policy between the two World Wars.
> ⚠️ Why did the guarantee to Poland fail to deter Germany in 1939?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, use the three-reading table and reach a graded verdict. ⚠️ For the second, the narrowing-options diagram is the structure: two options at every stage until 1939, one at the end. ⚠️ For the third, the chain is short and decisive - the guarantee could only be made effective through Russia; the negotiations were slow; Hitler got in first; and with Russia neutral he read the guarantee as a bluff.

### Traps

- Presenting the Pact as ideologically unthinkable. ✅ Rapallo in 1922 and the Treaty of Berlin in 1926 established a functioning special relationship, ended by Hitler in January 1934.
- Asserting a single date for the secret protocol. ✅ The evidence base gives 23 August for the pact and 24 August at one point for the secret agreement; both are displayed here.
- Omitting the partition protocol when stating the Soviet defence. The time-for-defence argument does not cover it.
- Saying the USSR was never offered collective security. ✅ It entered the League in 1934, signed with France in May 1935 and with Czechoslovakia in 1935, and supplied the Spanish Republicans - the instruments were offered and were weak.
- Forgetting Memel, which precedes the Danzig demand.
- Saying Britain and France declared war on 1 September. ✅ The invasion was 1 September; the British ultimatum expired at 11 a.m. on 3 September; France declared soon afterwards.
- Omitting Overy's observation that Britain and France declared war on Germany and not the other way round. It is required for a complete origins answer.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Comintern founded in Moscow, March 1919; Zinoviev's prediction; Radek in Berlin; western and Japanese intervention on the White side; Russia not invited to Versailles; Lenin's turn to coexistence at the Third Comintern Congress, June 1921.
2. ✅ Rapallo, Easter Sunday 1922: relations restored, reparations claims cancelled, co-operation to keep Poland weak, a buffer against the west, German aircraft and ammunition factories and officer training in Russia, Russian grain in return.
3. ✅ Treaty of Berlin 1926 renews Rapallo for five years with German neutrality if Russia is attacked.
4. ✅ Britain: trade treaty March 1921; Genoa offence 1922; recognition 1924; Arcos raid 1927 and a break in relations; resumption 1929; cancellation 1932; Stalin nervous about Hitler by June 1933.
5. ✅ Cooling from about 1930; the 1931 customs union proposal read as ominous; January 1934 Hitler ends the special relationship with a Polish non-aggression pact.
6. ✅ September 1934 the USSR enters the League at Barthou's urging; May 1935 the Franco-Soviet treaty without a military clause; 1935 the Czechoslovak-Soviet agreement conditional on French action; Soviet help to the Spanish Republicans.
7. ✅ September 1938: neither the Czechs nor the Russians were invited to Munich.
8. ✅ April 1939: after Memel, Hitler demands Danzig and a road and railway across the corridor; the demand is recorded as in fact not unreasonable since Danzig was mainly German-speaking; Beck refuses, fortified by the British promise and afraid of another Munich.
9. ✅ The British were so slow and hesitant in negotiating an alliance with Russia that Hitler got in first; the non-aggression pact of 23 August 1939 with a secret agreement to partition Poland - ⚠️ dated 24 August at one point in the same account.
10. ✅ 1 September: full-scale invasion. Chamberlain offers a conference if troops are withdrawn; no response; the ultimatum expires at 11 a.m. on 3 September and Britain is at war; France declares soon afterwards. ✅ Overy: the war was declared by Britain and France on Germany, not the other way round.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Nazi-Soviet Pact of August 1939 is better understood as the residue of a narrowing set of options than as a sudden reversal, and state the one element of it that the standard Soviet defence does not cover.

**Model answer:** It was not a reversal because the relationship it restored had existed for eleven years and had been ended by the German side. The Rapallo Treaty of Easter Sunday 1922 restored full diplomatic relations, cancelled mutual reparations claims, gave both parties an interest in co-operating to keep Poland weak, provided the USSR with Germany as a buffer against attack from the west, and allowed German aircraft and ammunition factories in Russia and German officer training there in weapons forbidden by Versailles, with Russian grain supplied in return; the Treaty of Berlin renewed it for a further five years in 1926 with German neutrality promised if Russia were attacked. Relations cooled from about 1930 as Soviet observers took the proposed Austro-German customs union of 1931 as an ominous sign and grew alarmed at the anti-communist Nazi party, and it was Hitler who abruptly ended the special relationship in January 1934 by signing a ten-year non-aggression pact with Poland. The alternative on offer thereafter was progressively weakened: the USSR entered the League in September 1934 at Barthou's urging, but the League's central provision had already been shown inoperative over Manchuria; the French alliance of May 1935 contained no provision for military co-operation because Laval distrusted the communists; the Czechoslovak agreement of the same year gave help only if France acted first; the Russians were not invited to Munich; and in 1939 the British were so slow and hesitant in negotiating an alliance that Hitler got in first. By August 1939 one partner was offering an immediate agreement and the other slow negotiations, and the pact was the residue. The element the standard defence does not cover is the secret agreement to divide Poland between Germany and the USSR. Russian historians justify the pact on the grounds that it gave the USSR time to prepare its defences against a possible German attack, and that argument supports a neutrality agreement; it does not support a territorial partition protocol, which is an acquisition rather than a precaution.

**Misconception to avoid:** Choosing between the charge and the defence. The evidence base records both - the accusation that Stalin made war inevitable and ought to have allied with the west and Poland, and the defence that the British were most reluctant, that Chamberlain and the Poles distrusted the Russians and that he thought them militarily weak - and it resolves neither. The disciplined answer states the recorded effect, which is that Hitler was convinced Britain and France would not risk intervention once Russia was neutral, and grades the motive question as contested.

*The narrative ends on 3 September 1939. The last two lessons are judgement. The first weighs the causes of the collapse against each other without collapsing them into a single explanation; the second takes the blame debate as the scholars actually state it, tests the counterfactuals, and converts the whole session into a procedure that can be executed in an examination hall.*

## Lesson 20 - Why collective security failed: weighting the causes without collapsing them

Progress: 20/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the five candidate causes and the necessity-and-sufficiency test; the recorded statement that something more was needed; Henig's corrective and the institutional legacy; the small-state defection; the non-European dimension and the two-theatre structure; the one bounded India and empire sentence; the two-level rule for responsibility; the graded verdict and how to state it

Book context: queried - the core account's blame chapter opening with the three background factors and the statement that something more was needed; its audit of the German position at the end of 1938; Henig's corrective on the League and the institutional transmission to the United Nations; and the advanced owner's two-level responsibility rule and its India and empire linkage note
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage was carried in Lesson 8
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five candidate causes, tested rather than listed

```text
   THE TEST: a cause is NECESSARY if the outcome could not have
   occurred without it; SUFFICIENT if it alone would have produced
   the outcome. Almost nothing in history is sufficient. The useful
   work is done by asking which causes are NECESSARY and which are
   merely CONTRIBUTORY.
   ==================================================================

   CANDIDATE 1: THE PEACE SETTLEMENT
     Recorded charge: "blamed for filling the Germans with
     bitterness and the desire for revenge"
     NECESSARY? ⚠️ NO. By the end of 1938 reparations were largely
     cancelled, the disarmament clauses ignored, the Rhineland
     remilitarised, Austria united with Germany and 3.5 million
     Germans brought into the Reich. "Germany was a great power
     again. So what went wrong?"
     VERDICT: CONTRIBUTORY - supplied grievance and, more
     durably, a structure of small multinational states

   CANDIDATE 2: THE LEAGUE AND COLLECTIVE SECURITY
     Recorded charge: "criticized because they failed to secure
     general disarmament and to control potential aggressors"
     NECESSARY? ⚠️ NO - an institution that fails to prevent is not
     thereby a cause. But PERMISSIVE: each unpunished act lowered
     the expected cost of the next
     VERDICT: PERMISSIVE - the mechanism, not the motive

   CANDIDATE 3: THE WORLD ECONOMIC CRISIS
     Recorded charge: "without it, Hitler would probably never
     have been able to come to power"
     NECESSARY? ⚠️ ARGUABLY YES, for the specific outcome -
     the source's own formulation is "probably never"
     VERDICT: NECESSARY-CONDITIONAL, but supplying no direction

   CANDIDATE 4: APPEASEMENT
     Recorded charge: Bullock - "success and the absence of
     resistance tempted Hitler to reach out further, to take
     bigger risks"; after Munich he was so convinced Britain and
     France would remain passive that he gambled on Poland
     NECESSARY? ⚠️ CONTESTED - Charmley, Roberts and Self defend
     Chamberlain; the source records both sides
     VERDICT: ENABLING, with the weight contested

   CANDIDATE 5: THE AIMS OF THE GERMAN GOVERNMENT
     Recorded charge: Poland attacked ON ALL FRONTS rather than
     merely Danzig and the Corridor; Mein Kampf; the Hossbach
     Memorandum; Hitler's Secret Book; Jäckel and Kershaw
     NECESSARY? ✅ YES on the recorded balance - and the source's
     own bridging sentence governs all of the above:
     "While these factors no doubt helped to create the sort of
      atmosphere and tensions which might well lead to a war,
      SOMETHING MORE WAS NEEDED."
     VERDICT: THE NECESSARY CAUSE, with intent beyond 1938
     contested (Taylor, Broszat, Mazower)
   ==================================================================
   THE SHAPE OF A GRADED VERDICT:
   necessary cause + permissive conditions + enabling policy,
   each named, each weighted, none collapsed into the others.
```

*The visual is a method, not a conclusion, and the method is what transfers. ⚠️ A list of causes earns the marks for knowledge; a test applied to each cause earns the marks for analysis. ⚠️ And the source supplies the bridging sentence that authorises the whole structure - that the background factors created atmosphere and tensions but that something more was needed.*

### 1. The audit that constrains every answer

✅ **Fact:** the blame chapter opens by naming three candidate explanations and then sets a constraint on all of them. ✅ The Versailles Treaties have been blamed for filling the Germans with bitterness and the desire for revenge; the League of Nations and the idea of collective security have been criticised because they failed to secure general disarmament and to control potential aggressors; and the world economic crisis has been mentioned, since without it Hitler would probably never have been able to come to power. ✅ While these factors no doubt helped to create the sort of atmosphere and tensions which might well lead to a war, something more was needed.

✅ **Fact:** it then supplies the audit. By the end of 1938, most of Germany's grievances had been removed: reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses had been ignored, the Rhineland was remilitarised, Austria and Germany were united, and 3.5 million Germans had been brought into the Reich from Czechoslovakia. ✅ Germany was a great power again. ✅ So what went wrong?

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two passages together are the most powerful analytical instrument the evidence base contains, and they should be used at the start of any causation answer. ⚠️ The first rules out monocausal background explanations; the second dates the moment at which the background explanations stop being available. ⚠️ Together they force the analysis onto the period after 1938 and onto the question of intention - which is exactly where the examiner wants it.

### 2. Applying the test, cause by cause

| Cause | Necessary? | Recorded basis | How to state it in an answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| The peace settlement | ⚠️ No | ✅ The end-1938 audit removes the grievances | "It supplied the grievance and, more durably, the structure of small multinational states between two great powers - but the grievances had been satisfied before the war came" |
| The League's failure | ⚠️ No, but permissive | ✅ The recorded sequence of unpunished acts; Manchuria noted by Hitler and Mussolini | "Each unpunished act lowered the expected cost of the next; the institution supplied the mechanism, not the motive" |
| The Depression | ⚠️ Conditionally necessary | ✅ Without it Hitler would probably never have been able to come to power | "It was a condition of the outcome and supplied none of its direction" |
| Appeasement | ⚠️ Contested | ✅ Bullock on temptation and risk; ✅ Charmley, Roberts and Self in defence | "It widened the opportunity; whether it was necessary is disputed inside the scholarship and should be stated as disputed" |
| German aims | ✅ Yes, on the recorded balance | ✅ Poland attacked on all fronts; the documentary sources; Jäckel and Kershaw; ⚠️ contested by Taylor and qualified by Broszat and Mazower | "The necessary cause, with the extent of long-term planning contested" |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the basic owner supplies the rehearsable thesis and it maps exactly onto this table: Hitler's aims were the necessary cause, and the Depression, the unenforced settlement and the sequence of unpunished aggressions were the conditions that made those aims achievable. ⚠️ Learn it verbatim; it is the fastest available opening for any origins-of-war question.

### 3. The League, finally assessed: Henig's corrective and the institutional legacy

✅ **Fact:** some historians believe the League should not be dismissed as a complete failure and a total irrelevance in world history. ✅ Ruth Henig feels that it is high time these verdicts are challenged and that the League is seen for what it was - a bold step towards international co-operation which failed in some of its aims but succeeded comprehensively in others - and she published *The League of Nations* in 2010 to mark the ninetieth anniversary of its beginning.

✅ **Fact:** she argues that its creation marked an important step on the road to our contemporary global system of international organisation, co-ordinated through the United Nations, which was built on the foundations of the League's experience. ✅ Expectations of what the League might achieve were far too high and completely unrealistic: how could it possibly have been expected to deal with aggressors when it had no army of its own and no mechanism to compel member states to provide their troops?

✅ **Fact:** the institutional transmission is recorded specifically. Its great contribution was that it provided the first experimental phase, the blueprint for a second, more effective and longer-lasting form of international co-operation. ✅ The Assembly, the Council and the Secretariat were adopted as a basis by the United Nations; the UN International Court of Justice reproduced almost identically the League's Permanent Court; the International Labour Organization is still operating today; and many other UN bodies, such as the Economic and Social Council and the World Health Organization, were built on the foundations of the pioneering work carried out by the League agencies before 1939.

✅ **Fact:** Henig concludes that the creation of an international body in 1920 promoted international collaboration and compromise and was a dynamic step forward in international diplomacy, and that rather than dwelling on its weaknesses or condemning its failings we should applaud the League's successes while continuing to learn important lessons from its history.

⚠️ **Boundary note:** the design, mandate and record of the United Nations belong to their own owner. ⚠️ What this session carries is the recorded fact of institutional transmission and nothing further; no comparison of the two organisations' effectiveness is made here.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Henig corrective is the single most valuable item in a League question and it is frequently absent from scripts. It converts the answer from a verdict into an argument by supplying a named scholarly position that resists the obvious conclusion. ⚠️ The strongest use is not to endorse it but to state precisely what it does and does not establish: it establishes an institutional legacy and a misplaced expectation, and it does not establish that the League restrained any aggressor.

### 4. The small states, and why their defection was decisive

✅ **Fact:** one of the three recorded results of the Abyssinian crisis is that small states lost all faith in the League.

✅ **Fact:** the Assembly's recorded advantage was that it gave small and medium-sized states a chance to raise issues that concerned them and to have their say on world developments.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** connect those two records and an argument appears that most answers miss. Collective security is worth most to states that cannot defend themselves alone; the League's most committed constituency was therefore its smallest members; and when that constituency lost faith the organisation lost not merely prestige but its base. ⚠️ The consequences were practical and are visible in the record: Yugoslavia turned to a German trade treaty in 1936 and by 1938 was sending over 40 per cent of its exports to Germany, finding itself uncomfortably aligned with the Axis by 1939; Hungary drew closer to Germany after Hitler came to power and took South Slovakia in 1938; Poland took Teschen at the same moment. ⚠️ A collective-security system whose weakest members begin making individual arrangements with the strongest challenger has ceased to be a system, and the mechanism of that transition is worth a paragraph.

### 5. The non-European dimension and the two-theatre structure

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this session has so far treated Manchuria and the Sino-Japanese War alongside the European crises, and the reason should now be stated explicitly, because it is an argument and not an organisational convenience.

1. ✅ The first defiance of the League was in Asia, not Europe, and both European revisionist leaders took note of it.
2. ✅ The Washington system of 1921-22 left Japan supreme in the Far East with a concentrated navy, while British and American fleets were spread more widely - a structural consequence that shaped the 1930s.
3. ✅ The one power capable of effectively resisting Japan was still bent on isolation, and British and French reluctance over Manchurian sanctions was explicitly connected to the absence of American help.
4. ✅ Japan took full advantage of Europe's preoccupation with Spain to launch the full invasion of China in 1937, and the resulting Sino-Japanese War eventually became part of the Second World War.
5. ✅ The Anti-Comintern Pact of 1936 linked Berlin and Tokyo, with Rome joining in 1937.
6. ✅ British military advice was explicitly multi-theatre: Britain was not strong enough to fight more than one country at a time, and the navy would have struggled to defend the Empire and protect merchant shipping against Germany, Japan and Italy simultaneously.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the six items together establish that the interwar security problem was not a European problem with an Asian episode but a two-theatre problem in which each theatre consumed the resources needed in the other. ⚠️ Stating that is one of the few genuinely original-sounding moves available in this topic, and every element of it is sourced.

**The single bounded empire sentence.** ⚠️ The advanced owner's rule is that this subject should be connected to Indian history only by background and not by repetition, and that one sentence is enough: the Depression, the crisis of imperial credibility and the collapse of collective-security language form the wider world context of 1930s anti-colonial politics, and interwar instability weakened faith in the liberal-imperial order worldwide. ⚠️ That sentence is used here and nowhere else in this session; India's interwar politics belong to the Modern Indian History owners and are not narrated.

### 6. The two-level rule, and the attribution discipline applied

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner records the rule: a blame question must be answered on at least two levels - the immediate decision-makers and the wider structural setting.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** applied to this topic, the two levels are as follows, and keeping them separate is what allows a graded verdict without collective attribution.

| Level | Content | How to phrase it |
|---|---|---|
| **Decision** | ⚠️ The identifiable people and bodies who took the decisions: the German government's decision to invade Poland; the British and French governments' decisions at Munich and on 3 September; the Soviet government's decision of August 1939; the Japanese army's decisions of 1931 and 1937, with the prime minister recorded as overruled in 1937; the Italian government's decision of October 1935 | Name governments, cabinets, ministries and individuals |
| **Structure** | ⚠️ The unenforced settlement, the institutional design, the credit dependency, the Depression, the eastern guarantee gap, the two-theatre problem and the exclusion of the USSR | Name mechanisms, not moods |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the prohibited move is the collective one - "the Germans wanted war", "the British were cowards", "the Italians were opportunists", "the Japanese were militaristic". ⚠️ Every one of those is both an inaccuracy and a lost mark, because the record contains a British cabinet minister who resigned over Munich, a German foreign minister who won a Nobel Peace Prize for reconciliation, an Italian government that garrisoned the Brenner against Germany in 1934 and a Japanese prime minister who opposed the 1937 escalation and was overruled.

### 7. The graded verdict, and how to state it

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three verdict scaffolds are supplied by the basic owner, and they should be memorised in this form.

> ⚠️ **On the League:** it failed at enforcement while succeeding at administration - which is the precise verdict, because it shows the failure was political, not organisational.
> ⚠️ **On collective security:** it failed because the doctrine requires states to accept risk in advance and its principal members would not; the institutional weaknesses made that refusal easier and did not compel it.
> ⚠️ **On the war:** Hitler's expansionism was the proximate and necessary cause; the permissive causes were an unenforced settlement, a collapsed economy, a discredited League and the deliberate exclusion of the USSR from collective resistance.

### 8. UPSC application

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did collective security fail in the interwar period?
> ⚠️ "The League of Nations failed, but the idea of collective security did not." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the relative importance of the factors that led to the breakdown of the interwar international order.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the necessity test rather than with a list. ⚠️ Concede the background causes and then deploy the end-1938 audit to limit them. ⚠️ Name the necessary cause and keep the contested element of it explicitly contested. ⚠️ Close with one of the three verdict scaffolds verbatim.

### Traps

- Listing causes without weighting them. The demand is almost always comparative.
- Omitting the end-1938 audit, which is the evidence that limits the settlement explanation.
- Using Henig to argue that the League succeeded. ✅ Her recorded claim is that it failed in some aims and succeeded comprehensively in others, and that its legacy was institutional.
- Comparing the League with the United Nations at length. The boundary belongs to another owner; the recorded transmission is one sentence.
- Forgetting the small-state defection and its consequences in south-eastern Europe.
- Treating the Far East as an appendix. The evidence base makes it structural in six recorded ways.
- Writing collective attributions. Name governments, cabinets and individuals.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three background charges are recorded - the settlement, the League and collective security, and the world economic crisis - followed by the constraint that something more was needed.
2. ✅ The end-1938 audit: reparations largely cancelled, disarmament clauses ignored, Rhineland remilitarised, Austria united, 3.5 million Germans brought into the Reich; Germany a great power again.
3. ⚠️ The necessity test applied: the settlement contributory; the League permissive; the Depression conditionally necessary but directionless; appeasement enabling and contested; German aims the necessary cause.
4. ✅ Henig: a bold step towards international co-operation that failed in some aims and succeeded comprehensively in others; expectations far too high; no army and no compulsion mechanism.
5. ✅ The institutional transmission: Assembly, Council and Secretariat adopted as a basis; the International Court reproducing the Permanent Court almost identically; the ILO still operating; ECOSOC and the WHO built on League agency work.
6. ✅ Small states lost all faith in the League after Abyssinia; Yugoslavia's 1936 trade treaty and its 40 per cent export share by 1938; Hungary's South Slovakia gain; Poland's Teschen.
7. ⚠️ Six recorded reasons why the Far East is structural and not an appendix, including the Washington distribution, American isolation, the 1937 exploitation of European preoccupation and British multi-theatre advice.
8. ⚠️ The single bounded empire sentence: interwar instability weakened faith in the liberal-imperial order worldwide.
9. ✅ The two-level rule: immediate decision-makers and the wider structural setting, stated separately.
10. ⚠️ Three verdict scaffolds: enforcement versus administration; risk accepted in advance; necessary cause plus permissive conditions.

### Concept check

**Question:** Apply the necessity test to the claim that the failure of the League caused the Second World War, and state the formulation that survives the test.

**Model answer:** The claim fails the necessity test as stated, and it survives in a weaker and more precise form. It fails because an institution that does not prevent an outcome is not thereby a cause of it: the League's inability to restrain Japan, Italy and Germany is an absence of restraint, and an absence cannot be the source of a positive decision to invade. The recorded charge - that the League and the idea of collective security have been criticised because they failed to secure general disarmament and to control potential aggressors - is explicitly placed among the background factors that created atmosphere and tensions, and the source immediately adds that something more was needed. The end-1938 audit reinforces the limit: by then reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses had been ignored, the Rhineland was remilitarised, Austria and Germany were united and 3.5 million Germans had been brought into the Reich, so that Germany was a great power again and the institutional failures of the previous decade had already delivered everything the original grievance demanded. The formulation that survives is permissive and sequential rather than causal: each unpunished act lowered the expected cost of the next, and the record supplies the transmission directly, since both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the League's failure to curb Japanese aggression, sanctions over Abyssinia excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned within weeks of the conquest, and the reintroduction of conscription in 1935 and the remilitarisation of the Rhineland in 1936 were never even raised at the Council because France and Britain feared Hitler would reject an adverse decision. The League therefore supplied the mechanism by which revisionist intentions became achievable at low cost; it did not supply the intentions, and an answer that says so has converted a loose accusation into a defensible finding.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that weighting causes means ranking them in a list. The useful operation is classificatory rather than ordinal: separate the necessary cause from the permissive conditions and the enabling policy, name each, and say what each explains. A ranked list invites the objection that the ranking is arbitrary; a classification can be defended item by item against the evidence.

*One lesson remains. The causes have been weighted; what has not yet been done is to state the blame debate as the scholars actually state it, to test the counterfactuals that the evidence permits, and to convert twenty lessons of material into something that can be produced under time pressure in an examination hall.*

*The map is the lesson. ⚠️ Historiography in an examination answer is not a parade of names; it is a statement of where a question is unsettled and why. ⚠️ Branch C is the one to get right, because it contains a live disagreement about a specific proposition - whether the aims beyond 1938 were programmatic - and a settled agreement about a different one, which is that the government in Berlin bears the principal responsibility.*

### 1. The appeasers: the case, the defence and the third position

✅ **Fact, the case:** some historians have suggested that appeasement was largely responsible for the situation deteriorating into war. They argue that Britain and France should have taken a firm line with Hitler before Germany had become too strong: an Anglo-French attack on western Germany in 1936 at the time of the Rhineland occupation would have taught Hitler a lesson and might have toppled him from power. ✅ By giving way to him, the appeasers increased his prestige at home. ✅ As Alan Bullock wrote, "success and the absence of resistance tempted Hitler to reach out further, to take bigger risks". ✅ He may not have had definite plans for war, but after the surrender at Munich he was so convinced that Britain and France would remain passive again that he decided to gamble on war with Poland.

✅ **Fact, the wrong-issue criticism:** Chamberlain has also been criticised for choosing the wrong issue over which to make a stand. German claims for Danzig and the corridor were more reasonable than the demands for the Sudetenland, which contained almost a million non-Germans; Poland was difficult for Britain and France to defend and was militarily much weaker than Czechoslovakia; Chamberlain should therefore have made his stand at Munich and backed the Czechs, who were militarily and industrially strong and had excellent fortifications.

✅ **Fact, the defence:** Chamberlain's defenders claim that his main motive at Munich was to give Britain time to rearm for an eventual fight against Hitler, and that Munich arguably did gain a crucial year during which Britain was able to press ahead with its rearmament programme. ✅ John Charmley, in *Chamberlain and the Lost Peace* (1989), argues that Chamberlain had very little option but to act as he did, and that his policies were far more realistic than any of the possible alternatives - such as building up a Grand Alliance including Britain, France, Poland, Czechoslovakia, Romania and the USSR, an idea suggested at the time by Churchill. ✅ Andrew Roberts (2006) argues that this was never a serious possibility because of the many points of disagreement between them. ✅ Chamberlain's most recent biographer recorded here, Robert Self (2007), believes that he had very few viable alternatives and deserves great credit for trying to prevent war. ✅ The source adds the counterfactual that underlies the defence: surely any "normal" leader, like Stresemann for example, would have responded positively to Chamberlain's reasonable policies - and sadly Hitler was not the typical German statesman.

✅ **Fact, the third position:** Richard Overy, in *The Origins of the Second World War* (2nd edition, 1998), points out that it must not be forgotten that war in 1939 was declared by Britain and France on Germany, and not the other way round; that Britain and France had complex interests and motives for war and had to take decisions with one eye on public opinion and another on potential enemies elsewhere; that British and French policy before 1939 was governed primarily by national self-interest and only secondarily by moral considerations; that the British and French, just like the Germans, were anxious to preserve or extend their power and safeguard their economic interests; and that in the end this meant going to war in 1939 to preserve Franco-British power and prestige.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Overy position is the one that lifts an answer out of the prosecution-defence frame, and it should be used carefully. ⚠️ It is not a claim of moral equivalence between the governments, and nothing here suggests it is. It is a claim about the *kind* of explanation appropriate to all of them: the western declarations are decisions taken by states pursuing interests, and they require explanation on the same terms as any other state's decisions. ⚠️ An answer that explains the German decision by interest and the Anglo-French decision by principle has applied two different standards of explanation to the same event, and Overy's observation is the correction.

### 2. The USSR: the charge and the defence

✅ **Fact, the charge:** the USSR has been accused of making war inevitable by signing the non-aggression pact with Germany on 23 August 1939, which also included a secret agreement for Poland to be partitioned between Germany and the USSR. ✅ It is argued that Stalin ought to have allied with the west and with Poland, thus frightening Hitler into keeping the peace.

✅ **Fact, the defence:** on the other hand, the British were most reluctant to ally with the Russians; Chamberlain distrusted them because they were communists, and so did the Poles, and he thought they were militarily weak. ✅ Russian historians justify the pact on the grounds that it gave the USSR time to prepare its defences against a possible German attack.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the charge and the defence address different questions and this should be said explicitly. The charge is about *effect* - whether the pact removed the last deterrent - and on that the recorded evidence is favourable to the charge, since Hitler was convinced that with Russia neutral Britain and France would not risk intervention. The defence is about *motive and alternatives* - whether a western alliance was available - and on that the recorded evidence is favourable to the defence. ⚠️ A candidate who separates effect from motive can give both their due and still reach a verdict; one who does not will be forced to choose between two well-evidenced positions.

### 3. The intention debate, stated as the scholars state it

✅ **Fact, the case:** during and immediately after the war there was general agreement outside Germany that Hitler was to blame. By attacking Poland on all fronts instead of merely occupying Danzig and the Corridor, he showed that he intended not just to get back the Germans lost at Versailles but to destroy Poland. ✅ Martin Gilbert argues that his motive was to remove the stigma of defeat in the First World War: "for the only antidote to defeat in one war is victory in the next". ✅ Hugh Trevor-Roper and many other historians believe that he intended a major war right from the beginning, that he hated communism and wanted to destroy Russia and control it permanently, that Germany would thereby acquire Lebensraum but only through a major war, that the destruction of Poland was an essential preliminary to the invasion of Russia, and that the pact with Russia was simply a way of lulling Russian suspicions and keeping her neutral until Poland had been dealt with.

✅ **Fact, the documentary basis:** evidence for this theory is taken from statements in *Mein Kampf*; from the Hossbach Memorandum, a summary made by Hitler's adjutant Colonel Hossbach of a meeting held in November 1937 at which Hitler explained his expansionist plans to his generals; and from *Hitler's Secret Book*, which he finished around 1928 but never published.

✅ **Fact, the implication drawn:** if this theory is correct, appeasement cannot be blamed as a cause of war, except that it made things easier for Hitler.

✅ **Fact, the challenge:** A. J. P. Taylor, in *The Origins of the Second World War* (1961), came up with the most controversial theory about the outbreak. He believed that Hitler did not intend to cause a major war and expected at most a short war with Poland; that his aims were similar to those of previous German rulers such as Bismarck, Kaiser Wilhelm II and Stresemann, the only difference being that his methods were more ruthless; that he was a brilliant opportunist taking advantage of the mistakes of the appeasers and of events such as the Czechoslovak crisis of February 1939; that the German occupation of the rest of Czechoslovakia in March 1939 was not the result of a sinister long-term plan but "the unforeseen by-product of events in Slovakia"; and that whereas Chamberlain miscalculated in thinking he could make Hitler respectable and civilised, Hitler misread the minds of Chamberlain and the British. ✅ Taylor's summary formulation is that "the war of 1939, far from being premeditated, was a mistake, the result on both sides of diplomatic blunders".

✅ **Fact, the reception:** many people in Britain were outraged at Taylor because they thought he was trying to whitewash Hitler. ✅ The source records that this was a misreading: Taylor was not defending Hitler but the opposite, and he insisted that responsibility was not confined to Hitler alone. ⚠️ **Attribution note:** Taylor's wording on that point extends responsibility in collective national terms. ⚠️ Consistent with the discipline applied throughout this session, that wording is reported as his position and is not adopted here; this session states responsibility about governments, movements, institutions and named individuals, and does not reproduce collective formulations about any people as its own.

✅ **Fact, the current state:** most recent interpretations have tended to play down Taylor's continuity theory and to highlight the differences in aims between earlier German rulers on the one hand and Hitler and the Nazis on the other. ✅ Until 1937 Nazi foreign policy could be seen as typically conservative and nationalistic; it was only when all the wrongs of Versailles had been put right - the main aim of the conservatives and nationalists - that the crucial differences began to be revealed, and the Hossbach Memorandum shows that Hitler was preparing to go much further and embark on an ambitious expansionist policy. ✅ Neil Gregor (2003) points out that what Hitler had in mind was "a racial war of destruction quite unlike that experienced in 1914-18", beginning with the dismemberment of Poland, continuing with the attack on the USSR and culminating in a genocidal war; and that "Nazism was a destructive new force whose vision of imperial domination was radically different" from anything that had gone before.

✅ **Fact, Tooze's economic reading:** Adam Tooze, in *The Wages of Destruction: The Making and Breaking of the Nazi Economy* (2006), argues that Hitler was afraid that the longer he delayed the inevitable war, the greater the danger that Britain and France would overtake German rearmament - that he knew he would eventually have to confront the western powers and attacked Poland in 1939 because he had decided to risk the wider war sooner rather than later, since the military advantage Germany currently enjoyed was fleeting. ✅ Germany had been steadily rearming even before Hitler came to power; from 1936, when the Four Year Plan was introduced, until 1939, no less than two-thirds of all investment in industry was for producing war materials; and Richard Overy points out that in 1939 about a quarter of the industrial workforce was employed on military orders, a figure unmatched by any other state in Europe. ✅ The constraint was that the German armaments industry was running short of raw materials, mainly because a shortage of foreign exchange made it impossible to import sufficient iron and copper ore, the Reichsmark having been chronically overvalued throughout the interwar period; and in March 1939, in response to the occupation of Prague, President Roosevelt placed punitive tariffs on imports from Germany.

✅ **Fact, the conclusion recorded:** very few historians now accept Taylor's theory that Hitler had no long-term plans for war, and some recent writers believe he ignored much evidence that did not fit his theory. ✅ It is true that some of Hitler's successes came through clever opportunism, but there was much more behind it: although he probably did not have a long-term, detailed step-by-step plan worked out, he clearly had a basic vision, which he was working towards at every opportunity - a Europe dominated by Germany, achievable only by war - which is why there was so much emphasis on rearmament from 1936 onwards. ✅ Eberhard Jäckel, writing in 1984, claimed that Hitler set himself two goals, a war of conquest and the elimination of the Jews, and that the way to a greater Germany was a war of conquest fought mainly at the expense of Soviet Russia. ✅ Alan Bullock believed that he did not want a war with Britain, asking only that the British should not interfere with his expansion in Europe; Richard Overy agrees, pointing out that there is no evidence that Hitler ever thought of declaring war on Britain and France, and that he hoped to keep the war with Poland localised before turning to the destruction of the USSR - so that he was responsible for the war because he failed to realise that, as far as Britain and France were concerned, the attack on Poland was one step too far. ✅ Ian Kershaw sees no reason to change the general conclusion that Hitler must take the blame, since he had never doubted, and had said so on innumerable occasions, that Germany's future could only be determined through war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the debate has a settled core and a live margin, and separating them is the whole skill. ⚠️ The settled core is that the government in Berlin bears the principal responsibility and that a basic vision of German domination of Europe, achievable only by war, is attested. ⚠️ The live margin is whether that vision amounted to a programme executed in stages, and on that the recorded positions run from Trevor-Roper at one end through Jäckel and Kershaw, then Tooze's timing argument, then Gregor's qualitative distinction, then Broszat's and Mazower's doubts, to Taylor at the other. ⚠️ A script that states the settled core flatly and the live margin as contested has reported the historiography correctly, which is all any examiner requires.

### 4. The four counterfactuals, and what the evidence licenses

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** counterfactual reasoning is legitimate in history when it is tied to a decision that was actually available and to evidence about what would have followed. Four such counterfactuals exist in this topic, and they are not of equal quality.

| Counterfactual | Available? | Evidence for | Evidence against | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Resistance at the Rhineland, March 1936** | ✅ Yes - French mobilisation was a decision not taken | ✅ The troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition; ✅ the recorded historians' argument that an Anglo-French attack might have toppled him; ✅ the Austrian precedent of July 1934 | ✅ The French were deeply divided and ultra-cautious and received no British backing; ⚠️ "might have" is the source's own modality | ⚠️ **The best-supported counterfactual in the topic, and still short of demonstration** |
| **A stand at Munich, September 1938** | ✅ Yes - the alternative was refusal to press the Czechs | ✅ Czech fortifications, an expanded army, hopes of French and Soviet help; ✅ it would certainly not have been a walkover; ✅ the wrong-issue criticism | ✅ The military chiefs' advice; ✅ pacifist opinion; ✅ the Soviet proviso requiring French action first; ✅ Charmley, Roberts and Self | ⚠️ **Strong but contested inside the evidence base; display the disagreement** |
| **A Grand Alliance** | ⚠️ Proposed at the time by Churchill | ✅ Recorded as an alternative Chamberlain declined | ✅ Roberts: never a serious possibility because of the many points of disagreement; ✅ Charmley: Chamberlain's policies more realistic | ⚠️ **Contested; do not assert availability** |
| **An earlier Anglo-Soviet alliance, 1939** | ✅ Yes - negotiations were under way | ✅ The recorded slowness and hesitancy; ✅ the recorded judgement that the British promise to Poland could only be made effective through Russia | ✅ Chamberlain's and the Poles' distrust; ✅ his belief that the Russians were militarily weak; ⚠️ no evidence that Soviet terms were acceptable | ⚠️ **Available in principle; outcome unknowable on this evidence** |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the discipline that earns marks is to distinguish the *availability* of an alternative from the *sufficiency* of its effect. ⚠️ All four alternatives were available in the weak sense that a different decision could have been taken; none is demonstrated to have produced a different outcome, and the source's modality - "might have", "arguably" - should be reproduced rather than upgraded.

### 5. The ninety-second decision procedure

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** given a cold stem in an examination hall, the following procedure produces the correct architecture in about ninety seconds.

```text
   STEP 1 (15 sec)  WHICH OF THE THREE QUESTIONS IS THIS?
                    institution | doctrine | responsibility
                    -> if the stem says "League", it is usually
                       institution + doctrine
                    -> if it says "appeasement", it is policy
                    -> if it says "causes/responsibility/outbreak",
                       it is the graded-verdict family

   STEP 2 (20 sec)  READ THE DIRECTIVE AND MATCH THE ROW
                    Why did the League fail? -> design AND will,
                      plus real successes. NOT a list of failures.
                    Assess the Locarno phase -> genuine or
                      prosperity-conditional. NOT "the spirit
                      was good".
                    Examine appeasement -> why rational THEN and
                      why it failed. NOT hindsight condemnation.
                    Who caused the war? -> aims vs systemic and
                      Allied contributions. NOT naming Hitler and
                      stopping.
                    Collective security -> why a doctrine needing
                      prior commitment failed among states that
                      would not commit. NOT defining the term.
                    Depression and IR -> the economic-to-diplomatic
                      transmission. NOT economics without diplomacy.

   STEP 3 (20 sec)  SELECT THE THESIS TEMPLATE (verbatim)
                    League / Locarno / appeasement / origins
                    - all four are in section 6 below

   STEP 4 (20 sec)  PICK THE EVIDENCE BANK
                    A: League successes + weaknesses + the two
                       decisive crises
                    B: the six instruments of the 1920s and their
                       built-in weaknesses
                    C: appeasement's six justifications, tested
                    D: the crisis sequence 1931-39

   STEP 5 (15 sec)  DECIDE THE VERDICT BEFORE WRITING IT
                    graded, qualified, and stated in the opening
                    -> then write to it
```

### 6. The four thesis templates, to be learned verbatim

> ⚠️ **League:** "The League failed not because its design was worthless but because collective security requires states to accept risk in advance, and its principal members would not - a political failure that its institutional weaknesses merely made easier."
> ⚠️ **Locarno:** "The Locarno settlement was genuine but conditional: it rested on economic recovery and on leaving the eastern frontiers unguaranteed, and both conditions failed together after 1929."
> ⚠️ **Appeasement:** "Appeasement was a defensible response to a misdiagnosed problem: it treated Hitler as a revisionist with finite grievances rather than as an expansionist with unlimited ones."
> ⚠️ **Origins of the war:** "Hitler's aims were the necessary cause; the Depression, the unenforced settlement and the sequence of unpunished aggressions were the conditions that made those aims achievable."

### 7. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **10 (150 words)** | Thesis → two structural weaknesses **and** one genuine success → the decisive crisis → verdict |
| ⚠️ **15 (250 words)** | Thesis → aims and design → the 1920s partial recovery → the Depression as turning point → the 1930s crisis sequence → verdict |
| ⚠️ **20 (250 words)** | All of the above → **plus** appeasement evaluated on its own terms, the non-European dimension of Manchuria and Abyssinia, and the origins-of-war question → verdict |

### 8. The complete factual-risk control list

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these are the points at which a fluent answer most often becomes a wrong one. Eight are supplied by the basic owner; eleven more are added by this session from the evidence examined above.

| # | Control | Correct formulation |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Locarno's scope | Western frontiers only; the eastern frontiers were expressly not guaranteed |
| 2 | ✅ Kellogg-Briand | War renounced; **no** sanctions clause and no enforcement machinery |
| 3 | ✅ The League's establishment | 10 January 1920 and no other date |
| 4 | ✅ Technical versus enforcement success | Name both; do not merge them |
| 5 | ✅ The cause of the League's collapse | Manchuria and Abyssinia had already exposed the weakness before Hitler |
| 6 | ✅ Reparations figures | Young Plan £2000 million; the 1921 figure £6600 million; no other figure |
| 7 | ✅ The declarations of 1939 | Britain and France declared **after** the September invasion of Poland, the ultimatum expiring at 11 a.m. on 3 September |
| 8 | ✅ Quotations | Attribute only what the evidence base holds; the basic owner's control warns specifically against unsourced attributions to Chamberlain |
| 9 | ⚠️ Italy's League departure | The membership figure held here is ambiguous; assert no year |
| 10 | ⚠️ The secret protocol's date | The pact is recorded as 23 August 1939; one passage dates the secret partition agreement to 24 August; display the difference |
| 11 | ⚠️ The Hoare-Laval detail | The page is damaged in the extraction held here; use only what is legible and declare the gap |
| 12 | ⚠️ Anti-Comintern membership | Germany and Japan in 1936; Italy joined in 1937 |
| 13 | ⚠️ The Sudeten grievance | Unemployment was worse among the Germans because of their concentration in industry during the Depression, not because of governmental discrimination |
| 14 | ⚠️ Abyssinia and conscription | The invasion is October 1935 and the conscription announcement March 1935; never present the first as encouraging the second. Carry the result forwards to the Rhineland of March 1936 |
| 15 | ⚠️ The Locarno Nobel awards | Two prizes in two years - 1925 to Austen Chamberlain with Charles G. Dawes, 1926 to Briand and Stresemann. Never "jointly awarded to the three" |
| 16 | ⚠️ The Sudeten German figure | 3 million in the composition table and the summary sentence, 3.5 million in the campaign and audit passages; state a range or name the passage, never a single reconciled number |
| 17 | ⚠️ American neutrality legislation | Statutory from 31 August 1935; loans banned from February 1936; civil wars covered and a two-year non-arms cash-and-carry concession in 1937; embargo lifted and full cash-and-carry only in November 1939 |
| 18 | ⚠️ The World History question allocation | Five World-History-tagged Mains items in 2018-2025 - three in 2018-2023 and two in 2024-2025 - plus one Modern-History-tagged adjacent item in 2024. Never say six World History items |
| 19 | ⚠️ Albania, April 1939 | An occupation of a state already under Italian economic control, recorded as imitation of Prague - not a strategic acquisition, and not a cause of the September crisis |

### 9. The seven boundary handovers

| Material | Owner | One-clause handover |
|---|---|---|
| Terms of Versailles, mandates, boundary redrawal | First World War and aftermath | "the settlement, whose terms belong to the preceding period, left four unresolved problems" |
| Fascist, Nazi and Japanese-militarist regimes | Rise of fascism | "the domestic systems that generated these policies are examined elsewhere" |
| The Russian Revolution and Stalin's USSR | Russian Revolution and USSR | "Soviet internal history aside, Soviet diplomacy followed a recorded sequence" |
| Causes and instruments of the Depression | World economy since 1900 | "after the Crash, American lending stopped and short-term loans were called in" |
| The Second World War's military course | Second World War | "the war that began on 1 September 1939 belongs to the following period" |
| The United Nations | United Nations and global governance | "the League's organs supplied the institutional blueprint for its successor" |
| India's interwar politics | Modern Indian History | "interwar instability weakened faith in the liberal-imperial order worldwide" |

### 10. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** *Is naming eight or ten historians not exactly the kind of name-dropping that examiners penalise?*

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, concede the risk: a list of names attached to no proposition is decoration and is correctly penalised. Second, state the rule that avoids it - name a scholar only when the name identifies a *position that another scholar denies*, so that the name marks a live disagreement rather than an authority. ⚠️ On that rule, this topic supports at most four names in a 250-word answer: Bullock or Charmley on appeasement, Taylor on intention, Kershaw or Jäckel on the current consensus, and Henig on the League. Third, note the alternative that is always available: the positions can be stated without the names - "a substantial body of scholarship holds that ..., while others argue that ..." - and the marks are for the disagreement, not for the surname. ⚠️ The basic owner's own guidance is consistent with this: naming historians is optional enrichment rather than a requirement for full marks.

### Traps

- Presenting any one blame position as settled. ✅ The evidence base records three branches and an unresolved margin inside the third.
- Using Taylor to argue that Hitler was not responsible. ✅ The recorded reception explicitly corrects that reading, and Taylor is recorded as insisting responsibility extended further, not less far.
- Reproducing collective national formulations from any source as this session's own voice.
- Upgrading "might have" to "would have" in a counterfactual.
- Citing the Hossbach Memorandum without identifying it - Colonel Hossbach's summary of a meeting of November 1937 at which Hitler explained his expansionist plans to his generals.
- Omitting Overy's reminder that Britain and France declared war on Germany, which a complete origins answer requires.
- Listing historians without attaching each to a proposition that another historian denies.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Branch A prosecution: firmness before Germany grew strong; an Anglo-French attack at the Rhineland in 1936 might have toppled him; Bullock on success tempting bigger risks; after Munich he gambled on Poland; the wrong-issue criticism.
2. ✅ Branch A defence: Munich bought a crucial year for rearmament; Charmley 1989; Roberts 2006 on the Grand Alliance; Self 2007; the Stresemann counterfactual.
3. ✅ Overy: the war was declared by Britain and France on Germany; their policy was governed primarily by national self-interest; in the end this meant going to war to preserve Franco-British power and prestige.
4. ✅ Branch B: the charge that the pact of 23 August 1939 with its secret partition made war possible; the defence of British reluctance, distrust of communists, a belief in Soviet military weakness, and the Russian argument that the pact bought time for defence.
5. ✅ Branch C case: Poland attacked on all fronts; Gilbert on the antidote to defeat; Trevor-Roper on a major war intended from the beginning; *Mein Kampf*, the Hossbach Memorandum of November 1937 and *Hitler's Secret Book*.
6. ✅ Branch C challenge: Taylor 1961 - no major war intended, a short Polish war at most, continuity with Bismarck, Wilhelm II and Stresemann, opportunism, March 1939 as an unforeseen by-product, the war a mistake on both sides; the outrage, and the record that he was not defending Hitler.
7. ✅ Broszat 1983 and Mazower 2008 qualify the programme reading; Gregor 2003 identifies a racial war of destruction radically different from what had gone before.
8. ✅ Tooze 2006: a fleeting military advantage; the Four Year Plan of 1936; two-thirds of industrial investment in war materials; Overy on a quarter of the industrial workforce on military orders; raw-material and foreign-exchange constraints; Roosevelt's punitive tariffs of March 1939.
9. ✅ The recorded conclusion: no detailed step-by-step plan but a basic vision of a Europe dominated by Germany achievable only by war; Jäckel 1984; Bullock and Overy on the intended localisation; Kershaw on the general conclusion that Hitler must take the blame.
10. ⚠️ Four counterfactuals: the Rhineland best supported, Munich strong but contested, the Grand Alliance disputed, an earlier Soviet alliance available but with an unknowable outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem reads: "Were Hitler or the appeasers more to blame for the outbreak of the Second World War?" Produce the plan you would write in the margin, and state the single sentence with which you would open.

**Model answer:** The plan has six elements. First, decode the demand: the comparative "more" requires a graded verdict on two named candidates, so a balanced survey without a ranking fails, and so does naming Hitler and stopping. Second, set the constraint that limits the appeasement case: the background factors of the settlement, the League's failure and the Depression created atmosphere and tensions but something more was needed, and by the end of 1938 reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses ignored, the Rhineland remilitarised, Austria united with Germany and 3.5 million Germans brought into the Reich, so that Germany was a great power again. Third, state the case against the appeasers at full strength: firmness before Germany grew strong, an Anglo-French response at the Rhineland in 1936 where the troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition, Bullock's judgement that success and the absence of resistance tempted Hitler to take bigger risks, and the wrong-issue criticism that the stand should have come at Munich, where the Czechs were militarily and industrially strong and had excellent fortifications, rather than over Danzig, whose claim was in fact more reasonable. Fourth, state the defence: Munich arguably bought a crucial year for rearmament, Charmley and Self hold that Chamberlain had very few viable alternatives, Roberts holds that the Grand Alliance was never a serious possibility, and the military chiefs had advised that Britain could not fight more than one country at a time. Fifth, state the case against Hitler with its live margin: Poland was attacked on all fronts rather than merely over Danzig and the Corridor, the documentary evidence of Mein Kampf, the Hossbach Memorandum of November 1937 and Hitler's Secret Book supports a programmatic reading, Taylor's denial of that reading is recorded and is now accepted by very few historians, and the current position is that there was probably no detailed step-by-step plan but clearly a basic vision of a Europe dominated by Germany achievable only by war, with Kershaw concluding that Hitler must take the blame. Sixth, add Overy's qualification that the war was nevertheless declared by Britain and France on Germany, in pursuit of their own power and prestige. The opening sentence would be: appeasement widened the opportunity and the aims of the German government supplied the intention, and since an opportunity cannot generate a decision while an intention can exploit any opening, the greater responsibility lies with the government that decided to invade - though the policy that made the decision look safe requires its own explanation rather than a verdict of cowardice.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a comparative blame question as an invitation to distribute blame evenly. The demand is for a ranking with reasons, and the evidence base supports one: the recorded scholarship converges on the German government's responsibility while remaining genuinely divided about the appeasers' alternatives. An answer that declines to rank has not answered the question; an answer that ranks without conceding the strongest version of the other case has not earned the ranking.

*The teaching is complete. What follows is the apparatus: the verified-question position, cumulative checks across the lesson blocks, original practice at 10, 15 and 20 marks with model answers, a remediation set built from the errors this topic actually produces, the master maps, the consolidated register notes, the coverage matrix and the source ledger.*
