# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - The inheritance and the institution, Lessons 1 to 5

### Cumulative check A1 - from an inheritance to an institution

**Question:** Assemble the problem the League was built to manage, and then state precisely why a complete description of the institution's machinery still does not explain what happened to it.

**Model answer:** The problem had four parts. Reparations were unfixed until 1921 and were then set at £6600 million over sixty-six annual instalments, explicitly in order to keep Germany economically weak for that period. Security was promised and withdrawn: France accepted an army limit of 100 000 men, a Rhineland demilitarised fifty kilometres east of the river and the use of the Saar for fifteen years in exchange for a British and American promise of help if Germany attacked again, and in March 1920 the United States Senate rejected the entire settlement and the League, whereupon Britain used the American withdrawal as an excuse to cancel its own promise. Revisionist dissatisfaction was widely distributed - Turkey prepared to defy the settlement, Italy resentful and seizing Fiume before bombarding Corfu, Germany falling behind on payments, and Soviet Russia excluded from Versailles and intervened against in its civil war. And a belt of successor states was created - Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Austria, Hungary and Poland - in which no frontier could be drawn without leaving a minority behind, only one of which sustained democracy for a significant length of time, and which divided at once between satisfied and revisionist members. The machinery built to manage this is fully describable: an Assembly of all members meeting annually with one vote each and unanimous decisions; a Council meeting at least three times a year with four permanent members and four elected members rising to nine by 1926, also unanimous; a Permanent Court of fifteen judges at the Hague, functioning from 1922 and concluding sixty-six cases by 1939; a Secretariat drawn from over thirty countries; and commissions for mandates, military affairs, minorities and disarmament. That description does not explain the outcome because the Covenant created an expectation rather than an obligation: the Council could only recommend what forces members should contribute, Article 16 expected troops without compelling them, and a resolution of 1923 left each member to decide for itself whether to fight - a decision taken by the members, not by the drafters. What happened to the League was therefore a political outcome produced inside an institutional frame, and the source says so, recording that none of the weaknesses necessarily doomed it provided members were prepared to refrain from aggression and accept its decisions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a fuller account of the Covenant's defects is a better explanation of the collapse. Beyond a certain point additional structural detail reduces explanatory power, because every extra design flaw makes it harder to explain the years between 1925 and 1930 in which, on the record, events ran fairly smoothly.

### Cumulative check A2 - the two ledgers

**Question:** Explain why the League's record has to be assessed on two separate ledgers, and state what each ledger establishes that the other cannot.

**Model answer:** The two ledgers correspond to the League's two stated aims, which were institutionally separable: maintaining peace through collective security, and encouraging international co-operation to solve economic and social problems. The co-operation ledger is in credit and its entries are specific - the International Labour Organization under Albert Thomas pressing governments on maximum hours, minimum wages, sickness and unemployment benefit and old-age pensions; the Refugee Organization under Fridtjof Nansen returning about half a million former prisoners of war from Russia and helping those fleeing Nazi persecution after 1933; the Health Organization's campaign against a typhus epidemic in Russia that had threatened to spread across Europe; the Mandates Commission; the efficient administration of the Saar, concluded by the 1935 plebiscite; and arbitrated settlements over the Aaland Islands in 1920, Upper Silesia in 1921, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier and Mosul. The security ledger is in deficit and its entries are equally specific - two overrulings by the Conference of Ambassadors at Vilna in 1920 and over Corfu in 1923 with no League response; sanctions never even raised over Manchuria; sanctions over Abyssinia that excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned within weeks of the conquest; and two open breaches of Versailles in 1935 and 1936 that were never brought to the Council at all. What the first ledger establishes is capability: the League could deliver programmes, govern a territory and arbitrate disputes, so its security failure cannot be attributed to institutional incompetence. What the second establishes is the limit of that capability, and it is a limit defined by the parties rather than by the task, since the source records that none of the successful political cases seriously threatened world peace and none of the decisions went against a major state that might have challenged the verdict. Neither ledger alone supports a verdict: the first without the second produces an apologia, the second without the first produces a caricature, and the graded finding - failure at enforcement, success at administration - requires both.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the co-operation ledger as a consolation prize. It is the evidential basis of the institutional legacy that Henig records - the Assembly, Council and Secretariat adopted as a basis by the United Nations, the International Court reproducing the Permanent Court almost identically, the ILO still operating, and later bodies built on the League agencies' work - and it is also the premise that forces the security analysis onto political will.

## Block B - Reconstruction and its conditions, Lessons 6 to 11

### Cumulative check B1 - the two conditions of the 1920s

**Question:** Identify the two conditions on which the reconstruction of 1924-29 depended, show that they were independent of each other, and explain why their simultaneous failure made the collapse so abrupt.

**Model answer:** The first condition was financial. The Dawes Plan of 1924 made no reduction in the total Germany owed but limited annual payments to what could reasonably be afforded and supplied a foreign loan of 800 million gold marks, mostly from the United States, in exchange for which France withdrew from the Ruhr; the German economy then recovered on the basis of American loans inside a circular arrangement in which America lent to Germany, Germany paid reparations to France and Britain, and they repaid almost twelve billion dollars of war debts to America, while American tariffs remained high. The second condition was geographical and diplomatic. Locarno in 1925 guaranteed the Franco-German and Belgo-German frontiers with Britain and Italy as guarantors while providing only arbitration in the east, Germany expressly declining to guarantee its frontiers with Poland and Czechoslovakia and Britain giving no eastern guarantee either; the settlement therefore purchased western reconciliation by leaving the eastern question open, and Austen Chamberlain's remark that no British government would risk the bones of a single British grenadier in defence of the Polish Corridor made the limit public. The two conditions were independent: the eastern gap was a drafting decision of 1925 that would have existed in any economic weather, and the credit dependency was a financial structure that would have existed whatever the frontier guarantees said. Their failure was nonetheless simultaneous in effect, because October 1929 removed both supports of the arrangement within a single month - Stresemann died, reportedly from overwork at the age of only fifty-one, removing one of the outstanding men of Locarno, and the Wall Street Crash began the contraction that by 1932 had left over six million unemployed in Germany. The collapse was abrupt because the reconstruction had never resolved the eastern question but had merely made it invisible while prosperity lasted, and the source records the judgement directly: the Locarno spirit proved an illusion because so much depended on economic prosperity, and when this evaporated all the old hostilities and suspicions surfaced again.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the Depression caused the eastern crisis. The eastern guarantee gap was designed into Locarno in 1925, publicly explained, and read correctly in Berlin at the time; the Depression determined when it would be tested and by which kind of government, not that it existed.

### Cumulative check B2 - what the 1920s settled and what it did not

**Question:** Show that the interwar order solved the financial question and failed on the security question, and explain why that finding damages the most popular explanation of the Second World War.

**Model answer:** The financial question was settled by a sequence that ran in one direction only. Coercion was tried first and failed: French and Belgian troops occupied the Ruhr in January 1923, met passive resistance, strikes and sabotage in which over a hundred people died, seized goods worth about £40 million, and produced galloping inflation and a mark that was completely valueless by November 1923, while Britain strongly disapproved and sympathised with Germany. Rescheduling followed in 1924 with the Dawes Plan; reduction followed in 1929 with the Young Plan, which cut reparations from £6600 million to £2000 million on a graded scale over fifty-nine years - the figure Keynes had urged at Versailles, whose acceptance ten years later was an admission of error by the Allies; and cancellation followed at Lausanne in 1932, when Britain and France released Germany from most of the remaining payments. The security question was never settled at all. The Anglo-American guarantee to France lapsed in March 1920; the Geneva Protocol of 1924, which would have pledged members to arbitration and to helping any victim of unprovoked aggression, was rejected by the British government that succeeded its author; Locarno guaranteed the west and left the east to arbitration; Kellogg-Briand renounced war among sixty-five states without a sanctions clause; and the World Disarmament Conference of 1932-33 collapsed because no guarantor existed for the state that would have to disarm first. The finding damages the popular explanation because that explanation rests on reparations and harshness, and the record shows the reparations question being conceded continuously from 1924 to 1932 while the enforcement question was never answered. The source's own audit completes the demolition: by the end of 1938 reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses had been ignored, the Rhineland was remilitarised, Austria and Germany were united and 3.5 million Germans had been brought into the Reich, so that Germany was a great power again - and the war came afterwards.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring from this that the settlement is irrelevant to 1939. Its durable contribution is structural rather than financial: a belt of small multinational successor states between a revisionist great power and a Soviet Russia excluded from the system, with a France whose promised guarantee had lapsed. That argument survives the audit; the harshness argument does not.

## Block C - The tests, Lessons 12 to 15

### Cumulative check C1 - what each crisis proved

**Question:** Explain what Manchuria, Abyssinia and Spain each proved about the international order, and show why the three findings are not interchangeable.

**Model answer:** Manchuria proved that the members would not begin. Japan, a permanent member of the Council, invaded a province of thirty million people in which it had been involved since the 1890s; China appealed; the League condemned and ordered withdrawal; the Lytton Commission reported in 1932 that there were faults on both sides and proposed League government of the province; Japan rejected this and withdrew from the League in March 1933; and the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, because Britain and France had serious economic problems and feared a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help - the United States having returned to a policy of strict isolation. The finding is about initiation, and the recorded damage is graded: prestige damaged, though not yet fatally. Abyssinia proved that the instrument fails when used at partial strength. Sanctions were introduced but not applied to exports of oil, coal and steel; the conquest was completed by May 1936 without too much inconvenience; sanctions were abandoned a few weeks later; and the three recorded results were that Mussolini was annoyed by the sanctions anyway and began to draw closer to Hitler, that small states lost all faith in the League, and that Hitler was encouraged to breach Versailles - with the rider that neither the conscription announcement nor the Rhineland was raised at the Council, and with the chronological caution that conscription in March 1935 predates the October invasion and can only be carried forward to the Rhineland of March 1936. The finding is about instruments, and the recorded damage is terminal: after 1935 the League was never taken seriously again. Spain proved that abstention transfers advantage. Germany and Italy sent decisive help to Franco while Britain and France refused to intervene and the Republicans received Soviet help; Germans and Italians gained military experience; the Condor Legion bombed Guernica; and the horrors of the war became the first recorded justification for British appeasement, with the great fear being the bombing of defenceless cities, while Japan took full advantage of Europe's preoccupation to invade northern China in 1937. The finding is about the effects of non-participation, and it operates outside the League entirely. The three are not interchangeable because they answer different questions - will, instrument and abstention - and an answer that uses all three, saying what each establishes, is doing something a list of crises cannot.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the three as a crescendo of the same failure. The evidence base grades them differently and for different reasons, and the grading is the analysis: the first shows that the procedure would not be started, the second shows that the procedure half-started is worse than useless, and the third shows that by 1936 the procedure was no longer being used at all.

### Cumulative check C2 - the demonstration effect, traced

**Question:** Trace the recorded chain by which each unpunished act lowered the cost of the next, using only links the evidence base states rather than links you infer.

**Model answer:** The chain has seven recorded links. First, both Hitler and Mussolini took note of the failure of the League of Nations to curb Japanese aggression in Manchuria - a link stated directly in the summary of the 1933-39 period. Second, the Stresa Front of April 1935 condemned German conscription but took no action, the League was helpless, and the Front collapsed anyway when Britain signed the Anglo-German Naval Agreement in June without consulting France and Italy, which disgusted both and destroyed the trust between the three. Third, the Anglo-French treatment of the Abyssinian affair led Mussolini, annoyed by the sanctions anyway, to draw closer to Hitler and to drop his objections to a German takeover of Austria - the same Mussolini who in July 1934 had moved three divisions to the Austrian frontier and caused the Austrian Nazi attempt to be called off. Fourth, Hitler took advantage of the general preoccupation with Abyssinia to send troops into the Rhineland in March 1936, where the troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition and no resistance was offered except the usual protests. Fifth, neither the reintroduction of conscription nor the Rhineland occupation was raised at the League Council, because France and Britain feared Hitler would reject any adverse decision and were reluctant to be forced into military action. Sixth, the Anschluss of March 1938 revealed the weakness of Britain and France, who again only protested, and Britain's lack of action encouraged Hitler to make demands on Czechoslovakia. Seventh, after the surrender at Munich he was so convinced that Britain and France would remain passive again that he decided to gamble on war with Poland - the formulation being Alan Bullock's, that success and the absence of resistance tempted him to reach out further and take bigger risks. Every link in that chain is stated in the evidence base rather than supplied by the reader, which is what makes it usable in an answer.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting the chain into a claim of inevitability. It is a claim about declining expected costs, not about determined outcomes, and the same evidence base records one occasion - July 1934 - on which resistance appeared and the operation was abandoned, which is precisely what a determinist reading cannot accommodate.

## Block D - Policy, judgement and the road to war, Lessons 16 to 21

### Cumulative check D1 - responsibility on two levels

**Question:** Answer the question "who was responsible for the outbreak of war in 1939?" on the two levels the evidence base requires, keeping the levels separate and avoiding collective attribution.

**Model answer:** At the level of decision, responsibility is distributed among identifiable governments. The German government decided to invade Poland on all fronts on 1 September 1939 rather than confining itself to Danzig and the Corridor, having demanded their return in April and having concluded a non-aggression pact with the USSR on 23 August with a secret agreement to partition Poland; the recorded conclusion of the scholarship, from Jäckel to Kershaw, is that Hitler must take the blame, with Bullock and Overy adding that he probably intended to localise the Polish war and failed to realise that for Britain and France it was one step too far. The British and French governments decided at Munich on 29 September 1938 to press the Czechs into surrendering the Sudetenland, excluding both the Czechs and the Russians from the conference and informing Prague that resistance would receive no help even though France had guaranteed the Czech frontiers at Locarno; they then decided on 3 September 1939 to declare war, and Overy records that the declaration came from them and not from Germany, and that their policy was governed primarily by national self-interest. The Soviet government decided in August 1939 to accept the German offer rather than continue negotiations the British were pursuing slowly and hesitantly, and Russian historians justify the decision as buying time for defence, a justification that covers the neutrality clause and not the partition protocol. The Italian and Japanese governments took the earlier decisions - Abyssinia in October 1935 and Manchuria in 1931 - that removed the deterrent. At the level of structure, the setting consisted of an unenforced settlement that left a belt of small multinational states between two great powers; an institution whose central provision required unanimity and left each member to decide for itself whether to fight; an economic collapse that brought extreme right-wing governments to power in Japan and Germany and removed the fiscal capacity for enforcement; a guarantee system whose eastern half was left unguaranteed at Locarno and whose 1935 instruments were routed through a single French decision; and a two-theatre problem in which the one power capable of resisting Japan was committed to isolation. Neither level is sufficient alone: the structural setting explains why the decisions were cheap, and the decisions explain why the setting produced war in 1939 rather than in 1936 or not at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Substituting national attributions for institutional ones. The record contains a British cabinet minister who resigned over Munich, a German foreign minister who shared a Nobel Peace Prize for reconciliation, an Italian government that garrisoned the Brenner against Germany in 1934 and a Japanese prime minister who opposed the 1937 escalation and was overruled by his war minister. Responsibility belongs to governments, cabinets, ministries, parties and named individuals, and stating it that way is more accurate as well as more disciplined.

### Cumulative check D2 - the six demand families, assembled

**Question:** For each of the six demand families this topic must support, state the thesis in one sentence and name the single most decisive piece of evidence.

**Model answer:** On the League's failure, the thesis is that it failed at enforcement while succeeding at administration, and that the enforcement failure was political rather than architectural; the decisive evidence is that over Manchuria the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, since sanctions required no army and were one of the two instruments the Covenant expressly named. On the Locarno phase, the thesis is that the settlement was genuine but conditional, resting on economic recovery and on leaving the eastern frontiers unguaranteed; the decisive evidence is that Germany signed arbitration agreements with Poland and Czechoslovakia but expressly would not guarantee those frontiers, and that Britain gave no guarantee either, so that it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe. On appeasement, the thesis is that it was a defensible response to a misdiagnosed problem, treating Hitler as a revisionist with finite grievances rather than an expansionist with unlimited ones; the decisive evidence is the March 1939 falsification, when non-German territory was seized and for the first time even the appeasers were unable to justify what had been done, whereupon Chamberlain's attitude hardened. On the origins of the war, the thesis is that the German government's aims were the necessary cause while the Depression, the unenforced settlement and the sequence of unpunished aggressions were the conditions that made those aims achievable; the decisive evidence is the source's own audit that by the end of 1938 the grievances had been removed and Germany was a great power again, followed by the observation that something more was needed. On collective security, the thesis is that a doctrine requiring states to accept risk in advance failed among members who would not accept it; the decisive evidence is the resolution of 1923 under which each member would decide for itself whether or not to fight in a crisis, which the source says made nonsense of the idea. On the Depression and international relations, the thesis is that economic contraction removed enforcement capacity and supplied revisionist motive without supplying direction; the decisive evidence is the recorded reason no sanctions were raised over Manchuria - that Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to risk a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help.

**Misconception to avoid:** Preparing six answers rather than six structures. This topic owns no verified question, so no specific wording can be anticipated; what can be anticipated is the directive, and the six rows above exhaust what the owners record as realistically askable.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ **Status of these items.** The seven questions below are **original to this session**. They are not examination questions, are not represented as such, and are constructed to cover the six demand families the owners record. Each model answer is written to the stated word limit and carries a scoring note identifying the one move that earns the marks.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ "The League of Nations failed as a guarantor of peace but succeeded as an experiment in international administration." Examine.

### Model answer

The proposition maps onto the League's two stated aims and is therefore a finding rather than a compromise. As administration it delivered: the International Labour Organization under Albert Thomas pressed governments on hours, wages and benefits; Nansen's Refugee Organization returned about half a million prisoners of war from Russia; the Health Organization contained a typhus epidemic there; the Saar was administered and transferred by plebiscite in 1935; and the Permanent Court concluded sixty-six cases by 1939. As guarantor it failed, politically. The Covenant required unanimity in both political organs, possessed no force, and was neutered by the 1923 resolution leaving each member to decide whether to fight. Over Manchuria sanctions were never even raised; over Abyssinia they excluded oil, coal and steel. Ruth Henig's corrective is decisive but limited: the League supplied the United Nations' institutional blueprint. It failed at enforcement because its members would not accept risk in advance.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in mapping the two verdicts onto the organisation's own two stated aims, which converts a balanced survey into a finding.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the Locarno Treaties of 1925 for European security.

### Model answer

Locarno's significance lies in what it graded rather than in what it guaranteed. Germany, France and Belgium promised to respect their joint frontiers, with Britain and Italy undertaking to assist whichever was attacked. In the east Germany signed arbitration agreements with Poland and Czechoslovakia but expressly refused to guarantee those frontiers, and Britain gave no guarantee either; France alone promised help if Germany attacked them. The immediate effects were substantial: the Locarno honeymoon, Germany's entry into the League in 1926 with a permanent Council seat, Nobel Peace Prizes in 1925 and 1926 for Locarno, and agreement in September 1926 on withdrawing French troops from the Rhineland. The recorded criticism is that the omission covered the very areas where trouble was most likely, and Austen Chamberlain's remark about the Polish Corridor made the limit public. Locarno reduced tension in western Europe while publishing the map of where British commitment ended.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in the signalling argument - that a publicly graded guarantee tells a revisionist where revision is cheapest - rather than in narrating the honeymoon.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "Appeasement was rational in motive and disastrous in effect." Critically examine.

### Model answer

The proposition is sustainable, provided "rational" is read as conditional on a premise that proved false. Appeasement was the British, later French, policy of avoiding war with aggressive powers by giving way to their demands provided they were not too unreasonable, and ran in two phases: drift from the mid-1920s, accepting Manchuria, Abyssinia, German rearmament and the Rhineland; then, from May 1937, Chamberlain's initiative to discover what Hitler wanted and show that reasonable claims could be met by negotiation.

The motives were serious. War was expected to be more devastating than ever, as the Spanish horrors demonstrated, the great fear being the bombing of defenceless cities; opinion was strongly pacifist, indicated by the Oxford Union vote of February 1933 and Baldwin's 1935 victory after pledging no great armaments; many believed Germany and Italy had genuine grievances; the League had proved helpless; fear of communism was widespread; and the military chiefs advised that Britain could not fight more than one country at a time.

The effects were disastrous because the policy tested each demand for reasonableness and never tested the series for finiteness. Bullock's judgement is that success and the absence of resistance tempted Hitler to take bigger risks, and after Munich he gambled on Poland. The one recorded case of resistance - Mussolini's divisions at the Austrian frontier in July 1934 - produced immediate abandonment. Appeasement's error was diagnostic, and it was falsified at Prague in March 1939, when even the appeasers could not justify the seizure of non-German territory.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in the diagnostic formulation plus the falsification date, which together show that the policy responded to evidence and identify exactly which evidence it ignored.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ How far did the Great Depression transform international relations in the 1930s?

### Model answer

It transformed capacity, not direction. The mechanism belongs to economic history: after the Crash, American lending stopped and short-term loans already made to Germany were called in, producing a currency crisis and a run on the banks. Its diplomatic effects run in three channels.

Enforcement capacity collapsed first. Over Manchuria in 1931 the question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised, because Britain and France had serious economic problems and were reluctant to risk a war they were ill-equipped to win, especially without American help - the United States having returned to strict isolation.

Regime composition changed next. The crisis brought unemployment and falling living standards to most countries and caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany, where over six million were unemployed by 1932; with Mussolini they refused to keep to the rules. Italy's Abyssinian motives included diverting attention from internal troubles and finding markets.

Small-state alignment shifted third. Yugoslavia signed a trade treaty with Germany in 1936, and by 1938 Germany was taking over 40 per cent of Yugoslav exports; meanwhile neighbouring states raised import duties, demand for Czech manufactures fell, and unemployment struck hardest where the Sudeten Germans lived.

The limits are clear. Japanese interest in Manchuria dated from the 1890s; German revisionism appears in Stresemann's private aims by 1925 and in the Nazi campaign against the Young Plan in 1929; and the eastern guarantee gap was written into Locarno. The Depression explains timing and intensity, not direction.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in the closing paragraph of limits, which converts a description of effects into a claim about causal type.

## Question 5 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "Collective security did not fail; it was never attempted." Discuss with reference to the interwar period.

### Model answer

The proposition is overstated but points at the right evidence. Collective security required members to act together to restrain an aggressor by economic or military sanctions - that is, to accept a definite cost in advance against an indefinite future benefit. The Covenant asked for this; it could not compel it. Unanimity governed both the Assembly and the Council, there was no League force, Article 16 merely expected troops, and a resolution of 1923 left each member to decide for itself whether to fight, which made nonsense of the idea. Amendment required unanimity, so the Geneva Protocol of 1924, pledging members to arbitration and assistance, had to be attempted and was rejected by the British government that followed its author, unwilling to commit Britain and the Empire to all the 1919 frontiers.

The record of attempts supports the proposition in Asia and qualifies it in Africa. Over Manchuria sanctions were never even raised. Over Abyssinia they were introduced but excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned weeks after the conquest, and Mussolini was annoyed anyway and drew closer to Hitler. German conscription in 1935 and the Rhineland in 1936 were never brought to the Council at all.

The correction is therefore that collective security was attempted once, at partial strength, and proved worse than either alternative. The precise finding is that it was never attempted at full strength by members who would not accept risk in advance - a political failure that the Covenant's design made easier.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in refusing the proposition's absolutism while conceding its evidential core, and in the Abyssinian finding that partial enforcement is worse than either extreme.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Assess the relative responsibility of the German government, the appeasing powers and the Soviet Union for the outbreak of war in 1939.

### Model answer

Responsibility is graded and must be stated on two levels. Three background factors - the settlement, the League's failure and the world economic crisis - created the atmosphere in which war became possible, but something more was needed, and by the end of 1938 reparations were largely cancelled, the disarmament clauses ignored, the Rhineland remilitarised, Austria united with Germany and 3.5 million Germans brought into the Reich. Germany was a great power again, and war came afterwards.

The German government carries the principal responsibility. Poland was attacked on all fronts rather than over Danzig and the Corridor alone, indicating destruction rather than revision; Mein Kampf, the Hossbach Memorandum of November 1937 and Hitler's Secret Book support a programmatic reading, and the recorded consensus from Jäckel to Kershaw is that Hitler must take the blame, although Taylor's denial of long-term planning, with Broszat's and Mazower's qualifications, keeps its extent contested.

The appeasing powers widened the opportunity. Bullock records that success and the absence of resistance tempted Hitler to take bigger risks, and that after Munich he gambled on Poland; the counter-case from Charmley, Roberts and Self is that few viable alternatives existed and Munich bought a year for rearmament.

The Soviet government removed the last deterrent by the pact of 23 August 1939 with its secret partition protocol; the defence that it bought time covers neutrality and not partition, while British slowness and distrust are equally recorded. Overy's reminder stands: Britain and France declared war, pursuing their own power and prestige.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in the ordering - necessary cause, enabling policy, permissive decision - with the contested margin of the first left explicitly contested.

## Question 7 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "The interwar order failed in Asia before it failed in Europe." Critically examine.

### Model answer

The proposition is chronologically correct and structurally more important than it looks. The Washington Conferences of 1921-22 limited the Japanese navy to three-fifths of the American and British navies, returned Kiaochow and Shantung, confirmed Japan's Pacific mandates, barred new bases within striking distance of Japan and guaranteed Chinese neutrality. Regarded as a success, the settlement left Japan supreme in the Far East with the world's third largest navy concentrated in one ocean, while larger British and American fleets were spread more widely, and the guarantee carried no enforcement.

The first defiance followed there. Japan invaded Manchuria in 1931, rejected the Lytton proposal and withdrew from the League in March 1933, while sanctions were never even raised because Britain and France had economic problems and feared war without American help. Both Hitler and Mussolini took note.

The two theatres then interacted. Japan exploited Europe's preoccupation with Spain to invade northern China in July 1937; the Anti-Comintern Pact of 1936 linked Berlin and Tokyo, with Rome joining in 1937; and British military advice was explicitly multi-theatre, holding that Britain could not fight more than one country at a time and that the navy could not simultaneously defend the Empire and protect shipping against three powers.

The qualification is that the decisive European weaknesses were independent of Asia: the eastern guarantee gap dates from Locarno in 1925 and the enforcement defect from the Covenant and the 1923 resolution. Asia supplied the first demonstration and a permanent second front, not the European fault lines.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the marks are in the interaction argument - that each theatre consumed the resources needed in the other - followed by an honest qualification that prevents over-claiming.

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

⚠️ The fifty errors below are the ones this topic reliably produces. Each is paired with the correction and the lesson that supplies it.

| # | Error | Correction | Lesson |
|---:|---|---|---:|
| 1 | "The League was founded in 1919" | ✅ It formally came into existence on 10 January 1920, the day the Versailles Treaty came into operation | 3 |
| 2 | "The League was Wilson's creation" | ✅ Collective authorship - Cecil, Smuts, Bourgeois, Hymans and Wilson; his contribution was insisting the Covenant be written into each treaty | 3 |
| 3 | "The Council had five permanent members" | ✅ Four - Britain, France, Italy and Japan; the USA was intended as a fifth and never took its seat | 3 |
| 4 | "Decisions were taken by majority" | ✅ Unanimity in both the Assembly and the Council | 3 |
| 5 | "The Covenant had no provision for sanctions" | ✅ It provided for economic or military sanctions; the defects were unanimity, the absence of a force and the 1923 opt-out | 3, 5 |
| 6 | "Article 16 obliged members to send troops" | ✅ It *expected* them to; the 1923 resolution left each member to decide for itself | 5 |
| 7 | "The League had no successes" | ✅ ILO, Nansen, typhus, the Saar, Aaland, Upper Silesia, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier, Mosul, and sixty-six Court cases | 4 |
| 8 | "The League's successes prove it worked" | ✅ None of those disputes threatened world peace and none went against a major state | 4 |
| 9 | "The Disarmament Commission was a success" | ✅ It made no progress, and its failure is recorded inside the successes section | 4 |
| 10 | "Corfu was a 1930s crisis" | ✅ 1923, and the Conference of Ambassadors, not the League, ordered Greece to pay | 4 |
| 11 | "The League ignored Vilna" | ✅ It supported Lithuania's claim and then allowed the Ambassadors' award to stand | 4 |
| 12 | "Nobody foresaw the League's weakness" | ✅ Balfour stated it publicly in 1923 | 4 |
| 13 | "The Geneva Protocol was blocked by France" | ✅ It was proposed by MacDonald and rejected by the succeeding British government | 5 |
| 14 | "Britain rejected the Protocol out of cowardice" | ✅ The recorded reason was unwillingness to commit Britain and the Empire to all the 1919 frontiers | 5 |
| 15 | "Versailles crippled Germany until 1939" | ✅ Rescheduled 1924, cut 1929, largely cancelled at Lausanne 1932 | 2, 9 |
| 16 | "Reparations were £2000 million from the start" | ✅ £6600 million fixed in 1921; £2000 million from the Young Plan of 1929 | 2, 9 |
| 17 | "France occupied the Ruhr alone" | ✅ French **and Belgian** troops, January 1923 | 6 |
| 18 | "The Ruhr occupation succeeded" | ✅ Goods worth about £40 million seized; galloping inflation; the mark valueless by November 1923 | 6 |
| 19 | "French policy was consistently vindictive" | ✅ Three methods tried - weakness, alliances, reconciliation; Briand was Foreign Minister in eleven successive governments from 1925 to 1932 | 6 |
| 20 | "German rearmament began in 1935" | ✅ Rapallo 1922 permitted German aircraft and ammunition factories and officer training in Russia | 6, 10 |
| 21 | "Genoa 1922 was a minor conference" | ✅ Its failure produced Rapallo, which is where the Russo-German relationship and the shared interest in a weak Poland begin | 6, 19 |
| 22 | "The Dawes Plan reduced reparations" | ✅ No reduction in the total; annual payment by capacity; a loan of 800 million gold marks; French withdrawal from the Ruhr | 7 |
| 23 | "America was isolationist throughout" | ✅ Politically yes at every decision point; economically no - Harding convened Washington, Americans chaired both reparations committees, and the circular flow is recorded | 7 |
| 24 | "American war loans are an inference" | ✅ Almost twelve billion dollars, with Harding and Coolidge insisting on repayment | 7 |
| 25 | "Locarno guaranteed all German frontiers" | ✅ Western only; Germany expressly refused to guarantee the eastern frontiers, and Britain gave no eastern guarantee | 8 |
| 26 | "Germany gave nothing in the east" | ✅ It signed arbitration agreements; it declined to guarantee the frontiers | 8 |
| 27 | "Germany joined the League in 1925" | ✅ 1926, with a seat on the Permanent Council | 8 |
| 28 | "Stresemann was a straightforward European" | ✅ Sincere in Taylor's account, and privately determined on redrawing the Polish frontier and union with Austria | 8 |
| 29 | "Kellogg-Briand outlawed war effectively" | ✅ Sixty-five signatories, no sanctions clause; Japan signed and attacked China three years later | 9 |
| 30 | "The Young Plan cancelled reparations" | ✅ It cut them to £2000 million over fifty-nine years; Lausanne 1932 cancelled most of the remainder | 9 |
| 31 | "Germany welcomed the Young Plan" | ✅ Welcomed by many, but the Nazi party campaigned against it as offering far too little | 9 |
| 32 | "The Locarno spirit ended with Hitler" | ✅ Stresemann died and the Crash occurred in October 1929; Hitler became Chancellor in January 1933 | 9 |
| 33 | "Nobody disarmed in the interwar period" | ✅ Only Germany had made moves towards disarmament, as Stresemann regularly pointed out, while world arms spending rose from $3.5 billion to around $5 billion | 10 |
| 34 | "France's refusal drove Germany from the League" | ✅ The source's word is "excuse"; Hitler withdrew in October 1933 and left the League a week later | 10 |
| 35 | "The Depression caused fascism and therefore the war" | ✅ Mass unemployment was important in enabling Hitler to come to power; the mechanism belongs to other owners, and the Depression supplies timing, not direction | 11 |
| 36 | "The League imposed sanctions on Japan" | ✅ The question of economic sanctions, let alone military ones, was never even raised | 12 |
| 37 | "Lytton condemned Japan" | ✅ It found faults on both sides and proposed League government of Manchuria | 12 |
| 38 | "Manchuria destroyed the League" | ✅ Prestige damaged, though not yet fatally; Abyssinia was the most serious blow | 12, 13 |
| 39 | "The League imposed no sanctions on Italy" | ✅ It did; they excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned weeks after the May 1936 conquest | 13 |
| 40 | "Italy was always aligned with Germany" | ✅ In July 1934 Mussolini sent three divisions to the Austrian frontier and the Austrian Nazi attempt collapsed | 13, 14 |
| 41 | "The Stresa Front was broken by Abyssinia" | ✅ It was broken in June 1935 by the Anglo-German Naval Agreement, signed without consulting France and Italy | 13 |
| 42 | "The Rhineland breached Versailles only" | ✅ It breached both Versailles and Locarno, and the troops had orders to withdraw at the first sign of French opposition | 14 |
| 43 | "Italy joined the Anti-Comintern Pact in 1936" | ✅ Germany and Japan in 1936; Italy joined in 1937 | 14 |
| 44 | "Hitler ordered the July 1934 Austrian coup" | ✅ The local Nazis took the initiative; he probably knew something of their plans, was not prepared to help, and denied responsibility | 14 |
| 45 | "Britain and France were neutral over Spain" | ✅ Non-intervention is recorded as an example of appeasement at work, while Germany and Italy sent decisive help | 15 |
| 46 | "Appeasement was cowardice" | ✅ Six recorded justifications; the named appeasers were convinced their policy was right | 16 |
| 47 | "Britain and France pursued the same policy" | ✅ Appeasement was mainly British; Poincaré, Briand and Barthou are recorded exceptions, and France fell in behind Britain only after the Hoare-Laval failure | 17 |
| 48 | "Czechoslovakia could not have resisted" | ✅ Fortifications, an expanded army and hopes of French and Soviet help; it would certainly not have been a walkover | 18 |
| 49 | "Munich ended appeasement" | ✅ Prague in March 1939 ended it, when even the appeasers could not justify the seizure of non-German territory | 18 |
| 50 | "The Nazi-Soviet Pact was an ideological impossibility" | ✅ Rapallo 1922 and the Treaty of Berlin 1926 created a special relationship that Hitler ended in January 1934 | 19 |

## Drill set B - one-line corrections

⚠️ Each line below is wrong. Supply the corrected version in one sentence before checking.

1. "Locarno guaranteed Germany's frontiers." → ✅ Locarno guaranteed the Franco-German and Belgo-German frontiers with Britain and Italy as guarantors and expressly did not guarantee Germany's eastern frontiers.
2. "The League expelled Japan in 1933." → ✅ Japan rejected the Lytton proposal and withdrew.
3. "Sanctions failed to stop Italy because the League had no army." → ✅ Sanctions failed because they excluded oil, coal and steel and were abandoned weeks after the conquest.
4. "Chamberlain invented appeasement." → ✅ He gave it new drive from May 1937; the first phase runs from the mid-1920s.
5. "France did nothing about German rearmament." → ✅ Barthou told the British that France refused to legalise German rearmament, and Laval signed an alliance with Russia in May 1935.
6. "The Franco-Soviet pact committed both to military action." → ✅ It contained no provision for military co-operation.
7. "The USSR refused to help Czechoslovakia." → ✅ Its 1935 obligation was conditional on France assisting first.
8. "Munich gave Germany all of Czechoslovakia." → ✅ The Sudetenland immediately, with Teschen to Poland and South Slovakia to Hungary, and a four-power guarantee of the rest.
9. "Britain declared war when Germany invaded Poland." → ✅ The invasion began on 1 September; the ultimatum expired at 11 a.m. on 3 September.
10. "The Depression is explained in this topic." → ✅ Its causes and instruments belong to the world-economy owner; this topic uses the credit-withdrawal mechanism and its diplomatic effects.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## The three questions and the framing discipline

- Institution, doctrine and responsibility are three different questions with different evidence and different verdicts. Say which one you are answering in the first sentence.
- Institution: a standing body with organs and rules. Doctrine: collective security. Order: the whole set of arrangements including credit. Instrument: a named agreement with stated obligations. Policy: appeasement. Do not use the words interchangeably.
- The period divides at January 1933, with the first phase subdivided 1919-23, 1924-29 and 1930-33.
- Boundaries: the settlement to the previous owner; the regimes to the fascism owner; the Depression's mechanics to the world-economy owner; the war's course and the United Nations to their owners; India to the Modern Indian History owners, in one sentence.

## The inheritance of 1920

- Security terms France obtained: German army capped at 100 000; Rhineland demilitarised 50 km east; the Saar to France for fifteen years; a British and American promise of help.
- March 1920: the US Senate rejects the settlement and the League; the guarantee lapses; Britain cancels its promise; the French feel betrayed.
- Reparations fixed 1921 at £6600 million over sixty-six annual instalments, designed to keep Germany weak for that period.
- The structural divergence: France wanted a weak Germany, Britain a solvent one able to buy British exports.
- Dissatisfied parties: Turkey; Italy (Fiume, Corfu); Germany (reparations); Soviet Russia (excluded and intervened against).
- Successor states - Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia, Austria, Hungary, Poland - with four recorded weaknesses; only Czechoslovakia's democracy survived to March 1939.
- Czechoslovak composition: 6.5m Czechs, 2.5m Slovaks, 3m Germans, 700 000 Hungarians, 500 000 Ruthenians, 100 000 Poles; about three-quarters of old Habsburg industry.
- Washington 1921-22: three-fifths naval ratio; Shantung returned; Pacific mandates retained; no new bases near Japan; a four-power guarantee of Chinese neutrality - leaving Japan supreme regionally.

## The League: machinery, record and limits

- Formally in existence 10 January 1920, the day Versailles came into operation; headquarters Geneva; 42 members at the start, 55 by 1926.
- Collective authorship - Cecil, Smuts, Bourgeois, Hymans, Wilson; Wilson's contribution was the Covenant written into each treaty.
- Two aims: peace through collective security by economic or military sanctions; international co-operation on economic and social problems.
- Assembly: annual, all members, one vote each, unanimous, could propose treaty revision, handled finances, gave small states a platform. Council: at least three meetings a year, four permanent members (Britain, France, Italy, Japan), four elected rising to nine by 1926, unanimous. Permanent Court: the Hague, fifteen judges, functioning 1922, sixty-six cases by 1939. Secretariat: staff from over thirty countries. Commissions: mandates, military affairs, minorities, disarmament.
- The Council *recommends* force contributions; Article 16 *expects* troops; the 1923 resolution left each member to decide for itself - which made nonsense of the idea.
- Successes: ILO under Albert Thomas; Nansen's refugee work, about half a million returned; the typhus campaign; the Mandates Commission; the Saar and its 1935 plebiscite; Aaland 1920, Upper Silesia 1921, the Greek-Bulgarian frontier, Mosul, and South American cases.
- The qualification: none of those disputes threatened world peace; none of the decisions went against a major state.
- Overruled twice by the Conference of Ambassadors - Vilna 1920 and Corfu 1923 - with no League response; supporters called them teething troubles.
- Balfour, 1923: no perfect guarantee against a powerful nation pursuing a realpolitik.
- Seven recorded weaknesses: the Versailles linkage; US rejection; Germany and the USSR excluded; the Conference of Ambassadors; Covenant defects; a French/British affair; the economic crisis from 1929.
- Geneva Protocol 1924: MacDonald's arbitration-and-assistance pledge, rejected by the succeeding Conservative government unwilling to commit Britain and the Empire to all the 1919 frontiers; the League left "lacking teeth"; the attitude was "leave it to the others".
- The qualification and the verdict: none of the weaknesses necessarily doomed it, and between 1925 and 1930 events ran fairly smoothly; but the League was only as strong as the determination of its leading members, which was lacking in the 1930s.

## Reparations, the Ruhr and the American connection

- Genoa 1922 failed on all three of its objects; Russia and Germany withdrew and signed Rapallo.
- Rapallo, Easter Sunday 1922: relations restored; mutual reparations claims cancelled; co-operation to keep Poland weak; a buffer against the west; German aircraft and ammunition factories and officer training in Russia; Russian grain in return. Renewed by the Treaty of Berlin 1926.
- January 1923: French and Belgian troops occupy the Ruhr; passive resistance, strikes and sabotage; over a hundred deaths; goods worth about £40 million; the mark valueless by November 1923; Britain disapproves.
- France's three methods - weakness, alliances, reconciliation - all failed. Little Entente: Poland 1921, Czechoslovakia 1924, Romania 1926, Yugoslavia 1927; impressive on paper.
- Dawes 1924: no reduction in the total; payment by capacity; 800 million gold marks mostly American; the Ruhr evacuated; the German economy recovers on American loans.
- US policy: politically isolationist at every decision point, economically central throughout. War loans of almost twelve billion dollars; Harding and Coolidge insisted on repayment; the circular flow - America to Germany, Germany to France and Britain, they to America - alongside high tariffs; strict isolation resumed over Manchuria.
- The statutory form of that isolation: Neutrality Act of 31 August 1935 (arms embargo on belligerents, export licences, travel at own risk); February 1936 renewal to May 1937 adding a ban on loans; the 1937 Act (no travel on belligerent ships, no American carriage of arms, presidential power to bar belligerent ships and widen the embargo, civil wars included, and a two-year cash-and-carry concession for non-arms goods); November 1939 (embargo lifted, all belligerent trade under cash-and-carry, loan ban retained, American ships barred from belligerent ports). Neutral in form, unequal in effect, and relaxed only after this topic closes.

## Locarno and the end of the reconstruction

- Locarno 1925: Germany, France and Belgium respect their joint frontiers, with Britain and Italy assisting whichever is attacked; arbitration agreements with Poland and Czechoslovakia; Germany would **not** guarantee the eastern frontiers and Britain gave no guarantee; France promised help to Poland and Czechoslovakia.
- The honeymoon; Nobel Peace Prizes for Locarno in two successive years - 1925 to Austen Chamberlain with Charles G. Dawes, 1926 to Briand and Stresemann; League entry 1926 with a permanent Council seat; September 1926 agreement on the Rhineland withdrawal.
- The glaring omission covered the very areas where trouble was most likely; the British thereby gave the impression they might not act; Austen Chamberlain's grenadier remark; it seemed to the Germans that Britain had turned her back on eastern Europe.
- Taylor: Briand and Stresemann were sincere but did not carry their peoples with them. Stresemann was privately determined on redrawing the Polish frontier, union with Austria and revision of Versailles.
- Kellogg-Briand 1928: Briand's bilateral idea universalised by Kellogg; sixty-five signatories; no sanctions clause; Japan signed and attacked China three years later.
- Young Plan 1929: chaired by Owen Young; £6600m reduced to £2000m over fifty-nine years; the Keynes figure; the Nazi party campaigned against it as too little.
- October 1929: Stresemann dies aged fifty-one; the Wall Street Crash. By 1932 over six million unemployed in Germany. Lausanne 1932 releases Germany from most remaining payments. January 1933, Hitler becomes Chancellor.

## Disarmament and the Depression

- All members had undertaken to reduce armaments; the Disarmament Commission made no progress; only Germany had moved, as Stresemann regularly pointed out; world arms spending rose from $3.5 billion in 1925 to around $5 billion in 1933.
- Geneva 1932-33: German equality demanded; French postponement for at least eight years, from alarm at the Nazi rise; British imperial rearmament. Hitler withdrew in October 1933, using the French attitude as an excuse; Germany left the League a week later.
- The technique on display: bold action with conciliatory speeches of the kind opponents wished to hear.
- The Depression's mechanism belongs to the world-economy owner: lending stopped and short-term loans were called in.
- Diplomatic effects: extreme right-wing governments in Japan and Germany; "every country for itself"; over Manchuria sanctions were never raised because Britain and France had serious economic problems and feared a war without American help; the 1931 Austro-German customs union blocked at the Hague after France withdrew its funds from the Kreditanstalt; Yugoslav exports over 40 per cent German-bound by 1938; Sudeten unemployment produced by the trade contraction.
- The Depression explains timing and intensity, not direction.

## The three tests

- **Manchuria 1931-33:** Japanese interests since the 1890s, Port Arthur, a province of thirty million; fear of a China strengthening under Chiang Kai-shek; Manchukuo declared; China appealed; Lytton reported faults on both sides in 1932 and proposed League government; Japan withdrew March 1933; sanctions never even raised; prestige damaged but not yet fatally; both Hitler and Mussolini took note. Advance from 1933; full invasion July 1937 after the Peking incident, Konoye overruled by Sugiyama; Shanghai, Nanking and Hankow taken; Chungking the new capital; the Sino-Japanese War eventually became part of the Second World War.
- **Abyssinia 1935-36:** July 1934 Mussolini's three divisions at the Austrian frontier; March 1935 German conscription; April 1935 Stresa with Abyssinia unmentioned; June 1935 the Anglo-German Naval Agreement at 35 per cent, signed without consulting France and Italy, destroying the Front; October 1935 invasion; Adowa 1896; Haile Selassie; sanctions excluding oil, coal and steel; conquest complete May 1936; sanctions abandoned. **Three recorded results:** Mussolini drew closer to Hitler; small states lost all faith; Hitler was encouraged to breach Versailles, with the rider that neither breach was raised at the Council. Chronology flag: conscription (March 1935) precedes the invasion (October 1935), so carry result three forwards to the Rhineland of March 1936 only. After 1935 the League was never taken seriously again. Italy's trajectory completes in 1939 - Albania occupied in April, already under Italian economic control and taken in imitation of Prague; the Pact of Steel in May, promising full military support if war came.
- **Spain 1936-39:** Germany and Italy helped Franco, the USSR helped the Republicans, Britain and France refused to intervene; Mussolini sought a third fascist state and naval bases against France; combat experience gained; Guernica bombed by the Condor Legion; the horrors became the first recorded justification of appeasement; Japan exploited the preoccupation in 1937.

## German revisionism and appeasement

- Recorded aims: destroy Versailles; build the army; recover the Saar and the Corridor; bring all German-speakers into the Reich. Beyond that, contested: Lebensraum to the Urals, then colonies and Atlantic bases - denied by Taylor, qualified by Broszat, doubted by Mazower.
- Nine steps: League exit October 1933; Polish pact January 1934, ruining the Little Entente; the failed Austrian coup of July 1934, the one instance of resistance; Saar January 1935 at 90 per cent; conscription March 1935, 36 divisions and about 600 000 men; the naval agreement June 1935; the Rhineland March 1936, breaching Versailles and Locarno, with withdrawal orders and a 25-year peace offer; Axis and Anti-Comintern 1936-37; Anschluss March 1938. End-1938: 51 divisions, about 800 000 men plus reserves, 21 large naval vessels, 47 U-boats, over 5000 aircraft - achieved without war and with the approval of Britain.
- Appeasement: avoiding war with aggressive powers by giving way, **provided the demands were not too unreasonable**. Two phases, the second from May 1937. Six justifications: the cost of war and pacifist opinion (Oxford Union February 1933; Baldwin November 1935); genuine grievances; the League's helplessness and leader-to-leader contact; economic co-operation; fear of communism; military unreadiness. Appeasers named: MacDonald, Baldwin, Simon, Hoare, Neville Chamberlain.
- Six examples at work: no action on rearmament (Lothian, January 1935); the naval agreement; half-hearted action over Abyssinia; no French mobilisation over the Rhineland (Londonderry's telegram; Lothian's "back garden"); non-intervention in Spain and the April 1938 recognition of Italian Abyssinia; protest only at the Anschluss.
- The French counter-current: Poincaré; Briand drawing the line at the 1931 customs union; **Barthou** in 1934 - firmness, an anti-German group with Italy and the USSR, Russia into the League in September 1934, a refusal to legalise German rearmament - assassinated at Marseilles in October 1934 with King Alexander of Yugoslavia; Laval's May 1935 Russian alliance without military provision; after the Hoare-Laval failure the French fell in behind the British.
- Four British constraints survive testing - multi-front incapacity, American isolation, French weakness, pacifist opinion. Two do not - that delay favoured Britain, and that no Grand Alliance existed.

## The test year and the end

- March 1938 Anschluss; Czechoslovakia now open from the south as well as the west and north.
- 3.5 million Sudeten Germans in the campaign and audit passages, against 3 million in the composition table and the summary sentence - a discrepancy inside the same account, displayed and not reconciled; Henlein's party second largest in the lower house with 44 seats in 1935; the unemployment was a Depression effect on industrial areas, not discrimination.
- Berchtesgaden 15 September; Godesberg a week later with raised demands; Czech mobilisation; fortifications and an expanded army meant it would not have been a walkover.
- Munich 29 September 1938: a plan produced by Mussolini but written by the German Foreign Office; the Sudetenland immediately; Teschen to Poland; South Slovakia to Hungary; a four-power guarantee of the rest; neither Czechs nor Russians invited; the Czechs told they would get no help though France had guaranteed their frontiers at Locarno; Benes resigned.
- The "scrap of paper"; "peace for our time"; Churchill's "total and unmitigated defeat"; Duff Cooper's resignation; the losses of 70 per cent of heavy industry, a third of the population, roughly a third of the territory and almost all the fortifications.
- 15 March 1939: Tiso, Hacha, Goering's threat, the occupation with the army in barracks, the protectorate, Slovakia protected, Ruthenia to Hungary; Chamberlain's "by invitation" defence; Kershaw on the German warships on the Danube; even the appeasers could not justify it; Chamberlain's attitude hardened.
- Soviet options narrowed from two to one: Rapallo and Berlin; the cooling from 1930; the League in 1934; the weak 1935 pacts; Munich exclusion; slow 1939 talks. The pact of 23 August 1939 with a secret partition protocol - one passage dates that protocol to 24 August.
- Memel; Danzig demanded April 1939, recorded as in fact not unreasonable; Beck's refusal, fearing another Munich; the invasion of 1 September; the ultimatum expiring at 11 a.m. on 3 September; France following.
- Italy inside the same test year: Albania occupied April 1939 - the same month as the Danzig demand - and the Pact of Steel signed in May 1939, converting an understanding into a treaty promise of full military support.

## Judgement

- Background causes create atmosphere and tensions, but something more was needed; and by the end of 1938 the grievances had been removed and Germany was a great power again.
- Necessity test: settlement contributory; League permissive; Depression conditionally necessary but directionless; appeasement enabling and contested; German aims the necessary cause.
- Henig: a bold step towards international co-operation that failed in some aims and succeeded comprehensively in others; the Assembly, Council and Secretariat adopted as a basis by the United Nations; the International Court reproducing the Permanent Court almost identically; the ILO still operating.
- Blame tree: Bullock and the wrong-issue criticism against the appeasers; Charmley, Roberts and Self in defence; Overy's reminder that Britain and France declared war in their own interest; the Soviet charge and the time-for-defence defence, which covers neutrality but not partition; Gilbert, Trevor-Roper, the Hossbach Memorandum and the documentary case; Taylor 1961 and its reception; Broszat, Mazower, Gregor, Tooze, Overy on the war economy, Jäckel and Kershaw.
- Counterfactuals: the Rhineland best supported; Munich strong but contested; the Grand Alliance disputed; an earlier Soviet alliance available but with an unknowable outcome.
- Attribution: governments, cabinets, ministries, parties and named individuals - never nations or peoples.

## Answer architecture in one page

- Four thesis templates - League, Locarno, appeasement, origins - learned verbatim.
- Six directive rows; mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20; four evidence banks.
- Nineteen factual-risk controls, of which Locarno's scope, the Kellogg sanctions clause, the two reparations figures, the September 1939 dates and the Abyssinia-conscription chronology account for most avoidable losses.
- Seven handovers, one clause each.
- Name a historian only where the name marks a disagreement; at most four in a 250-word answer.

## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf`, read through the locally held full-text extraction at `attempts\_gs_source_text\WORLD_HISTORY_NORMAN_LOWE_ENGLISH_pdf.txt` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. The three chapters both canonical owners cite - the League of Nations, international relations 1919-33 and international relations 1933-9 - were read in full across the line span recorded in the owners' file headers. They supplied: the League's origins, aims, five organs, decision rules, intended peacekeeping mechanism and great-power membership figure; the full successes section including the ILO under Albert Thomas, the Nansen refugee work, the Health Organization, the Mandates Commission, the Saar and the failed Disarmament Commission; the resolved political disputes and the qualification attached to them; the Vilna and Corfu overrulings and Balfour's remark; the seven-part failure analysis with Article 16, the 1923 resolution, the Geneva Protocol episode and the "leave it to the others" formulation; Henig's corrective with its quoted passages and the institutional transmission to the United Nations; the three-phase account of 1919-33; the Washington Conferences with their five terms and their strategic reality; Genoa and Rapallo; the Dawes Plan with its four terms; the Locarno Treaties with their parties, their guarantee structure, their eastern omission and the later historians' verdict; Kellogg-Briand with its origin, its sixty-five signatories and its missing sanctions clause; the Young Plan with its figures and the Nazi campaign against it; Stresemann's death and the Crash; the Lausanne release; the World Disarmament Conference with the three positions and the arms-expenditure figures; France's three methods with the peace terms, the reparations figure, Poincaré and the Ruhr, the Little Entente and the customs union episode; the USSR's relations with Britain, Germany and France including the Comintern, Rapallo, the Treaty of Berlin and the post-1930 cooling; the successor states with the Yugoslav, Czechoslovak, Polish, Austrian and Hungarian cases and their recorded figures; United States foreign policy 1919-33 with the war-debt total, the circular flow, the tariff point and the return to strict isolation; the Manchurian crisis with the Lytton Commission, the never-raised sanctions and the 1933-38 advance; Mussolini's foreign policy before and after 1934 with the Dollfuss episode, Stresa, the Abyssinian motives, the Axis, Albania and the Pact of Steel; Hitler's recorded aims with the scholarly disagreement and the nine-step sequence with its rearmament totals; the appeasement section with its definition, two phases, six justifications, named appeasers, French counter-current and six examples; the Czechoslovak crisis from the Sudeten campaign to Munich and the March 1939 occupation, with Kershaw's qualification; the Polish sequence from Memel to the declarations of 3 September; and the entire blame chapter with Bullock, Charmley, Roberts, Self, Overy, Gilbert, Trevor-Roper, Taylor, Broszat, Mazower, Gregor, Tooze, Jäckel and Kershaw.
2. **Period control on the book.** Only those three chapters were used. Nothing from the book's chapters on the war of 1914-18, the peace settlements, the Second World War, the fascist regimes, the Russian Revolution, the United Nations, the Cold War or the world economy appears anywhere in this session, because all of it belongs to other owners.
3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook, monograph or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.
4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above, from the canonical owners, or - in the single current linkage of Lesson 8 - from the official page identified in the live-source table. Participant quotations are confined to Smuts on the great miracles of history, Balfour on realpolitik, Austen Chamberlain on the British grenadier, Lord Lothian on Hitler's intentions and on the back garden, Mussolini on the Axis line, Chamberlain on peace for our time, and Churchill on a total and unmitigated defeat. Historians are quoted only where the core account itself quotes them - Henig, Taylor, Bullock, Gilbert, Overy, Gregor, Tooze, Jäckel and Kershaw - and each is reproduced as the account records it.
5. **Extraction-damage control.** One page of the extraction, covering the consequences of the Abyssinian invasion and the Anglo-French proposal associated with Hoare and Laval, is badly degraded in the copy held here. The gap is declared in Lesson 13 section 4 and in the coverage matrix; only the legible fragments are used; the existence and failure of the Hoare-Laval Pact are taken from a separate, legible passage in the same account; and **no figure, date or territorial description from the damaged page is supplied**.
6. **Internal-inconsistency control.** Five disagreements inside the evidence base are displayed rather than resolved: the dating of the secret protocol partitioning Poland, which the Poland narrative gives as 24 August while the blame chapter records the pact and its secret agreement as 23 August; the departure cell for Italy in the great-power membership figure, which is ambiguous in this copy and for which **no year is asserted**; the description of the Marseilles assassination of October 1934, which the appeasement chapter attributes to Croat terrorists while the successor-states chapter records the king's murderer as a Macedonian connected with Croat revolutionaries living in Hungary; the inclusion of the March 1935 conscription announcement among the results of an invasion that began in October 1935, which is a chronological impossibility and is disclosed in Lesson 13 rather than repeated; and the Sudeten German population figure, given as 3 million in the national-composition table and the chapter summary and as 3.5 million in the Sudeten-campaign section and the end-1938 audit. All five are flagged in the lessons, recorded in the factual-risk controls or the attribution discipline, and listed in the coverage matrix.
7. **One correction made against the core account.** The account states that Stresemann, Briand and Austen Chamberlain were "jointly awarded the Nobel Peace Prize". The award record shows two prizes in successive years: the 1925 Peace Prize to Austen Chamberlain, cited for his role in bringing about the Locarno Treaty, shared with Charles G. Dawes, cited for the Dawes Plan; and the 1926 Peace Prize shared by Aristide Briand and Gustav Stresemann, both cited for Locarno. The correction is stated in Lesson 8 section 3 with its basis, carried into the Lesson 9 visual, the Lesson 8 revision notes, the register notes and Mains Question 2, and recorded as factual-risk control 15. It is the only place in this session where a held source is corrected rather than displayed.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning session and solved workbook for this topic and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates it. The complete-topic-package file was opened only to confirm the recorded PYQ-integrity statement quoted in the verified-question section, and no teaching content from it appears anywhere.
- No workbook operation of any kind was performed: no objective corpus was constructed, copied, relocated or migrated, and no solved-practice workbook was created. This session is in concept-check mode, with exactly one concept check, one model answer and one misconception note in each of its twenty-one lessons, and **zero objective items anywhere in the file**.
- No model answer is supplied anywhere for a verified question, because this topic owns none. The seven model answers in this file belong to original questions written for this session and are labelled as such.
- The only quantities in this session are those the held sources record, together with the statutory contents of the registered material-fact extension: the reparations figures of £6600 million and £2000 million and the Versailles army limit of 100 000; the Dawes loan of 800 million gold marks and the Ruhr seizure of about £40 million; American war loans of almost twelve billion dollars; the arms-expenditure rise from $3.5 billion to around $5 billion between 1925 and 1933; the naval ratios of three-fifths and 35 per cent; the German military figures of 36 divisions and about 600 000 men in 1935 and 51 divisions, about 800 000 men, 21 large naval vessels, 47 U-boats and over 5000 aircraft by the end of 1938; membership counts of 42 and 55 and the Permanent Court's 66 cases; the Manchurian population of thirty million; the 3 million and 3.5 million Sudeten German figures, both held and displayed as a discrepancy, and Henlein's 44 seats; the Czechoslovak national composition and the Munich losses of 70 per cent of heavy industry, a third of the population and roughly a third of the territory; the Saar plebiscite result of 90 per cent; German unemployment of over six million by 1932; the Yugoslav export share of over 40 per cent by 1938; and Tooze's and Overy's two-thirds and one-quarter figures on German war-economy investment and employment. **No other number appears anywhere**, and no casualty figure of any kind is given, estimated or implied.
- Attribution is disciplined throughout: responsibility is stated about governments, cabinets, foreign ministries, general staffs, parties, named organisations and named individuals, and never about nations or peoples. Where a held source uses a collective national formulation - most notably in the reported reception of Taylor's argument - the formulation is reported as that source's wording and is explicitly **not** adopted as this session's own.
- The atrocities recorded in China in 1937-38 are stated as the source records them and are attributed to the army and the named ministers responsible, with the recorded fact that the prime minister opposed the escalation and was overruled by the war minister.
- No claim is made anywhere about the internal politics of Spain, the internal history of the USSR, the domestic systems of the fascist and militarist regimes, the causes of the Great Depression, the terms of the 1919-23 settlement beyond those still in dispute after 1920, the military course of the Second World War or the design and record of the United Nations. Each is named where it arises and routed to its owner.
- Contested points are displayed rather than resolved: whether the aims beyond 1938 were programmatic; whether appeasement was a necessary cause of the war; whether a Grand Alliance was available; whether the time bought after Munich favoured Britain or Germany; whether the Soviet motive in August 1939 was defensive; and the five internal discrepancies recorded above.
- Remaining open uncertainty, recorded rather than filled: the terms on which an Anglo-Soviet alliance might have been concluded in 1939; the military feasibility of a French operation in March 1936; the comparative rearmament rates of Britain and Germany between September 1938 and September 1939; the internal deliberations of the British cabinet at Munich; the extent of German involvement in the Sudeten disturbances of 1938; the detailed content of the Hoare-Laval proposal, which the damaged extraction page does not supply; and whether any of the four identified alternative policies would in fact have prevented a general war.
- **Named omitted items, enumerated rather than passed over.** The held sources do not supply the following standard interwar items, each of which is named here so that the coverage claims above are read as bounded rather than as complete: the text and operation of **Article 10** of the Covenant, the territorial-integrity and political-independence guarantee, which this session therefore never cites although it is the clause most often quoted against Article 16; the **Stimson non-recognition doctrine** of 1932, the American response to Manchukuo; the **Four-Power Pact of 1933**; the **Balkan Entente of 1934**; the **Franco-Italian agreements of January 1935** that preceded Stresa; the detailed terms of the **Hoare-Laval proposal**, lost to the damaged page; the **Nyon Conference of September 1937** on Mediterranean shipping; the **Bank for International Settlements** created under the Young Plan; the League's **minorities-petition procedure**; and the **Anglo-Italian agreement of 1938** beyond the recorded recognition of Italian Abyssinia in April of that year. ⚠️ Each is judged non-essential to the six demand families this topic must support, and none is described, dated beyond the year given here, or used as evidence anywhere in this session. ⚠️ A candidate meeting any of them elsewhere should treat this session as silent on it rather than as having ruled it out.
